# PREFACE.

<page-num>928</page-num>

<image-desc>A square ink seal in the top left corner containing four stylized Chinese characters in seal script: 釋松源色 <note>original: "釋松源色" (Shì Sōngyuán Sè), which translates roughly to "Buddhist [Monk] Songyuan's Form" or "The Appearance of the Buddhist Pine Source." This is likely a personal or temple ownership seal.</note></image-desc>

<margin>BL 1451 S84</margin>

The purpose of this book is twofold: (1) To disprove the many incorrect opinions held by Western critics regarding the fundamental teachings of Mahayana Buddhism <term>Mahayana</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "Mahāyāna," meaning "Great Vehicle." This branch of Buddhism emphasizes the universal path to enlightenment and the compassion of Bodhisattvas.</note>; (2) To awaken interest among scholars of comparative religion in the development of religious feeling and faith, as shown by the growth of one of the world's most powerful spiritual forces. The book is, therefore, both accessible to the general reader and academically rigorous. It is accessible in that it seeks to reveal the errors in the general attitude held by followers of other religions toward Mahayana Buddhism. On the other hand, it aims to be scholarly by attempting to explain some of the most prominent <note>original: "salient"</note> features of the doctrine from both a historical and systematic perspective.

In attempting to achieve this latter goal, however, the author makes no grand claims. It is impossible to present, within this limited space, all the data necessary for a complete and systematic explanation <note>original: "elucidation"</note> of Mahayana Buddhism. Its history began in the sixth century before the Common Era <note>original: "Christian era"</note> and spanned a period of more than two thousand years before it took the form in which it is currently taught in the East <note>original: "the Orient"</note>. During this long period, the Mahayana...

<vocab>Mahayana Buddhism, comparative religion, doctrine, East</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Suzuki outlines his two main goals: to correct Western misconceptions about Mahayana Buddhism and to encourage scholarly study of its historical and spiritual development. He acknowledges the difficulty of summarizing over two millennia of Buddhist history in a single volume.</summary>

<keywords>Mahayana Buddhism, Western critics, comparative religion, religious sentiment, Buddhist history, Daisetz Teitaro Suzuki, Orient, doctrine</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of D.T. Suzuki's "Outlines of Mahayana Buddhism." It follows the discussion of the long history of the tradition, explaining why the system is so complex and why it remains misunderstood in the West.</meta>

<page-num>VI</page-num>

<meta>Faint institutional stamp in the upper right margin. Pencil brackets frame the final paragraph.</meta>

This doctrine was developed <note>original: "elaborated"</note> by the greatest thinkers that India, Tibet, China, and Japan ever produced. It is no wonder, then, that so many diverse and apparently contradictory teachings are all grouped under the general name of Mahayana Buddhism. To explain <note>original: "expound"</note> all these theories, even briefly, would be completely outside the scope of a work like this. All I could hope to do was to discuss a few of the most general and essential topics of Mahayana Buddhism <note>original: "Mahâyânism"</note>, making this a sort of introduction to a more detailed explanation of the system as a whole, as well as its specific parts.

To achieve my first goal, I have occasionally gone outside the specific boundaries I set for this work. But this detour seemed necessary because some critics are so biased <note>original: "prejudiced"</note> that they fail to understand even seemingly obvious truths. I may have my own biases, but I have often wondered how completely and miserably some people can be misled by self-delusion.

The history of Mahayana Buddhist doctrine is very little known to Western <note>original: "Occidental"</note> scholars. This is mainly due to the difficulty of accessing materials that are mostly written in the Chinese language <note>original: "tongue"</note>, which is one of the hardest languages for foreigners to master. In this age of widespread education <note>original: "liberal culture"</note>, it is a great pity that so few of the "precious stones" (valuable insights) contained in the religion of Buddha are available to people in the West. Human nature is essentially the same the world over, and

<insert>Whenever and wherever conditions mature, we see the same spiritual phenomena; and this fact always <note>original: "ever"</note> strengthens our faith in the universality of truth and in the ultimate reign of lovingkindness <note>A term often used to translate the Buddhist concept of *maitri* or *metta*, meaning universal benevolence and goodwill</note>.</insert> It is my sincere desire that, as far as my intellectual attainments <note>original: "attainment"</note> permit, I shall be allowed to pursue my study and to share my findings with my fellow human beings. <margin>]</margin>

In concluding this preface <note>original: "prelude"</note>, the author wishes to say that this little book is presented to the public with a full knowledge of its many defects. I will not fail to make use of every opportunity offered to me to revise these shortcomings.

->Daisetz T. Suzuki.<-

# Introduction

## 1. Mahayana and Hinayana Buddhism <note>Mahayana (The "Greater Vehicle") and Hinayana (The "Lesser Vehicle") represent the two major historical traditions of Buddhist thought and practice.</note>

The terms "Mahayana" and "Hinayana" may sound unfamiliar to most of our readers, perhaps even to those who have spent some time studying Buddhism. Until now, they may have been led to believe that there is only one form of Buddhism and that distinctions such as "Mahayanism" and "Hinayanism" do not exist. But, in fact, there are diverse schools in Buddhism, just as there are in other religious systems. It is said that within a few hundred years after the death <note>original: demise</note> of the Buddha, there were more than twenty different schools,¹ all claiming...

***

¹ According to Vasumitra’s *Treatise on the Points of Contention by the Different Schools of Buddhism*—of which there are three Chinese translations, the earliest being by Kumarajiva (who came to China in 401 AD)—the first great split <note>original: schism</note> seems to have broken out about one hundred years after the Buddha's time. The leader of the dissenters was Mahadeva, and his school was known as the <term>Mahasanghika</term> <note>original: Mahâsangîka; meaning "The Great Council"</note>, while the orthodox group was called the school of the <term>Sthaviras</term> <note>original: Sthaviras; meaning "The Elders"</note>. Since then, the two schools divided themselves into a number of minor sections, twenty of which are mentioned by Vasumitra. The book is highly interesting for the light it sheds on the early history of Buddhism in India.

<summary>This page introduces the primary distinction between Mahayana and Hinayana Buddhism and discusses the historical schisms that led to the formation of over twenty different Buddhist schools shortly after the Buddha's death.</summary>
<keywords>Mahayana, Hinayana, Buddhist history, schism, Mahasanghika, Sthaviras, Vasumitra, Kumarajiva</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction's historical overview, transitioning from the initial diverse schools of Buddhism to the emergence of the two primary systems: Mahayanism and Hinayanism.</meta>

# Introduction

...to be the orthodox teaching of their master. These schools, however, seem to have vanished into insignificance one after another when a new school arose, quite different in its general constitution from its predecessors, but far more important in its significance as a religious movement. This new school—or rather, system—made itself so prominent in the meantime that it stood distinctly apart from all the other schools; these older schools eventually became a class of their own. Essentially, it taught everything that was considered to be Buddhist, but it was very comprehensive in its principles, methods, and scope. Because of this, Buddhism was now split into two great systems, <term>Mahayanism</term> and <term>Hinayanism</term>. The latter term indiscriminately included all the minor schools that had preceded Mahayanism in their formal establishment.

Broadly speaking, the difference between <term>Mahayanism</term> and <term>Hinayanism</term> is this: <term>Mahayanism</term> is more liberal and progressive, but in many respects it is too metaphysical and full of speculative thoughts that frequently reach a dazzling height. <term>Hinayanism</term>, on the other hand, is somewhat conservative and may be considered in many respects to be simply a rationalistic ethical system.

<term>Mahayana</term> literally means "great vehicle" and <term>Hinayana</term> "small or inferior vehicle"—that is, a vehicle of salvation. This distinction is recognized only by the followers of Mahayanism, because it was they who gave the unwelcome title of "Hinayanism" to their rival brethren, thinking that they themselves were more pro-

<summary>This page describes the historical emergence of Mahayanism as a comprehensive and progressive religious system that eventually led to the categorization of all earlier schools as "Hinayanism." It defines the literal meanings of these terms as the "great" and "small" vehicles of salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Mahayanism, Hinayanism, Buddhist schools, Great Vehicle, Small Vehicle, religious evolution, Buddhist metaphysics, ethical systems</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction's discussion on the historical and geographical divide between Mahayanism and Hinayanism, noting the specific regions where each flourished and addressing potential inaccuracies in these classifications.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

...progressive and had a greater capacity for assimilation than the latter. The followers of <term>Hinayanism</term>, as a matter of course, refused to sanction the <term>Mahayanist</term> doctrine as the authentic teaching of Buddha, and insisted that there could not be any other Buddhism than their own; to them, naturally, the <term>Mahayana</term> system was a kind of heresy.

Geographically, the progressive school of Buddhism found its supporters in Nepal, Tibet, China, Korea, and Japan, while the conservative school established itself in Ceylon, <note>1</note> Siam, and Burma. Hence, <term>Mahayana</term> and <term>Hinayana</term> are also known respectively as Northern and Southern Buddhism.

*In passing*, let me remark that this distinction, however, is not quite correct, for we have some

---

<note>1 The <term>Anagarika Dharmapala</term> of Ceylon objects to this geographical distinction. # INTRODUCTION

schools in China and Japan whose equivalent or counterpart cannot be found in so-called <term>Northern Buddhism</term>—that is, the <term>Buddhism</term> flourishing in Northern India. For instance, we do not have in Nepal or in Tibet anything like the <term>Sukhâvatî</term> sects of Japan or China. Of course, the general essential ideas of the <term>Sukhâvatî</term> philosophy are found in the sutra literature as well as in the writings of such authors as <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>, <term>Asanga</term>, and <term>Nâgârjuna</term>. But those ideas were not developed and made into a new sect as they were in the East. Therefore, it may be more appropriate to divide <term>Buddhism</term> into three geographical sections instead of two: Southern, Northern, and Eastern.

### *Why the two Doctrines?*

In spite of this distinction, the two schools, <term>Hinayanism</term> and <term>Mahayanism</term>, are no more than two main branches of one original source, which was first discovered by <term>Çâkyamuni</term>; and, naturally, we find many common traits which are essential to both of them. The spirit that animated the innermost heart of Buddha is perceptible in Southern as well as in <term>Northern Buddhism</term>. The difference between them is not radical or qualitative, as some have imagined. It is due, on the one hand, to a general unfolding of the religious consciousness and a constant broadening of the intellectual horizon; and, on the other hand, to the conservative efforts to literally preserve the monastic rules and traditions. Both schools started with the same spirit, pursuing the

<summary>The author proposes a three-fold geographical division of Buddhism—Southern, Northern, and Eastern—to account for unique sects in China and Japan. Despite these divisions, both Hinayana and Mahayana schools are presented as sharing the same original spirit and source in the teachings of Çâkyamuni.</summary>
<keywords>Eastern Buddhism, Sukhâvatî, Çâkyamuni, Mahayanism, Hinayanism, religious evolution, monastic tradition, Açvaghoṣa, Asanga, Nâgârjuna</keywords>

same course. But after a while, one group felt no need to broaden the spirit of the master and adhered to his words as literally as possible; while the other, motivated by a liberal and comprehensive spirit, has drawn nourishment from all available sources in order to unfold the seeds in the original system that were vigorous and productive. These diverse inclinations among early Buddhists naturally led to the division of <term>Mahayanism</term> and <term>Hinayanism</term>.

We cannot provide detailed accounts here of what external and internal forces were acting within the body of <term>Buddhism</term> to produce the <term>Mahayana</term> system, or how it gradually unfolded itself to absorb and assimilate all the conflicting ideas that came into contact with it. It is enough to state and answer in general terms the question which is frequently asked by the uninitiated: “Why did <term>Buddhism</term> ever allow itself to be split into two systems, which are seemingly in conflict with each other on more than one point?” In other words, “How can there be two forms of <term>Buddhism</term> that both equally represent the true doctrine of the founder?”

The reason is clear enough. The teachings of a great religious founder are generally very broad, comprehensive, and multifaceted; therefore, they contain great possibilities for various liberal interpretations by his disciples. And it is this very comprehensiveness that enables followers of diverse needs, characters, and backgrounds to

->INTRODUCTION<-

satisfy their spiritual appetite universally and individually with the teachings of their master. This comprehensiveness, however, is not due to the leader’s intentional use of ambiguous terms, nor is it due to the obscurity and confusion of his own ideas. The initiator of a movement—spiritual as well as intellectual—has no time to think out all its possible details and consequences. When the principle of the movement is understood by his contemporaries and its foundation is solidly laid, his own role as initiator is accomplished; and the rest can safely be left to his successors. The latter will take up the work and carry it out in all its particulars, while making all necessary alterations and improvements according to circumstances. Therefore, the role to be played by the originator is necessarily indefinite and comprehensive.

Kant, for instance, as a promoter of <term>German philosophy</term>, has become the father of such diverse philosophical systems as those of Jacobi, Fichte, Hegel, Schopenhauer, etc., while each of them endeavored to develop some points indefinitely, covertly, or indirectly stated by Kant himself. Jesus of Nazareth, as the instigator of a revolutionary movement against <term>Judaism</term>, did not have any stereotyped theological doctrines, such as were established later by <term>Christian doctors</term> <note>learned teachers or theologians of the early Church</note>. The indefiniteness of his views was so apparent that it caused a sort of dissension even among his personal disciples, while a majority of his disciples cherished a visionary hope for the advent

of a divine kingdom on earth. But those externalities that are destined to pass away do not prevent the spirit of a movement, once awakened by a great leader, from growing more powerful and noble.

The same thing can be said of the teachings of the Buddha. What he inspired in his followers was the spirit of that religious system which is now known as Buddhism. Guided by this spirit, his followers individually developed his teachings as required by their special needs and circumstances, finally giving birth to the distinction between Mahâyânism and Hînayânism.

### *The Original Meaning of Mahâyâna.*

The term Mahâyâna was first used to designate the highest principle, or being, or knowledge, of which the universe—with all its sentient and non-sentient beings—is a manifestation, and through which only they can attain final salvation (<term>mokṣa</term> or <term>nirvâṇa</term>). Mahâyâna was not the name given to any religious doctrine, nor did it have anything to do with doctrinal controversy, though later it was utilized in that way by the progressive party.

Açvaghoṣa, the first Mahâyâna interpreter known to us—living about the time of Christ—used the term in his religious-philosophical book called *Discourse on the Awakening of Faith in the Mahâyâna* <note>Translated into English by the author in 1900. The Open Court Publishing Co., Chicago.</note> as synonymous with <term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term> or <term>Dharmakâyâ</term>, <note>These terms are explained elsewhere.</note> the

<summary>This page explains that while the external forms of a religious movement may change, its core spirit allows followers to adapt teachings to their needs, as seen in the development of Mahâyâna and Hînayâna Buddhism. It defines the original meaning of Mahâyâna as a supreme universal principle rather than a specific doctrinal label.</summary>

<keywords>Buddhism, Mahâyânism, Hînayânism, Mahâyâna, mokṣa, nirvâṇa, Açvaghoṣa, Awakening of Faith, Bhûtatathâtâ, Dharmakâyâ</keywords>

highest principle of Mahâyânism. He likened the recognition of, and faith in, this highest being and principle to a vehicle which will carry us safely across the stormy ocean of birth and death (*samsâra*) to the eternal shore of Nirvâna.

<margin>pencil bracket mark ></margin>
Soon after him, however, the controversy between the two schools of Buddhism—conservatives and progressives, as we might call them—became more and more pronounced. When it reached its climax, which was most probably in the times of Nâgârjuna and Âryadeva (that is, a few centuries after Açvaghoṣa), the progressive party ingeniously invented the term Hînayâna in contrast to Mahâyâna, the latter having been adopted by them as the watchword of their own school. The Hînayânists and the Tîrthakas <note>Followers of any religious sects other than Buddhism. The term is sometimes used in a contemptuous sense, like the word "heathen" used by Christians.</note> were then sweepingly condemned by the Mahâyânists as inadequate to achieve the universal salvation of sentient beings.

### *An Older Classification of Buddhists.*

<margin>pencil bracket mark ></margin>
Before the distinction between Mahâyânists and Hînayânists became definite—that is to say, at the time of Nâgârjuna or even before it—those Buddhists who held a more progressive and broader view tried to distinguish three *yânas* among the followers of the Buddha: namely, Bodhisattva-yâna, Pratyekabuddha-yâna, and Çrâvaka-yâna; *yâna* being another name for "class."

<keywords>Mahâyânism, Hînayâna, Nâgârjuna, Âryadeva, Açvaghoṣa, Tîrthakas, Bodhisattva-yâna, Pratyekabuddha-yâna, Çrâvaka-yâna, Buddhist schools</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of Buddhists into three groups (yânas), providing detailed definitions for the Bodhisattva, Pratyekabuddha, and Çrâvaka within the context of Mahâyâna thought.</meta>

->INTRODUCTION<-

The Bodhisattva is that class of Buddhists who, believing in the Bodhi (intelligence or wisdom), which is a reflection of the Dharmakâya in the human soul, direct all their spiritual energy toward realizing and developing it for the sake of their fellow creatures.

<margin><</margin>
The Pratyekabuddha is a “solitary thinker” or a philosopher who, retiring into solitude and calmly contemplating the fleeting nature of worldly pleasures, endeavors to attain his own salvation, but remains unconcerned with the sufferings of his fellow beings. From a religious perspective, a Pratyekabuddha is cold, impassive, and egotistical, lacking love for all mankind.

<margin><</margin>
The Çrâvaka, which means “hearer,” is considered inferior by Mahâyânists even to the Pratyekabuddha, for he does not possess an intellect that enables him to think independently and to find the way to final salvation by himself. Being endowed, however, with a pious heart, he is willing to listen to the instructions of the Buddha, to believe in him, and to faithfully observe all the moral precepts given by him; he rests fully content within the narrow horizon of his mediocre intellect.

<margin><</margin>
We devote a special chapter below to a further explanation of Bodhisattvahood and its important implications for Mahâyâna Buddhism. For Mahâyânism is nothing more than the Buddhism of Bodhisattvas, while the Pratyekabuddhas and the Çrâvakas are considered by Mahâyânists to be followers of Hînayânism.

It establishes that Mahâyâna Buddhism is primarily the path of the Bodhisattva, while the other two classes are categorized as Hînayânism.</summary>

## *The Mahâyâna Buddhism Defined.*

We can now form a somewhat definite notion of what Mahâyâna Buddhism is. It is the Buddhism which, inspired by a progressive spirit, broadened its original scope as long as it did not contradict the inner significance of the Buddha's teachings, and which assimilated other religious and philosophical beliefs whenever it felt that, by doing so, people of widely different characters and intellectual abilities could be saved. Let us be satisfied with this statement for now, until we enter into a more detailed explanation of its doctrinal peculiarities in the pages that follow.

It may not be out of place to remark, in passing, that the term Mahâyânism is used in this work merely to distinguish it from that form of Buddhism which flourishes in Ceylon, Burma, and other central Asiatic nations, and whose literature is principally written in the language called Pâli, which comes from the same stock as Sanskrit. The term “Mahâyâna,” as it is used here, does not imply any sense of superiority over the Hînayâna. When the historical aspect of Mahâyânism is discussed, it may naturally emerge that its over-zealous and one-sided devotees unnecessarily emphasized its controversial and dogmatic phases at the expense of its true spirit; but the reader must not think that this work has anything to do with those complications. In fact, Mahâyânism professes to be a boundless ocean in which all form

<summary>This page defines Mahâyâna Buddhism as a progressive and inclusive tradition that expanded its scope and assimilated various beliefs to reach a broader range of people. It also clarifies that "Mahâyâna" is used as a descriptive term to distinguish it from Pâli-based traditions, rather than to claim superiority over them.</summary>

## 2. IS MAHÂYÂNA BUDDHISM THE GENUINE TEACHING OF THE BUDDHA?

What is generally known to Western nations by the name of Buddhism is <term>Hînayânism</term>, whose scriptures, as stated above, are written in <term>Pâli</term> and studied mostly in Ceylon, Burma, and Siam. It was through this language that <term>Orientalists</term> acquired their first knowledge of Buddhism; naturally, they came to regard <term>Hînayânism</term> or Southern Buddhism as the only genuine teachings of the Buddha. They insisted, and some of them still insist, that to have an adequate and thorough knowledge of Buddhism, they must confine themselves solely to the study of <term>Pâli</term>. They argue that whatever may be learned from other sources—that is, from <term>Sanskrit</term>, <term>Tibetan</term>, or <term>Chinese</term> documents—should be considered as throwing only a side-light on the reliable information obtained from <term>Pâli</term>. They further believe that the knowledge derived from those other sources should, in certain cases, be discarded as accounts of a degenerate form of Buddhism. Because of these unfortunate hypotheses, the significance of <term>Mahâyânism</term> as a living religion has been entirely ignored; even those who are regarded as the best authorities on the subject appear to be greatly misinformed and, what is worse, altogether prejudiced.

### *No Life Without Growth.*

This is very unfair on the part of the critics, because what religion is there in the whole history of mankind that has not undergone any development whatever, that has remained the same, like granite, throughout its entire course? Let us ask whether there is any religion which has shown some signs of vitality and yet retained its primitive form intact and unmodified in every respect. Is not changeability—that is, responsiveness to stimuli—the most essential sign of vitality? Every organism grows, which means it changes in some way or another. There is no form of life to be found anywhere on earth that does not grow or change, or that does not have an inherent power of adjusting itself to surrounding conditions.

Take, for example, Christianity. Is Protestantism the genuine teaching of Jesus of Nazareth? Or does Catholicism represent his true spirit? Jesus himself did not have any definite notion of the <term>Trinity</term> doctrine, nor did he propose any suggestion for ritualism. According to the <term>Synoptics</term>, he appears to have cherished a rather immature conception of the <term>kingdom of God</term> rather than a purely ideal one as conceived by <term>Paul</term>; and his personal disciples, who were just as philosophically illiterate as the master himself, were in all probability anxiously waiting for its earthly realization. But what Christians, Catholics or Protestants, in these days of enlightenment, would dare

It suggests that the discrepancy between the original teachings of Jesus and later doctrines like the Trinity is a natural result of this growth.</summary>

Again, consider Jesus’s view on marriage and social life. Is it not an established fact that he strongly advocated for celibacy and, in the case of married people, strict continence, and also that he greatly favored pious poverty and asceticism in general? In these respects, the monks of the Middle Ages and the Catholic priests of the present day (though I cannot say they are ascetic and poor in their living) must be said to be in closer agreement with the teaching of the master than their Protestant brothers. But which Protestants would seriously venture to defend all those views of Jesus, despite their declared claim that they are sincerely following in the footsteps of their Lord? Taking everything into account, these contradictions do not prevent them—Protestants as well as Catholics—from calling themselves Christians and even good, pious, devoted Christians, as long as they are, consciously or unconsciously, animated by the same spirit that burned in the son of the carpenter of Nazareth, an obscure village of Galilee, about two thousand years ago.

The same line of reasoning holds true in the case of <term>Mahâyânism</term>, and it would be absurd to insist on the authenticity of <term>Hînayânism</term> at the expense of the former. Suppose it is granted that the Mahâyâna school of Buddhism contains some elements absorbed from other Indian religious and philosophical systems; but what of it? In fact, every healthy and energetic religion is historical, in the sense that, in the course of its development, it has adapted itself to the ever-changing environment and has assimilated various elements that at first appeared to even threaten its own existence. In Christianity, this process of assimilation, adaptation, and modification has been going on from its very beginning. As a result, we see in the Christianity of today its original type so transformed, as far as its outward appearance is concerned, that nobody would now take it for a faithful copy of the prototype.

### *Mahâyânism a Living Faith.*

The same is true of <term>Mahâyânism</term>. Whatever changes it has made during its historical evolution, its spirit and central ideas are all those of its founder. The question of whether or not it is genuine depends entirely on our interpretation of the term "genuine." If we take it to mean the lifeless preservation of the original, we should say that <term>Mahâyânism</term> is not the genuine teaching of the <term>Buddha</term>; and we may add that <term>Mahâyânists</term> would be proud of that fact, because, as a living religious force, it would never condescend to be the corpse of a bygone faith. Fossils, however faithfully preserved, are nothing but rigid inorganic substances from which life has forever departed.

# INTRODUCTION

<term>Mahâyânism</term> is far from this; it is an ever-growing faith, ready at all times to cast off its old garments as soon as they are worn out. But its spirit, originally inspired by the "Teacher of Men and Gods" (*çâstadevamanuṣyânam*), is most jealously guarded against pollution and degeneration. Therefore, as far as its spirit is concerned, there is no room left to doubt its genuineness; and those who desire to have a complete survey of <term>Buddhism</term> cannot ignore the significance of <term>Mahâyânism</term>.

It is nothing but idle talk to question the historical value of an organism that is now full of vitality and active in all its functions, and to treat it like an archaeological object dug out from the depths of the earth, or like a piece of bric-à-brac discovered in the ruins of an ancient royal palace. <term>Mahâyânism</term> is not an object of historical curiosity. Its vitality and activity concern us in our daily life. It is a great spiritual organism; its moral and religious forces still exercise enormous power over millions of souls; and its further development is sure to be a very valuable contribution to the world progress of religious consciousness. What does it matter, then, whether or not <term>Mahâyânism</term> is the genuine teaching of the <term>Buddha</term>?

Here is an instance of the most flagrant contradictions present in our minds, of which we are not conscious because of our preconceived ideas. Christian critics vigorously insist on the genuineness of their own religion, which is no more than a

hybrid, at least outwardly; but they want to condemn their rival religion as degenerate because it went through various stages of development just like theirs. It is of no practical use to trouble ourselves with this nonsensical question—the question of the genuineness of <term>Mahâyânism</term>—which, by the way, is frequently raised by outsiders as well as by some unenlightened <term>Buddhists</term> themselves.

### 3. SOME MISSTATEMENTS ABOUT THE MAHÂYÂNA DOCTRINES.

Before entering fully into the subject proper of this work, let us glance over some erroneous opinions about the <term>Mahâyâna</term> doctrines held by some Western scholars and, naturally, by all uninitiated readers, who are like the blind leading the blind. It may not be an altogether superfluous task to give them a passing review in this chapter and to show broadly what <term>Mahâyânism</term> is not.

#### *Why Injustice is Done to Buddhism.*

People who have had their thoughts and sentiments habitually trained by one particular set of religious dogmas frequently misjudge the value of thoughts that are strange and unfamiliar to them. We may call this class of people bigots or religious enthusiasts. They may have fine religious and moral sentiments as far as their own religious training goes; but when examined from a broader point of view, they are to a great extent vitiated

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of religious bigotry, arguing that childhood indoctrination prevents many, particularly Christian missionaries, from appreciating the truths in other faiths.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

with prejudices, superstitions, and fanatical beliefs, which, since childhood, have been instilled into their receptive minds before they were sufficiently developed to form independent judgments. This fact so greatly spoils their purity of sentiment and obscures their clarity of intellect that they are disqualified from perceiving and appreciating whatever is good, true, and beautiful in the so-called heathen religions. This is the main reason why those Christian missionaries are incapable of rightly understanding the spirit of religion generally—I mean those missionaries who come to the East to substitute one set of superstitions for another.

This strong general indictment against Christian missionaries, however, is by no means prompted by any partisan spirit. My desire, on the contrary, is to do justice to those thoughts and sentiments that have been working consciously or unconsciously in the human mind from time immemorial and shall work on until the day of the last judgment, if there ever be such a day. To see what these thoughts and sentiments are—which, by the way, constitute the kernel of every religion—we must without any reluctance discard all the prejudices we are prone to hold, though quite unknowingly. Keeping always in view what is most essential in the religious consciousness, we must not confuse it with its accessories, which are doomed to die in the course of time.

## *Examples of Injustice.*

As examples of injustice done to Mahayana Buddhism by Christian critics, we quote the following passages from Monier-Williams’s *Buddhism*, Waddell’s *Buddhism in Tibet*, and Samuel Beal’s *Buddhism in China*, all of which are representative works in their respective fields.

### *Monier Monier-Williams.*

Monier Monier-Williams is a well-known authority on Sanskrit literature, and his works in this department will long remain as a valuable contribution to human knowledge. But, unfortunately, as soon as he attempts to enter the domain of religious controversy, his intellect becomes piteously obscured by his preconceived ideas. He thinks, for instance, that the principal feature of Mahayana Buddhism consists merely in increasing the number of Bodhisattvas, who are content, in his view, with their “perpetual residence in the heavens, and quite willing to put off all desires for Buddhahood and Parinirvana.” (P. 190.)

This remark is so absurd that it will at once be rejected by anyone who has first-hand knowledge of the Mahayana system as even unworthy of refutation, but Monier-Williams takes special pains to give his characterization of the Mahayana doctrine the appearance of a rational explanation. “Of course,” says he, “men instinctively recoiled from utter self-annihi-

<summary>The author identifies specific scholars, beginning with Monier Monier-Williams, who have misrepresented Mahayana Buddhism due to their religious biases. He particularly critiques the claim that Mahayana practitioners prefer heavenly residence over the pursuit of Nirvana.</summary>

<keywords>Mahayana Buddhism, Christian critics, Monier Monier-Williams, Bodhisattvas, Parinirvana, religious prejudice, scholarly misinterpretation</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Monier Monier-Williams, specifically addressing his mischaracterization of the Buddhist concept of heaven and the goals of Mahayana practitioners.</meta>

->INTRODUCTION.<-

lation, and so the Buddha’s followers ended by changing the true idea of Nirvana and converting it from a condition of non-existence into a state of lazy beatitude in celestial regions (!), while they encouraged all men—whether monks or laymen—to make a sense of dreamy bliss in Heaven (!), and not total extinction of life, the end of all their efforts.” (P. 156.)

This view of the Buddhist heaven as interpreted by Monier-Williams is nothing but the conception of the Christian heaven colored with paganism. Nothing is more foreign to Buddhists than this distinguished Sanskritist’s interpretation of celestial existence. The life of <term>devas</term> (celestial beings) is just as much subject to the law of birth and death as that of men on earth. What consolation would there be for the Mahayana Buddhists striving after the highest principle of existence, only to find themselves transmigrated to a celestial abode that is also full of sorrows and sufferings? Always working for the welfare of their fellow creatures, the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> never desire any earthly or heavenly happiness for themselves. Whatever merits, according to the law of <term>karma</term>, are stored up for their good work, they do not have any wish to enjoy them by themselves, but they will have all these merits turned over (*parivarta*) to the interests of their fellow beings. This is the ideal of Bodhisattvas—that is, of the followers of Mahayana Buddhism.

<summary>The author refutes Monier Monier-Williams's claim that Mahayana Buddhism seeks a "dreamy bliss" in heaven, explaining that celestial existence is still subject to suffering and that Bodhisattvas instead dedicate their merits to all beings.</summary>

<keywords>Nirvana, Monier Monier-Williams, Sanskritist, devas, Mahayana Buddhism, Bodhisattvas, karma, parivarta, reincarnation, merit</keywords>

->INTRODUCTION.<-

### Beal.

Samuel Beal, who is considered by Western scholars to be an authority on Chinese Buddhism, refers to the <term>Mahâyâna</term> conception of <term>Dharmakâya</term>.¹ He says in his *Buddhism in China* (p. 156): “We can have little doubt, then, that from early days worship was offered by <term>Buddhists</term> at several spots, consecrated by the presence of the <term>Teacher</term>, to an invisible presence. This presence was formulated by the later Buddhists under the phrase, ‘the Body of the <term>Law</term>’, <term>Dharmakâya</term>.”

Then, alluding to <term>Buddha</term>’s instruction stating that after his <term>Parinirvana</term> the <term>Law</term> given by him should be regarded as himself, Beal proceeds to say: “Here was the seed from which grew the idea or formula of an invisible presence: the teaching and power of the Law (*<term>Dharma</term>*) represented the <term>Dharmakâya</term> or <term>Law-Body</term> of Buddha, present with the order, and worthy of reverence.”

To interpret <term>Dharmakâya</term> as the Body of the Law is quite inadequate and misleading. To the <term>Hînayânists</term>, there is nothing besides the <term>Tripitaka</term> as the object of reverence, and, therefore, the notion of the Body of the Law has no meaning to them. The idea

<note>¹ The conception of <term>Dharmakâya</term> constitutes the central point in the system of <term>Mahâyânism</term>, and the correct understanding of it is of vital importance. The Body of the Law, as it is commonly translated into English, is not exact and frequently leads to a misconception of the entire system. This point is fully discussed below.</note>

<summary>The author critiques Samuel Beal's definition of Dharmakâya as the "Body of the Law," arguing that this interpretation is misleading and fails to capture the term's significance in Mahayana Buddhism.</summary>

->INTRODUCTION.<-

is distinctly <term>Mahâyânistic</term>, but <term>Beal</term> is not well-informed about its real significance as understood by the Buddhists. The chief reason for his misinterpretation, as I judge, lies in his rendering of <term>dharma</term> as "law," while <term>dharma</term> here means "that which subsists," or "that which maintains itself even when all the transient modes disappear"—in short, "being" or "substance." <term>Dharmakâya</term>, therefore, would be a sort of the Absolute, or Essence-Body of all things. This notion plays such an important role in <term>Mahâyânism</term> that an adequate knowledge of it is indispensable to understanding the constitution of <term>Mahâyânism</term> as a religious system.

->Waddell.<-

Let us state one more case of misrepresentation of <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhism by Western scholars. <term>Waddell</term>, author of *<term>Buddhism in Tibet</term>*, referring to the point of divergence between the so-called <term>Northern Buddhism</term> and the <term>Southern</term>, says (pp. 10–11): "It was the theistic <term>Mahâyâna</term> doctrine which substituted, for the agnostic idealism and simple morality of Buddha, a speculative theistic system with a mysticism of sophistic nihilism in the background."

And again: "This <term>Mahâyâna</term> <note>meaning Nâgârjuna's Mâdhyamika school; this note is underlined in red ink in the original transcription</note> was essentially a sophistic nihilism, or rather <term>Parinirvana</term>, while ceasing to be the extinction of life, was converted into a mystic state which admitted of no definition."

<summary>The author argues that Samuel Beal misinterprets "dharma" as "law" rather than "substance," which obscures the true meaning of the Dharmakâya. He also critiques L.A. Waddell for incorrectly labeling Mahâyâna Buddhism as a theistic system based on sophistic nihilism.</summary>

<keywords>Mahâyâna, Mahâyânistic, Beal, dharma, Dharmakâya, Waddell, Buddhism in Tibet, Northern Buddhism, Southern Buddhism, Nâgârjuna, Mâdhyamika school, Parinirvana</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's rebuttal of Waddell's interpretation of Mahâyâna Buddhism, specifically addressing the charge of "sophistic nihilism" before transitioning into a broader discussion on the nature of religion.</meta>

->INTRODUCTION.<-

It may not be wrong to call <term>Mahâyânism</term> a speculative theistic system in a broad sense, but it must be asked on what ground <term>Waddell</term> thinks that it has in its background “a mysticism of sophistic nihilism.” Could a religious system be called sophistry when it makes a close inquiry into the <term>science of dialectics</term> in order to show how futile it is to seek salvation through the intellect alone? Could a religious system be called a nihilism when it endeavors to reach the highest reality, which transcends the phenomenal nature of concrete individual existences? Could a doctrine be called nihilistic when it defines the absolute as neither <term>void</term> (<term>çûnya</term>) nor <term>not-void</term> (<term>açûnya</term>)?

I could select several more passages from other Buddhist scholars of the West and show how far <term>Mahâyânism</term> has been misrepresented by them. But since this work is not a polemic, but is instead devoted to a positive explanation of its basic doctrines, I refrain from doing so. It is enough to state that one of the main causes of the injustice done to Buddhism by Christian critics comes from their preconceptions—of which they may not be aware, but which all the more undermine their “impartial” judgments.

### 4. THE SIGNIFICANCE OF RELIGION.

Those misconceptions about Buddhism stated above induce me to digress in this introductory part and to say a few words concerning the distinction

<summary>The author defends Mahâyâna Buddhism against accusations of being a "sophistic nihilism," arguing that it seeks a reality beyond mere intellectualism and individual appearance. ->INTRODUCTION.<-

between the form and the spirit of religion. A clear knowledge of this distinction will greatly facilitate the formation of a correct notion about <term>Mahâyânism</term> and will also help us duly to appreciate its significance as a living religious faith.

By the spirit of religion, I mean that element in religion which remains unchanged throughout its successive stages of development and transformation; while the form of it is the external shell which is subject to any modification required by circumstances.

### *No Revealed Religion.*

It is beyond doubt that religion, like everything else under the sun, is subject to the laws of evolution, and that, therefore, there is no such thing as a revealed religion whose teachings are supposed to have been delivered to us directly from the hands of an <term>anthropomorphic</term> or <term>anthropopsychic</term> supernatural being, and which, like an inorganic substance, remains forever the same—without changing, without growing, and without modifying itself in accordance with surrounding conditions. Unless people are so blinded by a belief in this kind of religion that they insist its dogmas have suffered absolutely no change whatever since its “revelation,” they must recognize, like every clear-headed person, the fact that there are some ephemeral elements in every religion, which must be carefully distinguished from its <term>quintessence</term> which remains eternally the same.

When this discrimination is not observed, prejudice

He asserts that a religion's essential truth must be separated from its temporary external expressions to avoid prejudice.</summary>

However, this attitude of such religious people serves only to reveal their own narrowness of mind and lack of spiritual insight. No one who desires to penetrate the innermost reaches of the human heart and who longs to experience the fullest meaning of life should harbor even the slightest inclination toward bigotry.

### *The Mystery.*

Religion is the innermost voice of the human heart that, under the yoke of a seemingly finite existence, groans and labors in pain. Humankind, from their first appearance on earth, has never been satisfied with the finitude and impermanence of life. They have always yearned for something that will liberate them from the slavery of this mortal coil, or from the cursed bondage of <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>the transmigration of souls; the cycle of rebirth</note>, as Hindu thinkers express it. This "something," however, because it transcends all the principles of separation and individuation that characterize the phenomena of this mundane existence, has always remained something indefinite, inadequate, chaotic, and full of mystery. And, according to the different degrees of intellectual development in different ages and nations, people have endeavored to invest this

But religion cannot fabricate whatever it pleases; it must work in perfect accord with the intellect. Most of the superstitions fondly cherished by a pious heart are due to the disregard for the intellectual element in religion.

The imagination creates; the intellect discriminates. Creation without discrimination is wild; discrimination without creation is barren. Religion and science, when they do not work with mutual understanding, are sure to be one-sided. The soul makes an abnormal growth at one point, loses its balance, and is finally given up to a collapse of the entire system. Those pious religious enthusiasts who see a natural enemy in science and denounce it with all their energy are, in my opinion, as shortsighted and distorted in their view as those men of science who think that science alone must claim the whole field of soul-activities as well as those of nature. I am not in sympathy with either of them; for one is just as arrogant in its claim as the other. Without a careful examination of both sides of a shield, we are not competent to give a correct opinion upon it.

monopoly of science. They are reciprocal and complementary: one cannot do anything without the other. The difference between science and religion is not the difference between certainty and probability. The difference lies rather in their respective fields of activity. Science is solely concerned with things that are conditional, relative, and finite. When it explains a given phenomenon through fixed laws—which are, in turn, nothing but a generalization of particular facts—the task of science is complete, and any further attempt to go beyond this (that is, to inquire into the origin, destination, and purpose of things) lies beyond its realm. But the human soul is not satisfied with this; it asks for the ultimate principle underlying all so-called scientific laws and hypotheses. Science is indifferent to the <term>teleology</term> of things: a mechanical explanation of them satisfies its intellectual curiosity. But in religion, <term>teleology</term> is of paramount importance; it is one of the most fundamental problems, and a system that does not provide a definite concept on this point is not a religion. Science, again, does not care if there is something beyond or outside its many laws and theories; but a religion that does not possess a God, or something corresponding to one, ceases to be a religion, for it fails to provide consolation to the human heart.

If we could subject their conceptions of faith to a strict analysis, as a chemist does his materials, we should detect in them all the possible forms of differentiation. But all these things belong to the exterior of religion and have nothing to do with the essentials which underlie them.

The abiding elements of religion come from within, and consist mainly in the mysterious sentiment that lies hidden in the deepest depths of the human heart, and that, when awakened, shakes the whole structure of personality and brings about a great spiritual revolution, which results in a complete change of one's <term>world-conception</term>. When this mysterious sentiment finds expression and formulates its conceptions in the terms of intellect, it becomes a definite system of beliefs, which is popularly called religion, but which should properly be termed dogmatism—that is, an intellectualized form of religion. On the other hand, the outward forms of religion consist of those changing elements that are mainly determined by the intellectual and moral development of the times, as well as by individual aesthetic feelings.

My conviction is: If the Buddha and the Christ had exchanged their accidental places of birth, Gautama might have been a Christ rising against Jewish traditionalism, and Jesus might have been a Buddha, perhaps propounding the doctrine of the non-ego, Nirvâna, and Dharmakâya.

However great a man may be, he cannot help but be an echo of the spirit of the times. He never stands, as some suppose, so far removed and towering above the masses as to be practically by himself. On the contrary, "he," as Emerson says, "finds himself in the river of thoughts and events, forced onward by the ideas and necessities of his contemporaries." So it was with the Buddha, and so with the Christ. They were nothing but the concrete representatives of the ideas and feelings that were struggling in those times against established institutions, which were fast degenerating and threatened the progress of humanity. But at the same time, those ideas and sentiments were the outburst of the Eternal Soul, which occasionally makes a solemn announcement of its will through great historical figures or through great world events.

# CHAPTER I.

## A GENERAL CHARACTERIZATION OF BUDDHISM.

->*No God and no Soul.*<-

Buddhism is considered by some to be a religion without a God and without a soul. The statement is true and untrue according to what meaning we give to those terms.

Buddhism does not recognize the existence of a being who stands aloof from his "creations" and who meddles occasionally with human affairs when his capricious will pleases him. This conception of a supreme being is very offensive to Buddhists. They are unable to perceive any truth in the hypotheses that a being like ourselves created the universe out of nothing and first populated it with a pair of sentient beings; that, owing to a crime committed by them—which, however, could have been avoided if the creator so desired—they were condemned by him to eternal damnation; that the creator, in the meantime, feeling pity for the cursed or suffering the pangs of remorse for his somewhat rash deed, dispatched his only beloved son to the earth for the purpose of rescuing mankind from universal misery, etc., etc. If Buddhism is called atheism on account of its

<vocab>Buddhism, God, Soul, supreme being, atheism</vocab>

<summary>The author addresses the perception of Buddhism as an atheistic religion, explaining its rejection of a personal, capricious creator god and the traditional Western narrative of creation, original sin, and redemption.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, atheism, creator god, soul, creation, original sin, theology, Mahayana</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the general characterization of Buddhism, specifically focusing on the rejection of a permanent soul (atman) and the transition into a discussion of core Buddhist tenets.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

refusal to take poetry for actual fact, its followers would have no objection to the designation.

Next, if we understand "soul" to mean *atman*—which, secretly hiding itself behind all mental activities, directs them in the manner of an organist striking different notes as he pleases—Buddhists outspokenly deny the existence of such a mythical being. To assume an independent *atman* exists apart from the combination of the five *Skandhas* <note>They are: (1) form or materiality (*rupa*), (2) sensation (*vedana*), (3) conception (*samjna*), (4) action or deeds (*samkara*), and (5) consciousness (*vijnana*). These terms are explained elsewhere.</note>, which Buddhists believe constitute an individual being, is to unreservedly welcome egoism with all its harmful consequences. What distinguishes Buddhism most characteristically and emphatically from all other religions is the doctrine of *non-atman* or non-ego, which is the exact opposite of the assumption of a "soul-substance" cherished by most religious devotees. In this sense, Buddhism is undoubtedly a religion without a soul.

To make these points clearer in a general way, let us briefly discuss in this chapter the principal tenets of Buddhism, such as *Karma*, *Atman*, *Avidya*, *Nirvana*, *Dharmakaya*, etc. Since some of these doctrines are held in common by the two schools of Buddhism, Hinayanism and Mahayanism, a brief and comprehensive explanation of them here will provide our readers with a general understanding of the structure of Buddhism; it will also prepare them to follow the more specific explanation of the Mahayana doctrine that follows.

## *Karma.*

One of the most fundamental doctrines established by Buddha is that nothing in this world comes from a single cause, that the existence of a universe is the result of a combination of several causes (*hetu*) and conditions (*pratyaya*), and is at the same time an active force contributing to the production of an effect in the future. As far as phenomenal existences are concerned, this law of cause and effect is universally valid. Nothing, not even God, can interfere with the course of things regulated in this way, materially as well as morally. If a God really exists and has some concern about our worldly affairs, he must first conform to the law of causation. This is because the principle of karma, which is the Buddhist term for causation morally conceived, holds supreme everywhere and at all times.

The conception of karma plays the most important role in Buddhist ethics. Karma is the formative principle of the universe. It determines the course of events and the destiny of our existence. The reason why we cannot change our present state of affairs as we might wish, is that it has already been determined by the karma that was performed in our previous lives, not only individually but collectively. But, for this same reason, we shall be able to work out our destiny in the future, which is nothing but the result of several factors that are working and that are being worked by ourselves in this life.

<sig>3</sig>
<vocab>Buddha, karma, hetu, pratyaya, law of causation, Buddhist ethics</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Karma as the universal law of cause and effect that governs both the material and moral worlds. It explains that while our current circumstances are shaped by past actions, we retain the power to determine our future through our present deeds.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Buddha, causation, hetu, pratyaya, Buddhist ethics, destiny, moral law</keywords>

Therefore, Buddha says:

> “By oneself alone is evil done,
> By oneself is one disgraced;
> By oneself is evil left undone,
> By oneself alone is one purified;
> Purity and impurity belong to oneself:
> No one can purify another.” <note>1. *The Dhammapada*, v. 165. Translated by A. J. Edmunds.</note>

Again,

> “Not in the sky
> Nor in the midst of the sea,
> Nor entering a cleft of the mountains,
> Is found that realm on earth
> Where one may stand and be
> From an evil deed absolved.” <note>2. *The Dhammapada*, v. 127.</note>

This doctrine of <term>karma</term> may be regarded as an application of the theory of the conservation of energy within our ethical realm. Everything done is done once and for all; its footprints on the sands of our moral and social evolution are left forever; indeed, more than just left, they are generative—whether good or evil—and wait for further development under favorable conditions. In the physical world, even the slightest possible movement of our limbs cannot help but affect the general cosmic motion of the earth, however infinitesimal it may be; and if we had a proper instrument, we could surely measure the precise extent of its effect. The same is true even with our deeds. A deed once performed, together with its subjective motives, can never vanish without leaving some impressions either on the individual

<summary>This page explores the ethical implications of karma, emphasizing individual responsibility for purity and the inescapable consequences of one's actions. It likens the moral law of karma to the physical law of the conservation of energy, suggesting that every deed leaves a permanent, generative mark on the world.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha, karma, Dhammapada, ethics, conservation of energy, individual responsibility, moral evolution, A. J. Edmunds</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how deeds leave lasting impressions on either the individual or the supra-individual (social) consciousness.</meta>

consciousness or on the supra-individual, that is, social 
consciousness.

We do not need to state further that the conception of 
<term>karma</term> in its general aspect is scientifically verified. 
In our moral and material life, where the law of 
relativity rules supreme, the doctrine of <term>karma</term> must 
be considered thoroughly valid. And as long as its 
validity is admitted in this field, we can live our 
phenomenal life without resorting to the hypothesis of 
a personal God, as Lamarck declared when his 
significant work on evolution was presented to 
Emperor Napoleon.

But it would be an injustice to Buddhism if we desig-
nated it as agnosticism or naturalism, denying or ignoring 
the existence of the ultimate, unifying principle in 
which all contradictions are obliterated. <term>Dharmakâya</term> 
is the name given by Buddhists to this highest prin-
ciple, viewed not only from the philosophical but also 
from the religious standpoint. In the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, 
Buddhists find the ultimate significance of life, which, 
when seen from its phenomenal aspect, cannot escape 
the bondage of <term>karma</term> and its inescapable laws.

### *Avidyâ.*

What next claims our attention is the problem of 
ignorance (<term>nescience</term>), which is one of the most essential features 
of Buddhism. Buddhists believe that ignorance (in San-
skrit *<term>avidyâ</term>*) is the subjective aspect of <term>karma</term>, involv-
ing us in a series of rebirths. Rebirth, considered 
by itself, is no moral evil, but rather a necessary

<summary>This page explains that karma is a scientifically valid principle that allows for an ethical life without a personal God, while introducing Dharmakâya as the ultimate unifying reality. It also begins to define avidyâ (ignorance) as the subjective force driving the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>karma, Dharmakâya, avidyâ, nescience, Lamarck, evolution, Emperor Napoleon, Buddhism, rebirth, social consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ignorance (avidyâ) as a necessary condition for progress and a source of evil when misunderstood, leading into the explanation of the Twelve Nidânas.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

condition of progress toward perfection, if perfection is ever attainable here. It is an evil only when it is the outcome of ignorance—ignorance regarding the true meaning of our earthly existence.

Those who are ignorant do not recognize the transience of worldly things and tenaciously cling to them as final realities. They madly struggle to avoid the misery brought about by their own folly; they savagely cling to the self against the will of God, as Christians would say; they take individual details as final existences and ignore the one pervading reality that underlies them all; they build up an impenetrable wall between "mine" and "thine." In short, those who are ignorant do not understand that there is no such thing as an <term>ego-soul</term>, and that all individual existences are unified in the system of <term>Dharmakâya</term>. Buddhism, therefore, most emphatically maintains that to attain the bliss of <term>Nirvana</term>, we must radically dispel this illusion, this ignorance, which is the root of all evil and suffering in this life.

The doctrine of <term>nescience</term> or ignorance is technically expressed in the following formula, which is commonly called the <term>Twelve Nidânas</term> or *<term>Pratyayasamutpada</term>*, that is to say, the <term>Chains of Dependence</term>:

(1) There is Ignorance (*<term>avidyâ</term>*) in the beginning; (2) from Ignorance, Action (*<term>sanskâra</term>*) comes forth; (3) from Action, Consciousness (*<term>vijñâna</term>*) comes forth; (4) from Consciousness, Name-and-Form (*<term>nâmarûpa</term>*) comes forth; (5) from Name-and-Form, the Six Organs (*<term>ṣadâyâtana</term>*) come forth; (6) from the Six Organs

<summary>This page defines ignorance as the failure to see the transience of worldly things and the unity of all existence in the Dharmakâya. It introduces the Twelve Nidânas, or Chains of Dependence, as the technical explanation for how ignorance leads to individual existence.</summary>
<keywords>ignorance, avidyâ, Dharmakâya, Nirvana, Twelve Nidânas, Pratyayasamutpada, Chains of Dependence, ego-soul, nescience</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the list of the Twelve Nidânas (Chains of Dependence) and provides a detailed explanation of how past ignorance leads to present karma and future rebirth, transitioning into the doctrine of Non-Atman.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

Touch (*<term>sparça</term>*) comes forth; (7) from Touch, Sensation (*<term>vedanâ</term>*) comes forth; (8) from Sensation, Desire (*<term>tṛṣṇâ</term>*) comes forth; (9) from Desire, Clinging (*<term>upâdâna</term>*) comes forth; (10) from Clinging, Being (*<term>bhâva</term>*) comes forth; (11) from Being, Birth (*<term>jati</term>*) comes forth; and (12) from Birth, Pain (*<term>duḥkha</term>*) comes forth.

According to <term>Vasubandhu</term>’s *<term>Abhidharmakoça</term>*, the formula is explained as follows: By being ignorant in our previous life regarding the significance of our existence, we gave free rein to our desires and acted wantonly. Due to this <term>karma</term>, we are destined in the present life to be endowed with consciousness (*<term>vijñâna</term>*), name-and-form (*<term>nâmarûpa</term>*), the six organs of sense (*<term>ṣadâyâtana</term>*), and sensation (*<term>vedanâ</term>*). By the exercise of these faculties, we now desire, crave, and cling to these illusory existences which have no ultimate reality whatsoever. In consequence of this “Will to Live,” we potentially accumulate or create the <term>karma</term> that will lead us to further <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>the cycle of rebirth or transmigration of souls</note> of birth and death.

The formula is by no means logical, nor is it exhaustive, but the fundamental notion that life started in ignorance or blind will remains valid.

->*<term>Non-Atman</term>.*<-

The problem of <term>nescience</term> <note>ignorance or lack of knowledge</note> naturally leads to the doctrine usually known as that of <term>non-Atman</term> (that is, the non-ego), to which reference was made at the beginning

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the doctrine of Non-Atman (non-ego), contrasting the Buddhist rejection of a permanent ego-soul with common dualistic and materialistic conceptions of the soul found in other traditions.</meta>

of this chapter. This doctrine of Buddhism is one of the subjects that has caused much criticism by Christian scholars. Its thesis states: there is no such thing as an ego-soul, which, according to the popular interpretation, is the agent of our mental activities. And this is the reason why Buddhism is sometimes called a religion without a soul, as previously mentioned.

This Buddhist negation of the ego-soul is perhaps startling to people who, having no power of philosophical thought, blindly accept the traditional, materialistic view of the soul. They think they are being very spiritual by endorsing the dualism of soul and flesh, and by making the soul something like a physical entity, though far more ethereal than an ordinary object of the senses. They think of the soul as being shaped like an angel when they teach that it ascends to heaven immediately after its release from its material imprisonment.

They further imagine that the soul, because of its imprisonment in the body, groans in pain for its freedom, unable to bear its worldly limitations. The immortality of the soul is a continuation of this ethereal, astral, ghost-like entity after the breaking apart of the material elements—very much resembling the *<term>Samkhyan Lingham</term>* or the *<term>Vedantic sû-kṣama-çârîra</term>*. Self-consciousness will not suffer in the least in its continued activity, as it is the essential function of the soul. Brothers and sisters, parents, sons, daughters, wives, and husbands, all transfigured and sublimated, will meet again in the

The author notes that this doctrine is a primary point of contention for Christian scholars and differentiates the Buddhist view from other Indian philosophies like Samkhya and Vedanta.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the "ego-soul" concept, explaining that the Buddhist "non-âtman" doctrine views the soul not as a permanent entity but as a name for the coordination of mental activities, often confused with a concrete reality by those the author calls "naïve realists."</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

celestial abode and continue their home life much like their earthly one. People who hold this view of the soul and its immortality must feel great disappointment or even resentment when they are asked to recognize the Buddhist theory of <term>non-âtman</term>.

The absurdity of ascribing to the soul a kind of astral existence, as taught by some theosophists, is due to the confusion of a name with the object it represents. The soul—or what is equivalent to it according to the common notion, the ego—is a name given to a specific coordination of mental activities. We invent abstract names to simplify our intellectual work, and of course, they have no corresponding realities as specific entities in the concrete, objective world. Ordinary minds have forgotten the history of how abstract names are formed. Because they are used to finding specific objective realities or concrete individuals that correspond to certain names, these "naïve realists" imagine that all names, regardless of their nature, must have concrete, individual equivalents in the physical world. Their so-called idealism or spiritualism is actually a crude form of materialism, despite their baseless fear of materialism as being atheistic or even immoral—the curse of ignorance!

The <term>non-âtman</term> theory does not deny that there is a coordination or unification of various mental operations. Buddhism calls this system of coordination <term>vijñâna</term>, not <term>âtman</term>. <term>Vijñâna</term> is consciousness, while

<summary>The author argues that the "ego" or soul is simply an abstract name for the coordination of mental activities, rather than a concrete entity. He critiques the "naïve realism" that treats such names as physical realities and introduces the Buddhist term vijñâna as a more accurate description of consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>non-âtman, theosophists, ego, abstract names, naïve realists, materialism, vijñâna, consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Buddhist concept of vijñâna and the concept of âtman, specifically focusing on the analogy of a house or wheel to explain how names describe composite systems rather than independent, permanent entities.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

<term>âtman</term> is the ego conceived as a concrete entity—a hypostatic agent which, abiding in the deepest recesses of the mind, directs all subjective activities according to its own discretion. This view is radically rejected by Buddhism.

A familiar analogy illustrating the doctrine of <term>non-âtman</term> is the notion of a wheel or that of a house. "Wheel" is the name given to a combination in a fixed form of the spokes, axle, tire, hub, rim, and so forth; "house" is the name given to a combination of roofs, pillars, windows, floors, walls, and so on, according to a certain model and for a certain purpose. Now, take all these parts independently, and where is the house or the wheel to be found? House or wheel is merely the name designating a certain form in which parts are systematically and definitely arranged. What an absurdity, then, it must be to insist on the independent existence of the wheel or of the house as an agent behind the combination of certain parts arranged in such a definite way!

It is wonderful that Buddhism clearly anticipated the outcome of modern psychological research at a time when all other religious and philosophical systems were eagerly cherishing dogmatic superstitions concerning the nature of the ego. The refusal of modern psychology to allow "soul" to mean anything more than the sum total of all mental experiences—such as sensations, ideas, feelings, decisions, and so forth—is precisely a restatement of the Buddhist doctrine of <term>non-âtman</term>. It does not deny that there is a unity of consciousness,

<summary>This page explains the Buddhist doctrine of non-âtman by comparing the "soul" to a wheel or a house, which are merely names for organized collections of parts rather than independent entities. It highlights how this ancient Buddhist perspective aligns with modern psychology's definition of the soul as the sum total of mental experiences.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that while there is a unity of consciousness, it is not an absolute or independent soul, but rather a temporary result of various causes and conditions.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I</header>

For to deny this is to doubt our everyday experiences, but it refuses to assert that this unity is absolute, unconditioned, and independent. Everything in this phenomenal phase of existence is a combination of certain causes (<term>hetu</term>) and conditions (<term>pratyaya</term>) brought together according to the principle of <term>karma</term>; and everything that is compound is finite and subject to dissolution, and, therefore, always limited by something else. Even soul-life, as far as its phenomenality goes, is no exception to this universal law. To maintain the existence of a <term>soul-substance</term>, which is supposed to lie hidden behind the phenomena of consciousness, is not only misleading but harmful and leads to some morally dangerous conclusions. The belief that there is something where there is really nothing makes us cling to this chimerical form, with no other result than subjecting ourselves to an eternal series of suffering. Thus, we read in the <term>Lankâvatâra Sûtra</term>, III:

> "A flower in the air, or a hare with horns,
> Or a pregnant maiden of stone:
> To take what is not for what is,
> Is called a false judgment.
> 
> "In a combination of causes,
> The common people seek the reality of self.
> As they do not understand the truth,
> They transmigrate from birth to birth."

### *The Non-Atman-ness of Things.*

<term>Mahâyânism</term> has gone a step further than <term>Hînayânism</term> in the development of the doctrine of <term>non-âtman</term>, for it expressly disavows, besides the denial

<summary>This page argues that the soul is a composite and finite phenomenon governed by karma rather than a permanent substance, and that clinging to the illusion of a self causes continued suffering and rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>non-âtman, soul-substance, karma, hetu, pratyaya, Lankâvatâra Sûtra, Mahâyânism, Hînayânism, transmigration</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the Mahâyânist doctrine of non-âtman, expanding the denial of a permanent self from the individual person to the existence of things themselves.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

of the existence of the ego-substance—a noumenal conception of things; that is, the conception of particulars as having something absolute within them. <term>Hînayânism</term>, indeed, also disfavors this conception of "thing-ness," but it does so only implicitly. It is <term>Mahâyânism</term> that definitely insists on the non-existence of a personal (<term>pudgala</term>) as well as a thing-like (<term>dharma</term>) ego.

According to the common view, particular existences are real; they have permanent substantial entities, remaining forever as they are. People think, therefore, that organic matter remains forever organic just as much as inorganic matter remains inorganic; because they are essentially different, there is no mutual transformation between them. The human soul is different from that of the lower animals, and sentient beings are different from non-sentient beings; as the difference is well-defined and permanent, there is no bridge over which one can cross to the other. We may call this view naturalistic egoism.

<margin>/</margin> <term>Mahâyânism</term>, against this egoistic conception of the world, extends its theory of <term>non-âtman</term> to the realm lying outside us. It maintains that there is no irreducible reality in particular existences, so long as they are combinations of several causes and conditions brought together by the principle of <term>karma</term>. Things are here because they are sustained by <term>karma</term>. As soon as its force is exhausted, the conditions that made their existence possible lose efficiency and dissolve, and in their places, other conditions and existences will follow. Therefore, what is organic

<summary>This page explains that Mahâyânism denies the existence of a permanent "ego" in both persons and objects, unlike the common view of "naturalistic egoism." It asserts that all things are temporary combinations of causes and conditions sustained by karma, which dissolve when that force is exhausted.</summary>
<keywords>Mahâyânism, Hînayânism, pudgala, dharma, non-âtman, karma, ego-substance, noumenal, naturalistic egoism, organic matter</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the Mahâyânist doctrine of non-âtman, using the physical transformation of elements like carbon to illustrate that nothing has a permanent, independent self or soul.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>->CHAPTER I.<-</header>

today, may be inorganic tomorrow, and *vice versa*. Carbon, for instance, which is stored within the earth, appears in the form of coal, graphite, or diamond; but that which exists on its surface is found sometimes combined with other elements in the form of an animal or a vegetable, and sometimes in its free elementary state. It is the same carbon everywhere; it becomes inorganic or organic according to its <term>karma</term>. It has no <term>âtman</term> within itself which directs its transformation by its own self-determining will. Mutual transformation is observable everywhere; there is a constant shifting of forces, an eternal transmigration of the elements—all of which tend to show the transitoriness and <term>non-âtman-ness</term> of individual existences. The universe is moving like a whirlwind, with nothing in it proving to be stationary and nothing in it rigidly adhering to its own form of existence.

Suppose, on the other hand, there were an <term>âtman</term> behind every particular being; suppose, too, it were absolute, permanent, and self-acting. This phenomenal world would then come to a standstill, and life would be forever gone. For is not changeability the most essential feature and condition of life, and also the strongest evidence for the non-existence of individual things as realities? The physical sciences recognize this universal fact of mutual transformation in its positive aspect and call it the law of the conservation of energy and of matter. <term>Mahâyânism</term>, recognizing its negative side, proposes the doctrine of the <term>non-âtman-ness</term> of things, that is to say, the
