--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the Mahâyânist doctrine of non-âtman, asserting that the lack of a permanent individual self is a scientific and ethical reality.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

<margin>/</margin>
impermanency of all particular existences. Therefore, it is said, “*Sarvam anityam, sarvam çûnyam, sarvam anâtman.*” (All is transitory, all is void, all is without ego.)

Mahâyânists condemn the common view that denies the shared nature and mutual transformation of all beings, not only because it is scientifically untenable, but mainly because, considered ethically and religiously, it is filled with extremely dangerous ideas—ideas which may finally lead a “brother to deliver up the brother to death and the father the child,” and, again, it may compel “the children to rise up against their parents and cause them to be put to death.” Why? Because this view, born of egoism, would dry up the well of human love and sympathy, and transform us into creatures of animalistic selfishness; because this view is not capable of inspiring us with the sense of mutuality and compassion and of making us selflessly feel for our fellow-beings. Then, all fine religious and humane sentiments would depart from our hearts, and we should be nothing less than rigid, lifeless corpses, no pulse beating, no blood running. And how many victims are offered every day on this altar of egoism! They are not necessarily immoral by nature, but blindly led by the false conception of life and the world, they have been rendered incapable of seeing their own spiritual counterparts in their neighbors. Being ever controlled by their sensory impulses, they sin against humanity, against nature, and against themselves.

<summary>This page argues that denying the interconnectedness of all beings leads to a dangerous egoism that destroys human compassion and social bonds. It suggests that a false understanding of the self results in spiritual lifelessness and harm toward others and oneself.</summary>

### *The Dharmakâya.*

The Dharmakâya, which literally means “body or system of being,” is, according to the Mahâyânists,

<summary>This page quotes the Mahâyâna-abhisamaya Sûtra to describe the illusory and egoless nature of reality, comparing existence to dreams and bubbles. It introduces the concept of the Dharmakâya as the "body or system of being" in Mahâyâna philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Mahâyâna-abhisamaya Sûtra, Dharmakâya, âtman, self-nature, emptiness, transcendence, Buddhist scripture, non-ego</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of the Dharmakâya, expanding on its role as the ultimate reality in Mahâyâna Buddhism compared to other schools and religions.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

the ultimate reality that underlies all particular phenomena; it is that which makes the existence of individuals possible; it is the *raison d'être* of the universe; it is the norm of being, which regulates the course of events and thoughts. The conception of Dharmakâya is peculiarly Mahâyânistic, for the Hînayâna school did not go so far as to formulate the ultimate principle of the universe; its adherents stopped short at a positivistic interpretation of Buddhism. The Dharmakâya remained for them simply the Body of the Law, or the Buddha's personality as embodied in the truth taught by him.

The Dharmakâya may be compared in one sense to the God of Christianity and in another sense to the Brahman or Paramâtman of Vedantism. It is different, however, from the former in that it does not stand transcendentally above the universe—which, according to the Christian view, was created by God—but is instead, according to Mahâyânism, a manifestation of the Dharmakâya himself. It is also different from Brahman in that it is not absolutely impersonal, nor is it a mere state of being. The Dharmakâya, on the contrary, is capable of willing and reflecting, or, to use Buddhist phraseology, it is *Karunâ* (love) and *Bodhi* (intelligence), and not a mere state of being.

This pantheistic and at the same time entheistic Dharmakâya is working in every sentient being, for sentient beings are nothing but a self-manifestation of the Dharmakâya. Individuals are not isolated existences, as imagined by most people. If isolated,

<summary>This page defines the Dharmakâya as the ultimate reality and "norm of being" in Mahâyâna Buddhism, distinguishing it from the more limited Hînayâna interpretation. It compares the Dharmakâya to the Christian God and the Vedantic Brahman, noting that it is an immanent, purposeful force of love and intelligence manifest in all sentient beings.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakâya, Mahâyânism, Hînayâna, Buddhism, Brahman, Paramâtman, Vedantism, Christianity, Karunâ, Bodhi, pantheism, entheism, immanence</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Dharmakâya, focusing on how individual existences are manifestations of this universal reality and how enlightenment overcomes the illusion of the separate ego.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I. 47</header>

they are nothing; they are like many soap bubbles that vanish one after another in the emptiness of space. All particular existences acquire their meaning only when they are thought of in their oneness within the Dharmakâya. The veil of Mâya (that is, subjective ignorance) may temporarily create an obstacle to our perceiving the universal light of the Dharmakâya, in which we are all one. But when our Bodhi, or intellect—which, by the way, is a reflection of the Dharmakâya in the human mind—is so fully enlightened, we no longer build the artificial barrier of egoism before our spiritual eye; the distinction between the *meum* and *teum* is obliterated, and no dualism throws the nets of entanglement over us; I recognize myself in you and you recognize yourself in me; *tat tvam asi*. Or,

> "What is here, that is there;
> What is there, that is here:
> Whoever sees duality here,
> From death to death goes he." ¹

This state of enlightenment may be called the spiritual expansion of the ego, or, negatively, the ideal annihilation of the ego. A never-drying stream of sympathy and love, which is the life of religion, will now spontaneously flow out of the fountainhead of the Dharmakâya.

<margin>✓</margin>
The doctrine of the non-ego teaches us that there is no reality in individual existences, and that we do not have any transcendental entity called an ego-substance.

<page-num>48</page-num>
# CHAPTER I.

The doctrine of Dharmakâya, to supplement this, teaches us that we are all one in the System of Being and only as such are we immortal. The one shows us the folly of clinging to individual existences and of coveting the immortality of the ego-soul; the other convinces us of the truth that we are saved by living into the unity of the Dharmakâya. The doctrine of <term>non-âtman</term> liberates us from the shackles of unfounded egoism; but as mere liberation does not mean anything positive and may perhaps lead us to asceticism, we apply the energy released in this way to the execution of the will of the Dharmakâya.

<margin>/</margin>
The questions: "Why must we love our neighbors as ourselves? Why must we do to others all things whatsoever we would want them to do to us?" are answered in this way by Buddhists: "It is because we are all one in the Dharmakâya, because when the clouds of ignorance and egoism are totally dispersed, the light of universal love and intelligence cannot help but shine in all its glory. And, enveloped in this glory, we do not see any enemy, nor neighbor; we are not even conscious of whether we are one in the Dharmakâya. There is no 'my will' here, but only 'your will,' the will of the Dharmakâya, in which we live and move and have our being."

The Apostle Paul says: "For as in Adam all die, even so in Christ shall all be made alive." Why? Buddhists would answer, "because Adam asserted his egoism by giving himself up to ignorance (the tree of knowledge is in truth the tree of ignorance,

It interprets the Golden Rule and Christian concepts of Adam and Christ as the struggle between egoistic ignorance and universal will.</summary>

->*Nirvâna.*<-

The meaning of Nirvâna has been interpreted in various ways by non-Buddhist students from philological and historical standpoints; but it matters little what conclusions they have reached, as we are not going to repeat them here; nor do they affect our presentation of the Buddhists’ own view below at all. For it is the latter that concerns us most here and constitutes the all-important part of the problem. We have had too much non-Buddhist speculation on the question at issue. The majority of critics, while claiming to be fair and impartial, have been led by some preconceived ideas to a conclusion that is not at all acceptable to intelligent Buddhists. Furthermore, the fact has escaped their notice that the Pâli literature from which they chiefly derive their information on the subject represents the views of only one of the many sects that arose soon after the demise of the Master and were constantly branching off during and after the time of King Açoka. The probability is that Buddha himself did not have any stereotyped conception of Nirvâna and, as most great minds do, expressed his ideas directly as they were formed under various circumstances; though, of course, they could not be

These two aspects of Nirvâna—that is, negatively, the destruction of evil passions, and, positively, the practice of sympathy—are complementary to each other; and when we have one, we have the other. Because, as soon as the heart is freed from the <term>cangue</term> <note>A heavy wooden yoke used for punishment, here used as a metaphor for the restraints of egoism.</note> of egoism, the same heart, until then so cold and hard, undergoes a complete change, shows animation, and, joyously escaping from self-imprisonment, finds its freedom in the bosom of Dharmakâya. In this latter sense, Nirvâna is the "humanization" of Dharmakâya; that is to say, "God's will be done on earth as it is in heaven." If we make use of the

> the verge of dizzy precipices and look over . . . Nirvâna leads, in fact, to the annihilation of the individual and of the race, and to the logical absurdity that the vanquished are the victors over the trials and miseries of life.”

Then, the author recites the case of one of his acquaintances, who made a practical experiment with Nirvâna, rejecting variety in his diet, giving up meat, wine, every kind of ragout, every form of condiment, and reducing to its lowest possible terms the desire that is most fundamental in every living being—the desire for food—and substituting a certain number of cups of pure milk. “Having thus blunted his sense of taste and the grosser of his appetites, having abandoned all physical activity, he thought to find a recompense in the pleasure of abstract meditation and of aesthetic contemplation. He entered a state which was not that of dreamland, but neither was it that of real life, with its definite details.”

Again, psychologically, Nirvana is enlightenment—the actualization of the <term>Bodhicitta</term> <note>The Heart of Intelligence. For a detailed explanation of this term, see Chapter XI.</note>

The gospel of love and the doctrine of Nirvana may appear to some to contradict each other, for they think that the former is the source of energy and activity, while the latter is a lifeless, inhuman, ascetic quietism. But the truth is, love is the emotional aspect and Nirvana is the intellectual aspect of the innermost religious consciousness which constitutes the essence of Buddhist life.

That Nirvana is the destruction of selfish desires is plainly shown in this stanza:

> “To the giver, merit is increased;
> When the senses are controlled, anger does not arise;
> The wise forsake evil;
> By the destruction of desire, sin, and infatuation,
> A person attains Nirvana.” <note>The Udâna, Ch. VIII, p. 118. Translation by General Strong.</note>

The following, which was spoken by Buddha against a certain class of monks, testifies that when Nirvana is understood in the sense of quietism or pessimism, he vigorously repudiated it:

> “Fearing an endless chain of birth and death,
> And the misery of transmigration,
> Their hearts are filled with worry,
> But they desire only their own safety.

Rule, ¹ so called. The Golden Rule, however, does not give any reason why we should act in such a way; it is a mere command whose authority is ascribed to a certain superhuman being. This does not satisfy an intellectually inclined mind, which refuses to accept anything on mere authority, for it wants to get to the bottom of things and see on what ground they stand. Buddhism has solved this problem by finding the oneness of things in <term>Dharmakâya</term>, from which flows the eternal stream of love and sympathy. As we have seen before, when the cursed barrier of egoism is broken down, there remains nothing that can prevent us from loving others as ourselves.

Those who wish to see nothing but an utter barrenness of heart after the annihilation of egoism are much mistaken in their estimation of human nature. For they think its vitality comes from selfishness, and that all forms of activity in our life are propelled simply by the desire to preserve the self and the species. They, therefore, naturally shrink from the doctrine that teaches that all worldly things are empty, and that there is no such thing as an ego-substance whose

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformation of the self, arguing that the removal of the ego does not destroy one's vitality but instead redirects it toward a higher purpose, while clarifying that Nirvana is a state of enlightenment rather than non-existence.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>

immortality is so greatly desired by most people. But the truth is, the source of love does not lie in the idea of self, but in its removal. For the human heart, being a reflection of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> which is love and intelligence, recovers its inherent power and goodness only when the veil of ignorance and egoism is cast aside. The vitality, energy, and effort which were shown by a self-centered will—and which were therefore utterly despicable—will certainly not die out with the removal of the hateful atmosphere in which egoism had enveloped them. Instead, they will gain an even nobler interpretation and an even more elevating and satisfying significance; for they have gone through a baptism of fire, by which the last trace of egoism has been thoroughly consumed. The old, evil master is eternally buried, but the willing servants are still here and always ready to serve, now more efficiently, for their new legitimate and more authoritative lord.

<margin>—</margin>
In common speech, destruction is closely associated with nothingness; therefore, <term>Nirvâna</term>, the destruction of egoism, is usually understood as a synonym for nihilism. But the removal of darkness does not bring desolation; instead, it means enlightenment, order, and peace. It is the same room, and all the furniture is left there just as it was before. In darkness, chaos reigned and goblins ran wild; in enlightenment, everything is in its proper place. And did we not state plainly that <term>Nirvâna</term> was enlightenment?

CHAPTER I.

### *The Intellectual Tendency of Buddhism.*

One thing I wish to refer to in this connection is what makes Buddhism appear somewhat cold and impassive. By this, I mean its intellectuality.

The fact is that anything coming from India strongly savors of philosophy. In ancient India, everyone in the higher castes seems to have indulged in intellectual and speculative exercises. Because the land was rich in natural resources and the struggle for existence was thus reduced to a minimum, the <term>Brahmans</term> and the <term>Kṣatriyas</term> gathered under luxuriously growing trees or retired to mountain grottoes, undisturbed by the turmoil of the world; there, they devoted all their leisure hours to metaphysical speculations and discussions. <term>Buddhism</term>, as a product of these people, is naturally and deeply imbued with intellectualism.

Furthermore, in India, there was no distinction between religion and philosophy. Every philosophical system was at the same time a religion, and *vice versa*. Philosophy among the <term>Hindus</term> was not an idle display of logical subtlety that generally ends in entangling itself in the meshes of sophistry. Their aim in philosophizing was to gain an intellectual insight into the significance of existence and the destiny of humanity. They did not believe in anything blindly, nor did they accept anything based on mere tradition. <term>Buddha</term> most characteristically echoes this sentiment when he says, "Follow my teachings not as taught by a <term>Buddha</term>, but as

<vocab>Buddhism, Brahmans, Kṣatriyas, Hindus, Buddha, Intellectualism, Philosophy, India</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the intellectual nature of Buddhism, explaining how its philosophical character stems from ancient Indian culture where the higher castes had the leisure for metaphysical speculation. It notes that in India, religion and philosophy were integrated, focusing on intellectual insight rather than blind tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, Indian philosophy, Brahmans, Kṣatriyas, Hindus, Buddha, intellectualism, metaphysical speculation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellectual nature of Buddhism, focusing on its emphasis on self-reliance and logical inquiry rather than blind faith.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER I.</header>

being in accord with the truth."' This spirit of self-reliance and self-salvation later became uniquely Buddhist. Even when <term>Buddha</term> was still merely an enthusiastic seeker of <term>Nirvana</term>, he seems to have been strongly possessed by this spirit; for he most emphatically declared the following famous passage, in response to the pathetic persuasion of his father’s ministers, who wanted him to return home with them:

> "The doubt regarding whether anything exists or not is not to be settled for me by another’s words. Arriving at the truth either by mortification or by tranquilization, I will grasp for myself whatever can be ascertained about it. It is not for me to accept a view that is full of conflicts, uncertainties, and contradictions. What enlightened men would follow another's faith? The masses are like the blind led through the darkness by the blind." 1

To say simply, "Love your enemy," was not satisfactory to the <term>Hindu</term> mind; it wanted to see the reason why. And as soon as the people were convinced intellectually, they went even so far as to defend the faith with their lives. It was not an uncommon event that before a group of <term>Hindu</term> philosophers entered into a discussion, they made an agreement that the penalty for defeat should be the sacrifice of their lives. They were, above all, a people of intellect, though of course not lacking in religious sentiment.

It is no wonder, then, that <term>Buddha</term> did not begin the first proclamation of his message with "Repent, for

"the kingdom of heaven is at hand," but with the establishment of the Four Noble Truths. ¹ One appeals to feeling, and the other to the intellect. That which appeals to the intellect naturally seems to be less passionate, but the truth is, feeling without the support of the intellect leads to fanaticism and is always ready to give way to bigotry and superstition.

The doctrine of <term>Nirvana</term> is undoubtedly more intellectual than the Christian gospel of love. It first recognizes the misery of human life as proven by our daily experiences; it then finds its cause in our subjective ignorance regarding the true meaning of existence, and in our ego-centered desires which, by obscuring our spiritual insight, make us cling tenaciously to illusions; it then proposes the complete annihilation of egoism, the root of all evil, through which peace of heart is restored subjectively, and the realization of universal love becomes possible objectively. Thus, Buddhism proceeds most logically in the development of its doctrine of <term>Nirvana</term> and universal love.

Victor Hugo says (*Les Misérables*, vol. II): "The reduction of the universe to a single being, the expansion of a single being even to God, this is love." When a man clings to the self and does not want

## CHAPTER II.
### HISTORICAL CHARACTERIZATION OF MAHÂYÂNISM.

We are now in a position to begin a specific explanation of the <term>Mahâyâna</term> doctrine. But, before doing so, it would be beneficial for us first to consider the views that were held by the Hindu Buddhist thinkers regarding its characteristic features; in other words, to make a historical survey of its specific traits.

<margin>⚆</margin>As stated in the Introduction, the term <term>Mahâyâna</term> was created in the time of <term>Nâgârjuna</term> and <term>Âryadeva</term> (about the third or fourth century AD), when doctrinal struggles between the <term>Çrâvaka</term> and the <term>Bodhisattva</term> classes reached a climax. The progressive Hindu Buddhists, wishing to declare the essential features of their doctrine, did so naturally at the expense of their rivals and by pointing out why theirs was greater than, or superior to, <term>Hînayânism</term>. Their views were thus necessarily marred by a partisan spirit; instead of impartially and critically listing the main characteristics of <term>Mahâyânism</term>, they placed too much emphasis on those points that do not in these modern days appear to be very essential, but that were then considered by them to be of paramount importance. These points, never-

<summary>This page introduces a historical survey of Mahâyâna Buddhism, noting that the term originated during a period of intense doctrinal conflict. The author suggests that early definitions of the doctrine were often biased by the sectarian rivalries of the third and fourth centuries.</summary>

<keywords>Mahâyâna, Nâgârjuna, Âryadeva, Çrâvaka, Bodhisattva, Hînayânism, Buddhist history, religious doctrine</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical characterization of Mahâyânism, specifically focusing on the definitions provided by the scholar Sthiramati.</meta>

theless, throw some light on the nature of <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhism as historically distinguished from its closely related rival and fellow doctrine.

### *Sthiramati's Conception of Mahâyânism.*

<term>Sthiramati</term><sup>1</sup> in his *Introduction to Mahâyânism* states that <term>Mahâyânism</term> is a special doctrine for the <term>Bodhisattvas</term>, who are to be distinguished from the other two classes, namely, the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>. The essential difference of the doctrine consists in the belief that objects of the senses are merely phenomenal and have no absolute reality; that the indestructible <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which is all-pervading, constitutes the norm of existence; and that all <term>Bodhisattvas</term><sup>2</sup> are incarnations of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, who—not because of their evil <term>karma</term> previously accumulated, but because of their boundless love for all mankind—assume

---

<sup>1</sup> <note>His date is not known, but judging from the contents of his works, of which we currently have two or three among the Chinese <term>Tripitaka</term>, it seems that he lived after <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>, but before or at the same time as <term>Nâgàrjuna</term>. This little book occupies a very important position in the development of <term>Mahâyânism</term> in India. Next to <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>'s *Awakening of Faith*, the work must be carefully studied by scholars who want to grasp every phase of the history of the <term>Mahâyâna</term> school as far as it can be learned through the Chinese documents.</note>

<sup>2</sup> <note>Let it be noted here that a <term>Bodhisattva</term> is not a particularly favored person in the sense of a "chosen people" or "the elect." We are all, in a way, <term>Bodhisattvas</term>; that is, when we recognize the truth that we are equally in possession of the *<term>Samyak-sambodhi</term>* (Highest True Intelligence), through which everyone without exception can attain final enlightenment.</note>

<summary>This page discusses Sthiramati's definition of Mahâyânism as a doctrine for Bodhisattvas, emphasizing the phenomenal nature of reality and the all-pervading Dharmakâya. It also clarifies that the status of a Bodhisattva is an inherent potential within all people rather than a privilege for a selected few.</summary>

corporeal existences, and that persons who thus appear in the flesh, as avatars of the supreme Buddha, associate themselves with the masses in all possible social relations, so that they might lead them to a state of enlightenment.

While this is a very brief statement of the Mahâyâna doctrine, a more elaborate and extended list of its specific features, in contrast to those of Hînayânism, is found in the *Miscellanea on Mahâyâna Metaphysics*,¹ *The Spiritual Stages of the Yogâcâra*,² *An Exposition of the Holy Doctrine*,³ *A Comprehensive Treatise on Mahâyânism*,⁴ and others. Let us first explain the "Seven General Characteristics" as described in the first three works mentioned here.

### *Seven Principal Features of Mahâyânism.*

According to Asanga, who lived slightly later than Nâgârjuna—that is, at the time when Mahâyânism was further divided into the Yogâcârya and the Mâdhyamika schools—the seven features peculiar to Mahâyânism, as distinguished from Hînayânism, are as follows:

(1) *Its Comprehensiveness.* Mahâyânism does not confine itself to the teachings of only one Buddha;

---

¹ <note>*Mahâyâna-abhidharma-sangîti-çâstra*, by Asanga. Nanjo, No. 1199.</note>
² <note>*Yogâcârya-bhûmi-çâstra*, Nanjo, No. 1170. The work is supposed to have been dictated to Asanga by a mythical Bodhisattva.</note>
³ <note>By Asanga. Nanjo, 1177.</note>
<margin>></margin> ⁴ <note>*Mahâyâna-samparigraha-çâstra*, by Asanga. Nanjo, 1183.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the "Seven General Characteristics" of Mahâyâna Buddhism as outlined by the scholar Asanga, contrasting the school's comprehensiveness with Hînayânism. It lists several key metaphysical and doctrinal texts that define these differences during the period when Mahâyânism split into the Yogâcârya and Mâdhyamika schools.</summary>

<keywords>Mahâyâna, Hînayânism, Asanga, Nâgârjuna, Yogâcârya, Mâdhyamika, Bodhisattva, Nanjo, avatar, Seven Principal Features, Buddhist metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the "Seven General Characteristics" of Mahayana Buddhism, building upon the first point regarding its comprehensiveness.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II.</header>

but wherever and whenever truth is found, even under the disguise of most absurd superstitions, it does not hesitate to winnow the grain from the husk and incorporate it into its own system. Countless good laws taught by Buddhas <gloss>1</gloss> of all ages and localities are all taken up into the coherent body of Mahayanism.

(2) *Universal love for All Sentient Beings*. Hinayanism limits itself to the salvation of individuals only; it does not extend its bliss universally, as each person must achieve his own deliverance. Mahayanism, on the other hand, aims at general salvation; it endeavors to save us not only individually, but universally. All the motives, efforts, and actions of the Bodhisattvas revolve around the promotion of universal welfare.

(3) *Its Greatness in Intellectual Comprehension*. Mahayanism maintains the theory of <term>non-atman</term> not only in regard to sentient beings but in regard to things in general. While it denies the hypothesis of a metaphysical agent directing our mental operations, it also rejects the view that insists on the noumenal or inherent reality of things as they appear to our senses.

(4) *Its Marvelous Spiritual Energy*. The Bodhisattvas never become tired of working for universal salvation,

---

¹ Perceiving an incarnation of the <term>Dharmakaya</term> in every spiritual leader regardless of his nationality and professed creed, Mahayanists recognize a Buddha in Socrates, Muhammad, Jesus, Francis of Assisi, Confucius, Laozi, and many others.

<summary>This page details the characteristics of Mahayana Buddhism, including its ability to assimilate truth from any source, its focus on universal love and salvation for all beings, its intellectual theory of non-self (non-atman) regarding all things, and the tireless spiritual energy of the Bodhisattvas.</summary>

<keywords>Mahayanism, Hinayanism, Bodhisattvas, non-atman, Dharmakaya, universal salvation, sentient beings, intellectual comprehension, spiritual energy, religious pluralism</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the "Seven General Characteristics" of Mahayana Buddhism from page 63, concluding the fourth point regarding spiritual energy and detailing the fifth and sixth characteristics.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER II.</header>

nor do they lose hope because of the long time required to achieve this significant goal. Attempting to reach enlightenment in the shortest possible time and being self-sufficient without paying any attention to the welfare of the masses is not the teaching of Mahayanism.

(5) *Its Greatness in the Exercise of the <term>Upâya</term>.* The term *<term>upâya</term>* literally means expediency or skillful means. The great, fatherly, compassionate heart of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> has endless resources at his command so that he might lead the masses to final enlightenment, each according to their own nature and circumstances. Mahayanism does not ask its followers to escape the cycle of birth and death (<term>metempsychosis</term>) simply to enter the lethargic peace of <term>Nirvâna</term>; for the cycle of rebirth in itself is not an evil, and a <term>Nirvâna</term> that is like a coma produces no good. As long as there are souls suffering in pain, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> cannot rest in <term>Nirvâna</term>; there is no rest for his unselfish heart, so full of love and compassion, until he leads all his fellow beings to the eternal bliss of Buddhahood. To achieve this goal, he uses countless methods (*<term>upâya</term>*) inspired by his selfless loving-kindness.

(6) *Its Higher Spiritual Attainment.* In Hinayanism, the highest bliss one can achieve does not go beyond Arhatship, which is ascetic saintliness. But the followers of Mahayanism attain even to Buddhahood with all its spiritual powers.

(7) *Its Greater Activity.* When the <term>Bodhisattva</term>

<summary>This page describes how Mahayanism rejects a quick, selfish path to enlightenment in favor of using "upaya" (skillful means) to save all suffering beings. These seven characteristics are listed as the reasons why the doctrine defended by progressive Buddhists is called <term>Mahâyânism</term>, or the doctrine of the "great vehicle," in contrast to <term>Hînayânism</term>, the doctrine of the "small vehicle." In each case, therefore, Asanga takes care to clearly distinguish between the two schools of Buddhism, rather than between Buddhism and all other religious doctrines that existed in his time.

(1) It teaches an immanent existence of all things in the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term> or All-Conserving Soul. The conception of an All-Conserving Soul, it is claimed, was suggested by Buddha in the so-called <term>Hînayâna</term> sutras; but because of its deep meaning and the risk of it being confused with the ego-soul conception, he did not disclose its full significance in those sutras, but made it known only in the <term>Mahâyâna</term> sutras.

The <term>Ālayavijñāna</term>, to use the terminology of Samkhya philosophy, is a composition, so to speak, of the Soul (*puruṣa*) and Primordial Matter (*prakṛti*). It is the Soul insofar as it is neutral and indifferent to all those phenomenal manifestations occurring within as well as outside of us. It is Primordial Matter in that it is the reservoir of everything; when its lid is lifted by the hands of Ignorance, this universe of limitation and relativity instantly springs up. Enlightenment or <term>Nirvāṇa</term>, therefore, consists in recognizing the error of Ignorance and not in clinging to the products of imagination.</note>

...are terribly shocked for that reason. But when they approach and carefully examine it, they are immediately convinced that this fear is groundless, as it was the natural result of an illusion. This can be considered to correspond to what Kant calls *Schein*.

Most people, however, do not investigate any further. They are satisfied with the sensory, empirical knowledge of the objects they encounter. When they realize that what they mistook for a snake was actually just a yard of harmless rope, they believe their knowledge of the object is complete and do not bother with a philosophical inquiry into whether the rope—which seems to be exactly what it appears to be—has any real existence in itself. They do not pause for a moment to reflect that their knowledge is merely relative, as it does not go beyond the phenomenal appearance of the things they perceive.

But is an object in reality exactly as it appears to our senses? Are specific phenomena, as such, truly real? What is the value of our knowledge regarding these so-called realities? When we investigate such problems as these, the <term>Yogācāra</term> school says we find that their existence is

(3) When we attain perfect knowledge, we recognize the mental nature of the universe. There is no such thing as an objective world; it is actually an illusory manifestation of the mind called the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term>. But even this supposedly real existence of the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term> is a product of discrimination brought about by the ignorant <term>Manovijñâna</term>. The <term>Manovijñâna</term>, or "empirical ego" as it might be called, lacks adequate knowledge regarding the true nature of the <term>Âlaya</term>. It treats the <term>Âlaya</term> as a metaphysical agent that, like a puppet master, manages all mental operations according to its whims. Just as a silkworm imprisons itself in a cocoon of its own making, the <term>Manovijñâna</term>, entangling itself in ignorance and confusion, mistakes its own illusory creations for true realities.

(4) To regulate moral life, the <term>Yogâcâra</term>, along with other <term>Mahâyâna</term> schools, proposes practicing the six <term>Pâramitâs</term> (virtues of perfection). The <term>Bodhisattva</term>, on the other hand, does not wish to be bound within the narrow circle of moral restriction. (7) Since <term>Mahâyânism</term> insists on the purification of the inner life, its teaching does not apply to outward things, and its principles are not of an ascetic or exclusive kind. The Mahayanists do not avoid mingling with the “dust of worldliness”; they aim at the realization of the <term>Bodhi</term>; they are not afraid of being thrown into the whirlpool of <term>metempsychosis</term>; they endeavor to provide spiritual benefits to all sentient beings regardless of their attitude, whether hostile or friendly, toward themselves. Having immovable faith in the <term>Mahâyâna</term>, they never become contaminated by vanity and worldly pleasures with which they may be in constant contact; they have a clear insight into the doctrine of <term>non-âtman</term>; being free from all spiritual faults, they live in perfect accord with the laws of <term>Suchness</term> and discharge their duties without the...

(8) The intellectual superiority of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> is shown by his possession of the knowledge of non-particularization (*<term>anânârtha</term>*).<note>1</note> This knowledge, considered philosophically, is the knowledge of the absolute or the knowledge of the universal. The <term>Bodhisattva</term>’s mind is free from the dualism of <term>samsâra</term> (birth-and-death) and <term>nirvâna</term>, of positivism and negativism, of being and non-being, of object and subject, and of ego and non-ego. In short, his knowledge transcends the limits of finite realities, soaring high to the realm of the absolute and the dwelling place of non-particularity.

(9) As a result of this intellectual elevation, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> perceives the working of birth and death within <term>nirvâna</term>, and <term>nirvâna</term> within the transmigration of birth and death. He sees the “ever-changing many” in the “never-changing one,” and the “never-

---

<note>1. <term>Mahâyânism</term> recognizes two “entrances” through which a comprehensive knowledge of the universe is obtained. One is called the “entrance of sameness” (*<term>samatâ</term>*) and the other the “entrance of diversity” (*<term>nânâtva</term>*). The first entrance introduces us to the universality of things and suggests a pantheistic interpretation of existence. The second leads us to the particularity of things, culminating in monotheism or polytheism, as it is viewed from different standpoints. The Buddhists declare that neither entrance alone can lead us to the inner sanctuary of existence; and in order to obtain a sound, well-balanced knowledge of things in general, we must pass through both the entrances of universality and particularity.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Bodhisattva's intellectual ability to transcend dualism and perceive the absolute within the finite world. It explains that true spiritual knowledge requires understanding both the universal sameness and the individual diversity of all things.</summary>

According to the former, he does not recoil as ascetics do when he comes into contact with the world of the senses; he is not afraid of suffering the "ills that the flesh is heir to"; but, according to the latter, he never clings to fleeting things, as his inmost consciousness forever dwells in the serenity of eternal Suchness.

(10) The final characteristic to be mentioned as distinctly <term>Mahâyânistic</term> is the doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term>. It is asserted that there is a highest being who is the ultimate cause of the universe and in whom all existences find their essential origin and significance. This is called <term>Dharmakâya</term> by the <term>Mahâyânists</term>. The <term>Dharmakâya</term>, however, does not remain in its absoluteness; it reveals itself in the realm of cause and effect. It then takes a particular form. It becomes a devil, a god, a <term>deva</term>, a human being, or an animal of a lower grade, adapting itself to the levels of the people's intellectual development. For it is the people's inner needs which require these special forms of manifestation. This is called <term>Nirmânakâya</term>, that is, the body of transformation. The <term>Buddha</term> who manifested himself in the person of <term>Gautama</term>, the son of King <term>Çuddhodâna</term> about two thousand five hundred years ago on the Ganges, is a form of <term>Nirmânakâya</term>. They offer only a general and somewhat obscure description of it, and do so in opposition to <term>Hînayânism</term>. In fact, <term>Mahayanism</term> is a school of Buddhism and has many characteristics in common with <term>Hînayânism</term>. # SPECULATIVE MAHÂYÂNISM.

## CHAPTER III.

### PRACTICE AND SPECULATION.

Mahâyânism can perhaps best be treated in two main divisions, as it has two distinct principal features in its doctrinal development. I may call one the speculative phase of Mahâyânism and the other the practical phase. The first part is essentially a kind of Buddhist metaphysics, where the mind is engaged solely in reasoning and abstraction. Here, the intellect plays a very prominent part, and some of the most complex problems of philosophy are freely discussed. Speculative followers of Buddhism have taken great interest in discussing them and have written many volumes on various subjects. <note>No efforts have yet been made to systematically trace the history of the development of Mahâyâna thought in India, as well as in China and Japan. We have enough material at least to follow the general course it has taken, as far as the Chinese and Tibetan collections of the <term>Tripitaka</term> are concerned. When impartial, unprejudiced scholars have made a thorough comparison of these documents with <term>Pali</term> and <term>Sanskrit</term> literature, we will then be able to write a comprehensive history of the human thoughts that</note>

<summary>The author divides Mahâyânism into two phases: speculative (metaphysical) and practical. He notes that while a comprehensive history of Mahâyâna thought has not yet been written, sufficient records exist in various languages to eventually do so.</summary>

<keywords>Mahâyânism, Buddhist metaphysics, philosophy, Tripitaka, Mahâyâna thought, Pali, Sanskrit, doctrinal development</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mahâyânism's two phases, moving from the definition of speculative and practical components to the importance of religious sentiment and the relationship between religion and philosophy. It also concludes the long footnote regarding the historical study of Buddhist thought.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

The second, or practical, phase of Mahâyânism deals with the religious beliefs that constitute the life and essence of the system. Mahâyânists might have reasoned incorrectly to explain their practical faith, but the faith itself is the expression of the religious sentiment inherent in human nature. This practical part, therefore, is far more important; in fact, it can be said that the speculative part is merely a preparatory step toward it. Since Mahâyânism is a religion and not just a philosophical system, it must be practical—that is, it must appeal directly to the inner life of the human heart.

### *The Relation of Feeling and Intellect in Religion.*

Much has been said about the relationship between philosophy and religion. There are many scholars who so firmly believe that religion is identical to either superstition or supernatural revelation that they consider any denial of this assertion to be a rejection of all religion. According to them, there is no middle ground in religion. A religion that is rational and yet practical is no religion at all. Now, Buddhism is neither a whim of the imagination nor a revelation from above, and for this reason, some have declared it to be a philosophy. The title “Speculative Mahâyânism,” therefore, is likely to

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between philosophy and religion in Mahāyānism, specifically addressing why the system is often mistaken for a purely intellectual philosophy rather than a practical religion.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

...be taken as a confirmation of that opinion. To remove all the misconceptions, therefore, which might be held concerning the religious nature of <term>Mahāyānism</term> and its attitude toward intellectualism, I have thought it wise to say a few words here about the relationship between feeling and intellect in religion.

There is no doubt that religion is essentially practical; it does not necessarily require theorizing. The latter, properly speaking, is the business of philosophy. If religion were solely a product of the intellect, it could not satisfy the needs of a person's whole being. Reason constitutes only a part of the organized totality of an individual being. No matter how high the abstraction or how deep the speculation, these things do not, by themselves, satisfy the deepest yearnings of the human heart. However, they can do so when they enter into one's inner life and character; that is, when <margin>✓</margin> abstraction becomes a concrete fact and speculation becomes a living principle in one's existence. In short, this is when philosophy becomes religion.

Therefore, philosophy as such is generally distinguished from religion. But we must not suppose that religion, as the deepest expression of a human being, can entirely eliminate the intellectual element. The most dominant role in religion may be played by imagination and feeling, but <term>ratiocination</term> must not fail to assert its legitimate right in the coordination of beliefs. When this right is denied, religion becomes fanaticism, superstition, a <term>fata morgana</term>, and even a threat to the progress of humanity.

<summary>The author argues that while religion is primarily practical and rooted in feeling, it must incorporate reason to avoid becoming superstition. He explains that philosophy transforms into religion when its abstract speculations become living principles within an individual's life.</summary>

<page-num>79</page-num>

<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

The intellect is critical and objective, and it always tries to stand apart from the things it examines. This alienation, or the intellect’s tendency to keep itself aloof from concrete facts, constantly tends to disregard the real significance of life, of which the intellect is also a manifestation. Therefore, the conflict between feeling and reason, religion and science, and instinct and knowledge has been ongoing since the awakening of consciousness.

Recognizing this fact, intellectual people are generally prone to condemn religion for barring freedom and obstructing the progress of scientific investigations. It is true that religion frequently went to the other extreme and tried to suppress the just claims of reason; it is true that this was especially the case with Christianity, whose history abounds with regrettable incidents resulting from its violent encroachments upon the domain of reason. It is also true that feeling and the intellect are sometimes at odds—that what feeling values as its most precious treasure is at times relentlessly crushed by reason, while feeling looks with utter contempt at the results the intellect has reached after much <term>lucubration</term> <note>Intense study or scholarly labor</note>. But this fatal conflict is no better than the fight that takes place between the head and the tail of a <term>hydra</term> when it is cut in two; it always results in self-destruction.

We cannot live under such a miserable condition forever, especially when we know that it is entirely due to a myopia on the part of our understanding. The

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interdependence of feeling and intellect, arguing that reason and emotion must work together for human development, and notes the lack of a distinction between religion and philosophy in Indian thought.</meta>

<margin>-</margin> The truth is that feeling and reason "cannot do without one another, and must work together inseparably in the process of human development, since reason without feeling would have nothing to act for and would be powerless to act, while feeling without reason would act tyrannically and blindly—that is to say, if either could exist and act at all without the other; for in the end it is neither feeling nor reason that acts, but it is the man who acts according to how he feels and reasons." (H. Maudsley's *Natural Causes and Supernatural Seemings*, p. vii).

If it is thus admitted that feeling and reason must coordinate and cooperate in the realization of human ideals, religion, though essentially a phenomenon of the emotional life, cannot be indifferent to the significance of the intellect. Indeed, religion, just as much as philosophy, has always been speculating on the problems that are of the most vital importance to human life. In Christianity, speculation has been carried on under the name of theology, though it claims to be fundamentally a religion of faith. In India, however, as mentioned elsewhere, there was no dividing line between philosophy and religion; and every teaching, every system, and every doctrine, however abstract and speculative it might appear to the Western mind, was fundamentally religious and always aimed at the deliverance of the soul. There was no philosophical system that did not have some practical purpose.

Indian thinkers could not separate religion from

If their thinking was not in the right direction and led to a fallacy which made life more miserable, they were ever ready to surrender themselves to a superior doctrine as soon as it was discovered. But when they thought they were on the right track, they did not hesitate to sacrifice their lives for it. Their philosophy had as much fire as religion.

->*Buddhism and Speculation.*<-

Owing to this fact, Buddhism, as much as Hinduism, is full of abstract speculations and philosophical reflections—so much so that some Christian critics are inclined to deny the religious nature of Buddhism. But no student of the science of comparative religion would endorse such a view nowadays. Buddhism, in spite of its predominant intellectualism, is really a religious system. There is no doubt that it emphasizes the rational element of religion more than any other religious teachings, but on 그 account, we cannot say that it altogether disregards the importance of the part to be played by feeling. Its speculative, philosophical phase is really a preparation for fully appreciating the subjective significance of religion; for religion is ultimately subjective—that is to say, the essence of religion is love and faith, or, to use Buddhist terminology, it is the expression of the <term>Bodhi</term> which

and have acquired technical meaning. In this work, both *prajñâ* and *bodhi* are mostly translated as intelligence, for their meanings overlap closely. But this is rather vague, and wherever I thought the term "intelligence" alone was misleading, I either left the originals untranslated or inserted them in parentheses. Might Bodhi be considered in some respects synonymous with the "divine wisdom" as understood by Christian dogmatists? This seems to be due to the difference of philosophical interpretation by Buddhists and Christians regarding the conception of God. It will become clearer as we proceed further.

Indeed, the more severely the religious sentiment is tested in the crucible of the intellect, the more glorious and illuminating its intrinsic virtue becomes. True religion is, therefore, never reluctant to appear before the tribunal of scientific investigation. In fact, by ignoring the ultimate significance of religious consciousness, science is digging its own grave. For what purpose does science have other than the unraveling of the mysteries of nature and interpreting the meaning of existence? And is this not what constitutes the foundation of religion? Science cannot be final; it must find its reason in religion. As a mere intellectual exercise, it is not worthy of our serious consideration.

# CHAPTER IV. CLASSIFICATION OF KNOWLEDGE.

### *Three Forms of Knowledge.*

MAHÂYÂNISM generally distinguishes between two or three forms of knowledge. This classification is a type of epistemology, since it proposes to determine the extent and nature of human knowledge from a religious perspective. Its object is to see what kind of human knowledge is most reliable and valuable for the destruction of ignorance and the attainment of enlightenment. The Mahāyāna school which has given the most attention to this division of Buddhist philosophy is the Yogâcâra of Asanga and Vasubandhu. The *Lankâvatarâ* and the *Sandhinirmocana* and some other Sûtras, on which the school claims to have its doctrinal foundation, teach three forms of knowledge. 

The sûtra literature, however, as a rule does not provide any detailed explanation of the subject; it merely classifies knowledge and points out what form of knowledge is most desirable for Buddhists. To obtain a fuller and more comprehensive explanation, we must turn to the Abhidharma Pitaka of that school. Among the textbooks most generally studied of the

<summary>This page introduces the Buddhist classification of knowledge as a form of epistemology intended to help practitioners reach enlightenment. It identifies the Yogācāra school and its primary texts as the main sources for this three-fold division of knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Mahāyānism, epistemology, enlightenment, Yogācāra, Asanga, Vasubandhu, Lankâvatarâ Sûtra, Sandhinirmocana Sûtra, Abhidharma Pitaka, Buddhist philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition into the specific epistemological theories of the Yogâcâra school, identifying foundational texts and defining the first of three types of knowledge: Illusion (parikalpita).</meta>

<language>English</language>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV.</header>

Regarding <term>Yogâcâra</term>, we may mention <term>Vasubandhu’s</term> *Vijñānamâtra* with its commentaries and <term>Asanga’s</term> *Comprehensive Treatise on Mahâyânism*. The following statements are summarized mainly from these documents.

The three forms of knowledge as classified by the <term>Yogâcâra</term> are: (1) Illusion (*parikalpita*), (2) Relative Knowledge (*paratantra*), and (3) Absolute Knowledge (*pariniṣpanna*).

### *Illusion.*

Illusion (*parikalpita*), to use Kantian terminology, is a sense perception not coordinated by the categories of the understanding; that is to say, it is a purely subjective elaboration, not verified by objective reality and critical judgment. So long as we make no practical application of it, it will harbor no danger; there is no evil in it, at least from a religious perspective. Perceptual illusion is a psychological fact, and as such it is justified. A straight rod in water appears bent because of the refraction of light; a sensation is often felt in a limb after it has been amputated, for the nervous system has not yet adjusted itself to the new condition. These are all illusions, however. They are undoubtedly the correct interpretation of the sense impressions in question, but they are not confirmed by other sense impressions whose coordination is necessary to establish an objective reality. The moral involved in this is: all sound inferences and correct behavior must be based on critical knowledge and not on illusory premises.

<language>English</language>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV.</header>

Reasoning in this way, the Mahâyânists declare that the egoism fostered by ordinary minds belongs to this category of knowledge, though of a different order. They argue that those who stubbornly cling to egoism as their final stronghold are believers in an intellectual mirage; they are like the thirsty deer that madly <margin>seeks</margin> after visionary water in the desert, or like the clever monkey that tries to catch the moon’s reflection in the water. This is because the belief in the existence of a metaphysical agent behind our mental phenomena is not confirmed by experience and sound judgment, as it is merely a product of unenlightened subjectivity.

In addition to this ethical and philosophical egoism, all forms of worldviews founded on the sandy basis of subjective illusion—such as fetishism, idolatry, anthropomorphism, anthropopsychism, and the like—must be classified under the *parikalpita-lakṣaṇa* as doctrines with illusory premises.

### *Relative Knowledge.*

Next comes the *paratantra-lakṣaṇa*, a worldview based upon relative knowledge, or more accurately, upon the knowledge of the law of relativity. According to this view, everything in the world has a relative and conditional existence, and nothing can claim an absolute reality free from all limitations. This closely corresponds to the theory advanced by most modern scientists, whose agnosticism denies our intellectual ability to transcend the law of relativity.

<vocab>Mahâyânists, egoism, mirage, metaphysical agent, subjectivity, fetishism, anthropomorphism, parikalpita-lakṣaṇa, paratantra-lakṣaṇa, worldview, law of relativity, agnosticism</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the critique of illusion (parikalpita) by identifying egoism and various "isms" as subjective illusions, then introduces relative knowledge (paratantra) as a worldview based on the conditional nature of existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the second form of knowledge in Yogâcâra Buddhism, paratantra-lakṣaṇa (relative knowledge), characterizing it as a form of positivism or agnosticism that recognizes the limits of sensory experience but fails to satisfy the deeper spiritual yearnings of the human heart.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>90</page-num>
<header># CHAPTER IV.</header>

<margin>/</margin> The *paratantra-lakṣaṇa*, therefore, consists of the knowledge derived from our daily interaction with the external world. It deals with the highest abstractions we can draw from our sensory experiences. It is positivistic in its strictest sense. It says: The universe has only a relative existence, and our knowledge is necessarily limited. Even the highest generalization cannot go beyond the law of relativity. It is impossible for us to know the first cause and the final end of existence; nor do we have any need to go beyond the sphere of existence in this way, which would inevitably involve us in the maze of mystical imagination.

The *paratantra-lakṣaṇa*, therefore, is a form of positivism, agnosticism, or empiricism in its spirit. Although the Yogâcâra Buddhists do not use all these modern philosophical terms, the interpretation given here is truly what they intended to mean by the second form of knowledge. A worldview based on this perspective, as the Mahâyânists declare, is sound as far as our perceptual knowledge is concerned; but it does not exhaust the entire field of human experience, for it does not take into account our spiritual life and our inmost consciousness. There is something in the human heart that refuses to be satisfied with merely systematizing those many impressions we receive from the external world under the so-called laws of nature. There is a singular feeling, sentiment, or yearning—whatever we may call it—in our inmost heart, which defies any simpler

<vocab>paratantra-lakṣaṇa, Yogâcâra, Mahâyânists, positivism, agnosticism, empiricism, law of relativity, spiritual life, consciousness</vocab>

<summary>This page defines paratantra-lakṣaṇa as a form of relative knowledge akin to modern positivism or agnosticism, which limits itself to sensory experience and natural laws. However, the author notes that Mahayana Buddhism considers this view incomplete because it ignores the spiritual depths and yearnings of human consciousness.</summary>

description than a mere suggestion or an indirect statement. This somewhat mystical consciousness, despite its obscurity, seems to contain the meaning of our existence as well as that of the universe. The intellect may try to persuade us with all its subtle reasoning to suppress this disquieting feeling and to remain content with the so-called systematization of natural laws. But it deceives itself by doing so; because the intellect is merely a servant to the heart, and as long as it is not forced into self-contradiction, it must accommodate itself to the heart's needs. That is to say, we must transcend the narrow limits of conditionality and see which indispensable postulates underlie our life and experiences. The recognition of these indispensable postulates of life constitutes the Yogâcâra's third form of knowledge, called *pariniṣpanna-lakṣaṇa*.

->*Absolute Knowledge.*<-

*Pariniṣpanna-lakṣaṇa* literally means the worldview founded on the most perfect knowledge. According to this view, the universe is a monistic and pantheistic system. While phenomenal existences are regulated by natural laws characterized by conditionality and individuation, they by no means exhaust all our experiences, which are stored in our innermost consciousness. There must be something—this is the absolute demand of humanity, the ultimate postulate of experience—whether it be Will or Intelligence, which, underlying and animating all existences, forms

### *Worldviews Founded on the Three Forms of Knowledge.*

The reason why the Yogâcâra school distinguishes 
three classes of world-conceptions founded on the 
three kinds of knowledge will be obvious to the reader. 
The *parikalpita-lakṣaṇa* is most 
primitive and most puerile. However, in these days 
of enlightenment, what is believed by the masses is 
nothing else than a *parikalpita* conception of the world. 
Material existence, as it appears to our senses, 
is to them all in all. They seem to be unable to

How different is the world in which the multitudes of unreflecting minds live from that which is conceived by Buddhas and Bodhisattvas! Hartmann, a German thinker, is right when he says that the masses are at least a century behind in their intellectual culture. But the most strange thing in the world is that, in spite of all their ignorance and superstitious beliefs, the waves of universal transformation are ever carrying them onward to a destination of which, perhaps, they have not the slightest suspicion.

The *paratantra-lakṣaṇa* advances a step further, but the fundamental error involved in it is its persistent, self-contradictory disregard for what our inmost consciousness is constantly revealing to us. The intellect alone can by no means unravel the mystery of our entire existence. In order to reach the highest truth, we must boldly plunge with our whole being into a region where absolute darkness, defying the light of intellect, is supposed to prevail. This region, which is nothing more nor less than the field of religious consciousness, is shunned by most intellectual people on the grounds that the intellect, by its very nature, is unable to fathom it. But the only way that leads us to the final peace for the heart's yearnings is to go beyond the horizons of limiting reason and to resort to the faith that has been planted in the heart as the *sine qua non* of its own existence and vitality. And by faith I mean *Prajñâ* (wisdom), transcendental

Buddhism calls this exalted spiritual state Nirvâna or Mokṣa; and *pariniṣpanna-lakṣaṇa* is a world-conception which naturally follows from this subjective, ideal enlightenment. ¹

### *Two Forms of Knowledge.*

The other Hindu Mahâyânism, the Mâdhyamika school of Nâgârjuna, distinguishes two, instead of three, orders of knowledge, but practically the Yogâcâra and the Mâdhyamika come to the same conclusion. ²

---

<note>¹ For a detailed exposition of the three forms of knowledge, the reader is requested to read Asanga's *Comprehensive Treatise on Mahâyânism* (Nanjo's Catalogue, No. 1183), Vasubandhu's work on Mahâyâna idealism (*Vijnânamâtra Çâstra*, Nanjo, No. 1215), the *Sûtra on the Mystery of Deliverance* (*Sandhinirmocana-sûtra*, Nanjo Nos. 246 and 247), etc.</note>

<note>² When the eminent representatives of both parties, such as Dharmapala and Bhavaviveka, were at the height of their literary activity in India about the fifth or sixth century after Christ, their partisan spirit incited them to denounce each other bitterly, forgetting the common ground on which their principles were established. Their disagreement, upon which they placed an undue emphasis, was in fact of a very trifling nature. It was merely a quarrel over phraseology, for one insisted on using certain words in exactly the sense which the other rejected.</note>

<margin>)</margin>The two kinds of knowledge or truth distinguished by the <term>Mâdhyamika</term> philosophy are <term>Samvṛtti-satya</term> and <term>Paramârtha-satya</term>, that is, conditional truth and transcendental truth. We read in Nâgârjuna's <term>Mâdhyamika Çâstra</term> (Buddhist Text Society edition, pp. 180, 181):

> "On two truths is founded
> The holy doctrine of Buddhas:
> Truth conditional,
> And truth transcendental.
>
> "Those who truly do not know
> The distinction between the two truths
> Do not know the essence
> Of Buddhism, which is meaningful." ¹

The conditional truth includes the illusion and relative knowledge of the <term>Yogâcâra</term> school, while the transcendental truth corresponds to absolute knowledge.

In explaining these two truths, the <term>Mâdhyamika</term> philosophers have made constant use of the terms <term>çûnya</term> and <term>açûnya</term>, void and not-void, which unfortunately became a cause of misunderstanding by Christian scholars of Nâgârjuna's transcendental philosophy. Absolute truth is void in its ultimate nature, for it contains nothing concrete or real or individual that makes it an object of particularization. But this must not be understood, as some superficial critics do, in the sense of absolute

nothingness. The Mâdhyamika philosophers consider the *satya* (transcendental truth) empty when contrasted with the reality of phenomenal existences. This is because it is not real in the sense that a particular being is real; rather, it is empty since it transcends the principle of individuation. When considered absolutely, it can be neither empty nor not-empty, neither *çûnya* nor *açûnya*, neither *asti* nor *nâsti*, neither *abhâva* nor *bhâva*, neither real nor unreal. All these terms imply relation and contrast, while the *Paramârtha Satya* is above them; or rather, it unifies all contrasts and antitheses in its absolute oneness. Therefore, even to designate it at all may lead to a misunderstanding of the true nature of the *Satya*, for naming is a form of particularizing. It is not, as such, an object of intellectualization or of demonstrative knowledge. It underlies everything conditional and phenomenal, and does not allow itself to be a particular object of discrimination.

### *Transcendental Truth and Relative Understanding*

One may say: If transcendental truth is of such an abstract nature, beyond the reach of the understanding, how can we ever hope to attain it and enjoy its blessings? But Nâgârjuna says that it is not absolutely out of the range of the understanding; it is, on the contrary, through the understanding that we become acquainted with the direction toward which our spiritual efforts should be directed, only

For it is one of the functions of science that it should purify the contents of a belief and that it should point out in which direction our final spiritual truth and consolation have to be sought. Science alone, which is built on relative knowledge, is not able to satisfy all our religious cravings, but it is certainly able to direct us to the path of enlightenment. # CHAPTER V.

## BHÛTATATHÂTÂ (SUCHNESS).

From an ontological point of view, Paramârtha-satya or Pariniṣpanna (transcendental truth) is called Bhûtatathâtâ, which literally means "the suchness of existence." Because Buddhism does not separate being from thought, nor thought from being, what is "suchness" in the objective world is transcendental truth in the subjective world, and *vice versa*. Bhûtatathâtâ, then, is the Godhead of Buddhism; it marks the culmination of all our mental efforts to reach the highest principle, which unifies all possible contradictions and spontaneously directs the course of world events. In short, it is the ultimate postulate of existence. Like Paramârtha-satya, as stated above, it does not belong to the domain of demonstrative knowledge or sensory experience. It is unknowable through the ordinary processes of reasoning which the natural sciences use to formulate general laws; Buddhists declare that it is grasped only by minds capable of exercising what might be called religious intuition.

In his *Awakening of Faith*, Açvaghoṣa argues for the indefinability of this first principle. When we say it is çûnya, or empty, because it is indepen-

<summary>This page defines Bhûtatathâtâ (Suchness) as the ultimate principle of existence and the "Godhead" of Buddhism, which unifies objective and subjective reality. It explains that this principle transcends scientific logic and sensory experience, being accessible only through religious intuition.</summary>
<keywords>Bhûtatathâtâ, Suchness, Paramârtha-satya, Pariniṣpanna, Buddhism, Açvaghoṣa, Awakening of Faith, ontology, transcendental truth, religious intuition, çûnya</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Bhûtatathâtâ from page 99, specifically addressing the difficulty of defining this first principle and the linguistic choices made by Açvaghoṣa.</meta>

dent of all the thinkable qualities which we attribute to relative and conditional things, people would mistake it for the nothingness of an absolute void. But when we define it as a real reality, standing above the fleeting nature of phenomena, they would imagine that there is something individual existing outside the limits of this universe which, though as concrete as we are, lives an eternal life. It is like describing to the blind what an elephant looks like; each of them receives only a very indistinct and imperfect conception of the huge creature, yet every one of them thinks he has a true and most comprehensive idea of it. <note>¹ See *The Udâna*, chapter VI.</note> Açvaghoṣa, therefore, wishes to avoid all definite statements concerning the ultimate nature of being; but as language is the only way that we mortals can express our ideas and communicate them to others, he thinks the best expression that can be given to it is Bhûtatathâtâ—that is, the "suchness of existence," or simply, "suchness."

Bhûtatathâtâ (suchness), viewed absolutely in this way, does not fall under the category of being and non-being; and minds that are kept within the narrow circle of contrasts must be considered incapable of grasping it as it truly is. As Nâgârjuna says in his Çâstra (Ch. XV.):

> "Between thisness (*svabhâva*) and thatness (*parabhâva*),
> Between being and non-being,
> Whoever discriminates,
> Does not perceive the truth of Buddhism." <note>² Svabhâvam parabhâvanca, bhâvancâbhâvameva ca, Ye paçyanti, na paçyante tatvam hi buddhaçâsane.</note>

Or,
> "To think 'it is' is eternalism,
> To think 'it is not' is nihilism:
> From being and non-being,
> The wise do not cling to either."¹

Again,
> "The dualism of 'being' and 'not being,'
> The dualism of pure and impure:
> Having abandoned such dualism,
> The wise do not stand even in the middle."²

To quote, again, from the *Awakening of Faith* (pp. 58—59): "In its metaphysical origin, Bhûtatathâtâ has nothing to do with defiled things—that is, the conditional. It is free from all signs of individualization, such as those that exist in phenomenal objects; it is independent of an unreal, particularizing consciousness."

### *Indefinability.*

Absolute Suchness, by its very nature, thus defies all definitions. We cannot even say that it exists, for everything that exists presupposes that which does not. Existence and non-existence are relative terms, just as much as subject and object, mind and matter, this and that, or one and another; one cannot be conceived

---

<summary>This page explains that Bhûtatathâtâ (Suchness) transcends dualistic categories like being and non-being, making it ultimately indefinable through relative language. It quotes Buddhist texts to show that the wise avoid the extremes of eternalism and nihilism.</summary>

without the other. "It is not so (*na iti*)" $^1$, therefore, may be the only way our imperfect human tongue can express it. So the <term>Mahâyânists</term> generally designate absolute Suchness as <term>Çûnyatâ</term> or void.

But when this most significant word, <term>çûnyatâ</term>, is to be more fully interpreted, we would say with <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> that "Suchness is neither that which is existence nor that which is non-existence; neither that which is at once existence and non-existence, nor that which is not at once existence and non-existence; it is neither that which is unity nor that which is plurality; neither that which is at once unity and plurality, nor that which is not at once unity and plurality." $^2$

---

<note>¹ This is the famous phrase in the *Brhadaranyaka Upanisad* appearing in several places (II, 3, 6; III, 9, 26; IV, 2, 4; IV, 4, 22; IV, 5, 5). The <term>Atman</term> or <term>Brahman</term>, it says, "is to be described as 'No, No!' He is incomprehensible, for he cannot be comprehended; he is imperishable, for he cannot perish; he is unattached, for he does not attach himself; unfettered, he does not suffer and does not fail. These two things do not overcome the one who knows, whether he says that for some reason he has done evil, or for some reason he has done good—he overcomes both, and neither what he has done, nor what he has omitted to do, affects him."</note>

<note>² *The Awakening of Faith*, p. 59. Compare this with the utterances of <term>Dionysius the Areopagite</term>, as quoted by Professor <term>W. James</term> in his *Varieties of Religious Experience*, pp. 416—417: "The cause of all things is neither soul nor intellect; nor does it have imagination, opinion, reason, or intelligence; nor is it spoken or thought. It is neither number, nor order, nor magnitude, nor littleness, nor equality, nor inequality, nor similarity, nor dissimilarity. It neither stands, moves, nor rests. . . . It is neither essence, nor eternity, nor time."</note>

It draws parallels between the Buddhist teachings of Açvaghoṣa and the descriptions of the absolute found in the Upanishads and the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite.</summary>

# CHAPTER V.

<term>Nagarjuna</term>'s famous doctrine of "The <term>Middle Path</term> of <term>Eight No's</term>" breathes the same spirit, which declares:

> "There is no death, no birth, no destruction, no persistence, No oneness, no manyness, no coming, no departing, <note>1</note>"

Elsewhere, he expresses the same idea in a somewhat paradoxical manner, making the historical <term>Buddha</term> a real concrete manifestation of <term>Suchness</term>:

> "After his passing, do not think this:
> 'The <term>Buddha</term> still is here,'
> He is above all contrasts,
> Of being and not being.
>
> "While living, do not think this:
> 'The <term>Buddha</term> is now here.'
> He is above all contrasts,
> Of being and not being." <note>2</note>

This view of <term>Suchness</term> as no-ness abounds in the literature of the <term>Dhyana</term> school of <term>Mahayanism</term>. To cite one instance: When <term>Bodhidharma</term> <note>3</note>, the founder

> Even intellectual contact does not belong to it. It is neither science nor truth. It is not even royalty nor wisdom; not one; not unity; not divinity or goodness; nor even spirit as we know it." . . . *at libitum*.

## CHAPTER V.

of the <term>Dhyana</term> sect, saw <term>Emperor Wu</term> of the <term>Liang dynasty</term> (A.D. 502—556), he was asked what the first principle of the Holy Doctrine was. He did not give any lengthy, roundabout statement in the manner of a philosopher, but concisely replied, “Vast emptiness and nothing holy.” The Emperor was bewildered and did not know how to interpret the words of his holy adviser. Naturally, he did not expect such an abrupt answer, and, being greatly disappointed, he asked another question: “Who is he, then, that stands before me?” By this, he meant to reject the doctrine of absolute <term>Suchness</term>. His line of argument was this: If there is nothing in the ultimate nature of things that distinguishes between holiness and sinfulness, why does this world of contrasts exist, where some are revered as holy—for instance, <term>Bodhidharma</term>, who is at this very moment standing in front of him with the mission of spreading the holy teachings of <term>Buddha</term>? <term>Bodhidharma</term>, however, was a mystic and was fully convinced of the inadequacy of the human tongue to express the highest truth which is revealed only

without making any further attempt to spread his mysticism. But finally, he found a most devoted disciple in the person of <term>Shen Kuang</term>, who was once a Confucian, and through whom the <term>Dhyana</term> school became one of the most powerful <term>Mahayana</term> sects in China as well as in Japan. <term>Dharma</term> died in the year 535. Besides the encounter mentioned here, he had another audience with the Emperor. At that time, the Emperor said to <term>Dharma</term>: “I have dedicated so many monasteries, copied so many sacred books, and converted so many <term>bhiksus</term> <note>monks</note> and <term>bhiksunis</term> <note>nuns</note>: what do you think my <term>merit</term> is or ought to be?” To this, however, <term>Dharma</term> replied curtly, “No <term>merit</term> whatever.”

<summary>This page recounts the famous exchange between Bodhidharma and Emperor Wu, where the Zen patriarch asserts that the ultimate truth is "vast emptiness" and rejects the Emperor's claims to religious merit. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Bodhidharma's response to Emperor Wu, specifically explaining that his answer "I do not know" refers to the ineffable nature of Suchness rather than agnosticism.</meta>

intuitively to the religious consciousness. His conclusive answer was, "I do not know."

This "I do not know" is not to be understood in the spirit of agnosticism, but in the sense that "God, when understood, is no God," for *in se est et per se conceptur* <note>He exists in Himself and is conceived through Himself</note>. This way of describing <term>Suchness</term> using only negative terms—excluding all differences of name and form (<term>nâmarûpa</term>) to reach a higher kind of affirmation—seems to be the most appropriate method, since human understanding is limited in so many respects. Nevertheless, it has caused much misinterpretation even among Buddhists themselves, not to mention those Christian Buddhist scholars of today who sometimes appear almost willfully to misconstrue the significance of the <term>çûnyatâ</term> philosophy. It was to avoid these unfortunate misinterpretations that the <term>Mahâyânists</term> frequently made the paradoxical assertion that absolute <term>Suchness</term> is empty and not empty (<term>çûnya</term> and <term>açunya</term>), being and non-being (<term>sat</term> and <term>asat</term>), one and many, this and that.

### *The "<term>Thundrous Silence</term>."*

There still remains another way of explaining absolute <term>Suchness</term>, which, though most practical and effective for religiously disposed minds, may prove very inadequate to a skeptical intellect.

<note>Another interesting utterance by a Chinese Buddhist, who, after earnestly pondering the absoluteness of <term>Suchness</term> for several years, understood it suddenly one day, is: "The very instant you say it is something (or nothing), you miss the mark."</note>
