# CHAPTER V.

It is the "thunderous silence" of Vimalakîrti in response to an inquiry concerning the nature of Suchness or the "Dharma of Non-duality," as it is called in the Sûtra.¹

<insert>red underline: Vimalakîrti once asked a multitude of Bodhisattvas led by Mañjuçri,</insert> who came to visit him, to express their views on how to enter into the Dharma of Non-duality. Some replied, "Birth and death are two, but the Dharma itself was never born and will never die. Those who understand this are said to enter into the Dharma of Non-duality." Some said, "'I' and 'mine' are two. Because I think 'I am,' there are things called 'mine.' But since there is no 'I am,' where shall we look for things that are 'mine'? By reflecting in this way, we enter into the Dharma of Non-duality." Some replied, "Samsâra and Nirvâna are two. But when we understand the ultimate nature of Samsâra, Samsâra vanishes from our consciousness, and there is neither bondage nor release, neither birth nor death. By reflecting in this way, we enter into the Dharma of Non-duality." Others said, "Ignorance and enlightenment are two. Where there is no ignorance, there is no enlightenment, and there is no dualism. Why? Because those who have entered a meditation in which there is no sense-impression or thought are free from ignorance as well as from enlightenment. This holds true for all the other dualistic categories. Those who enter into the thought of sameness in this way are

said to be entering into the Dharma of Non-duality." Still others answered, "To long for Nirvana and to avoid worldliness is dualistic. Do not long for Nirvana, do not avoid worldliness, and we are free from dualism. Why? Because bondage and release are relative terms, and when there is no bondage from the beginning, who wishes to be released? No bondage, no release, and therefore no longing and no avoiding: this is called entering into the Dharma of Non-duality."

Many more answers of a similar nature came from all the Bodhisattvas in the assembly except for the leader Mañjuçri. Vimalakîrti now requested that he give his own view, and to this Mañjuçri responded, "What I think may be stated thus: That which is wordless and speechless in all beings, shows no signs, cannot be cognized, and is above all questions and answers—to know this is said to be entering into the Dharma of Non-duality."

Finally, Mañjuçri demanded that the host Vimalakîrti himself express his idea of Non-duality, but he remained completely silent and did not utter a word. Thereupon, Mañjuçri admiringly exclaimed, "Well done, well done! The Dharma of Non-duality is truly above letters and words!" ¹

### CHAPTER V.

Now, concerning this **Suchness**, the Mahâyânists distinguish two aspects as it is understood by our consciousness: the conditional and the non-conditional, or the phenomenal world of causality and the transcendental realm of absolute freedom. This distinction corresponds to the one made in the field of knowledge between relative truth and transcendental truth. <note>¹ It is a well-known fact that the Vedanta philosophy also makes a similar distinction between **Brahman** as *sagunam* (qualified) and **Brahman** as *nirgunam* (unqualified). The former is relative, phenomenal, and has characteristics of its own; but the latter is absolute, having no qualification whatever to speak of—it is absolute **Suchness**. (See Max Mueller's *The Six Systems of Indian Philosophy*, p. 220 etc.)</note>

Here, a very interesting question suggests itself: Which is the original and which is the copy, Mahâyânism or Vedantism? Most European Sanskrit scholars would like to dismiss it at once by declaring that Buddhism must be the borrower. But I am strongly inclined toward the opposite view, for there is reliable evidence in favor of it. In a writing by <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>, who dates much earlier than <term>Çankara</term> or <term>Badarayana</term>, we notice this distinction of absolute **Suchness** and relative **Suchness**. He writes in his *Awakening of Faith* (p. 55 etc.) that although **Suchness** is free from all modes of limitation and conditionality, and therefore cannot be thought of by our finite consciousness, yet on account of <term>Avidyâ</term> (ignorance) inherent in the human mind, absolute **Suchness** manifests itself in the phenomenal world. In doing so, it subjects itself to the law of causality and relativity. He proceeds to say that there is a twofold aspect in **Suchness** from the point of view of its explicability. The first aspect is trueness as negation (***çûnyatâ***), in the sense that it is completely set apart from the attributes of all things unreal, and that it is a veritable reality. The second aspect is trueness as affirmation (***açûnyatâ***), in the sense that it contains infinite merits and that it is self-existent. Considering the fact that <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> comes

<summary>The author examines the two aspects of Suchness—the phenomenal and the transcendental—and argues that Mahâyâna Buddhism influenced Vedanta philosophy. He cites Açvaghoṣa’s distinction between Suchness as negation (çûnyatâ) and affirmation (açûnyatâ) to support this historical claim.</summary>

<keywords>Suchness, Mahâyânists, Vedanta, Brahman, Açvaghoṣa, Çankara, Awakening of Faith, Avidyâ, çûnyatâ, açûnyatâ, causality, transcendental truth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Suchness, explaining how the absolute must become conditioned to have significance in the world, and asserting the historical priority of Buddhist thought over Vedanta.</meta>

# CHAPTER V.

### *Suchness Conditioned.*

Absolute, transcendental Suchness, which defies all means of characterization, does not have any direct significance in the phenomenal world and human life as long as it remains in that state. When it does, it must become conditional Suchness—as the *orderliness* (*Gesetzmässigkeit*) of nature and as the ethical order in our practical life. Suchness in its absolute form is too remote and abstract, and it may have only metaphysical value. Its existence or non-existence does not seem to affect us in our daily social life, because it is transcendental. In order to enter into our limited consciousness, to become the norm of our conscious activities, and to regulate the course of the evolutionary tide in nature, Suchness must surrender its "splendid isolation" and abandon its absoluteness.

When Suchness thus descends from its sovereign seat in the realm of the unthinkable, we have this universe unfolded before our eyes in all its diversity and magnificence: twinkling stars set in the vaulted sky; the planet elaborately decorated with green meadows, towering mountains, and rolling waves; birds singing cheerfully in the woods; beasts running wildly through the thickets; summer heavens adorned with white, fleecy clouds and so on <note>There appears to be a printer's error or missing line of text between "and on" and "earlier than".</note> earlier than any <term>Vedanta</term> philosophers, it stands to reason to say that the latter might have borrowed the idea of distinguishing the two aspects of <term>Brahma</term> from their Buddhist predecessors.

<term>Çankara</term> also makes a distinction between *saguna* and *nirguna vidya*, a parallel of which we find in the Mahayana *samvṛtti* and *paramârtha satya*.

<summary>This page explains that absolute Suchness must become "conditioned" to manifest as the laws of nature and human ethics. It also argues that the dual nature of reality found in Vedanta philosophy likely originated from earlier Buddhist thought.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the natural world as a manifestation of conditional Suchness, transitioning from the celestial and biological to the seasonal and human realms.</meta>

# CHAPTER V.

On the earth, all branches and leaves grow in abundant luxury; the winter prairie is destitute of all animation, with only naked trees here and there trembling in the dreary north winds. All these manifestations—not varying a hair's breadth from their mathematical, astronomical, physical, chemical, and biological laws—are nothing else than the work of conditional Suchness in nature.

When we turn to human life and history, we see the work of conditional Suchness manifested in all forms of activity, such as passions, aspirations, imaginations, intellectual efforts, and so on. It makes us desire to eat when hungry and drink when thirsty; it makes a man long for a woman and a woman for a man; it keeps children in merriment and play; it strengthens men and women to bravely carry the burden of life. When we are oppressed, it causes us to cry, "Give us liberty or death"; when we are treated with injustice, it leads us even to murder, fire, and revolution; when our noble sentiments are aroused to the highest pitch, it makes us ready to sacrifice everything that is most dear to us. In brief, all the kaleidoscopic changes of this phenomenal world—subjective as well as objective—come from the playing hands of conditional Suchness. It not only constitutes the goodness and blessings of life, but also the sins, crimes, and misery which the flesh is heir to. <note>In passing, I cannot help but digress and enter into a polemic in this footnote. The fact is that Western Buddhist critics stubbornly refuse to correctly understand what is</note>

---

<note>...insisted upon by Buddhists themselves. Even scholars who are supposed to be well-informed about the subject go astray and make false charges against Buddhism. Max Mueller, for example, declares in his *Six Systems of Indian Philosophy* (p. 242) that “An important distinction between Buddhists and Vedantists is that the former holds the world to have arisen from what is not, the latter from what is, the <term>Sat</term> or <term>Brahman</term>.” The reader who has carefully followed my exposition above will at once detect in Max Mueller’s conclusion an incorrect statement of Buddhist doctrine. As I have repeatedly said, Suchness, though described in negative terms, is not a state of nothingness, but the highest possible synthesis that the human intellect can reach. The world did not come from the void of Suchness, but from its fullness of reality. If it were not so, where does Buddhism want us to go after deliverance from the transience and nothingness of the phenomenal world?

Max Mueller in another place (in the work cited above, p. 210) speaks of the Vedantists’ assertion of the reality of the objective world for practical purposes (<term>vyavahârârtham</term>) and of their antagonistic attitude toward “the nihilism of the Buddhists.” “The Buddhists”—this seems to refer to the followers of the <term>Mâdhyamika</term> school, but a careful reading of their texts will reveal that what they denied was not the reality of the world as a manifestation of conditional Suchness, but its independent reality and our attachment to it as such. The <term>Mâdhyamika</term> school was not in any sense a nihilistic system. True, its advocates used many negative terms, but what they meant by them was obvious enough to any careful reader.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Açvaghoṣa’s *Awakening of Faith*, specifically the relationship between the Heart of Suchness and the Heart of Birth-and-Death, and begins to explore the nature of enlightenment as the essence of intelligence.</meta>

# CHAPTER V.

Suchness is the <term>Heart of Birth-and-Death</term>. It is because of our limited senses and finite mind that we have a world of particulars, which, as it is, is no more than a fragment of the absolute <term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term>. And yet, it is through this fragmentary manifestation that we are finally able to reach the fundamental nature of being in its entirety. <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> says: “Depending on the <term>Tathâgata-garbha</term>, the <term>Heart of Birth-and-Death</term> evolves. What is immortal and what is mortal are harmoniously blended, for they are not one, nor are they separate. . . . . Herein all things are organized. Through this, all things are created.”

The above is from the ontological standpoint.

When viewed psychologically, the Heart of Suchness is enlightenment, for Buddhism makes no distinction between being and thought, or world and mind. The ultimate nature of the two is considered to be absolutely one. Now, speaking of the nature of enlightenment, Açvaghoṣa says: “It is like the emptiness of space and the brightness of a mirror in that it is true, real, and great. It completes and perfects all things. It is free from the condition of destructibility. Within it is reflected every phase of life and activity in the world. Nothing leaves it, nothing enters it, nothing is annihilated, and nothing is destroyed. It is one eternal soul; no forms of defilement can defile it. It is the essence of intelligence. Because of the numerous immaculate virtues that exist within it, it perfumes the hearts of all beings.” Thus, <margin>①</margin>the Heart of Suchness, which is enlightenment and

<summary>Açvaghoṣa explains that the Heart of Birth-and-Death arises from the absolute Heart of Suchness, blending the mortal and immortal. Psychologically, Suchness is identified with enlightenment, described as an eternal, immaculate mirror of reality that influences all beings.</summary>
<keywords>Suchness, Heart of Birth-and-Death, Açvaghoṣa, Bhûtatathâtâ, Tathâgata-garbha, enlightenment, ontology, psychology, essence of intelligence</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Suchness as the essence of intelligence, arguing that truth is found in everyday life rather than abstract formulas, and introduces the fundamental philosophical questions regarding why the absolute becomes the manifest world.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>
<sig>8</sig>

the essence of intelligence, constantly works in and through the hearts of all human beings, that is, in and through our finite minds. In this sense, Buddhism declares that truth is not to be sought in highly abstract philosophical formulas, but in the phenomena of our everyday life such as eating, dressing, walking, sleeping, etc. The Heart of Suchness acts and does not abstract; it synthesizes and does not "dissect to murder."

### *Questions Defying Solution.*

Speaking of the world as a manifestation of Suchness, we are here beset with the most puzzling questions that have baffled the best minds ever since the dawn of intellect. They are: Why did Suchness ever leave its abode in the mysterious realm of transcendence and descend to earth where every form of misery greets us on all sides? What inherent necessity was there for it to mingle in the dust of worldliness while it could enjoy the unspeakable bliss of its own absoluteness? In other words, why did absolute Suchness ever become conditional Suchness? To dispose of these questions as not concerning human interests is the creed of agnosticism and positivism; but the fact is, they are not questions whimsically framed by the human mind when it was in the mood of playing with itself. They are queries of the most vital importance ever put to us, and the significance of life entirely depends on our interpretation of them.

<summary>Buddhism teaches that truth is found in the practical activities of daily life rather than abstract philosophy, though this raises difficult questions about why absolute "Suchness" would manifest in a world of suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Suchness, Buddhism, enlightenment, manifestation, transcendence, agnosticism, positivism, absolute vs. conditional</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how truth is found in the practical aspects of daily life, transitioning into why Buddhism acknowledges the limits of the human intellect in solving the ultimate mysteries of existence.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>114</page-num>
<header># CHAPTER V.</header>

Buddhism acknowledges that the mystery is unsolvable purely by the human mind, for it lies absolutely beyond the reach of the finite intellect and the power of logical proof. The mystery can only be solved in a practical way when we attain the highest spiritual enlightenment of Buddhahood, in which the *Bodhi*—with its unimpeded supernatural light—looks directly into the very abyss of Suchness. The *Bodhi*, or Intelligence, which constitutes the core of our being, is a partial realization of Suchness within us. When this intelligence merges and expands into the Body of Suchness, like water from a vessel poured into the waters of the boundless ocean, it immediately perceives and realizes its own nature, its destiny, and its significance in life.

Buddhism is a religion and leaves many metaphysical topics unsolved, at least from a logical standpoint. Though it is more intellectual and philosophical than any other religion, it does not pretend to build a complete system of speculation. Regarding theorization, Buddhism is dogmatic and assumes many propositions without revealing their dialectical processes. However, these are all necessary and fundamental hypotheses of the religious consciousness; they are the ultimate demands of the human soul. Religion has no absolute obligation to prove its propositions in the same way as the natural sciences. It is enough for religion to state the facts as they are, and the intellect, though hampered by its inherent limitations, must try its best to organize them into a coherent system.

<vocab>Buddhism, Buddhahood, Bodhi, Suchness, metaphysics, speculation, theorization, religious consciousness, natural sciences</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that the ultimate mystery of existence cannot be grasped by logic alone but only through the spiritual enlightenment of Buddhahood. While Buddhism is philosophical, it prioritizes the needs of religious consciousness over building a purely speculative system like the natural sciences.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, Bodhi, Suchness, spiritual enlightenment, metaphysics, religious consciousness, intellect vs. religion</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Buddhism’s approach to metaphysical mysteries, transitioning from the limits of human intellect to the specific doctrine used to explain the relationship between the absolute and the phenomenal world.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>115</page-num>
<header># CHAPTER V.</header>

Buddhism's solution to the questions stated above cannot be said to be strictly logical or free from serious difficulties, but in practice, it serves all required purposes and is helpful for religious discipline. By this, I refer to the Buddhist theory of Nescience or Ignorance (*avidyâ*).

### *Theory of Ignorance.*

The theory of nescience or ignorance (*avidyâ*) is an attempt by Buddhists to explain the relationship between the one and the many, between absolute Suchness and conditional Suchness, between Dharmakâya and Sarvasattva, between wisdom (*bodhi*) and sin (*kleça*), and between Nirvâna and Samsâra. However, Buddhism does not provide any systematic exposition of this doctrine. Its assertions are categorical and dogmatic.

> "This universe is truly the Dharmadhâtu; ^1 it is characterized by sameness (*samatâ*); there is no differentiation (*visama*) in it; it is even emptiness itself (*çûnyatâ*); all things lack a *pudgala* (self). But, because of nescience, there are four or six *mahâbhûta* (elements), five *skandha* (aggregates), six (or eight) *vijñâna* (senses), and twelve *nidâna* (chains of causation). All these names and forms (*nâmarûpa*) proceed from nescience or ignorance."

Or, according to Açvaghosa,

> "The Heart of Suchness is the vast All of one Dharmadhâtu; it is the essence of all doctrines. The ultimate nature does not perish, nor does it

---

<note>1. Dharmadhâtu is the world as seen by an enlightened mind, where all forms of particularity do not contradict one another but form one harmonious whole.</note>

<header># CHAPTER V.</header>

decay. All particular objects exist because of confused subjectivity (<term>smṛti</term>).¹ Independent of confused subjectivity, there is no outside world to be perceived and discriminated." "Everything that is subject to the law of birth and death exists only because of ignorance and <term>karma</term>." Such statements as these are found almost everywhere in Buddhist literature; but as to the question of how and why this negative principle of ignorance came to assert itself in the body of Suchness, we are at a loss for where to find an authoritative and definite answer.

One thing, however, is certain: Ignorance (<term>avidyā</term>) is the <term>principium individium</term>, which creates the multiplicity of phenomena within the absolute oneness of being. It tosses up the roaring waves of existence in the eternal ocean of Suchness, breaks the silence of <term>Nirvāna</term>, and starts the wheel of <term>metempsychosis</term> rolling perpetually. By veiling the transparent mirror of <term>Bodhi</term>, it affects the reflection of Suchness within it; it transforms the sameness (<term>samaṭā</term>) of Suchness into the duality of "thisness" and "thatness," and leads many confused minds toward egoism and all its harmful consequences.

Perhaps the best way to approach the problem of ignorance is to understand that Buddhism is a thoroughly idealistic doctrine—as every true religion should be—and that psychologically, and not ontologically—

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header># CHAPTER V. 117</header>

ontologically, should <term>Suchness</term> be conceived. Furthermore, <term>nescience</term> is inherent in <term>Suchness</term>, though only hypothetically, illusively, and apparently—not truly in any sense.

According to Brahmanism, there was in the beginning only one being; and this being willed to be two, which naturally resulted in the differentiation of subject and object, mind and nature. In Buddhism, however, <term>Suchness</term> is not explicitly stated as having had any desire to be other than itself, at least when it is conceived purely metaphysically. But as Buddhism interprets this world of particularization as a manifestation of <term>Suchness</term> conditioned by the principle of ignorance, ignorance must be considered—however illusory in its ultimate nature—to have potentially or rather negatively existed in the being of <term>Suchness</term>. When <term>Suchness</term>, by its transcendental freedom of will, affirmed itself, it did so by negating itself; that is, by permitting itself to be conditioned by the principle of ignorance or individuation. 

The latter, as is expressly stated everywhere in Buddhist <term>sutras</term> and <term>shastras</term>, is no more than an illusion and a negative quantity; it is merely the veil of <term>Maya</term>.

This chimerical nature of ignorance preserves the essential absoluteness of the first principle and makes the <term>monism</term> of the <term>Mahayana</term> doctrine thoroughly consistent. What should be noted here, however, is this: Buddhism does not necessarily regard this world of particulars as altogether evanescent and dream-like. When ignorance alone is taken notice

<summary>This page explains that while Brahmanism views creation as the divine will to differentiate, Buddhism views the world of particulars as a manifestation of Suchness conditioned by the negative principle of ignorance (nescience). This "veil of Maya" allows for a consistent monism where the absolute remains untainted by the illusory multiplicity of the world.</summary>

if the presence of <term>Suchness</term> in all this multiplicity of things is denied, this existence is positively declared to be void. But when an enlightened mind perceives <term>Suchness</term> even in the midst of the utter darkness of ignorance, this life assumes an entirely new aspect, and we come to realize the illusory nature of all evils.

To return to the subject, <term>ignorance</term> or <term>nescience</term> is defined by <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> as a spark of consciousness <note>*Smṛti*, *citta*, or *vijñāna*. These are all used by <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> and other Buddhist authors as synonyms. *Smṛti* literally means memory; *citta* means thought or mental activity; and *vijñāna* is generally translated as consciousness, though not very accurately.</note> that spontaneously flashes from the unfathomable depths of <term>Suchness</term>. According to this, <term>ignorance</term> and <term>consciousness</term> are interchangeable terms, though with different shades of meaning. <term>Ignorance</term> is, so to speak, the *reason for being* of <term>consciousness</term>; it is that which makes the appearance of the latter possible, while <term>ignorance</term> itself is in turn an illusory emanation of <term>Suchness</term>. It is then evident that the awakening of <term>consciousness</term> marks the first step toward the rising of this universe from the abyss of the self-identity of <term>Suchness</term>. For the unfolding of <term>consciousness</term> implies the separation of the perceiving and the perceived, the <term>viṣayin</term> and the <term>viṣaya</term>, of subject and object, mind and nature.

The eternal abyss of <term>Suchness</term>, so called, is the point where subjectivity and objectivity are merged in absolute oneness. It is the time, though strictly

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V.</header>

Strictly speaking, chronology does not apply here, when all "the ten thousand things" of the world have not yet been differentiated and even when the God who "created the heaven and earth" has not yet made his debut. To use psychological terms, it is a state of transcendental or transmarginal consciousness, where all sense perceptions and conceptual images vanish, and where we are in a state of absolute unconsciousness. This sounds mystical; but it is an established fact that in the field of our mental activities there is an abyss where consciousness sometimes suddenly disappears. This region beyond the threshold of awareness, though often a meeting place for psychic abnormalities, has a great religious significance, which cannot be ignored by superficial scientific arguments. Here is the region where the consciousness of subject and object is completely annihilated, but here we do not have the silence and darkness of a grave, nor is it a state of absolute nothingness. The self is here lost in the presence of something indescribable, or better, it expands so as to embrace the whole world within itself, and is not conscious of any egoistic elation or arrogance; but it merely feels the fullness of reality and a touch of celestial joy that cannot be imparted to others by anything human. The most convincing spiritual insight into the nature of being comes from this source. <term>Enlightenment</term> is the name given by <term>Buddhists</term> to the actual gaining of this insight. <term>Bodhi</term> or <term>Prajñâ</term> or intelligence is the term for the

<summary>The author describes a state of transcendental consciousness beyond subject and object, where the self expands to embrace all reality. This state of profound spiritual insight is identified by Buddhists as Enlightenment, also known as Bodhi or Prajñâ.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, Bodhi, Prajñâ, transcendental consciousness, ten thousand things, subject and object, mysticism, religious experience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychological and spiritual nature of enlightenment, focusing on the return of consciousness and the origin of ignorance from the state of absolute calmness.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V.</header>

spiritual power that brings about this enlightenment

When the mind emerges from this state of sameness, consciousness spontaneously returns just as it vanished before, retaining the memory of an experience so unique and now confronting the world of contrasts and mutual dependence in which our empirical ego moves. The transition from one state to the other is like a flash of lightning scintillating from behind the clouds; though the two—the subliminal and the superficial consciousness—seem to be one continuous form of activity, permitting no gap between them. At any rate, this awakening of subjectivity and the leaving behind of transmarginal consciousness marks the start of ignorance. Therefore, psychologically speaking, ignorance must be considered synonymous with the awakening of consciousness in a sentient being.

Here we have the most mysterious fact that baffles all our intellectual efforts to unravel, which is: How and why has ignorance—or what is equivalent, consciousness—ever been awakened from the absolute calmness (<term>çānti</term>) of being? How and why have the waves of mentation ever been stirred up in the ocean of eternal tranquility? <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> simply says, "spontaneously." This by no means explains anything, or at least it is not in line with our so-called scientific interpretations, nor does it give us any reason why. Nevertheless, viewed religiously and practically, "spontaneous" is the most graphic and vigorous term there is for describing the actual state of things.

<summary>The author describes the transition from transcendental sameness back to individual consciousness, equating this awakening of subjectivity with the beginning of ignorance. He explores the mystery of how mental activity arises from eternal tranquility, noting that the philosopher Ashvaghosha attributes this shift to spontaneity.</summary>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V. 121</header>

as they pass before our mental eye. In fact, there is always something vague and indefinite in all our psychological experiences. Regardless of the scientific accuracy or objective precision with which we may describe the phenomena that take place in the mind, there is always something that eludes our scrutiny; it is too slippery, as it were, to grasp. Thus, after all our strenuous intellectual efforts to be exact and clear in our expositions, we are still compelled to leave much to the reader's imagination. If he happens to lack the experience we have endeavored to describe, we shall hope in vain to awaken that impression in him with the same degree of intensity and reality.

It is for this reason that <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> and other <term>Mahâyânists</term> declare that the rising of consciousness out of the abysmal depths of <term>Suchness</term> is *felt* only by <term>Buddhas</term> and other enlightened minds who have actually gone through the experience. Nobody can explain the "why" of ignorance any more than the "why" of <term>Suchness</term>. But when we personally experience this spiritual fact, we no longer feel the need to harbor any doubt about how or why. Everything becomes transparent, and the rays of supernatural enlightenment shine like a halo around our spiritual personality. We move as dictated by the behest of <term>Suchness</term>—that is, by the <term>Dharmakâya</term>—in which we feel infinite bliss and satisfaction. This religious experience is the most unique phenomenon in the life of a sentient being.

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>

### *Dualism and Moral Evil.*

Since we cannot conceive of the essence of the external world as being different from that of our own mind—that is to say, since we cannot think of subject and object as being different in their ultimate nature—our conclusion is naturally that the same principle of Ignorance that gathers the clouds of subjectivity also calls forth the vast variety of phenomena in the world-mind of <term>Suchness</term>. The universe in its entirety is an infinite mind, and our limited mind, with its transmarginal consciousness, is a microcosm. What the finite mind feels in its innermost self must also be what the cosmic mind feels; indeed, we can go one step further and say that when the human mind enters the region lying beyond the borders of subjectivity and objectivity, it is in communion with the heart of the universe, where secrets are revealed without reserve. Therefore, Buddhism does not make any distinction between knowing and being, or between enlightenment and <term>Suchness</term>. When the mind is free from ignorance and no longer clings to particular things, it is said to be in harmony—and even one—with <term>Suchness</term>.

We must, however, remember that ignorance, as the principle of individuation and a spontaneous expression of <term>Suchness</term>, is not a moral evil. The awakening of subjectivity or the dawn of consciousness forms part of the necessary cosmic process. The separation of subject and object, or the appearance of a phenomenal world, is nothing but a realization

<summary>The author explains that the individual mind is a microcosm of the infinite cosmic mind, and that enlightenment consists of reaching a state beyond the division of subject and object. He clarifies that the initial rise of individual consciousness from "Suchness" is a natural cosmic process rather than a moral evil.</summary>
<keywords>Dualism, Suchness, Ignorance, subjectivity, objectivity, transmarginal consciousness, microcosm, cosmic mind, Buddhism, individuation, phenomenal world</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the role of ignorance as a necessary cosmic principle rather than a moral evil, explaining its role in creating the world of relativity and dualism.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>
<page-num>123</page-num>

of the cosmic mind (<term>Dharmakâya</term>). As such, Ignorance performs an essential function in the evolution of the world as a whole. Ignorance is inherent in Buddhas as well as in all sentient beings. Each of us cannot help but perceive an external world (<term>viṣaya</term>) and form conceptions, reasoning, feeling, and willing. We do not see any moral fault here. If there is really anything morally wrong with this, then we can do nothing about it; we are utterly helpless before it, for it is not our fault, but that of the cosmic soul from which and in which we exist.

Ignorance has produced everywhere a state of relativity and mutual dependence. Birth is inseparably linked with death, gathering with separation, evolution with involution, attraction with repulsion, centripetal with centrifugal force, spring with fall, the high tide with the ebb, joy with sorrow, God with Satan, Adam with Eve, Buddha with Devadatta, and so on, *infinitely*. These are necessary conditions of existence; and if existence is an evil, they must be abolished. With their abolition, the very reason for existence is abolished, which means absolute nothingness—an impossibility as long as we exist. The work of ignorance in the world of conditional <term>Suchness</term> is quite innocent, and Buddhists do not recognize any fault in its existence, provided it is not contaminated by confused subjectivity. Those who speak of the curse of existence, or those who conceive <term>Nirvâna</term> to be the realm of non-existence

<summary>The author explains that ignorance is a necessary cosmic function that creates the world of relativity and dualities, and is not a moral evil in itself. He argues that these opposing forces are essential conditions of existence, and that Nirvana should not be mistaken for absolute non-existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Ignorance, specifically refuting the view of those who see Nirvana as mere non-existence and explaining how Ignorance leads to suffering only when misunderstood.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER V.</header>
<page-num>124</page-num>

and the happiness of absolute annihilation, are considered by Buddhists to be unable to understand the significance of Ignorance.

Is there then no fault to be found with Ignorance? Not in Ignorance itself, but in our defiled attachment to it—that is, when we are ignorant of Ignorance. It is wrong to cling to the dualism of subject and object as final and to act accordingly. It is wrong to take the work of ignorance as ultimate and to forget the foundation on which it stands. It is wrong, while thinking that the awakening of consciousness reveals the whole world, to ignore the existence of unseen realities. In short, evils quickly follow our steps when we try to realize the conclusions of ignorance without knowing its true relation to <term>Suchness</term>. <term>Egoism</term> is the most fundamental of all errors and evils.

When we speak of ignorance as hindering the light of intelligence from penetrating to the bottom of reality, we usually understand the term ignorance not in the philosophical sense of *principium individuum* <note>The principle of individuation</note>, but in the sense of confused subjectivity, which conceives the work of Ignorance as the final reality culminating in egoism. So, we might say that while the principle of Ignorance is philosophically justified, its unenlightened actualization in our practical life is altogether unwarranted and brings upon us a series of dire calamities.

# CHAPTER VI.

## THE <term>TATHÂGATA-GARBHA</term> AND THE <term>ÂLAYA-VIJÑÂNA</term>.

<term>Suchness</term> (<term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term>), the ultimate principle of existence, is known by many different names because it is viewed in so many different phases of its manifestation. <term>Suchness</term> is the Essence of Buddhas, as it constitutes the reason for <term>Buddhahood</term>; it is the <term>Dharma</term> when it is considered the norm of existence; it is the <term>Bodhi</term> when it is the source of intelligence; <term>Nirvana</term> when it brings eternal peace to a heart troubled with egoism and its vile passions; <term>Prajñâ</term> (wisdom) when it intelligently directs the course of nature; the <term>Dharmakâya</term> when it is religiously considered as the fountainhead of love and wisdom; the <term>Bodhicitta</term> (enlightenment-heart) when it is the awakener of religious consciousness; <term>Çûnyatâ</term> (emptiness) when viewed as transcending all particular forms; the *summum bonum* (<term>kuçalam</term>) when its ethical phase is emphasized; the Highest Truth (<term>paramârtha</term>) when its epistemological feature is put forward; the Middle Path (<term>mâdhyamârga</term>) when it is considered above the one-sidedness and limitation of individual existences; the Essence of Being (<term>bhûtakoti</term>) when its ontological aspect is taken into

<summary>This page explains that Suchness, the ultimate principle of existence, is called by many different names depending on which aspect of its manifestation is being emphasized. These names reflect its role as the source of wisdom, the ethical highest good, the norm of existence, and the essence of enlightenment.</summary>

### *The Tathâgata-Garbha and Ignorance.*

The <term>Tathâgata-garbha</term>, therefore, may be explained ontologically as a state of <term>Suchness</term> quickened by <term>Ignorance</term> and ready to be realized in the world of particulars—that is, when it is about to transform itself into the duality of subject and object, though there is yet no perceptible manifestation of motion in any form. Psychologically, it is the transcendental soul of man just coming under the bondage of the law of <term>karmic</term> causation. be regarded as an individuation of the <term>Tathâgata-Garbha</term>. And it is in this capacity that the <term>Garbha</term> is called *<term>Âlayavijñâna</term>*.

### *The <term>Âlayavijñâna</term> and its Evolution.*

As we have seen, the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term> or All-Conserving Soul is a particularized expression of the <term>Tathâgata-Garbha</term> in the human mind. It is an individual, ideal reflex of the cosmic <term>Garbha</term>. It is this “psychic germ,” as the <term>Âlaya</term> is often designated, that stores all the mental possibilities, which are set in motion by the impetus of an external world that works on the <term>Âlaya</term> through the six senses (*<term>vijñâna</term>*).

<term>Mahâyânism</term> is essentially idealistic and does not make a radical, qualitative distinction between subject and object, thought and being, mind and nature, or consciousness and energy. Therefore, the existence and activity of the <term>Âlaya</term> are essentially those of the <term>Garbha</term>; and again, as the <term>Garbha</term> is the joint creation of universal <term>Ignorance</term> and <term>Suchness</term>, so is the <term>Âlaya</term> the product of desire (*<term>kleça</term>*) and wisdom (*<term>bodhi</term>*). The <term>Garbha</term> and the <term>Âlaya</term>, however, are each in itself innocent and absolutely irresponsible for the existing state of affairs. And let it be noted here that <term>Buddhism</term> does not condemn this life and universe for their wickedness, as was done by some religious teachers and philosophers. The so-called wickedness is not radical in nature and life. It is merely superficial. It is the work of ignorance and desire, and when they are converted to serve the

<summary>This page defines the Âlayavijñâna as the individual human expression of the Tathâgata-Garbha, acting as a "psychic germ" that stores mental possibilities. It explains that in Mahâyâna idealism, the world's perceived wickedness is merely a superficial product of ignorance and desire, rather than an inherent quality of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Âlayavijñâna, Tathâgata-Garbha, Mahâyânism, psychic germ, vijñâna, kleça, bodhi, idealism, Ignorance, Suchness</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the inherent goodness of the Tathâgata-Garbha and its relationship to the Âlayavijñâna, explaining how the transition from universal indifference to psychological particularization occurs.</meta>

# CHAPTER VI.
129

<term>Bodhi</term>, they cease to be wicked, sinful, or evil. Buddhists, therefore, strongly insist on the innate and intrinsic goodness of the <term>Âlaya</term> and the <term>Garbha</term>.

Açvaghoṣa says in his *Awakening of Faith* (p. 75):
> “In the All-Conserving Soul (<term>Âlaya</term>), Ignorance prevails, and from the non-enlightenment [thus produced] starts that which sees, that which represents, that which apprehends an objective world, and that which constantly particularizes.”

Here we have the evolution of the <term>Garbha</term> in its psychological manifestation; in other words, we have here the evolution of the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term>. When the <term>Garbha</term> or <term>Âlaya</term> comes under the influence of birth-and-death (<term>samsâra</term>), it no longer retains its primeval indifference or sameness (<term>samatâ</term>); but there come to exist that which sees (<term>viṣayin</term>) and that which is seen (<term>viṣaya</term>), a mind and an objective world. From the interaction of these two forms of existence, we have now before our eyes the entire panorama of the universe swiftly and noiselessly moving with its never-tiring steps. A most favorite simile among Buddhists to illustrate these incessant activities of the phenomenal world is to compare them to the waves that are seen forever rolling in a boundless ocean, while the body of water which makes up the ocean is compared to Suchness, and the wind that stirs up the waves is compared to the principle of birth-and-death or ignorance, which are the same thing. So we read in the *<term>Lankâvatâra Sûtra</term>*:

<sig>9</sig>

<summary>This page describes how the universal Garbha evolves into the psychological Âlayavijñâna through the influence of ignorance, creating the distinction between subject and object. It uses the metaphor of ocean waves to explain how the phenomenal world arises from the stillness of Suchness.</summary>

<keywords>Âlaya, Garbha, Açvaghoṣa, Âlayavijñâna, samsâra, samatâ, Suchness, ignorance, Lankâvatâra Sûtra, Buddhist similes</keywords>

## CHAPTER VI.
130

> “Like the ocean waves,
> Which, maddened by a raging storm,
> Strike against the rugged precipice
> Without interruption;
> Even so, in the Alaya-sea,
> Stirred by the wind of objectivity,
> All kinds of waves of mentation
> Arise, dancing and rolling.” ¹

But all the mental activities thus brought into full view should not be conceived as different from the Mind (<term>citta</term>) itself. It is simply the nature of our understanding that we think of attributes as separate from their substance, imagining the latter to be in possession and control of the former. However, in fact, there is no substance *in itself*, independent of its attributes, and no attributes detached from that which unites them. And this is one of the fundamental concepts of Buddhism: that there is no soul-in-itself considered apart from its various manifestations, such as imagination, sensation, thinking, etc. The innumerable ripples, waves, and billows of mentation that are stirred in the depths of the <term>Tathâgata-Garbha</term> are not things foreign or external to it, but they are all particular expressions of the same essence; they are working out its inherent destiny. So the *<term>Lankâvatâra Sûtra</term>* continues:

---

> Nṛtyamânâh pravartante vyucchedaç ca na vidhyate:
> Âlayodhyas tathâ nityam viṣayapavana îritaḥ,
> Cittâis tarangavijñânâir nṛtyamânâḥ pravartate.”

> "The salt crystal and its reddish-blue hue,

> <term>Manas</term> reflects an objective world,
> <term>Manovijñâna</term> is the faculty of judgment,
> The five <term>Vijñânas</term> are the differentiating senses." ¹

### *The Manas.*

The <term>Âlayavijñâna</term>, which is sometimes (as in the preceding quotations) simply called <term>citta</term> (mind), is, in itself, nothing more than a state of <term>Suchness</term>. It allows itself to be influenced by the principle of birth and death—that is, by <term>Ignorance</term>. As of yet, no "awakening" or "stirring up" (<term>vṛtti</term>) has taken place within it from which a consciousness results. However, when the <term>Manas</term> is evolved, we see a sign of mentality set in motion; for the <term>Manas</term>, according to the <term>Mahâyânists</term>, marks the dawn of consciousness in the universe.

The <term>Manas</term>, deriving its basis for consciousness from the <term>Citta</term> or <term>Âlaya</term>, reflects on it as well as on an external world, and becomes conscious of the distinction between "me" and "not-me." But since this "not-I" or external world is nothing but an unfolding of the <term>Âlaya</term> itself, the <term>Manas</term> must be said to be truly self-reflecting when it distinguishes between subject and object. While the <term>Âlaya</term> is not yet conscious of itself, the <term>Manas</term> is, as the latter comes to realize the state of self-awareness. The <term>Âlaya</term> is perhaps to be compared, in a sense, to the <term>Kantian</term> "<term>ego of transcendental apperception</term>"; while the <term>Manas</term> is the actual center of self-consciousness. However, the <term>Manas</term> and the <term>Âlaya</term> (or <term>Citta</term>) are not two different things in the sense that one stems from the other or that one is created by the other. It is better to under-

<summary>This page defines the Manas as the center of self-consciousness that emerges from the universal Âlayavijñâna, marking the distinction between subject and object. It explains that while the Âlaya is a state of Suchness, the Manas introduces self-awareness and the perception of an external world.</summary>
<keywords>Manas, Âlayavijñâna, citta, Suchness, Ignorance, vṛtti, Mahâyânists, Kantian philosophy, ego of transcendental apperception, self-consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the mind, specifically focusing on the transition from the universal Âlayavijñâna to the individual consciousness of the Manas.</meta>

# CHAPTER VI.

stand the <term>Manas</term> as a state or condition of the <term>Citta</term> in its evolution.

Now, the <term>Manas</term> is not only contemplative, but capable of volition. It awakens the desire to cling to the state of <term>individuation</term>; it harbors egoism, passion, and prejudice; it wills and creates: for Ignorance, the principle of birth and death, is there in its full force, and the absolute identity of <term>Suchness</term> is here forever departed. Therefore, the <term>Manas</term> really marks the beginning of concrete, particularizing consciousness-waves in the eternal ocean of the <term>All-Conserving Mind</term>. The mind which was previously indifferent and neutral here acquires a full consciousness; it discriminates between ego and non-ego; feels pain and pleasure; clings to that which is agreeable and shrinks from that which is disagreeable; urges activities according to judgments, whether false or truthful; memorizes what has been experienced, and stores it all: — in short, all the modes of <term>mentation</term> come into play with the awakening of the <term>Manas</term>.

According to <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>, with the evolution of the <term>Manas</term> there arise five important psychical activities which characterize the human mind. They are: (1) motility, that is the capability of creating <term>karma</term>; (2) the power to perceive; (3) the power to respond; (4) the power to discriminate; and (5) individuality. Through the exercise of these five functions, the <term>Manas</term> is able to create according to its will, to be a perceiving subject, to respond to the stimuli of an external world, and to deliver judgments

<summary>This page explains how the Manas introduces volition, egoism, and discrimination into the previously neutral All-Conserving Mind. It also outlines the five psychical activities, such as motility and perception, that emerge with the development of the Manas according to Açvaghoṣa.</summary>
<keywords>Manas, Citta, individuation, Suchness, All-Conserving Mind, mentation, Açvaghoṣa, karma, motility, egoism, consciousness</keywords>

# CHAPTER VI.

over what it likes and what it dislikes, and finally to retain all its own "karma-seeds" from the past and to mature them for the future, according to circumstances.

With the advent of the <term>Manas</term>, the evolution of the <term>Citta</term> is complete. Practically, it is the culmination of mentality, for self-consciousness is now fully developed. The will can affirm its egocentric, dualistic activities, and the intellect can exercise its discriminating, reasoning, and image-retaining faculties. The <term>Manas</term> now becomes the center of psychic coordination. It receives messages from the six senses and pronounces over these impressions whatever judgments—intellectual or volitional—are needed at the time for its own preservation. It also reflects on its own sanctum, and, perceiving the presence of the <term>Âlaya</term> there, wrongly jumps to the conclusion that this is the real, ultimate ego-soul, from which it derives the notions of authority, unity, and permanence.

As is evident, the <term>Manas</term> is a double-edged sword. It may destroy itself by clinging to the error of ego-conception, or it may, through a judicious exercise of its reasoning faculty, destroy all the misconceptions that arise from a wrong interpretation of the principle of Ignorance. The <term>Manas</term> destroys itself by being overwhelmed by the dualism of <term>ego</term> and <term>alter</term>, by taking them for final, irreducible realities, and by thus fostering absolute egocentric thoughts and desires, making itself a willing prey to an indomitable egoism, both religiously and morally. On the other hand, when it

<summary>This page describes the Manas as the completion of mental evolution, serving as the center of coordination and judgment while warning of its tendency to create a false sense of a permanent ego-soul.</summary>
<keywords>Manas, Citta, Âlaya, karma-seeds, ego-soul, self-consciousness, dualism, Buddhist psychology, Ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the Manas can either fall into egoism or achieve enlightenment by correctly interpreting the nature of the Âlaya.</meta>

sees an error in the conception of the absolute reality of individuals, when it perceives a play of Ignorance in the dualism of "me" and "not-me," when it recognizes the *raison d'être* of existence in the essence of <term>Tathâgatahood</term> (that is, in <term>Suchness</term>), and when it realizes that the <term>Âlaya</term>, which is mistaken for the ego, is no more than an innocent and irreproachable reflection of the cosmic <term>Garbha</term>, it at once transcends the sphere of particularity and becomes the very harbinger of eternal enlightenment.

Buddhists, therefore, do not see any error or evil in the evolution of the Mind (<term>âlaya</term>). There is nothing faulty in the awakening of consciousness, in the dualism of subject and object, or in the individualizing operation of birth-and-death (<term>samsâra</term>), as long as our <term>Manas</term> keeps aloof from the contamination of false egoism. The gravest error, however, permeates every fiber of our mind with all its wickedness and irrationality as soon as the nature of the evolution of the <term>Âlaya</term> is wrongfully interpreted by the abuse of the functions of the <term>Manas</term>. ¹

It has frequently been affirmed regarding the ethics of <term>Mahâyânism</term> that, since it has a nihilistic tendency, its morality turns toward asceticism, ignoring the significance of sentiment and instinct. It is true that <term>Mahâyânism</term> perfectly agrees with <term>Vedantism</term> when the latter declares: "If the killer thinks that he kills, if the killed thinks that he is killed, they do not understand; for this one does not kill, nor is that one killed." (*The Katopanishad*, II. 19.) This belief in non-action (the Laotzean <term>Wu Wei</term>) apparently denies the existence of a world of relativity, but he would be a superficial critic who stops short at this absolute aspect of <term>Mahâyâna</term></note>

# CHAPTER VI.

Though <term>Mahâyânism</term> most emphatically denies the existence of a personal ego which is imagined to be residing within the body and acting as its spiritual master, it does not necessarily follow that it denies the unity of consciousness, personality, or individuality. In fact, the Buddhist assumption of <term>Manovijñâna</term> most conclusively proves that they have an ego in a sense; denying its empirical existence would be the same as denying the most concrete facts of our daily experiences. What they most persistently negate is not the existence of the ego, but its final, ultimate reality. However, to discuss this subject more fully, we have a special chapter below devoted to "<term>Âtman</term>."

->*The <term>Sâmkhya</term> Philosophy and <term>Mahâyânism</term>*<-

If we compare the <term>Sâmkhya</term> philosophy and <term>Mahâyânism</term>, the <term>Âlayavijñāna</term> may

philosophy and refuses to consider its practical side. As we have seen above, Buddhists do not conceive the evolution of the <term>Manovijñâna</term> as a fault on the part of the cosmic mind, nor do they think the assertion of <term>Ignorance</term> is altogether wrong or morally evil. Therefore, <term>Mahâyânism</term> does not deny the claim of reality to the world of the senses, though of course this is relative, and not absolute.

Again, "<term>Tat tvam asi</term>" (thou art it) or "I am the Buddha"—though this assertion may seem arrogant to some—is perfectly justifiable in the realm of absolute identity, where the serene light of <term>Suchness</term> alone pervades. But when we descend to earth and mix in the commotion of our practical, dualistic life, we cannot help suffering from its mundane limitations. We hunger, we thirst, we grieve at the loss of our dearest ones, and we feel remorse over errors committed.

<summary>This page explains that Mahâyânism denies the ultimate reality of a personal ego while acknowledging its functional role in daily experience. It also begins a comparison between Mahâyânism and Sâmkhya philosophy regarding the nature of reality and human suffering.</summary>

According to the *<term>Sâmkhyakârika</term>* (11), the essential nature of <term>Prakṛtî</term> is the power of creation, or, to use Buddhist phraseology, it is blind activity; while that of <term>Puruṣa</term> is witnessing (*<term>sakṣitvâ</term>*) and perceiving (*<term>draṣṭitvâ</term>*). <term>Mahâyânism</term> does not teach the annihilation of those human passions and feelings.

There was once a recluse philosopher, who was considered by the villagers to have completely conquered all natural desires and human ambitions. They almost worshiped him and thought him to be superhuman. One day early in winter, a devotee approached him and reverently inquired after his health. The sage at once responded in verse:

> “A hermit truly I am, world-renounced;
> Yet when the ground is white with snow,
> A chill goes through me and I shiver.”

A false conception of religious saintliness, as cherished by so many pious-hearted but nevertheless ignorant minds, has led them into some of the grossest superstitions, whose curse still lingers even among us. Our earthly life has so many limitations and tribulations. The ailments that the flesh is subject to must be relieved by material, scientific methods.

The <term>Sâmkhya</term> philosophy is an open dualism and allows for the existence of two principles independent of each other. <term>Mahâyânism</term> is fundamentally monistic and makes <term>Ignorance</term> merely a condition necessary for the unfolding of <term>Suchness</term>.<note>That the Buddhist <term>Ignorance</term> corresponds to the <term>Sâmkhya</term> <term>Prakṛti</term> can also be seen from the fact that some <term>Sâmkhya</term> commentators give <term>Prakṛti</term> synonyms such as <term>çâkti</term> (energy), which recalls <term>karma</term> or <term>sankâra</term>, <term>tamas</term> (darkness), <term>mâyâ</term>, and even the very word <term>avidyâ</term> (ignorance).</note> Therefore, what <term>Sâmkhya</term> divides into two, <term>Mahâyânism</term> joins into one.

Similarly, there is a parallelism between the <term>Manovijñâna</term>, and <term>Buddhi</term> and <term>Ahankâra</term>. <term>Buddhi</term>, or intellect, is defined as <term>adhyavasâya</term> (*<term>Kârika</term>*, 23), while <term>Ahankâra</term> is interpreted as <term>abhimanas</term> (*<term>Kârika</term>*, 24), which is evidently self-consciousness. Regarding the exact meaning of <term>adhyavasâya</term>, there is a difference of opinion: “ascertainment,” “judgment,” “determination,” and “apprehension” are some of the English equivalents chosen for it. But the inner meaning of <term>Buddhi</term> is clear enough; it indicates the awakening of knowledge, the dawn of rationality, and the first shedding of light on the dark depths of unconsciousness. Thus, the commentators provide synonyms such as <term>mati</term> (understanding), <term>khyâti</term> (cognition), <term>jñânam</term>, and <term>prajñâ</term>. The last two of these, which mean knowledge or intelligence, are also technical terms in <term>Mahâyânism</term>. And, as we have seen above, these meanings are what the Buddhists give to their <term>Manovijñâna</term>, except that the

# CHAPTER VI.

The latter, in addition, has the faculty of discriminating between *teum* and *meum* <note>Latin: "thine" and "mine"</note>, while in the <term>Sâmkhya</term> this is reserved for <term>Ahankâra</term>. Thus, here too, in place of the <term>Sâmkhya</term> dualism, we have the Buddhist unity.

Another point we must take notice of here in comparing the two great Hindu religio-philosophical systems is that the <term>Sâmkhya</term> philosophy pluralizes the Soul (<term>puruṣa</term>, *<term>Kârika</term>*, 18), while <term>Buddhism</term> postulates one universal <term>Citta</term> or <term>Âlaya</term>. According to the followers of <term>Kapila</term>, therefore, there must be as many souls as there are individuals, and at every departure or arrival of an individual, one must assume a corresponding soul passing away or coming into existence, though we do not know where it comes from or where it goes. <term>Buddhism</term>, on the other hand, denies the existence of any individual mind apart from the All-Conserving Mind (<term>Âlaya</term>), which is universal. Individuality first appears at the awakening of the <term>Manovijñâna</term>. The quintessence of the Mind is <term>Suchness</term> and is not subject to the limitations of time and space, nor to the law of causation. But as soon as it asserts itself in the world of particularization, it negates itself by doing so, and, by becoming specialized, gives rise to individual souls. <note>This view of the oneness of the <term>Âlaya</term> or <term>Citta</term> (mind) may not be acceptable to some <term>Mahâyânists</term>, particularly to those who advocate the <term>Yogâcâra</term> philosophy; but the present author is here trying to expound a more orthodox and more typical—and therefore more widely recognized—doctrine of <term>Mahâyânism</term>, that is, the doctrine of <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>.</note>

<summary>This page contrasts the Sâmkhya belief in a plurality of individual souls with the Mahâyâna Buddhist doctrine of a single, universal All-Conserving Mind (Âlaya). It argues that while Sâmkhya is dualistic, Buddhism maintains unity by viewing individual souls as specialized manifestations of the universal Suchness.</summary>

# CHAPTER VII.

## THE THEORY OF NON-ATMAN OR NON-EGO.

If I am asked to define the basic principles of the philosophy of <term>Mahâyâna Buddhism</term>, and indeed of all schools of <term>Buddhism</term>, I would suggest the following:
(1) All is momentary (*sarvam kṣaṇikam*),
(2) All is empty (*sarvam çûnyam*).
(3) All is without self (*sarvam anâtmam*).
(4) All is such as it is (*sarvam tathâtvam*).
These four tenets are so closely interrelated that, whether they stand or fall, they all inevitably share the same fate. Whatever different views the various schools of <term>Buddhism</term> may hold on points of minor importance, they all agree at least on these four principal propositions.
Of these four propositions, the first, the second, and the fourth have been explained above, more or less explicitly. If the existence of a relative world is the work of ignorance and as such has no final reality, it must be considered illusory and empty; though it does not necessarily follow that on this account our life is not worth living. We must not

<summary>This page introduces the four fundamental principles of Buddhism—momentariness, emptiness, non-self, and suchness—noting that they are the shared foundation for all Buddhist schools. It suggests that while the relative world is a product of ignorance and lacks final reality, this does not render life meaningless.</summary>

# CHAPTER VII.

confuse the moral value of existence with the ontological problem of its phenomenality. It all depends on our subjective attitude whether or not our world and life become full of significance. When the illusory nature or phenomenality of individual existences is granted and we use the world accordingly—that is, "as not abusing it"—we escape the error and curse of egoism and take things as they are presented to us, as reflecting the <term>Dharma</term> of <term>Suchness</term>. We no longer cling to forms of particularity as something ultimate and absolutely real, or as that in which the essence of our life lies. We take them for what they are and recognize their reality only insofar as they are considered a partial realization of <term>Suchness</term>, and do not go any further. <term>Suchness</term>, indeed, lies not hidden behind them, but exists immanently *in them*. Things are empty and illusory so long as they are particular things and are not thought of in reference to the All that is <term>Suchness</term> and Reality.

From this, it logically follows that in this world of relativity all is momentary, and that nothing is permanent so far as isolated, particular existences are concerned. Even independently of the statement made above, the doctrine of universal impermanence is an almost self-evident truth experienced everywhere, and does not require any special demonstration to prove its validity. The desire for immortality, which is so conspicuous and persistent in all the stages of development of the religious consciousness that the very desire has been thought to be the essence of all

<summary>This page explains that while individual existences are illusory and momentary, they are significant when viewed as immanent reflections of Suchness. It transitions into the doctrine of universal impermanence, noting it as a self-evident truth in the world of relativity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of universal impermanence, explaining that the human desire for immortality serves as evidence for the transient nature of existence, and outlines the Buddhist perspective on why things change.</meta>

# CHAPTER VII.

religious systems, is the most conclusive proof that things on this earth are in a constant flux of becoming, and that there is nothing permanent or stationary in our individual existences; if it were otherwise, people would never have sought immortality.

If this is granted as a fact of our everyday experience, we naturally ask: "Why are things so changeable? Why is life so fleeting? What is it that makes things so mutable and transitory?" To this, the Buddhist’s answer is: Because the universe is the resulting product of many efficient forces that are acting according to different karmas; the destiny of those forces is that no single force or set of forces can constantly be predominant over all the others, but when one has exhausted its potential karma, it is replaced by another that has been steadily coming forward in the meantime. Hence the universal rhythm of birth and death, of spring and fall, of the rising and falling tide, and of integration and disintegration. Where there is attraction, there is repulsion; where there is centripetal force, there is centrifugal force. This is because it is the law of karma that at the very moment of birth, the arms of death are already around the neck of life. The universe is nothing but a grand rhythmic manifestation of certain forces working in conformity to their predetermined laws; or, to use Buddhist terminology, this *<term>lokadhâtu</term>* (material world) consists of a linking of *<term>hetus</term>* (causes) and *<term>pratyayas</term>* (conditions) regulated by their karma.

<summary>This page explains that the universal desire for immortality proves that earthly existence is in a constant state of flux. It describes the Buddhist view that the universe is a rhythmic manifestation of karmic forces, causes, and conditions that make birth and death inseparable.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, karma, impermanence, lokadhâtu, hetus, pratyayas, flux, immortality, causality</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the universe as a product of karmic forces, explaining why constant change and transitoriness are necessary conditions of existence.</meta>

# CHAPTER VII.

If this were not so, there would be either a certain fixed state of things in which a perfect equilibrium would be maintained, or an inexpressible confusion of things of which no knowledge or experience would be possible. In the former case, we would have universal stagnation and eternal death; in the latter case, there would be no universe, no life, and nothing but absolute chaos. Therefore, as long as we have the world before us, in which all possible varieties of particularization are manifested, it cannot be anything other than in a state of constant vicissitude and, therefore, universal transitoriness.

Now, the Buddhist argument for the theory of the non-ego is this: If individual existences are due to the relationships existing between diverse forces, which act sometimes in unison and sometimes in opposition to one another as predetermined by their <term>karma</term>, they cannot be said to have any transcendental agency behind them that is a permanent unity and absolute dictator. In other words, there is no <term>atman</term> or ego-soul behind our mental activities, and no "thing-in-itself" (<term>svabhava</term>), so to speak, behind each particular form of existence. This is called the Buddhist theory of non-<term>atman</term> or the non-ego.

### *Atman*

Buddhists use the term “<term>atman</term>” in two senses: first, in the sense of a personal ego, <note>1</note> and secondly, in

# CHAPTER VII.

that of "thing-in-itself," perhaps with a slight modification of its commonly accepted meaning. Let us use the term "*atman*" here in its first sense as equivalent to *bhutatman*, for we are going first to treat the doctrine of the non-ego, and later the doctrine of no-thing-in-itself.

*Atman* is usually translated as "life," "ego," or "soul," <note>1</note> and is a technical term used both by Vedanta philosophers and Buddhists. But we must note at the beginning that they do not use the term in the same sense. When the Vedanta philosophy, especially the later version, speaks of *atman* as our inmost self—which is identical with the universal Brahma—it is used in its most abstract metaphysical sense and does not mean the soul at all. This is because the latter, in the sense of an ego-substratum, may be considered to correspond to the Vedantist *Jivatman*, which is used in contrast to *Paramatman*, the supreme being or Brahma.

---

<note>1 Mahayana Buddhists generally understand the essential characteristic of *atman* to consist of freedom; and by freedom, they mean eternality, absolute unity, and supreme authority. A being that is transitory is not free, as it is conditioned by other beings, and therefore it has no *atman*. A being that is an aggregate of elemental matter or forms of energy is not absolute, for it is in a state of mutual relationship, and therefore it has no *atman*. Again, a being that has no authoritative command over itself and other beings is not free, for it will be subjected to a power other than itself, and therefore it has no *atman*. Now, take anything that we encounter in this world of particulars; does it not possess one or all of these three qualities: transitoriness, compositeness, and helplessness or dependence? Therefore, all concrete individual existences, not excluding human beings, have no *atman*—they have no ego that is eternal, absolute, and supreme.</note>

On the other hand, Buddhists understand by <term>âtman</term> this ordinary, materialistic conception of the soul (<term>bhûtâtman</term>) and positively deny its existence as such. If we, for the sake of convenience, distinguish between the phenomenal and the noumenal in our notion of the ego or soul, the <term>âtman</term> of Buddhism is the phenomenal ego—namely, a concrete agent that is supposed to do the acting, thinking, and feeling; while the <term>âtman</term> of Vedantists is the noumenal ego as the *raison d'être* of our psychical life. The one is in fact material, however ethereal it might be imagined. The other is a highly metaphysical conception transcending the reach of human discursive knowledge. The latter may be identified with <term>Paramâtman</term> and the former with <term>Jîvâtman</term>. <term>Paramâtman</term> is a universal soul from which, according to Vedantism, this world of phenomena originates, and in a certain sense it may be said to correspond to the <term>Tathâgata-garbha</term> of Buddhism. <term>Jîvâtman</term> is the ego-soul as it is conceived by ignorant people as an independent entity directing all the mental activities. It is this latter <term>âtman</term> that was found to be void by Buddha when he arose from his long meditation, declaring:

### *Buddha's First Line of Inquiry.*

<margin>→</margin> Buddhism finds the source of all evils and sufferings in the common material conception of the ego-soul, and concentrates its entire ethical force upon the destruction of ego-centric notions and desires. The Buddha seems, since the beginning of his wandering life, to have conceived the idea that the way of salvation must lie somehow in the removal of this egoistic prejudice; for as long as we are not liberated from this curse, we are liable to become the prey of the three venomous passions: greed, infatuation, and anger, and to suffer the misery of birth, death, disease, and old age. Thus, when he received his first instructions from the Sâmkhya philosopher, Arada, he was not satisfied, because Arada did not teach how to abandon this ego-soul itself. The Buddha argued: "I consider that the embodied ego-soul, though freed from the evolvent-evolutes, ²

earnest search after the ego that was supposed to be snugly sitting behind our mental experiences, and the result was this utterance.

The seed may remain dormant as long as it is deprived of the opportunity to come into contact with the necessary conditions for awakening and being awakened; but since its germinating power has not been destroyed, it will surely develop all its potential as soon as it is brought into that necessary contact. Even though the ego-soul, free from entanglement <note>that is, from the bondage of <term>Prakṛti</term></note>, is declared to be liberated, yet, as long as the ego-soul remains, there can be no absolute abandonment of it; there can be no real abandonment of egoism.'' ¹

The Buddha then proceeds to indicate the path through which he reached his final conclusion and declares: “There is no real separation of the qualities and their subject; for fire cannot be conceived apart from its heat and form.” When this argument is logically carried out, it leads nowhere but to the Buddhist doctrine of <term>non-ātman</term>, which says: The existence of an ego-soul cannot be conceived apart from sensation, perception, imagination, intelligence, volition, etc., and, therefore, it is absurd to think that there is an independent individual soul-agent which makes our consciousness its workshop.

<header>->CHAPTER VII.<-</header>

The possession of specific characteristic marks (*lakṣaṇa*) through which a thing makes itself perceived by our senses is, according to Buddhism, a wrong concept and unsupported by reason. Fire cannot be imagined apart from its form and heat; waves cannot be imagined apart from the water and its movement; a wheel cannot exist separately from its rim, spokes, axle, and other parts. Thus, all things are composed of <term>hetus</term> and <term>pratyayas</term>—of causes and conditions, of qualities and attributes. It is impossible for our <term>pudgala</term>, <term>âtman</term>, ego, or soul to be an exception to this universal condition of things.

In this context, let me describe an interesting incident from the history of Chinese Buddhism. Hui-K'e, the second patriarch of the <term>Dhyâna</term> sect in China, was troubled by this problem of the ego before his conversion. He was at first a faithful Confucian, but Confucianism did not satisfy all his spiritual needs. His soul wavered between agnosticism and skepticism; consequently, he felt an unspeakable anguish in his innermost heart. When he learned that Bodhidharma had arrived in his country, he hurried to his monastery and begged him for spiritual advice. But Bodhidharma did not say a word, appearing to be absorbed in deep meditation. Hui-K'e, however, was determined to receive religious instruction at any cost. It is reported that he stood in the same spot for seven days and nights, until he finally cut off his left arm with the sword he was carrying (being

My soul is troubled and disturbed; please, let your grace show me the way to pacify it." Dharma quietly arose from his meditation and said: "Where is your soul? Bring it here, and I will have it pacified." Hui-K'e replied: "I have been searching for it all these years, but I have never succeeded in laying a hand on it." Dharma then exclaimed: "There, I have pacified your soul!" At this, it is said, a flash of spiritual enlightenment crossed the mind of Hui-K'e, and his "soul" was pacified once and for all.

### *The <term>Skandhas</term>.*

When the five <term>skandhas</term> are combined according to their previous <term>karma</term> and present a temporary existence in the form of a sentient being, common minds imagine that they have here an individual entity sustained by an immortal ego-substratum. In fact, the material body (<term>rûpakâya</term>) alone is not what makes the ego-soul, nor is the sensation (<term>vedanâ</term>), nor the deeds (<term>sanskâra</term>), nor the consciousness (<term>vijñâna</term>), nor the conception (<term>samjñâ</term>); but only when they are all combined in a certain form do they make a sentient being. Yet this combination is not the work of a specific independent entity which, according to its own will, combines the five <term>skandhas</term> in one form and then hides itself within it. elements, Buddhism declares, is achieved by the elements themselves according to their karma. When a certain number of atoms of hydrogen and oxygen are brought together, they attract each other of their own accord or because of their own karma, and the result is water. The "ego" of water, so to speak, did not will to bring the two elements together and create itself from them. It is the same with the existence of a sentient being, and there is no need to postulate a mythical "ego-monster" behind the combination of the five <term>skandhas</term>.

<term>Skandha</term> (*<term>khanda</term>* in <term>Pâli</term>) literally means "aggregate" or "agglomeration." According to Chinese interpreters, it is called this because our personal existence is an aggregate of the five constituent elements of being, and because it takes a definite individual form when the skandhas are brought together according to their previous <term>karma</term>. The first of the five aggregates is matter (*<term>rûpa</term>*), whose essential quality is thought to consist of resistance. The material <margin>> </margin> part of our existence is found in the five sense organs called *<term>indryas</term>*: the eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and the body. The second <term>skandha</term> is called sensation or sense impression (*<term>vedanâ</term>*), which results from the contact of the six *<term>vijñânas</term>* (senses) with the <term>viṣaya</term> (external world). The third is called *<term>samjñâ</term>*, which corresponds to our conception. It is the psychic power by which we are enabled to form abstract images of particular objects. The fourth is *<term>sanskâra</term>*, which may be translated as action or deed. Our intelligent consciousness,
