<term>Sanskâra</term>, the fourth constituent of being, comprises two categories: mental (<term>*caitta*</term>) and non-mental (<term>*cittaviprayukta*</term>). The mental category is subdivided into six types: fundamental (<term>*mahâbhûmi*</term>), good (<term>*kuçala*</term>), tormenting (<term>*kleça*</term>), evil (<term>*akuçala*</term>), minor tormenting (<term>*upakleça*</term>), and indefinite (<term>*aniyata*</term>). It may be interesting to list what all these <term>sanskâras</term> are, as they shed light on the practical ethics of Buddhism.

There are ten fundamental <term>sanskâras</term> belonging to the category of mental or psychic activities: 1. <term>*cetanâ*</term> (mentation), 2. <term>*sparça*</term> (contact), 3. <term>*chanda*</term> (desire), 4. <term>*mati*</term> (understanding), 5. <term>*smṛti*</term> (recollection), 6. <term>*manaskara*</term> (concentration), 7. <term>*adhimokṣa*</term> (unfettered intelligence), and 8. <term>*samâdhi*</term> (meditation). The ten good <term>sanskâras</term> are: 1. <term>*çraddhâ*</term> (faith), 2. <term>*vîrya*</term> (energy), 3. <term>*upekṣa*</term> (complacency), 4. <term>*hrî*</term> (modesty), 5. <term>*apatrapâ*</term> (shame), 6. <term>*alobha*</term> (non-covetousness), 7. <term>*adveṣa*</term> (freedom from hatred), 8. <term>*ahimsa*</term> (gentleness of heart), 9. <term>*praçradbhi*</term> (mental repose), and 10. <term>*apramâda*</term> (attentiveness).

The eight indefinite <term>sanskâras</term> are: 1. <term>*kâukṛtya*</term> (repentance), 2. <term>*middha*</term> (sleep), 3. <term>*vitarka*</term> (inquiry), 4. <term>*vicâra*</term> (investigation), 5. <term>*râga*</term> (excitement), 6. <term>*pratigha*</term> (wrath), 7. <term>*mâna*</term> (self-reliance), 8. <term>*vicikitsâ*</term> (doubting).

The second major category of <term>sanskâra</term>, which is not included under "mental" or "psychic," comprises fourteen items as follows: 1. <term>*prâpti*</term> (attainment), 2. <term>*aprâpti*</term> (non-attainment), 3. <term>*sabhâgatâ*</term> (grouping), 4. <term>*asanjñika*</term> (unconsciousness), 5. <term>*asanjñisamâpatti*</term> (unconscious absorption in religious meditation), 6. <term>*nirodhasamâpatti*</term> (the annihilation-trance of a heretic), 7. <term>*jîvita*</term> (vitality), 8. <term>*jâti*</term> (birth), 9. <term>*sthiti*</term> (existence), 10. <term>*jarâ*</term> (decay), 11. <term>*anityatâ*</term> (transitoriness), 12. <term>*nâmakâya*</term> (name), 13. <term>*padakâya*</term> (phrase), 14. <term>*vyañjanakâya*</term> (sentence).

Now, to return to the main problem. The fifth <term>skandha</term> is called <term>*vijñâna*</term>, commonly rendered as consciousness, which, however, is not quite correct. The <term>*vijñâna*</term> is intelligence or mentality; it is the psychic power of discrimination, and in many cases it can be translated as sense. There are, according to <term>Hînayânists</term>, six <term>*vijñânas*</term> or senses: visual, auditory, olfactory, gustatory, tactual, and cogitative; according

According to Mahayana Buddhism, there are eight <term>vijñânas</term>: the <term>manovijñâna</term> and the <term>âlayavijñâna</term> are added to the six mentioned above. This psychological phase of Mahayana philosophy is primarily developed by the Yogacara school, whose leading thinkers are Asanga and Vasubandhu.

->*King Milinda and Nâgasena*<-

Buddhist literature, both Northern and Southern, is full of explanations of the doctrine of the "non-ego," as it is one of the most important foundation stones on which the magnificent temple of Buddhism is built. The dialogue <insert>¹</insert> between King Milinda and Nagasena, among many others, is very interesting for various reasons and full of thought-provoking ideas; we have the following discussion of theirs concerning the problem of the ego, taken from that dialogue.

At their first meeting, the King asks Nagasena, “How is your Reverence known, and what is your name?”

To this, the monk-philosopher replies: “I am known as Nagasena, and it is by that name that my brothers in the faith address me. But although parents give such names as Nagasena, or Surasena, Virasena, or Sihasena, yet this 'Nagasena'—and so on—is only a generally understood term, a designation in common use. For there is no permanent self involved in the matter.”

Being greatly surprised by this answer, the King

### *Ananda's Attempts to Locate the Soul.*

In the *Surangama Sutra*<note>There seem to be two Chinese translations of this Sutra, one by Kumârajîva and the other by Paramârtha, but they are apparently different texts bearing the same title. Although this translation is not entirely satisfactory in many respects, the reader may find a detailed account of the discussion there, which is only partially and roughly summarized here.</note>, Buddha exposes the absurdity of the hypothesis of an individual concrete soul-substance by overturning Ânanda's seven successive attempts to determine its location. Most people who firmly believe in personal immortality will see how vague, illusory, and logically indefensible their notion of the soul is when it is critically examined, as in the following case. Ânanda's conception of the soul is somewhat childish, but I doubt whether even in our enlightened age the belief

When questioned by the Buddha as to the location of the soul, Ânanda asserts that it resides within the body. Thereupon, the Buddha says: "If your intelligent soul resides within your physical body, why is it that it does not see your interior first? To illustrate, what we see first in this lecture hall is the interior, and it is only when the windows are thrown open that we are able to see the outside garden and woods. It is impossible for us who are sitting in the hall to see only the outside and not see the inside. Reasoning in a similar way, why does the soul that is considered to be within the body not see the internal organs first, such as the stomach, heart, veins, etc.? If, however, it does not see the inside, surely it cannot be said to reside within the body."

Ânanda now proposes to solve the problem by locating the soul outside the body. He says that the soul is like the light of a candle placed outside this hall. Where the light shines, everything is visible, but within the room there are no candles burning, and therefore nothing but darkness prevails here. This explains the inability of the soul to see the inside of the body. But the Buddha argues that "it is impossible for the soul to be outside. If so, what the soul feels might not be felt by the body, and what the body feels might not be felt by the soul, as there is no relationship between the two. The fact, however, is that when you, Ânanda, see my hand stretched out like this, you are conscious that you have the perception of

<summary>The Buddha refutes Ânanda's claims that the soul resides either inside or outside the body, using logical analogies to show that neither location accounts for internal awareness or sensory connection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Buddha and Ânanda regarding the physical location of the soul, specifically refuting the idea that the soul resides outside the body or behind the sense organs.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

it. As far as there is a connection between the soul and the body, the soul cannot be said to reside outside the body.”

The third hypothesis assumed by Ânanda is that the soul hides itself just behind the sense organs. Suppose a man put a pair of lenses over his eyes. Cannot he see the outside world through them? The reason why it cannot see the inside is that it resides within the sense organs.

But the Buddha says:
> “When we have a lens over an eye, we perceive this lens as well as the outside world. If the soul is hidden behind the sense organ, why does it not see the sense organ itself? Since it does not in fact see it, it cannot be residing in the place you mention.”

Ânanda proposes another theory.
> “Inside, we have the stomach, liver, heart, and so on; outside, we have many orifices. Where the internal organs are, there is darkness; but where we have openings, there is light. Close the eyes and the soul sees the darkness inside. Open the eyes and it sees the brightness outside. What do you say to this theory?”

The Buddha says:
> “If you take the darkness you see when the eyes are closed to be your interior, do you consider this darkness as something confronting your soul, or not? In the first case, wherever darkness prevails, that must be thought to be your internal organs. In the second case, seeing is impossible, for seeing presupposes the existence of both a subject and an object. Besides this, there is another difficulty. Grant—”

<summary>The Buddha refutes Ânanda’s theories that the soul is located behind the sense organs or that it perceives internal darkness when the eyes are closed. He argues that these positions fail to account for the nature of perception and the relationship between the observer and the observed.</summary>

—ing your supposition that if the eye could turn itself inward or outward and see the darkness of the interior or the brightness of the external world, it could also see your own face when the eye is opened. If it could not do so, it must be considered incapable of turning its sight inward."

The fifth assumption made by Ânanda is that the soul is the essence of understanding or intelligence, which is not within, nor without, nor in the middle, but which comes into actual existence as soon as it confronts the objective world; for it is taught by the Buddha that the world exists because of the mind and the mind because of the world.

To this the Buddha replies: "According to your argument, the soul must be said to exist before it comes into contact with the world; otherwise, the contact cannot make any sense. The soul, then, exists as an individual presence—not after, nor at the time of contact with the external world, but certainly before the contact. Granting this, we return again to the previous difficulties: Does the soul come out from your inside, or does it come in from the outside? In the case of the first alternative, the soul must be able to see its own face."

Ânanda interrupts: "Seeing is done by the eyes, and the soul has nothing to do with it."

The Buddha objects: "If so, a dead man has eyes just as perfect as those of a living man. <note>1</note> He must be able

to see things, but if he sees at all, he cannot be dead. Well, if your intelligent soul has a concrete existence, should it be thought of as simple or compound? Should it be thought of as filling the body or being present only in a particular spot? If it is a simple unit, when one of your limbs is touched, all four will at once be conscious of the touch, which really means no touch. If the soul is a compound body, how can it distinguish itself from another soul? If it fills the body entirely, there will be no localization of sensation, as would be the case according to the first supposition of a simple soul-unit. Finally, if it occupies only a particular part of the body, you may experience certain feelings on that spot only, and all the other parts will remain perfectly anesthetic. All these hypotheses are against the actual facts of our experience and cannot be logically maintained.

For the sixth time, Ânanda ventures to untie the Gordian knot of the soul-problem. "As the soul can be located neither within nor without, it must be somewhere in the middle." But the Buddha again refutes this, saying: "This ‘middle’ is extremely indefinite. Should it be located as a point in space or somewhere on the body? If it is on the surface of the body,

it is not the middle; if it is in the body, it is then within. If it is said to occupy a point in space, how should that point be indicated? Without an indication, a point is no point; and if an indication is needed, it can be fixed anywhere arbitrarily, and then there will be no end to the confusion.”

Ânanda interposes and says that he does not mean this kind of “middle.” Because the eye and color condition each other, visual perception comes to exist. The eye has the faculty to discriminate, while the world of color has no sensibility; but perception takes place in their “middle”—that is, in their interaction—and thus it is said that a soul exists.

The Buddha says: “If the soul, as you say, exists in the relationship between the sense-organs (*<term>indṛya</term>*) and their respective sense-objects (*<term>viṣaya</term>*), should we consider the soul as uniting and sharing the natures of these two incongruous things, <term>viṣaya</term> and <term>indṛya</term>? If the soul shares something of each, it has no characteristics of its own. If it unites the two natures, the distinction between subject and object no longer exists. ‘In the middle’ is a meaningless phrase; that is to say, to conceive the soul as the relationship between the <term>indṛyas</term> and the <term>viṣayas</term> is to make it a mere nothingness.”

The seventh and final hypothesis offered by Ânanda is that the soul is the state of non-attachment, and that, therefore, it has no particular place where it lives. But this is also relentlessly attacked by the Buddha, who declares: “Attachment presupposes the existence of things to which a mind may be attached.

<meta>This page concludes the Buddha's refutation of Ânanda’s seventh hypothesis regarding non-attachment and begins a summary of Buddhist views on the ego and reality, referencing the philosophy of Vasubandhu.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII. 163</header>

Now, should we consider these things (<term>dharmas</term>)—such as the world, space, land, water, birds, beasts, and so on—as existing or not existing? If the external world does not exist, we cannot speak about non-attachment, as there is nothing to be attached to in the first place. If the external world really exists, how can we manage not to come in contact with it? When we say that things are devoid of all characteristic marks, it amounts to the declaration that they are non-existent. But they are not non-existent; they must have certain characteristics that distinguish them. Now, the external world certainly has some marks (<term>lakṣaṇa</term>), and it must by all means be considered as existing. There, then, is no room for your theory of non-attachment."

At this, Ânanda surrenders, and the Buddha discloses his theory of <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which we shall explain at some length in the chapter specially devoted to it.

---

To summarize the points above, let me remark that Buddhists do not deny the existence of the so-called empirical ego in contrast to the noumenal ego; the latter can be considered to correspond to the Buddhist <term>âtman</term>. Vasubandhu, in his treatise on the <term>Yogâcâra</term>'s idealistic philosophy, declares that the existence of <term>âtman</term> and <term>dharma</term> is only hypothetical, provisional, and apparent, and not in any sense real and ultimate. To express this in modern terms, the soul and the world, or subject and object, have only relative existence, and no absolute reality can

<sig>z</sig>

<summary>The Buddha refutes Ânanda's final theory by arguing that non-attachment requires an existing external world to detach from, leading to a discussion on the relative nature of the ego and the world in Yogâcâra philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Ânanda, Buddha, Dharmakâya, non-attachment, lakṣaṇa, empirical ego, noumenal ego, âtman, Vasubandhu, Yogâcâra, dharma, relative existence</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ego and reality from the previous page, transitioning from the argument that the soul and the world have only relative existence and no absolute reality.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

be attributed to them. Psychologically speaking, everyone of us has an ego or soul, which means the unity of consciousness; and physically, this world of phenomena is real either as a manifestation of one energy or as a composite of atoms or electrons, as is considered by physicists.

To limit ourselves to the psychological question, what Buddhism most emphatically insists on is the non-existence of a solid, individual, irreducible soul-substance, the immortality of which is so greatly desired by most unenlightened people. Individuation is only relative and not absolute. Buddhism knows how far the principle can safely and consistently be applied, and its followers will not forget where to stop and break down the wall of individualism—a wall that seems as hard as a diamond to some religious believers. Absolute individualism, as the Buddhists understand it, prevents us from following the natural flow of sympathy; from bathing in the eternal sunshine of divinity which not only surrounds but penetrates us; from escaping the curse of individual immortality which is, strangely, so much sought after by some people; from tracing this worldly life to its fountainhead, from which it drinks so freely, yet quite unknowingly; and from rising rejuvenated from the consuming fire of <term>Kâla</term> (<term>Chronos</term>). To think that there is a mysterious "something" behind the empirical ego and that this something emerges triumphantly, in the manner of the immortal phoenix from the funeral pyre of the physical body, is not Buddhist.

What I would note here in connection with this

<summary>This page argues that Buddhism rejects the concept of a permanent, individual soul-substance, viewing individualism as a barrier to sympathy and spiritual connection. It characterizes the belief in an immortal entity surviving the body's death as contrary to Buddhist teachings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Buddhist concept of the ego, transitioning from the critique of absolute individualism to the specific relationship between the individual soul and the universal Âlaya-vijñāna.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

problem of the soul, is its relation to that of *Âlaya-vijñāna*, of which it is said that the Buddha was very reluctant to talk, on account of its being easily confounded with the notion of the ego. The *Âlaya*, as was explained, is a sort of universal soul from which our individual empirical souls are considered to have evolved. The *Manas*, which is the first offspring of the *Âlaya*, is endowed with the faculty of discrimination, and from the wrongful use of this faculty, there arises in the *Manas* the conception of the *Âlaya* as the ego—the real concrete soul-substratum.

The *Âlaya*, however, is not a particular phenomenon, for it is a state of Suchness in its evolutionary disposition and has nothing in it yet to suggest its concrete individuality. When the *Manas* finds out its error and lifts the veil of Ignorance from the body of the *Âlaya*, it soon becomes convinced of the ultimate nature of the so-called soul. For the soul is not individual, but supra-individual.

### *Âtman and the “Old Man.”*

When the Buddhists exclaim: “Put away your egoism, for the ego is an empty notion, a mere word without reality,” some of our Christian readers may think that if there is no ego, what will become of our personality or individuality? Though this point will become clearer as we proceed, let us remark here that what Buddhism understands by ego or *âtman* may be considered to correspond in many respects to the Christian notion of “flesh” or the

<summary>This page discusses the relationship between the individual soul and the Âlaya-vijñāna, explaining that the ego is a mistaken conception arising from the faculty of discrimination. It concludes by beginning a comparison between the Buddhist concept of Âtman and the Christian concept of "the flesh."</summary>
<keywords>Âlaya-vijñāna, Buddha, Manas, Suchness, Ignorance, Âtman, Buddhism, Christianity, ego, soul, discrimination, supra-individual</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Buddhist and Christian concepts of the soul, specifically relating the Buddhist "ego" to the Christian concepts of the "flesh" and the "old man."</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

"old man," which is the source of all our sinful acts. Paul says: "I am crucified with Christ; nevertheless I live; yet not I, but Christ lives in me: and the life which I live now in the flesh I live by the faith of the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me." (Galatians 2:20.) When this passage is interpreted by Buddhists, the "I" that was annihilated through crucifixion is our false notion of an ego-soul (*âtman*); and the "I" that lives through the grace of God is the Bodhi, a reflection within us of the Dharmakâya.

When Christians contrast the spirit and the flesh and advise us to "walk in the spirit" and not to "fulfill the lust of the flesh," it must be said that they understand the flesh to mean our concrete, material existence, which is predominantly individual in character; and they understand the spirit as that which transcends particularity and egoism. For "love, joy, peace, long-suffering, faith, meekness, temperance," and similar virtues are possible only when our egocentric, *âtman*-made desires are utterly renounced. Buddhism is more intellectual than Christianity or Judaism and prefers philosophical terms that are better understood than popular language, which often leads to confusion. Compared with the Buddhist conception of *âtman*, the "flesh" lacks clarity and precision, to say nothing of its dualistic tendency, which is highly disagreeable to Buddhists.

<summary>This page compares the Christian concepts of the "flesh" and "old man" to the Buddhist concept of the ego-soul (atman), explaining how both traditions seek to transcend the individual self. It argues that Buddhism uses more precise philosophical language and avoids the dualism inherent in Christian terminology.</summary>
<keywords>Paul, Galatians, Buddhism, Christianity, Judaism, atman, Bodhi, Dharmakaya, ego-soul, flesh, spirit, dualism, ethics</keywords>

### *The Vedantic Conception.*

Although the doctrine of non-âtman is pre-eminently Buddhist, other Hindu philosophers did not neglect to acknowledge its importance in our religious life. Having grown in the same soil under similar circumstances, the following passage, which is taken from the *Yogavâsistha* (which is supposed to be a Vedantic work, Upaçama P., ch. LII, 31, 44), sounds almost Buddhist:

> "I am absolute, I am the light of intelligence, I am free from the defilement of egoism. O you who are unreal! I am not bound by you, the seed of egoism." <gloss>1</gloss>

The author then argues: Where shall we consider the so-called ego-soul to reside in this body of flesh and bones? And what does it look like? We move our limbs, but the movement is due to the vital airs (<term>vâta</term>). We think, but consciousness is a manifestation of the great mind (<term>mahâcitta</term>). We cease to exist, but extinction belongs to the body (<term>kâya</term>). Now, take apart what we imagine to constitute our personal existence. The flesh is one thing, the blood is another, and so on with mentation (<term>bodha</term>) and vitality (<term>spanda</term>). The ear hears, the tongue tastes, the eye sees, the mind

---

->*The Non-Atman Nature of Things.*<-

The word "<term>âtman</term>" is used by Buddhists not only psychologically, in the sense of soul, self, or ego, but also ontologically, in the sense of substance, the "thing-in-itself," or "thinginess"; its existence in this capacity is also strongly denied by them. For the same reason that the existence of an individual ego-soul is untenable, they reject the hypothesis of the permanent existence of an individual object as such. Just as there is no transcendent agent in our soul-life, there is no real, eternal existence of individuals as individuals. Instead, there is a system of different attributes which, when the force of <term>karma</term> is exhausted, ceases to exist. Individual existences cannot be real by their inherent nature; they are illusory and will never remain permanent. They are in a state of constant becoming and have no selfhood, though they may appear so to our discriminating senses because of our subjective ignorance. In reality, they are <term>cûnya</term> and <term>anâtman</term>; they are empty and void of <term>âtman</term>.

->*Svabhâva.*<-

The term "<term>sabhâva</term>" (self-essence or noumenon) is sometimes used by the Mahâyânists in place of <term>âtman</term>, and they would say that all <term>dharmas</term> have no self-

<summary>Buddhists apply the doctrine of 'anâtman' (no-self) to both psychology and ontology, arguing that neither individual souls nor physical objects possess a permanent, independent substance. All things are described as 'cûnya' (empty) and lack a permanent 'self-essence' or 'svabhâva.'</summary>
<keywords>âtman, karma, cûnya, anâtman, Svabhâva, sabhâva, dharmas, Buddhism, Mahâyânists, ontology, soul, emptiness, non-self</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Buddhist concept of 'svabhāva' (self-essence), explaining how it is synonymous with 'ātman' in the context of the non-existence of permanent substances in objects.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VII. 171</header>

essence, *sarvam dharmam <term>niḥsvabhāvam</term>*, which is to say that all things in their phenomenal aspect are devoid of individual selves, and that it is only due to our ignorance that we believe in the "thinginess" of things, whereas there is no such thing as <term>svabhāva</term>, <term>ātman</term>, or <term>noumenon</term> which resides in them. <term>Svabhāva</term> and <term>ātman</term> are thus habitually used by Buddhists as quite synonymous.

What exactly do they understand by "<term>svabhāva</term>," the existence of which is denied in a particular object as perceived by our senses? This has never been explicitly defined by the <term>Mahâyânists</term>, but they seem to understand by <term>svabhāva</term> something concrete and individual, yet independent, unconditioned, and not subject to the law of causation (<term>pratyayasamutpâda</term>). It, therefore, stands in opposition to <term>çûnyatâ</term> (emptiness), as well as to conditionality. Since all beings are transient and empty in their inherent being, they cannot logically be said to possess a self-essence which defies the law of causation. <margin>✓</margin> All things are mutually conditioning and limiting, and apart from their relativity, they are non-existent and cannot be known by us. Therefore, says <term>Nâgârjuna</term>, "If substance is different from attribute, it is then beyond comprehension." <note>1. *Lakṣyâl lakṣaṇam anyac cet syât tal lakṣyam alakṣaṇam.*</note> For "a jug is not to be known independently of matter and so forth, and matter in turn is not to be known independently of ether and so forth." <note>2. *Rūpâdi vyatirekena yathâ <term>kumbho</term> na vidyate, Vâhyâdi vyatireṇa tathâ rûpam na vidyate.*</note>

***

<summary>This page defines 'svabhāva' as an imagined independent and unconditioned essence that Buddhists deny exists within objects. Nâgârjuna is cited to argue that things only exist through mutual relativity and cannot be known apart from their attributes.</summary>
<keywords>svabhāva, ātman, noumenon, Mahâyânists, pratyayasamutpâda, çûnyatâ, Nâgârjuna, causation, relativity, niḥsvabhāvam</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Buddhist concept of self-essence (svabhāva), specifically examining its relationship to causation and the inherent emptiness of all conditioned things.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER VII.</header>

Just as there is no <term>subject</term> without an <term>object</term>, there is no <term>substance</term> without an <term>attribute</term>; for one is the condition for the other. Does <term>self-essence</term> then exist in <term>causation</term>? No, "whatever is subject to <term>conditionality</term> is by its very nature tranquil and empty." (*Pratîtya yad yad bhavati, tat tac çântam svabhâvatah.*) Whatever owes its existence to a combination of causes and conditions is without <term>self-essence</term>, and therefore it is tranquil (*<term>çânta</term>*), it is empty, and it is unreal (*<term>asat</term>*). The ultimate nature of this universal <term>emptiness</term> is not within the sphere of logical proof, for human understanding is not capable of rising above its inherent limitations.

<term>Pingalaka</term>, a commentator on <term>Nâgârjuna</term>, says: "The cloth exists because of the thread; the matting is possible because of the rattan. If the thread had its own fixed, unchangeable <term>self-essence</term>, it could not be made from the flax. If the cloth had its own fixed, unchangeable <term>self-essence</term>, it could not be made from the thread. But as in point of fact the cloth comes from the thread and the thread from the flax, it must be said that neither the thread nor the cloth has a fixed, unchangeable <term>self-essence</term>. It is just like the relationship that exists between the burning and the burned. They are brought together under certain conditions, and thus a phenomenon called burning occurs. Neither the burning nor the burned has a reality of its own. For when one is absent, the other is extinguished. It is the same with all things in this world: they are all empty,"

<summary>This page argues that substance and attribute are mutually dependent and that no object possesses an independent self-essence because all things arise through conditionality. Using the analogy of cloth and fire, the text demonstrates that phenomena lack an inherent, unchanging reality and are ultimately empty.</summary>
<keywords>emptiness, śūnyatā, causation, self-essence, svabhāva, conditionality, Pingalaka, Nâgârjuna, substance and attribute</keywords>

without self, without absolute existence, they are like the will-o'-the-wisp." <note>Abstracted from Pingalaka's *Commentary on the Mâdhyamika Çâstra*, Chapter VII. The Chinese translation is by Kumârajîva.</note>

->*The Real Significance of Emptiness.*<-

From these statements it will be apparent that the <term>emptiness</term> of things (<term>çûnyatâ</term>) does not mean nothingness, as it is sometimes interpreted by some critics; it simply means the <term>conditionality</term> or <term>transitoriness</term> of all phenomenal existence. It is a synonym for <term>aniyata</term> or <term>pratîtya</term>. Therefore, <term>emptiness</term>, according to the Buddhists, signifies, negatively, the absence of particularity—the non-existence of individuals as such—and positively, the ever-changing state of the phenomenal world: a constant flux of becoming and an eternal series of causes and effects. It must never be understood in the sense of annihilation or absolute nothingness, for <term>nihilism</term> is as much condemned by Buddhism as <term>naive realism</term>. "The Buddha proclaimed emptiness as a remedy for all doctrinal controversies, but those <margin>✓</margin> who in turn cling to emptiness are beyond treatment." A medicine is indispensable as long as there is a disease to heal, but it turns poisonous when applied after the restoration of perfect health. To make this point completely clear, let me quote the following from Nâgârjuna's *Mâdhyamika Çâstra* (Chapter XXIV). "[Someone may object to the Buddhist doctrine of <term>emptiness</term>, declaring:] If all is void (<term>çûnya</term>) and

<summary>This page clarifies that Buddhist emptiness (shunyata) refers to the conditional and transitory nature of phenomena, not to absolute nothingness or nihilism. It highlights that while emptiness is a conceptual remedy for dogma, clinging to the concept itself is a spiritual danger.</summary>
<keywords>emptiness, çûnyatâ, conditionality, transitoriness, Nâgârjuna, Mâdhyamika Çâstra, Buddhism, nihilism, pratîtya</keywords>

If they are altogether unrealizable, there cannot be any of the four states of saintliness; and without these states, there cannot be anyone who will aspire to them. If there are no wise men, the <term>Sangha</term> is then impossible. Therefore, those who speak of emptiness must be said to negate the <term>Triple Treasure</term> (<term>*triratna*</term>) altogether. Emptiness not only destroys the law of causation and the general principle of retribution (phalasadbhâvam), but utterly annihilates the possibility of a phenomenal world."

“Furthermore, if the Fourfold Noble Truth could not exist, the Law would be impossible, and without the Sangha and the Law, how could the Buddha exist? You might say: ‘A Buddha does not exist because of wisdom (*Bodhi*), nor does wisdom exist because of the Buddha.’ But if a person did not possess Buddha-essence <note>that is, Bodhi</note> he could not hope to attain Buddhahood, no matter how strenuously he might exert himself in the ways of the Bodhisattva.

“Further, if everything is not empty but possesses self-essence <note>that is, if everything is in a fixed, unchanging state of sameness</note>, how could there be any action? How could there be good and evil? If you maintain that there is an effect (*phala*) which does not come from a cause, whether good or evil <note>which is the practical conclusion of the hypothesis of self-essence</note>, then it means that retribution is independent of our deeds, whether good or evil. <note>But is this justified by our experience?</note>

“If it must then be admitted that our good or evil deeds become the cause of retribution, and retribution must be said to come from our good or evil deeds, then how could we say there is no emptiness?

The Mahâyânistic doctrines thus formulated and transmitted down to the present day are: There is no such thing as the ego; mental activity is produced by the coordination of various *vijñânas* or senses.

The world of particulars is the work of Ignorance, as declared by the Buddha in his Formula of Dependence (Twelve Nidânas). When this veil of Mâya is lifted, the universal light of Dharmakâya shines in all its magnificence. Individual existences then lose their significance as such and become sublimated and ennobled in the oneness of Dharmakâya. # CHAPTER VIII.

## KARMA.

### *Definition.*

<margin>✓</margin>
Karma, or Sanskâra, which is sometimes used as its synonym—though the latter gives a slightly different shade of meaning—comes from the Sanskrit root *kṛ*, meaning "to do," "to make," "to perform," "to effect," "to produce," etc. Both terms mean activity in both its concrete and its abstract senses, and they form an antithesis to intelligence, contemplation, or ideation in general. When karma is used in its most abstract sense, it becomes equivalent to "beginningless ignorance," which is universally inherent in nature and corresponds to the "Will" or blind activity described by Schopenhauer. For ignorance, as we have seen above, is a negative manifestation of Suchness (*Bhûta-tathâtâ*) and marks the beginning or unfolding of a phenomenal world, whose existence is characterized by incessant activities driven by the principle of karma. When Goethe says in *Faust*, "In the beginning was the deed" <note>"In Anfang war die That" in the original German.</note>, he uses the term "deed" in the sense of karma as it is understood here.

When karma is used in its concrete sense, it is the

<summary>This page defines Karma and its synonym Sanskâra as activity in both abstract and concrete senses, contrasting them with intelligence or ideation. It explains that in an abstract sense, Karma corresponds to a "beginningless ignorance" that drives the unfolding of the phenomenal world, similar to Schopenhauer’s concept of the Will.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Sanskâra, Sanskrit, activity, ignorance, Schopenhauer, Suchness, Bhûta-tathâtâ, Goethe, Faust, phenomenal world</keywords>

As we have a special chapter devoted to “Ignorance” as an equivalent of karma in its abstract sense, let us here treat the Buddhist conception of karma in the realm of names and forms, that is, of karma in its concrete sense. But we shall restrict ourselves to the activity of karmic causation in the moral world, as we are not concerned with physics or biology.

### *The Working of Karma.*

The Buddhist conception of karma, briefly stated, is this: Any act, good or evil, once committed and conceived, never vanishes like a bubble in water, but lives, potentially or actively as the case may be, in the world of minds and deeds. This mysterious moral energy, so to speak, is embodied in and emanates from every act and thought, for it does not matter whether it is actually performed or merely conceived in the mind. When the time comes, it is sure to germinate and grow with all its vitality. Buddha says:

> “Karma even after the lapse of a hundred <term>kalpas</term>,
> Will not be lost nor destroyed;
> As soon as all the necessary conditions are ready,
> Its fruit is sure to ripen.” <note>From a Chinese <term>Mahâyâna</term> sutra.</note>

<summary>This page defines the concrete application of karma within the moral realm, describing it as an indestructible moral energy that persists until conditions allow it to bear fruit. It specifically links these concepts to the Twelve Chains of Dependence and the All-conserving Soul (âlayavijñâna).</summary>

<keywords>karma, Twelve Chains, âlayavijñâna, Vijñâna, Manovijñâna, Nâmârûpa, moral energy, causation, kalpas, Mahâyâna sutra</keywords>

# CHAPTER VIII.

Again,

> "Whatever a man does, he will find the same within himself;
> The good man finds good, and the man who designed evil finds evil;
> And so our deeds are all like seeds, and they bring forth fruit
> of their own kind." <note>The Pâli Jâtaka, no. 222. Translated by W. H. Rouse.</note>

It is said that a grain of wheat, which was accidentally preserved in good condition in a tomb more than a thousand years old, did not lose its power to germinate. When it was planted with proper care, it actually began to sprout. It is the same with karma: it is endowed with an enormous vitality—indeed, it is even immortal. Regardless of how long ago they were committed, the karma of our deeds never dies; it must work out its own destiny at whatever cost, unless it is overcome by some counteracting force. The law of karma is irrefragable.

The irrefragable nature of karma means that the law of causation is supreme in our moral sphere just as much as in the physical world. It means that life consists of a chain of causes and effects regulated by the principle of karma, and that nothing in the life of an individual, a nation, or a race happens without a proper cause and a sufficient reason—that is, without previous karma. Therefore, Buddhists do not believe in any special acts of grace or divine revelation within the religious and moral life. The idea of a *deus ex machina* is rejected in Buddhism. Everything we suffer or enjoy morally in our present life is due to the karma accumulated

## CHAPTER VIII.

since the beginning of life on earth. Nothing is sown, nothing is reaped.

Whatever has been done leaves an indescribable mark in the individual’s life and even in that of the universe; and this mark will never be erased except by the sheer exhaustion of the karma or by the interruption of an overwhelming counter-karma. In case the karma of an act is not realized during one’s own lifetime, it will be in that of one’s successors, who may be physical or spiritual. Not only “the evil that men do lives after them,” but also the good, for it will not be “buried with their bones,” as common minds imagine. We read in the *Samyukta Nikâya*, III, 1 — 4:

> “Assailed by death, in life’s last struggles,
> At the leaving of this human state,
> What is it one can call his own?
> What does he take with him as he goes from here?
> What is it that follows after him,
> And like a shadow never departs?
>
> “His good deeds and his wickedness,
> Whatever a mortal does while here;
> It is this that he can call his own,
> This he takes with him as he goes from here.
> This is what follows after him,
> And like a shadow never departs.
> 
> “Let all, then, perform noble deeds,
> A treasure-store for future well-being;
> For merit gained within this life,
> Will yield a blessing in the next.” <note>Warren’s Buddhism in Translations, p. 214.</note>

### CHAPTER VIII.

In accordance with this karmic preservation, Buddhists do not expect to have their sins expiated by other innocent people as long as their own hearts remain as unsoftened as ever. But when the all-embracing love of Buddhas for all sentient beings kindles even the smallest spark of repentance and enlightenment in the heart of a sinner, and when this ever-wavering light grows to its full magnitude under favorable conditions, the sinner becomes fully awakened from the evil karma of eons, and enters, free from all curses, into the eternity of Nirvâna.

->*Karma and Social Injustice.*<-

The doctrine of karma is very frequently utilized by some Buddhists to explain a state of affairs which must be considered cases of social injustice.

There are some people who are born rich and noble and are destined to enjoy all forms of earthly happiness and all the advantages of social life, even though they have done nothing that justifies them living in luxury in such a way any more than their poor neighbors. These people, however, are declared by some pseudo-Buddhists to be merely harvesting the crops of good karma they had prepared in their former lives. On the other hand, the poor, needy, and lowly who are struggling to scrape by with a mere existence despite their moral integrity and honest industry, are considered to be suffering the evil karma which had been accumulated during their previous lives. The law of moral retribution is never

<summary>This page explains that personal repentance and the help of Buddhas are necessary for spiritual awakening from evil karma, rather than the sacrifice of others. It also notes how the concept of karma is sometimes used to justify social inequality by attributing present wealth or poverty to deeds from past lives.</summary>

<meta>Checkmark in right margin next to "karmic conservation."</meta>

suspended, as they argue, on account of the changes which may take place in a mortal being. An act, good or evil, once performed, will not be lost in the eternal succession and interaction of events, but will certainly find the person who must experience its due consequence; it does not matter whether the actor has gone through the vicissitudes of birth and death. For the Buddhist conception of individual identity is not one of personal continuity, but of karmic conservation. Whatever deeds we may commit, they invariably bear their legitimate fruit and follow us even after death. Therefore, if the rich and noble neglect their duties or abandon themselves to the enjoyment of sensual pleasures, then they are sure in their future births, if not in their present life, to harvest the crops they have thus unknowingly prepared for themselves. The poor, however hard their lot in this life, can claim their rightful rewards, provided they do not despair over their present sufferings and give themselves up to temptations, but instead dutifully continue to do things that are good and meritorious. Because just as their present fate is the result of their former deeds, so will their future fortune be the fruit of their present deeds.

This view, as held by some pseudo-Buddhists, gives us a wrong impression about the practical working of the principle of karma in this world of nâmarûpas <note>The world of "name and form," referring to the mental and physical components of existence.</note>, for it tries to explain through karmic theory the phenomena which lie outside the sphere of its applicability. As I understand it, what the theory of karma

# CHAPTER VIII.

<margin>✓</margin>proposes to explain is not cases of social injustice and economic inequality, but facts of moral causation.

The overbearing attitude of the rich and the noble, the unnecessary sufferings of the poor, the production of too many criminals, and similar social phenomena arise from the imperfections of our current social organization, which is based on the doctrine of absolute private ownership. People are allowed to amass wealth without limit for their own use and to bequeath it to successors who do not deserve it in any way. And they do not consider the injuries this system may inflict upon the general welfare of the community to which they belong, or upon other individual members. The rich might have economically—and consequently politically and morally—slaughtered millions of their brothers before they could reach the positions of social eminence they now occupy and enjoy to their full extent. They might have sacrificed hundreds of thousands of victims on the altar of Mammon in order to carry out their vast scheme of self-aggrandizement. And, what is worse, the law allows the wealth an individual accumulates to be handed down to his descendants, who are, in a sense, the parasitic members of the community. They are privileged to live off the sweat and blood of others who do not know where to lay their heads, and who daily succumb to a heavy burden that was not their free choice, but was forced upon them by society.

Let us look closely into the facts here. There is one portion of society that does almost nothing toward

These sad phenomena which, owing to the imperfection of social organization, we daily witness around us—should we attribute them to the diversity of individual karma and make individuals responsible for what is really due to the faulty organization of the community to which they belong? No, the doctrine of karma certainly must not be understood to explain the cause of our social and economic imperfection.

The region where the law of karma is made to work supreme is our moral world, and it cannot be made to extend also over our economic field. Poverty is not necessarily the consequence of evil deeds, nor is abundance that of good acts. Whether a person is affluent or needy is mostly determined by the principle of economy as far as our present social system is concerned. Morality and economy are two different realms of human activity. Honesty and moral rectitude do not necessarily guarantee well-being. Dishonesty and the violation of the moral law, on the contrary, are very frequently utilized as handmaids of material prosperity. Do we not thus see many good, conscientious people around us who are wretchedly poverty-stricken? Shall we take them as suffering the curse of evil karma in their previous lives, when we can understand the fact perfectly well as a case of social injustice? It is not necessary by any means—in fact, it is even productive of evil—to establish a rela-

A virtuous man is contented with his clean conscience and purity of heart. As obscure as his present social position is, and as miserable as his present financial conditions are, he has no mind to look backward and find the cause of his social insignificance there, nor is he anxious about his future earthly fortune which might be awaiting him when his karmic energy appears in a new form. His heart is altogether free from such vanities and anxieties. He is sufficient unto himself as he is here and now. And, as for the altruistic aspect of his moral deeds, he is well aware that their karma would spiritually benefit everyone who is inspired by it, and also that it would largely contribute to the realization of goodness on this earth. Why, then, must we invent such a poor theory of karma as is maintained by some, just so they might give him a spiritual solace for his material misfortune?

Worldly people are too eager to see everything and every act they perform working for the accumulation of earthly wealth and the promotion of material welfare. They want to turn even moral deeds, which have no relation to the economic condition of life, into opportunities to attain mundane things. They would desire to have the law of karmic causation applied to a realm where an entirely different set of laws prevails. In point of fact, what proceeds from

And what more would a person of pious heart desire to gain from being good? “Take no thought for your life, what you shall eat, or what you shall drink, nor yet for your body, what you shall put on. Is not life more than food and the body more than clothing?” Let us then do away with the worldly interpretation of karma, which is so contrary to the spirit of Buddhism.

As long as we live under the present state of things, it is impossible to escape the curse of social injustice and economic inequality. Some people must be born rich and noble, enjoying a superabundance of material wealth, while others must groan under the unbearable burden imposed upon them by a cruel society. Unless we make a radical change in our present social organization, we cannot expect every one of us to enjoy equal opportunity and a fair chance. ### *An Individualistic View of Karma.*

There is another popular misconception concerning the doctrine of karma, which seriously spoils the true interpretation of Buddhism. By this, I mean an individualistic view of the doctrine. This view asserts that deeds, whether good or evil, committed by a person determine only his own fate, with no one else being affected by them in any possible way. It claims that the reason we should refrain from doing wrong is that we, and not others, must suffer its evil consequences. This conception of karma, which I call individualistic, assumes the absolute reality of an individual soul and its continuation as such in a new physical existence made possible by its previous karma. In this view, an individual soul is understood as an independent unit that stands in no relation to others, and which therefore neither influences nor is influenced by them in any way. All that is done by oneself is suffered by oneself alone, and no other people have anything to do with it, nor do they suffer in the slightest from it.

Buddhism, however, does not advocate for this individualistic interpretation of karmic law, for it does not agree with the theory of <term>non-âtman</term>, nor with that of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.

According to the orthodox theory, karma simply means the conservation or immortality of the inner

<summary>The author critiques the "individualistic" view of karma, which falsely assumes that an individual's actions only affect their own isolated soul. He argues that this view contradicts the core Buddhist tenets of non-self (non-âtman) and the interconnectedness of the Dharmakâya.</summary>
<keywords>karma, individualistic view, Buddhism, non-âtman, Dharmakâya, soul, ethics, karmic law</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from an individualistic view of karma to a universal one, explaining how the consequences of actions radiate through the entire spiritual community rather than affecting only the individual.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
# CHAPTER VIII.

...force of deeds regardless of their author's physical identity. Deeds once committed, whether good or evil, leave permanent effects on the general system of sentient beings, of which the actor is merely a component part. It is not the actor alone, but everyone constituting a grand psychic community called the <term>Dharmadhâtu</term> (spiritual universe), who suffers or enjoys the outcome of a moral deed.

Because the universe is not a theater for only one particular soul; on the contrary, it belongs to all sentient beings, each forming a psychic unit. These units are so intimately knitted together in blood and soul that the effects of even apparently trifling deeds committed by an individual are felt by others just as much and just as surely as by the doer themselves. Throw an insignificant stone into a vast expanse of water, and it will certainly create an almost endless series of ripples—however imperceptible—that never stop until they reach the furthest shore. The vibration thus caused is felt by the sinking stone as much as by the water it disturbed. The universe, which may seem to crude observers as merely a system of gross physical forces, is in reality a great spiritual community, and every sentient being forms a component part of it. This most complicated, most subtle, most sensitive, and best-organized mass of spiritual atoms transmits its current of "moral electricity" from one particle to another with the utmost rapidity and certainty. This is because this community is at its foundation an expression of one <term>Dharmakâya</term>. However diversified

and dissimilar it may appear in its individual material aspect, it is after all nothing more than an evolution of one pervading essence, in which the multiplicity of things finds its unity and identity. Therefore, it is for the interests of the community at large, and not for their own welfare alone, that sincere Buddhists refrain from breaking moral laws and are encouraged to promote goodness. Those whose spiritual insight thus penetrates deep into the inner unity and interaction of all human souls are called <term>Bodhisattvas</term>.

It is with this spirit, let me repeat, that pious Buddhists do not wish to keep for themselves any <term>merit</term> created by their acts of love and benevolence, but wish to turn them over (<term>parivarta</term>) to the deliverance of all sentient creatures from the darkness of ignorance. The most typical way for Buddhists to conclude any religious treatise, therefore, generally runs in the following manner:

> “The deep significance of the three <term>karmas</term> as taught by Buddha,
> I have thus finished explaining in accord with the <term>Dharma</term> and logic:
> Through the power of this merit I pray to deliver all sentient beings
> And to make them soon attain to perfect <term>enlightenment</term>.” <note>¹ *On the Completion of Karma*, by <term>Vasubandhu</term>. Nanjo, No. 1222.</note>

->Or,<-

> “All the merits arising from this, my exposition,

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why Buddhists dedicate their spiritual merits to all beings, moving into the philosophical reasoning regarding the interconnectedness of souls and the collective nature of karma.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
# CHAPTER VIII.

> ->"And soon attain to a supreme, perfect, great, and far-reaching <term>enlightenment</term>." <note>¹ *The Distinguishing of the Mean*, by <term>Vasubandhu</term>. Nanjo, 1248.</note><-

The reason why a moral deed performed by one person contributes to the attainment of supreme <term>enlightenment</term> by others is that <term>souls</term>, which are usually thought to be individual and independent of others, are not actually so. Instead, they are very closely intermingled with one another, so that a stir produced in one is sooner or later transmitted to another, influencing it for better or worse. The <term>karmic</term> effect of my own deed determines not only my own future but also, to a significant extent, the future of others. This explains the invocations just quoted by pious <term>Buddhists</term> who desire to dedicate all the <term>merit</term> they can achieve to the general welfare of the masses.

Humanity's ever-increasing tendency to widen and improve communication in every possible way is a phenomenon that illustrates the intrinsic oneness of human <term>souls</term>. Isolation kills, for it is another name for death. Every soul that lives and grows desires to embrace others, to be in communion with them, to be supplemented by them, and to expand infinitely so that all individual <term>souls</term> are brought together and united in the one soul. Only under this condition can a person's <term>karma</term> influence other people, and their <term>merits</term> be used for the promotion of general <term>enlightenment</term>.

<summary>This page explains that human souls are interconnected rather than independent, meaning one person's moral actions and karma can influence the spiritual progress of others. This unity of souls allows for the Buddhist practice of dedicating personal merit to the enlightenment of all beings.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER VIII.</header>

### *Karma and Determinism.*

If the undeniable nature of <term>karma</term> means the <term>predetermination</term> of our moral life, some would reason that the doctrine is fatalism pure and simple. It is quite true that our present life is the result of the <term>karma</term> accumulated in our previous existences, and that as long as the <term>karma</term> preserves its vitality, there is no chance whatever to escape its consequences, whether good or evil. It is also true that just as the lowliest sparrow shall not fall to the ground without the knowledge of God, and as the very hairs of our heads are all numbered by him, so even a single blade of grass does not quiver before the evening breeze without the force of <term>karma</term>. It is also true that if our <term>intellect</term> were not as shortsighted as it is, we could reduce the possible complexity of the conditions under which our life exists into its simplest terms, and thus predict with mathematical precision the course of a life through which it is destined to pass. If we could record all our previous <term>karma</term> from time immemorial and all its consequences, both for ourselves and for those who come in contact with us, there would be no difficulty in determining our future life with the utmost certainty. The human <term>intellect</term>, however, as it happens, is incapable of undertaking a work of such enormous magnitude, and so we cannot perceive the full significance of <term>determinism</term>; but, from the divine point of view, <term>determinism</term> seems to be perfectly justified, for there cannot be any shortsightedness on the part of a <term>world-soul</term> regarding the <term>destiny</term> of the universe, which

<summary>This page explores whether the inescapable nature of karma implies fatalism, acknowledging that while human life is determined by past actions, the complexity of these karmic threads is beyond human comprehension. It suggests that from a higher, "world-soul" perspective, this determinism is a perfect and predictable system.</summary>
<keywords>karma, determinism, fatalism, world-soul, predetermination, intellect, destiny</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of karma and determinism, emphasizing that while Buddhism accepts a form of universal causality, it rejects a fatalism that would deny personal effort and moral responsibility.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VIII.</header>

is nothing but its own expression. It is only from the human point of view that we feel uncertain about our final disposition and endeavor to explain existence now from a mechanical, now from a teleological standpoint; and yet, strangely enough, at the bottom of our soul we feel that there is something mysterious here which makes us cry out, either in despair or in trustful resignation, “Let your will be done.” While this very confidence in “your will” proves that we have in our inmost consciousness—and outside the scope of intellectual analysis—a belief in the supreme order, which is absolutely preordained and which at least is not controllable by our finite, limited, fragmentary mind, yet the doctrine of <term>karma</term> must not be understood in the strictest sense of <term>fatalism</term>.

As far as a general theory of <term>determinism</term> is concerned, Buddhism has no objection to it. Grant that there is a law of causation, that every deed, actualized or thought of, leaves something behind, and that this something becomes a determining factor for our future life; then how could we escape the conclusion that “each of us is inevitable” as Whitman sings? Religious confidence in a divine will that is supposed to give us always the best of things is, in fact, no more than a form of <term>determinism</term>. But if, in applying the doctrine to our practical life, we forget to strive to unfold all the possibilities that might lie within us—which can only be awakened after strenuous efforts—there will be no moral character, no personal responsibility, and no noble aspirations; the mind will be nothing but a reflex nervous system and life a mere machine.

<summary>This page argues that while Buddhism acknowledges a universal law of causation and determinism, it must not be confused with fatalism. It emphasizes that human effort and personal responsibility are necessary to develop moral character and avoid viewing life as mere machinery.</summary>
<keywords>karma, fatalism, determinism, Buddhism, law of causation, Whitman, divine will, personal responsibility, teleological, mechanical</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of karma, moving from a rejection of fatalism to an explanation of karma as a living, organic force that allows for spiritual growth and redemption.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>

## CHAPTER VIII.

In fact, <term>karma</term> is not a machine; it is not incapable of regeneration and self-multiplication. <term>Karma</term> is a wonderful organic power; it grows, it expands, and it even gives birth to new <term>karma</term>. It is like a mustard seed, the smallest of all seeds, but, being full of vitality, it grows as soon as it comes into contact with the nourishing soil and becomes a tree, so that the birds of the air come and lodge in its branches. Its mystery is like that of sympathetic waves that pass through all hearts that feel the great deeds of a hero or listen to the story of a self-sacrificing mother. <term>Karma</term>, whether good or evil, is contagious and sympathetic in its work. Even the most insignificant act of goodness reaps an unexpectedly rich harvest. Even to the vilest rogue comes a chance for repentance by means of a single good <term>karma</term> ever performed in his life, which has extended through many a <term>kalpa</term>. And the most wonderful thing in our spiritual world is that the <term>karma</term> that brings repentance and <term>Nirvâna</term> to the heart of the lowliest awakens and rekindles a similar <term>karma</term> potentially slumbering in other hearts and leads them to the final abode of enlightenment.

Insofar as we confine ourselves to a general, superficial view of the theory of <term>karma</term>, it leads to a form of <term>determinism</term>; but in our practical life, which is a product of extremely complicated factors, the doctrine of <term>karma</term> allows us all kinds of possibilities and all chances of development. We thus escape the mechanical conception of life; we are saved from the despair of predetermination, though this is true to a great extent;

<vocab>karma, kalpa, Nirvâna, determinism, enlightenment</vocab>

<summary>The author describes karma as an organic, self-multiplying power rather than a mechanical process, comparing its growth and influence to a mustard seed and sympathetic resonances. This perspective allows for the possibility of repentance and spiritual progress, even for the most wicked, thereby avoiding the hopelessness of strict fatalism.</summary>

Though the curse of evil karma may sometimes hang upon us very heavily, there is no reason to bury our aspirations altogether in the grave; on the contrary, let us bear it bravely and perform all acts of goodness to destroy the last remnant of evil and to mature the stock of good karma.

->*The Maturing of Good Stock (kuçalamûla) and the Accumulation of Merits (punyaskandha).*<-

One of the most significant facts, which we cannot well afford to ignore while discussing the doctrine of karma, is the Buddhist belief that Çâkyamuni reached his supreme Buddhahood only after a long practice of the six virtues of perfection (*pâramitâs*) through many rebirths. This belief constitutes the very foundation of the ethics of Buddhism and has all-important bearings on the doctrine of karma.

The doctrine of karma, ethically considered, is this: Sentient beings can attain perfection not by an intervention from on high, but through long, steady, unflinching personal efforts toward the actualization <insert>v</insert> of ideals; or, in other words, toward the maturing of good stock (*kuçalamûla*) and the accumulation of merits (*punyaskandha*). This can be accomplished only through the karma of good deeds untiringly practiced throughout many generations. Each single act of goodness we perform today is recorded with

On the contrary, every deed of ill-will, every thought of self-aggrandizement, every word of impurity, and every assertion of egoism is a drawback to the perfection of humanity. To speak concretely, the Buddha represents the crystallization in the historical person of Çâkyamuni of all the good karma that was accumulated in innumerable <term>kalpas</term> previous to his birth. And if Devadatta, as legend describes him, was really the enemy of the Buddha, he symbolizes in himself the evil karma that was being stored up alongside the good deeds of all Buddhas. Later Buddhism has thus elaborated to represent in these two historical figures the concrete results of good and evil karma, and tries to show in what direction its followers should exercise their spiritual energy.

The doctrine of karma is, therefore, really the theory of evolution and heredity as it works in our moral field. As Walt Whitman fitly sings, in every one of us, “converging objects of the universe” are perpetually flowing, and through every one of us is “afflatus surging and surging — the current and index.” And these converging objects and this afflatus are no more than our karma, which is interwoven in our being and which has been maturing from the very beginning of consciousness upon the earth. Each generation either retards or furthers the maturing of karma and transmits to the succeeding one its stock, either impaired or augmented. Those who are blind enough not to

Such are the suggestions which the hidden parts of the mind bring forth, and in such a subtle manner they are born.”

### *Immortality*

We read in the *Milinda-pañha*:

> “Your Majesty, it is as if a man were to go up to the upper story of a house with a lamp and eat there; and the lamp, while burning, set fire to the thatch; and the thatch, while burning, set fire to the house; and the house, while burning, set fire to the village; and the people of the village were to seize him and say, ‘Why, man, did you set fire to the village?’ and he were to say, ‘I did not set fire to the village. The fire of the lamp by whose light I ate was a different one from the fire that set fire to the village’;

<summary>The author explains that individual spiritual development benefits all of humanity and quotes Walt Whitman and the Milinda-pañha to illustrate the continuity of karma and identity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the *Milinda-pañha* started on the previous page, where the transition of fire from a lamp to a village is used as an analogy for the continuity of karma between lives.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>

and they, quarreling, were to come to you. Whose cause, Your Majesty, would you support?”

“That of the people of the village, Reverend Sir,” etc.

“And why?”

“Because, in spite of what the man might say, the later fire sprang from the former.”

“In exactly the same way, Your Majesty, although the name and form that is born into the next existence is different from the name and form that ends at death, it has nonetheless sprung from it. Therefore, one is not freed from one’s evil deeds.”

<margin>✓</margin>The above is the Buddhist notion of individual identity and its conservation, which denies the immortality of the ego-soul and upholds that of karma.

Another good way, perhaps, of illustrating this doctrine is to follow the growth and perpetuation of the seed. The seed is, in fact, a concrete expression of karma. When a plant reaches a certain stage of development, it blooms and bears fruit. This fruit contains within it a latent energy which, under favorable conditions, grows into a mature plant of its own kind. The new plant now repeats the processes that its predecessors went through, and an eternal perpetuation of the plant is attained. The life of an individual plant cannot be permanent; according to its inherent nature, it is destined to be cut short at some point in its course. But this is not the case with the current of an everlasting vitality that has been running in the plant ever since the beginning of the world. Because this current is not individual in its nature and stands above the vicissi-

tudes that take place in the life of particular plants. It may not be manifested in its kinetic form all the time, but potentially it is ever-present in the being of the seed. Changes are simply a matter of form and do not interfere with the current of life in the plant, which is preserved in the universe as the energy of vegetation.

This energy of vegetation is what is manifested in a mature plant, what makes it blossom in the springtime, what goes to seed, what lies apparently dormant in the seeds, and what revives them to sprout among favorable surroundings. This energy of vegetation, this mysterious force, when stated in Buddhist terms, is nothing else than the vegetative expression of karma. In the biological world, this constitutes the law of heredity, the transmission of acquired characteristics, or other laws which might be discovered by biologists. And it is when this force manifests itself in the moral realm of human affairs that karma obtains its proper significance as the law of moral causation.

Now, there are several forms of transmission by means of which the karma of a person, a people, a nation, or a race is able to perpetuate itself for eternity. A few of them are described below.

One may be called genealogical, or perhaps biological. Suppose there are descendants of an illustrious family, some of whose ancestors distinguished themselves by bravery, benevolence, intelligence, or by some other praiseworthy deeds or faculties. These

People are as a rule respected by their neighbors as if their ancestral spirits were transmitted through generations and were still lingering among their biological successors. Some individuals in the lineage might have even been below the normal level in their intellect and morals, but this fact does not altogether cancel the possibility and belief that others of their family might some day develop the abilities possessed by their forefathers—dormant as they appear now—through the inspiration they could receive from the noble examples of the past. The respect they enjoy and the possibility of inspiration they may have are all the work of the karma generated by their ancestors. The creator or creators of that noble karma are all gone now; their bones have long returned to their elements, their individual souls are no more, and their specific individual personalities are things of the past; but their karma is still here and as fresh as it was on the day of its creation, and it will remain so until the end of time. If some of them, on the other hand, left a dark record behind them, that evil karma will tenaciously cling to the history of the family, and the descendants will have to suffer the curse as long as its vitality is maintained, no matter how innocent they themselves are.

Here, one important thing I wish to note is the mysterious way in which evil karma works. Evil does not always generate only evil; it very frequently turns out to be a condition, if not a cause, which will induce a moral being to overcome it with his

Thus, everything in this world seems to become merely a means for the final realization of Good. Buddhists attribute this spiritual phenomenon to the virtues of the <term>upâya</term> (expediency) of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> or <term>Amitâbha Buddha</term>. ¹

To return to the subject: No further illustration is needed to show that everything said about the family is also true of the race, the tribe, clan, nation, or any other form of community. of karma are at work in this actual life. One act provokes another, and that a third, and so on to eternity, without ever losing the chain of karmic causation.

Next, we come to a form of karma which might be called historical. By this I mean that a man's karma can be immortalized by certain historical objects, such as buildings, literary works, works of art, implements, or instruments. In fact, almost any object, human or natural, which—no matter how insignificant in itself—is associated with the memory of a great man, carries his karma and transmits it to posterity.

Everyone is familiar with the fact that all literary work embodies within itself the author's soul and spirit, and that posterity can feel his living presence in the thoughts and sentiments expressed there. Whenever the reader draws his inspiration from the work and actualizes it in action, the author and the reader—though physically separate and living in different times—must be said to be spiritually feeling the pulsation of one and the same heart. 

And the same thing is true of works of art. When we enter a gallery decorated with the noble works of Grecian or Roman artists, we feel as if we were breathing right in the midst of these art-loving people, and the same impressions they received seem to reawaken in us. We forget, as they did, the reality of our particular existence; we are unconsciously raised above it, and our imagination is filled with things that are not earthly. What a mysterious power it is!—the

<summary>This page defines "historical karma," explaining how a person's influence is immortalized and transmitted to future generations through literature, art, and physical objects. It describes how these creative works bridge time, allowing people in the present to spiritually connect with the creators of the past.</summary>

power by which those inanimate objects carry us away to a world of ideals! What a mysterious power it is that reawakens the spirits of bygone artists on a sheet of canvas or in a piece of marble! Indeed, it was not entirely without truth that primitive or ignorant people intuitively believed in the spiritual power of idols. What they failed to grasp was the distinction between the subjective presence of a spirit and its objective reality. As far as their religious feeling, rather than their critical intellect, was concerned, they were perfectly justified in believing in idolatry. All in all, these facts unmistakably testify to the Buddhist doctrine of the immortality of karma. A chord of karma touched by mortals of bygone ages still vibrates in their works, and that vibration is transmitted with its full force to sympathetic souls down to the present day.

Architectural creations support the doctrine of karma with no less force than works of art and literature. Just as the highest bricks on an Egyptian pyramid would fall to the ground with the same amount of energy that was required to raise them up in the times of the Pharaohs; just as a burning piece of coal in a furnace, which was dug from the heart of the earth, emits the same quantity of heat that it absorbed from the sun some hundred thousand years ago; even so every insignificant bit of rock, brick, or cement we may find among the ruins of Babylonian palaces, Indian <term>topes</term>, Persian kiosks, Egyptian obelisks, or Roman pantheons, is filled with the same spirit and soul that motivated

It is because the karma of those ancient peoples still breathes its immortality in those architectural productions and sends its sympathetic waves out to the beholders. When we are thus convinced of the truth of the immortality of karma, we can truly exclaim with Christians,

> "O death, where is your sting? O grave, where is your victory?"

It is hardly necessary to provide any further illustration to establish the historical significance of the doctrine of karma. All scientific apparatus and instruments are undying witnesses to the genius of their inventors. All industrial machines and agricultural implements most concretely testify to the immortality of karma created by their builders, in exact proportion to how much they benefit the general welfare and progress of humanity. The instruments, machines, or implements may be superseded by later and better ones, and possibly altogether forgotten by succeeding generations, but this does not negate the fact that the

Whatever form the karma of a person may take on its way down to posterity, it is always there where its inspiration is felt. Even in an article of most trivial significance—even in a piece of rag or in a slip of time-worn paper—only let there be an association with the memory of the deceased, and an unutterable feeling imperceptibly creeps into the heart of the beholder. If the deceased were known for their saintliness or righteousness, this would be an opportunity for our inspiration and moral elevation, according to how our own karma at that moment is constituted.

We now come to see more closely the spiritual meaning of karmic activity. Any intelligent reader could infer from what has been said above what an important bearing the Buddhist doctrine of karma has on our moral and spiritual life. The following remarks, however, will greatly help them to understand the full extent of the doctrine and to pass an impartial judgment on its merits.

Here, if not anywhere else, the characteristic difference between Buddhism and Christianity regarding their conception of soul-activity looms up most conspicuously. Christianity, if I understand it rightly, conceives our soul-phenomena as the work of an

individual ego-entity, which keeps itself mysteriously hidden somewhere within the body. To Christians, the soul is a metaphysical being, and its incarnation in the flesh is imprisonment. It groans for emancipation; it craves the celestial abode where, after bodily death, it can enjoy all the blessings due to its naked existence. It finds the nectar of immortality in Heaven and in the presence of God the Father and Christ the Son, and not in the perpetuation of karma in this universe. The soul of the wicked, on the other hand, is eternally damned, if it is even admitted that they have any soul. As soon as it is liberated from its bodily imprisonment, it is hurled into the infernal fire, and is there consumed, suffering unspeakable agony. Christianity, therefore, does not believe in the transmigration or reincarnation of a soul. A soul once departed from the flesh never returns to it; it is either living an eternal life in Heaven or suffering an instant annihilation in Hell. This is the necessary conclusion from their premises of an individual concrete ego-soul.

Buddhism, however, does not teach the metaphysical existence of the soul. All our mental and spiritual experiences, it declares, are due to the operations of karma, which inherits its efficiency from its previous "seeds of activity" (karmabîja), and which has brought the five skandhas into the present state of coordination. The present karma, while in force, generates in turn the "seeds of activity" which, under favorable conditions, grow to maturity again. Therefore, as long
