<header>CHAPTER VIII.</header>

As the force of karma is thus successively generated, there are the five <term>skandhas</term> constantly coming into existence and working coordinately as a person. Karma-reproduction, so to speak, achieved in this manner, is the Buddhist conception of the transmigration of a soul.

A Japanese national hero, General Kusunoki Masashige, who was an orthodox Buddhist, is said to have uttered the following words when he fell on the battlefield: “I will be reborn seven times yet and finish fulfilling my duties for the Imperial House.” And he did not utter these words to no purpose. Because even today, after the passage of more than seven hundred years, his spirit is still alive among his countrymen, and indeed his bronze statue on horseback is solemnly guarding the Japanese Imperial palace. He was reborn more than seven times and will be reborn as long as the Japanese as a nation exist on earth. This constant rebirth or reincarnation means nothing more nor less than the immortality of karma. Buddha says: “You disciples, after my death, take those moral precepts and doctrines which were taught to you in place of my own person, for I live in them.” To live in karma, and not as an ego-entity, is the Buddhist conception of immortality. Therefore, Buddhists will perfectly agree with the sentiment expressed by a noted modern poet in these lines:

> “We live in deeds, not years; in thoughts, not in breaths:
> In feelings, not in figures on a dial,
> We should count time by heart-throbs. He most lives
> Who thinks most, feels the noblest, acts the best.”

Some may like to call this kind of immortality unsatisfactory, and impetuously demand that the ego-soul, instead of the mysterious force of karma, should be made immortal, as it is more tangible and better appreciated by the masses. The Buddhist response to such a demand would be: "If their intellectual and moral insight is not developed enough to see truth in the theory of karma, well, we shall let them adhere as long as they please to their crude, primitive faith and rest content with it." Even the Buddha could not make children find pleasure in abstract metaphysical problems, regardless of whatever truth and genuine spiritual consolation there might be in them. What their hearts seek are toys, fairy tales, and parables. Therefore, a motto of Buddhism is: "Minister to the patients according to their wants and conditions." We cannot make a plant grow even an inch higher by artificially pulling its roots; we must only wait until it is ready for development. Unless a child becomes a man, we must not expect him to put away childish things.

The conclusion that can be drawn from the above is obvious: if we desire immortality, let there be the maturing of good karma and the cleansing of the heart from the contamination of evil. In good karma, we are made to live eternally, but in an evil one we are doomed—not only ourselves, but everyone who follows our steps on the path of evil. Karma is always generative; therefore, good karma is infinite bliss, and an evil one is an eternal curse. It was for this reason that at the appearance of the Buddha in <term>Jambudvîpa</term>

## CHAPTER VIII.

Heaven and earth resounded with the joyous acclamation of gods and men. It was a signal triumph for the cause of goodness. The ideal of moral perfection found a concrete example in the person of Çâkyamuni. It showed how the stock of good karma—accumulated and matured from the beginning of consciousness on earth—could be crystallized in one person and brought to realization even in this world of woes. The Buddha, therefore, was the culmination of all the good karma previously stored up by his spiritual ancestors. And he was, at the same time, the starting point for the generation of new karma, because his moral "seeds of activity," which were generated during his lifetime, have been scattered liberally wherever his virtues and teachings could be spread. That is, his karma-seeds have been sown in the souls of all sentient beings. Every one of these seeds, which are infinite in number, will become a new center of moral activity. In proportion to how strong it grows and begins to bear fruit, it destroys the seeds of evil-doers. Good karma is a combined shield and sword; while it protects itself, it destroys all that is against it. Therefore, good karma is not only statically immortal, but it is dynamically so; that is to say, its immortality is not a mere absence of birth and death, but a constant positive increase in its moral efficiency.

Pious Buddhists believe that every time the Buddha's name is invoked with a heart free from evil thoughts, he enters directly into the soul and becomes an integral part of one's being. This does not mean, however, that

the Buddha’s ego-substratum—which might otherwise have been enjoying immortal spiritual bliss in the presence of an anthropomorphic God—descends to Earth at the invocation of his name and, in that capacity, renders whatever help the supplicant needs. It means, on the other hand, that the Buddhist awakens within their personal karma that which constituted Buddhahood in the Buddha and nourishes it to maturity. That which constitutes Buddhahood is not the personal ego of the Buddha, but his karma. Every chemical element, whenever it is freed from a compound, never fails to generate the heat which it absorbed at the time of its combination with other elements; and this takes place no matter how long ago that combination occurred. It is the same with the karma-seed of the Buddha. It might have been in the barren soil of a sinful heart and, being deeply buried there for many years, might have been forgotten altogether by the owner. But, sooner or later, it will never fail to grow under favorable conditions and generate what it gained from the Buddha at the beginning of the world. And this regeneration will not be merely chemical, but predominantly biological; for it is the law which governs the immortality of karma.

# PRACTICAL BUDDHISM.

## CHAPTER IX.

### THE <term>DHARMAKÂYA</term>.

We have considered the doctrine of <term>Suchness</term> (*<term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term>*) under "Speculative Buddhism," where it appeared much too abstract to be of any practical use to our earthly life. The theory as such did not seem to have any immediate bearing on our religious consciousness. The fact is, it must undergo some practical modification before it fully satisfies our spiritual needs. Just as there is no concrete figure in this world that is a perfect example of mathematical exactness—since everything here must be perceived through our more or less distorted physical organs—the same is true for pure reason. However perfect it may be in itself, it must appear to us more or less modified as it passes through our emotional and intellectual perspectives. This modification of pure reason, however, is necessary from the human point of view because mere abstraction is empty of content, lifeless, and has no value for our practical lives. Furthermore, our religious cravings will not be satisfied with empty concepts that lack vitality.

We may sometimes ignore the claims of reason

He argues that pure reason alone is too lifeless and contentless to address the emotional and intellectual requirements of people.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakâya, Suchness, Bhûtatathâtâ, Practical Buddhism, religious consciousness, pure reason, spiritual needs</keywords>

If it ever harbored some flagrant contradictions in the name of faith, it was because its ever-pressing demands had to be met, even at the expense of reason. The truth is: the religious consciousness first of all demands fact, and when it attains that, it is of little consequence to it whether or not its intellectual interpretation is logically tenable. If, on the other hand, logic were all-important and demanded the first consideration, and the sentiment had to follow its trail without a murmur, our life would surely lose its savory aspect and turn tasteless; our existence would become void, the world would be a mere succession of meaningless events, and what remained would be nothing else than devastation, barrenness, and universal misery. The truth is, in this life the will predominates and the intellect serves; this explains the fact that while all existing religions, on the one hand, display some logical inaccuracy and, on the other hand, a mechanical explanation of the world is gaining ground more and more, religion is still playing an important part everywhere in our practical life. Abstraction is good for exercising the intellect, but when it is a question of life and death, we must have something more substantial and of more vitality than theorizing. It may not be a mathematically exact

What practical transformations, then, must the doctrine of Suchness undergo in order to meet religious demands?

### *God.*

Buddhism does not use the word God. The word is rather offensive to most of its followers, especially when it is closely associated in common minds with the idea of a creator who produced the world out of nothing, caused the downfall of humanity, and, touched by a pang of remorse, sent down his only son to save the depraved. But, because of this, Buddhism must not be judged as a form of atheism which endorses an agnostic, materialistic interpretation of the universe. Far from it. Buddhism explicitly acknowledges the presence in the world of a reality which transcends the limitations of phenomena, but which is nevertheless present everywhere and manifests itself in its full glory, and in which we live and move and have our being.

God, or the religious object of Buddhism, is generally called Dharmakâya-Buddha and occasionally Vairocana-Buddha or Vairocana-Dharmakâya-Buddha; still another name for it is Amitâbha-Buddha or Amitâyur-Buddha—the latter two being mostly used by the followers of the Sukhâvatî sect of Japan and China.

<vocab>Buddhism, Suchness, God, Dharmakâya-Buddha, Vairocana-Buddha, Amitâbha-Buddha, Amitâyur-Buddha, Sukhâvatî sect</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that for the doctrine of Suchness to satisfy religious needs, it must be a living faith rather than a mere theory. It further clarifies that while Buddhism avoids the term "God" to distance itself from creator-deity myths, it acknowledges a transcendent and immanent reality known by names such as Dharmakâya-Buddha.</summary>

## CHAPTER IX.

Again, very frequently we find Çâkyamuni, the Buddha, and the Tathâgata stripped of his historical personality and identified with the highest truth and reality. These, however, by no means exhaust the legion of names invented by the fertile imagination of Buddhists for their object of reverence, as called forth by their various spiritual needs.

->*Dharmakâya.*<-

Western scholars usually translate Dharmakâya as "Body of the Law," meaning by the "Law" the doctrine set forth by Çâkyamuni the Buddha. It is said that when the Buddha was preparing himself to enter into eternal Nirvâna, he commanded his disciples to revere the Dharma, or the religion taught by him, as his own person, because a man continues to live in the work, deeds, and words he leaves behind. Thus, Dharmakâya came to be understood by Western scholars as meaning the person of the Buddha incarnated in his religion. This interpretation of the term is not very accurate, however, and leads to some very serious misinterpretations concerning the fundamental doctrines of Mahâyânism. Historically, the "Body of the Law" as the Buddha incarnate might have been the meaning of Dharmakâya, as we can infer from the occasional use of the term in some Hînayâna texts. But as it is used by Eastern Buddhists, it has acquired an entirely new significance, having nothing to do with the body of religious teachings established by the Buddha.

<summary>This page explains how the Buddha is identified with absolute reality and critiques the common Western translation of "Dharmakâya" as "Body of the Law." The author argues that in Mahâyâna Buddhism, the term has a spiritual significance that transcends the historical Buddha's specific teachings.</summary>

<page-num>221</page-num>

## CHAPTER IX.

This transformation in the conception of Dharmakâya has been brought about by the different interpretation that the term Dharma received from the Mahayanists. Dharma is a very significant word and covers a wide range of meanings. It comes from the root *dhṛ*, which means “to hold,” “to carry,” or “to bear.” The original sense of dharma was “that which carries, bears, or supports,” and then it came to signify “that which forms the norm or regulates the course of things”—that is, “law,” “institution,” “rule,” or “doctrine.” Later, it came to mean “duty,” “justice,” “virtue,” “moral merit,” “character,” “attribute,” “essential quality,” “substance,” “that which exists,” “reality,” “being,” and so on. 

The English equivalent most frequently used for dharma by scholars of the East is "law" or "doctrine." This may be acceptable as far as the Pâli texts are concerned; but when we wish to apply this interpretation to Mahâyâna terms, such as Dharmadhâtu, Dharmakâya, Dharmalakṣa, Dharmaloka, and others, we are placed in an awkward position and are at a loss for how to grasp the meaning of those terms. There are passages in Mahâyâna literature in which the entire significance of the text depends upon how we understand the word dharma. It may even be said that one of the many reasons why Christian students of Buddhism so frequently fail to recognize the importance of Mahayanism is due to their misinterpretation of dharma. Max Mueller, therefore, rightly remarks in his introduction to an English translation of the *Vajracchedîka Sûtra* when he says: “If we

<vocab>Dharmakâya, Dharma, Mahâyâna, Pâli, Dharmadhâtu, Dharmalakṣa, Dharmaloka, Max Mueller, Vajracchedîka Sûtra, dhṛ</vocab>

<summary>This page analyzes the etymology and diverse meanings of the term "Dharma," explaining that it encompasses everything from "law" to "reality." The author argues that misinterpreting this word leads to a failure to understand the core depth of Mahayana Buddhism.</summary>
<keywords>Dharma, Dharmakâya, Mahayana, etymology, Pâli, translation, Max Mueller, Buddhist philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the translation and meaning of the term "Dharma," specifically critiquing the narrow translation of the word as "law" and explaining its broader significance in Mahayana Buddhism as part of the concept of "Dharmakâya."</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

were always to translate dharma as law, it seems to me that the whole direction of our treatise would become unintelligible.” Not only that particular text of Mahâyânism, but its entire literature would become completely incomprehensible.

In Mahâyânism, Dharma means in many cases “thing,” “substance,” “being,” or “reality,” in both its particular and its general sense, although it is also frequently used in the sense of law or doctrine. Kâya may be translated as “body,” not in the sense of personality, but in that of a system, unity, and organized form. Dharmakâya, the combination of dharma and kâya, thus means the organized totality of things or the principle of cosmic unity, though not as a purely philosophical concept, but as an object of religious consciousness. Throughout this work, however, the original Sanskrit form will be retained in preference to any English equivalents that have been used until now; for Dharmakâya conveys a peculiar religious flavor to the minds of Eastern Buddhists, which suffers considerably when translated as either God, the All, or some abstract philosophical terms.

### *Dharmakâya as a Religious Object.*

As previously stated, the Dharmakâya is not a product of philosophical reflection and is not exactly equivalent to Suchness; it has a religious significance as the object of religious consciousness. The Dharmakâya is a soul, a willing and knowing being, one that is

It further describes the Dharmakâya as the organized totality of reality and an object of religious consciousness rather than a mere philosophical abstraction.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Dharmakâya, moving from its definition as a system or unity to its characterization as a living, religious object of consciousness.</meta>

# CHAPTER IX

will and intelligence, thought and action. As understood by the Mahâyânists, it is not an abstract metaphysical principle like Suchness; rather, it is a living spirit that manifests itself in nature as well as in thought. The universe, as an expression of this spirit, is not a meaningless display of blind forces, nor is it an arena for the struggle of various mechanical powers. Furthermore, Buddhists ascribe to the Dharmakâya innumerable merits and virtues and an absolutely perfect intelligence, and they make it an inexhaustible fountainhead of love and compassion. It is in this way that the Dharmakâya finally assumes a totally different aspect from a mere metaphysical principle, which is cold and lifeless.

The *Avatamsaka Sûtra* gives some comprehensive statements concerning the nature of the Dharmakâya as follows:

> “The Dharmakâya, though manifesting itself in the triple world, is free from impurities and desires. It unfolds itself here, there, and everywhere, responding to the call of karma. It is not an individual reality, nor is it a false existence, but is universal and pure. It comes from nowhere and goes nowhere; it does not assert itself, nor is it subject to annihilation. It is forever serene and eternal. It is the One, devoid of all determinations. This Body of Dharma has no boundary and no location, but is embodied in all bodies. Its freedom or spontaneity is incomprehensible; its spiritual presence in physical things is incomprehensible. All forms of corporeality are involved within it, and it is able to create all things. Assuming any concrete

<summary>The author describes the Dharmakâya as a living, compassionate spirit rather than a cold metaphysical concept, citing the Avatamsaka Sûtra to illustrate its eternal, universal, and creative nature. It is presented as a spiritual presence that responds to karma and exists within all physical forms.</summary>

There is no place in the universe where this Body does not prevail. The universe comes into being, but this Body remains forever. It is free from all opposites and contraries, yet it works in all things to lead them to Nirvâna.”

### *More Detailed Characterization.*

The description above gives us a general, concise view of what the Dharmakâya is, but let me quote the following more detailed description of it, so that we may more clearly and definitely see into the characteristically Buddhist conception of the highest being. <gloss>1</gloss>

> “O you, sons of Buddha! The Tathâgata <gloss>2</gloss> is not a particular dharma, nor a particular form of activity, nor does it have a particular body, nor does it dwell in a particular place, nor is its work of salvation confined to one particular people. On the contrary, it involves within itself infinite dharmas, infinite activities, infinite bodies, and infinite spaces, and it works universally for the salvation of all things.
>
> “O you, sons of Buddha! It is like space. Space <gloss>3</gloss> contains within itself all material existences and all the vacuums that exist between them. Again, it establishes—”

# CHAPTER IX.

*Body of the <term>Tathâgata</term> universally emits the rays of the Light of Intelligence.*

“O you, sons of <term>Buddha</term>! When the day breaks, the rising sun shines first on the peaks of all the higher mountains, then on those of high mountains, and finally all over the plains and fields; but the sunlight itself does not have this thought: ‘I will shine first on all the highest mountains and then, gradually ascending higher and higher, shine on the plains and fields.’ The reason why one part receives the sunlight earlier than another is simply because there is a gradation of height on the surface of the earth.

“O you, sons of <term>Buddha</term>! It is just so with the <term>Tathâgata</term>, who possesses innumerable and immeasurable suns of universal intelligence. The innumerable rays of the <term>Light of Intelligence</term>, emanating everlastingly from the spiritual Body of the <term>Tathâgata</term>, will first fall on the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> and <term>Mahâsattvas</term>, who are the highest peaks among mankind, then on the <term>Nidânabuddhas</term>, then on the <term>Çrâvakas</term>, then on those beings who are endowed with a definitely good character, as they will each, according to their own capacity, unhesitatingly embrace the <term>doctrine of deliverance</term>, and finally on all common mortals whose character may be either indefinite or definitely bad, providing them with those conditions which will prove beneficial in their future births. But the <term>Light of Intelligence</term> emanating from the <term>Tathâgata</term> does not have this thought: ‘I will first shine on the Bodhi-

### *Dharmakâya and Individual Beings.*

From these statements, it is evident that the <term>Dharmakâya</term>—or the Body of the <term>Tathâgata</term>, or the Body of Intelligence, whatever it may be called—is not a mere philosophical abstraction. It does not stand aloof from this world of birth and death, of joy and sorrow, calmly contemplating the folly of mankind; instead, it is a spiritual existence which is “absolutely one, is real and true, and forms the reason for the existence of all beings; it transcends all modes of <term>upâya</term>, is free from desires and struggles [or compulsion], and stands outside the limits of our finite understanding.” $^1$ It is

$^1$ Asanga's *General Treatise on Mahâyânism*. (*Mahâyâna samparigraha*).

<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>
It is also evident that the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, though itself free from ignorance (*avidyâ*), passion (*kleça*), and desire (*tṛṣṇâ*), is revealed in the finite and fragmentary consciousness of human beings. Thus, we can say in a sense that “this body of mine is the <term>Dharmakâya</term>”—though not absolutely. We can also say in a generalized form that “the body of all beings is the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, and the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is the body of all beings”—though in the latter, it is only imperfectly and fractionally realized. As we thus partake of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> within ourselves, we all are ultimately destined to attain <term>Buddhahood</term> when human intelligence, <term>Bodhi</term>, is perfectly identified with, or absorbed in, that of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, and when our earthly life becomes the realization of the will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.

### *The Dharmakâya as Love.*

Here, an important consideration forces itself upon us: the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is not only an intelligent mind but a loving heart. It is not only a god of rigor who does not allow a hair’s breadth of deviation from the law of <term>karma</term>, but also an incarnation of mercy that is constantly laboring to develop the most insignificant merit into a field yielding rich harvests. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> relentlessly punishes wrong and does not permit the exhaustion of <term>karma</term> without sufficient reason; and yet its hands are always directing our life toward the actualization

Every good we do is absorbed into the universal stock of merits, which is nothing more or less than the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. Every act of lovingkindness we practice is conceived in the womb of <term>Tathâgata</term>, and being nourished and matured there, it is again brought out to this world of <term>karma</term> to bear its fruit. Therefore, no life walks on earth with aimless feet; no chaff is thrown into the unquenchable fire. Every existence, whether great or insignificant, is a reflection of the glory of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> and as such is worthy of its all-embracing love.

### *Later <term>Mahâyânists’</term> view of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.*

The text above has been quoted almost exclusively from the so-called <term>sûtra</term> literature of <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhism. This literature is distinguished from the school's other religious and philosophical treatises because the <term>sûtras</term> are considered to be the accounts of Buddha himself as recorded by his immediate disciples. <note>1. This is by no means the case, for some of the <term>Mahâyâna sûtras</term> are undoubtedly the productions of much later writers than the immediate followers of the Buddha, although of course it is very likely that some of the most important <term>Mahâyâna</term> canonical books were compiled within a few hundred years after the Nirvana of the Master.</note> Let us now see, by way of further clarification, what views were held concerning the <term>Dharmakâya</term> by such writers as <term>Asanga</term>, <term>Vasubandhu</term>, and others.

We read the following statement in the *General Treatise on <term>Mahâyânism</term>* by <term>Asanga</term> and <term>Vasubandhu</term>:

> “When the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> think of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, how should they picture it to themselves?
> “Briefly stated, they will think of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> by picturing to themselves its seven characteristics, which constitute the faultless virtues and essential

<summary>This page explores how later Mahâyâna scholars like Asanga and Vasubandhu defined the Dharmakâya, moving beyond purely devotional sutra descriptions toward a systematic philosophical analysis. It also notes that many sutras were likely composed centuries after the Buddha's lifetime.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakâya, Mahâyâna Buddhism, Asanga, Vasubandhu, Bodhisattvas, sûtra literature, Buddhahood, Nirvana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of seven characteristics used to contemplate the <term>Dharmakâya</term> and begins <term>Asanga</term>'s description of its five forms of saving activity.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
CHAPTER IX.

...functions of the <term>Kâya</term>. (1) Think of the free, unrivaled, and unimpeded activity of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which is manifested in all beings; (2) Think of the eternity of all perfect virtues in the <term>Dharmakâya</term>; (3) Think of its absolute freedom from all prejudice, whether intellectual or emotional; (4) Think of those spontaneous activities that uninterruptedly emanate from the will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>; (5) Think of the inexhaustible wealth, both spiritual and physical, stored in the Body of the <term>Dharma</term>; (6) Think of its intellectual purity, which has no stain of one-sidedness; (7) Think of the earthly works achieved for the salvation of all beings by the <term>Tathâgatas</term>, who are reflections of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.”

Regarding the activity of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which is shown in the work of salvation of every <term>Buddha</term>, <term>Asanga</term> enumerates five forms of operation: (1) It is shown in his power of removing evils that may befall us in the course of life, though the <term>Buddha</term> is unable to cure any physical defects that we may have, such as blindness, deafness, mental aberration, etc. (2) It is shown in his irresistible spiritual domination over all evil-doers, who, as base as they are, cannot help but do some good if they ever come into the presence of the <term>Buddha</term>. (3) It is shown in his power of destroying various unnatural and irrational methods of salvation that are practiced by followers of asceticism, hedonism, or <term>Ishvaraism</term>. (4) It is shown in his power of curing those diseased minds that believe in the reality, permanency, and indivisibility of the ego-soul—that is, in the <term>pudgalavâda</term>. (5) It is shown in his inspiring

<summary>This page concludes a list of seven contemplative characteristics of the Dharmakâya and begins detailing Asanga's five ways the Buddha acts for the salvation of beings. These operations include influencing evil-doers toward good and correcting false beliefs about the self and religious asceticism.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakâya, Asanga, Buddha, salvation, Tathâgatas, pudgalavâda, Mahayana Buddhism, Ishvaraism</keywords>

<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

influence over those <term>Bodhisattvas</term> who have not yet attained the stage of immovability, as well as over those <term>Crâvakas</term> whose faith and character are still in a state of vacillation.

### *The Freedom of the Dharmakâya.*

Those spiritual influences of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> over all beings through the enlightened mind of a <term>Buddha</term>, which we have seen stated by <term>Asanga</term> above, are fraught with religious significance. According to the <term>Buddhist</term> view, those spiritual powers everlastingly emanating from the Body of <term>Dharma</term> have no trace of human elaboration or constrained effort; instead, they are a spontaneous overflow from its immanent necessity or, as I take it, from its free will. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> does not make any conscious, struggling efforts to shower its innumerable merits, benefits, and blessings upon all sentient creatures. If there were any trace of elaboration in it, that would mean a struggle within itself of diverse tendencies, with one trying to gain ascendancy over another. And it is apparent that any struggle and its necessary ally, compulsion, are incompatible with our conception of the highest religious reality. Absolute spontaneity and perfect freedom are among those necessary attributes which our religious consciousness cannot help but ascribe to its object of reverence. Buddhists therefore repeatedly affirm that the activity of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is perfectly free from all effort and coercion, both external and internal. Every act of its creation or salvation
<vocab>Bodhisattvas, Crâvakas, Dharmakâya, Buddha, Asanga, Buddhist view, Dharma, religious reality, spontaneity, freedom, creation, salvation</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the list of the Dharmakâya's influences on different classes of practitioners and explores the concept of its absolute freedom and spontaneity. The author argues that the Dharmakâya's saving activities are not the result of forced effort or internal struggle, but are a natural, free overflow of its divine nature.</summary>

or love emanates from its own free will, unhampered by any struggling exertion that characterizes the actions of mankind. This free will, which is divine, stands in such a striking contrast with our own "free will"—which is human and at best very much limited—and is called by the <term>Buddhists</term> the <term>Dharmakâya</term>'s "<term>Purvapranidhânabala</term>."<note>"<term>Purvapranidhânabala</term>" is frequently translated as "the power of original (or primitive) prayer." Literally, *pûrva* means "former," "original," or "primitive"; *pranidhâna* means "desire," "vow," or "prayer"; and *bala* means "power." As far as a literary translation is concerned, "power of original prayer" seems to be the sense of the original Sanskrit. But when we speak of the primitive prayers of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> or <term>Tathâgata</term>, how should we understand it? Does prayer make any sense in this connection? The <term>Dharmakâya</term> can, by its own free will, manifest in any form of existence and finish its work in whatever way it deems best. There is no need for it to utter any prayer in the agony of a struggle to accomplish its ends. There is in the universe no force whatever working against it so powerfully as to make it cry for help; and there cannot be any struggle or agony in the activity of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. The term "prayer," therefore, is completely misleading and inaccurate, and it involves us in a grave error that tends to contradict the general <term>Buddhist</term> conception of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. This point will receive further consideration later.</note>

<margin>✓</margin>
As the <term>Dharmakâya</term> works of its own accord, it does not seek any recompense for its deeds; and it is evident that every act of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is always for the best welfare of its creatures, for they are its manifestations and it must know what they need. We do not have to ask for our "daily bread,"

<header>CHAPTER IX.</header>

nor do we have to praise or eulogize its virtues to seek its special grace, nor is there any necessity for us to offer prayer or supplication to the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. Consider the lilies of the field, which neither toil nor spin—and I might add—which do not ask for any favoritism from above; yet are they not arrayed even better than Solomon in all his glory? The <term>Dharmakâya</term> shines in its august magnificence everywhere there is life—indeed, even where there is death. We are all living in the midst of it and yet, strange to say, just as "the fish does not know the presence of water around itself," and also as "the mountaineers do not recognize the mountains among which they hunt," even so we do not know where that power comes from, whose work is made manifest in us, and where it finally leads us. In spite of this profound ignorance, we truly feel that we are here, and because of this, we rest supremely contented. For we believe that all this is accomplished through the mysterious and miraculous will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, who does all excellent works and seeks no recompense whatever.

### *The Will of the Dharmakâya.*

Briefly speaking, the <term>Dharmakâya</term> assumes three essential aspects as reflected in our religious consciousness: first, it is intelligence (<term>prajñâ</term>); second, it is love (<term>karunâ</term>); and third, it is the will (<term>pranidhânabala</term>). We know that it is intelligence from the declaration that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> directs the course of the universe, not blindly but rationally; we know again that it is love because it embraces all

beings with fatherly tenderness; <note>1. "I am the father of all beings, and they are my children." (*The <term>Avatamsaka</term>, the <term>Pundarîka</term>, etc.*)</note> and finally we must assume that it is a will, because the <term>Dharmakâya</term> has firmly set down its aim of activity so that good shall be the final goal of all evil in the universe. Without the will, love and intelligence will not be realized; without love, the will and intelligence will lose their impulse; without intelligence, love and the will will be irrational. In fact, the three are coordinates and constitute the oneness of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>; and by oneness I mean the absolute, and not the numerical, unity of all these three things in the being of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, for intelligence and love and the will are differentiated as such only in our human, finite consciousness.

Some Buddhists may not agree entirely with the view explained here. They may declare: "We conform to your view when you say the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is intelligence and love, as this is expressly stated in the <term>sûtras</term> and <term>çâstras</term>; but we do not see how it could be made a will. Indeed, the Scriptures say that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is in possession of the <term>Pranidhânabala</term>, but this <term>bala</term> or power is not necessarily the will; it is the power of prayers or intense vows. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> actually made solemn vows, and their spiritual energy abiding in the world of particulars works out its original plan and makes possible the universal salvation of all creatures."

It is quite true that the word <term>pranidhânabala</term> means

literally "the power of original prayers." But this literal translation totally ignores its inner significance, without which the nature of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> would become incomprehensible. We admit that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> knows no higher existence by which it is conditioned, nor does it have any fragmentary, limited consciousness like that of a human being, nor does it have any intrinsic need that forces it to appeal to something other than itself. It is, therefore, completely nonsensical to speak of its prayer, whether "original" or borrowed, as some Buddhists are inclined to think. On the other hand, we are perfectly justified in saying that whatever is done by the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is done by its own free will, independent of all the external factors that might affect it.

But I can guess the reason why they speak of the prayers of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> instead of its will. Here we have an instance of emotional expression. The fervor of intense religious sentiment often carries us beyond the limits of the intellect, landing us in a region full of mysteries and contradictions. It anthropomorphizes everything beyond the proper measure of reasoning and ascribes all earthly human feelings and passions to an object which a well-balanced mind requires to be above all forms of human helplessness. The Buddhists, especially those of the <term>Sukhâvatî</term> sect,¹ recognize the exist—

## CHAPTER X.

### THE DOCTRINE OF TRIKÂYA.

### (BUDDHIST THEORY OF TRINITY.)

->*The Human and the Super-human Buddha.*<-

One of the most remarkable differences between the <term>Pâli</term> and the <term>Sanskrit</term>—that is, between the <term>Hînayâna</term> and the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhist literature—is in the manner of introducing the characters or persons who take principal parts in the narratives. In the former, sermons are delivered by the <term>Buddha</term> as a rule in such natural and plain language that it makes the reader feel the presence of the teacher, who is fatherly-hearted and philosophically serene; while in the latter, we generally have a mysterious, transcendent figure, more celestial than human, surrounded and worshipped by beings of all kinds—human, celestial, and even demonic—and this mystical central character performs some supernatural feats that might well be narrated by an intensely poetical mind.

In the <term>Pâli</term> scriptures, the texts as a rule open with the formula, "Thus it was heard by me" (<term>Evam me sutam</term>), then relate the events, if any, which induced the <term>Buddha</term> to deliver them, and finally lead the reader to the main subjects which are generally written in

<summary>This chapter introduction highlights the contrast between the Pâli (Hînayâna) tradition, which depicts the Buddha as a serene and human teacher, and the Sanskrit (Mahâyâna) tradition, which portrays him as a mystical, supernatural figure.</summary>
<keywords>Trikâya, Buddhist Trinity, Hînayâna, Mahâyâna, Pâli, Sanskrit, Buddha, Evam me sutam</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Hînayâna and Mahâyâna literature, moving from the simple style of the former to the dramatic, supernatural introductions typical of the latter, specifically citing the Mahâvaipulya-Tathâgatagarbha Sûtra.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>

lucid style. Their opening or introductory matter is very simple, and we do not notice anything extraordinary in its further development. But with the <term>Mahâyâna</term> texts, it is quite different. Here, as soon as the curtain rises with the standard formula, “*<term>Evam mayâ çrutam</term>*,” we have a majestic prologue represented dramatically—or rather, grotesquely—which prepares the mind of the audience for the succeeding scenes, in which some of the boldest religious and philosophical proclamations are brought forth. Reading this introductory part alone will astound the reader with its massive grandeur, and they may conclude without much hesitation that what follows must be extraordinary and may even be nonsensical.

As is clearly shown here, the Buddha in the <term>Mahâyâna</term> scriptures is not an ordinary human being walking in a sensory world; he is altogether different from that son of <term>Suddhodana</term>, who gave up the royal life, wandered in the wilderness, and after six years of profound meditation and penance discovered the <term>Fourfold Noble Truth</term> and the <term>Twelve Chains of Dependence</term>; and we cannot help but think that the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddha is the fictitious creation of an intensely poetic mind. Let it be so. But the question which engages us now is, “How did the Buddhists come to relegate the human Buddha to oblivion, as it were, and assign a mysterious being in his place invested with all possible or sometimes impossible majesty and supernatural power?” This question, which marks the rise of <term>Mahâyâna Buddhism</term>, brings us to the doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term>—which in a sense corresponds to the Christian theory of the trinity.

According to this doctrine, the Buddhists assume a triple existence of the <term>Tathâgata</term>; that is, they conceive of the <term>Tathâgata</term> as manifesting himself in three different forms of existence: the <term>Body of Transformation</term>, the <term>Body of Bliss</term>, and the <term>Body of Dharma</term>. Though they are conceived as three, they are in fact all the manifestations of one <term>Dharmakâya</term>—the <term>Dharmakâya</term> that revealed itself in the historical <term>Çâkyamuni</term>

<summary>This page explores the distinction between the historical, human Buddha and the mystical, supernatural Buddha described in Mahâyâna scriptures. It introduces the doctrine of Trikâya, which posits that the Buddha exists in three forms: the Body of Transformation, the Body of Bliss, and the Body of Dharma.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER X.</header>

<term>Buddha</term> as a <term>Body of Transformation</term>, and in the <term>Mahâyâna</term>, <term>Buddha</term> as a <term>Body of Bliss</term>. However differently they may appear from a human point of view, they are nothing but the expression of one eternal truth, in which all things have their *raison d’être*.

### *A Historical View.*

At present, we do not possess any historical documents that shed light on how early this doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term>, or the Buddhist trinity conception, became firmly established among Northern Buddhists and found its way, in an already finished form, into the <term>Mahâyâna</term> scriptures. As far as we know, it was <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>, the first <term>Mahâyâna</term> philosopher, who incorporated this conception into his *Discourse on the Awakening of Faith in the Mahâyâna* as early as the first century B.C. This work, as the author declares, is a sort of synopsis of the <term>Mahâyâna</term> teachings, elucidating their principal features as taught by the <term>Buddha</term> in his various <term>sûtras</term>. It is not an original work that expounds the individual views of <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> concerning Buddhism. He wrote the book in a concise and comprehensive form so that later generations—who, being remote from the <term>Buddha</term>, could not have the privilege of being inspired by his august presence—might read it with concentration and synthetically grasp the full significance of many lengthy and voluminous <term>sûtras</term>. Therefore, in the *Awakening of Faith*, we are supposed

<summary>This page explores the historical origins of the Trikâya doctrine, noting its inclusion in the "Awakening of Faith" by the philosopher Açvaghoṣa. The text describes this work as a concise synthesis of complex Mahâyâna teachings intended for generations who lived after the Buddha's time.</summary>
<keywords>Trikâya, Mahâyâna, Açvaghoṣa, Awakening of Faith, Body of Transformation, Body of Bliss, Buddhist trinity, Sûtras</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Açvaghoṣa's role in Buddhist history, specifically arguing that his work was a compilation of existing Mahâyâna doctrines rather than a presentation of new ideas.</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X.</header>

not to find any <term>Mahâyâna</term> doctrines that were not already taught by the <term>Buddha</term> and incorporated in the <term>sûtras</term>. Everything <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> discusses in his work must be considered merely a summary of the doctrines which were not only formulated but firmly established as the <term>Mahâyâna</term> faith long before him. His work is simply that of a recorder. He carefully examined all the <term>Mahâyâna</term> scriptures that existed before his time and faithfully collected all the principal teachings of <term>Mahâyânism</term> that were scattered throughout them. His merit lies in his compilation and systematization.

This being the case, we must assume that all the doctrines found in <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> that are distinct from those usually considered <term>Hînayânistic</term> are the teachings developed by Buddhists from the time of the <term>Buddha</term>’s death down to the time of <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>. But since <term>Açvaghoṣa</term> apparently believes all these doctrines to be the <term>Buddha</term>'s own and raises no doubt regarding their later origin—even if they were of a later date—we must assume again that these doctrines were fully developed long before <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>'s time. If our calculation is correct that he lived in the first century B.C., the <term>Mahâyâna</term> faith must be said to have been formulated at least two hundred years before his era—taking this as the presumed time required for the formulation and dogmatic establishment of a doctrine. This calculation places the development of the <term>Mahâyâna</term> faith during the first century after the <term>Buddha</term>; and we know it was during this time that so many schools and divisions—

<summary>Açvaghoṣa is presented as a systematizer and recorder of earlier Mahâyâna traditions rather than an innovator, implying the faith was established long before the first century B.C. Did <term>Mahâyânism</term> come out of this turmoil of contention? Did it boldly raise itself from this chaos and claim to have solved all the questions and doubts that agitated the minds of Buddhists after the <term>Nirvâna</term>? We do not know for certain anything concerning the chronology of the development of Buddhist philosophy and dogmas in India, at least before <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>; but, as far as our Chinese Buddhist literature records, we must conclude that this was most probably the case.

To give our readers a glimpse of the state of things that were taking place in those early days of <term>Buddhism</term> in India, I will quote some passages from <term>Vasumitra</term>’s *Discourse on the Points of Controversy by the Different Schools of Buddhism*—the work once referred to at the beginning of this book. Though they were further divided into a number of smaller sections and their views became so complex and intermixed that some of the <term>Elders</term> shared similar views with the <term>Great Council School</term> and vice-versa, we can still fairly distinguish one from the other and describe the essential peculiarities of each school. (1) According to the <term>School of the Great Council</term>, the <term>Buddha</term>'s personality is transcendental (*lokottara*), and all the <term>Tathâgatas</term> are free from the defilements that might come from material existence (*bhâva-âçrava*). <note>Âçrava literally means “oozing” or “flowing out,” and the Chinese translators rendered it as *lou*, dripping or leaking. Roughly speaking, it is a general name for evils, principally material and sensuous. According to an Indian Buddhist scholar, Âçrava has a threefold meaning: (1) “keeping,” because it keeps all sentient beings in the whirlpool of birth and death; (2) “flowing,” because it causes all sentient beings to run in the stream of birth and death; (3) “leaking” or “oozing,” because it allows such evils as avarice, anger, lust, etc., to leak out from the six sense organs like an ulcer which discharges blood and filthy substances. The cause of Âçrava is a blind will, and its result is birth and death. Specifically, Bhâvâçrava is one of the three Âçravas, which are (1) kâmâçrava, (2) vidyâçrava, and (3) bhâvâçrava. The first is egotistic desires, the second is ignorance, and the third is the material existence which we must suffer on account of our previous karma.</note> For in the <term>Buddha</term>, all evil passions—both hereditary and acquired—were eternally uprooted, and his presence on earth was absolutely spotless. (*The Vibhâşa*, CLXXIII.) Challenging this view, the <term>Elders</term> held that the <term>Buddha</term>'s personality was not free from <term>Bhâvâçrava</term>, though his mind was fully enlightened. His physical existence was the product of blind love veiled with ignorance and tangled with attachment. If this were not so, the <term>Buddha</term>'s appearance would not have awakened an impure affection in the heart of a maiden, ill-will in the heart of a highwayman, stupidity in the mind of an ascetic, and arrogance in that of a haughty <term>Brahman</term>. These incidents which

(2) The <term>Great Council School</term> insists that every word spoken by a <term>Tathâgata</term> has a religious and spiritual meaning and is intended for the spiritual improvement of his fellow beings. They believe that his single utterances are interpreted in various ways by his audience according to each person's temperament, but always for their spiritual benefit; they also hold that every instruction given by the <term>Buddha</term> is rational and perfect. In opposition to these views, the <term>Elders</term> believe that the <term>Buddha</term> occasionally said things that had nothing to do with the <term>enlightenment</term> of others. They argue that even for the <term>Buddha</term>, some things were beyond his reach; for instance, he could not make every one of his listeners perfectly understand his preaching. Although the <term>Buddha</term> never taught anything irrational or heretical, the <term>Elders</term> claim that not all of his speeches were perfect, as he said some things that were unrelated to reason or orthodoxy.

(3) The physical body (*<term>rûpakâya</term>*) of the <term>Buddha</term> has no limits (*<term>koṭi</term>*); his majestic power is boundless; the life of every <term>Buddha</term> is unlimited. A <term>Buddha</term> never tires and does not know when to rest, always occupying himself with the <term>enlightenment</term> of all sentient beings and the awakening of pure faith in their hearts. Opposing these tendencies of the <term>Great Council School</term> to deify the historical <term>Buddha</term>, the <term>Elders</term> generally insist on the humanity of <term>Buddhahood</term>. <page-num>251</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X. 251</header>

While the Elders agree with the Great Council that the body assumed by the Buddha—resulting from his tireless accumulation of good karma through eons of his successive existences—possesses a wonderful power, both spiritual and material, they do not believe it to be beyond all limitations.

(4) The Great Council School says that sleep is not necessary for the Buddha and that he has no dreams. The Elders admit that the Buddha never dreams, but they deny that he does not need any sleep.

(5) Since the Buddha is always in a state of deep, exalted spiritual meditation, it is not necessary for him to think about what to say when requested to answer certain questions. Although he might appear to inquirers as if he is thoroughly contemplating the problems presented to him for a solution, the Buddha's response is, in fact, immediate and effortless. The Elders, on the other hand, assume the Buddha performs a mental calculation as to how to express his ideas in the way best suited to the understanding of the audience. Indeed, he does not contemplate the problem itself, for everything is transparent to him, but he thinks over the best method of presenting his ideas to his pupils. <note>Our thoughtful readers must have noticed here that the conceptions of the Buddha as held by the Mahâsaṇgika School (Great Council) closely resemble those of Mahâyâna Buddhism. Although we are still unable to trace step by step the development of Mahâyânism in India, the hypothesis assumed by most Japanese Buddhist scholars is that the Mahâsaṇgika was Mahâyânistic in tendency.</note>

<page-num>252</page-num>
# CHAPTER X.

Now to return to the doctrine of Dharmakâya and Trikâya. When we consider these controversies as stated above, it is apparent that among many other questions which arose soon after the death of the Buddha Çâkyamuni, there was one which in all probability most agitated the minds of his disciples. I mean the question of the personality of the Buddha. Was he merely a human being like ourselves? If so, how could he reach such a height of moral perfection? Or was he a divine being? But the Buddha himself did not communicate anything to his disciples concerning his divinity, nor did he tell them to accept the Dharma on account of his divine personality, but solely for the sake of truth. 

But for all that, how could the disciples ever eradicate from their hearts the feeling of sacred reverence for their teacher, which was so indelibly engraved there? Whenever they recalled the sermons, anecdotes, or gâthâs of their master, the truth and spirit embodied in them and the author must have become so closely associated that they could not but ask themselves: "What in the Buddha caused him to perceive and declare these solemn, profound truths? What was it that formed in him such a noble, majestic character? What was there in the mind of the Buddha that raised him to such a perfection of intellectual and religious life? How was it possible that, possessed of such exalted moral and spiritual virtues, the Buddha too had to succumb to the law of birth and death that is the lot of common mortals?" Some such questions must have been repeatedly asked before they

### *Who was the Buddha?*

The evidence that these questions were constantly disturbing the minds of the disciples ever since the Master’s entrance into Parinirvâna is found scattered throughout Buddhist texts, both Southern and Northern. The regret of his immediate followers that they did not ask the Buddha to prolong his earthly life—while the Buddha told them that he could do so if he wished—and their lamentation over the remains of the Blessed One, “How soon the Light of the World has passed away!” <note>The Mahâparinibbâna sutta.</note> — these utterances may be considered the first drops foretelling the showers of doubt and speculation regarding his personality.

According to the *Suvarna Prabhâ Sûtra*, <note>There are three Chinese translations of this sutra: the first, by Dharmarakṣa during the first two decades of the fifth century AD; the second, by Paramârtha of the Liang dynasty, who came to China in AD 546 and died in AD 569; and the third, by I-tsing of the Tang dynasty, who returned from his Indian pilgrimage in the year 695 and translated this sutra in AD 703. The last is the only complete Chinese translation of the *Suvarnâ Prabhâ*. A part of the original Sanskrit text recovered in Nepal was published by the Buddhist Text Society of India in 1898. Nanjo, Nos. 126, 127, 130.</note> a Bodhisattva named Ruciraketu was greatly troubled by the doubt of why Çâkyamuni Tathâgata had such a short life, terminating at only eighty years of age. He

Spiritually, they would never perish because the ideas that moved them and made them prominent in the history of humanity are born of truth. In this sense, every person who possesses worthy thoughts is immortal, while souls made of worthless things are certainly doomed to annihilation. But the masses are not satisfied with this kind of immortality. They must have something more tangible, more sensory, and more individual. The notion of the bodily resurrection of Christ is a fine illustration of this truth. His virtues and merits, which were accumulated throughout innumerable <term>kalpas</term>, could not come to nothing so abruptly. ### *The <term>Trikâya</term> as Explained in the <term>Suvarna Prabhâ</term>.*

What then is the <term>Trikâya</term>, or triple body of the <term>Tathâgata</term>? It consists of (1) <term>Nirmâna Kâya</term>, the Body of Transformation; (2) <term>Sambhoga Kâya</term>, the Body of Bliss; and (3) <term>Dharma Kâya</term>, the Body of Dharma. If we draw a parallel between the Buddhist and the Christian <term>trinity</term>, the Body of Transformation may be considered to correspond to Christ in the flesh, the Body of Bliss to either Christ in glory or the Holy Spirit, and the <term>Dharmakâya</term> to the Godhead.

Let us again quote from the *<term>Suvarna Prabhâ</term>*, in which (<term>I-tsing</term>'s translation, chapter III) we find the following statements concerning the doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term>.

> “The <term>Tathâgata</term>, when he was still at the stage of discipline, practiced various deeds of morality for the sake of sentient beings. This practice finally attained perfection, reached maturity, and by virtue of its merits, he acquired a wonderful spiritual power. This power enabled him to respond to the thoughts, deeds, and lives of sentient beings. He thoroughly understood them and never missed the right opportunity

> “The Body has infinite forms. The form has infinite attributes. The attribute has infinite excellences. And the accompanying fruition—that is, the region where they are destined to be born <note>by their previous karma</note>—also has infinite merits and ornamentations. Manifesting itself everywhere, the Body of Bliss is infinite, boundless, limitless, and uninterrupted <note>in its activity</note>, which comes directly from the Mind <note>Dharmakâya</note>.”

He was essentially a manifestation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, and as such, we ordinary people also partake somewhat of him. But the masses, unless favored by good <term>karma</term> accumulated in the past, are generally under the spell of ignorance. They do not see the glory of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> in its perfect purity, shining in the lilies of the field and sung by the birds of the air. They are blindly groping in the dark wilderness; they are seeking in vain; they are knocking wildly. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> responds to the needs of these people by assuming an earthly form as a human <term>Buddha</term>.

### *Revelation in All Stages of Culture.*

*In passing*, let us remark that it is in this sense that <term>Christ</term> is also conceived by Buddhists as a manifestation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> in a human form. He is a <term>Buddha</term> and, as such, is not essentially different from <term>Çâkyamuni</term>. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> revealed itself as <term>Çâkyamuni</term> to the Indian mind because that was in harmony with its needs. The <term>Dharmakâya</term> appeared in the person of <term>Christ</term> on the Semitic stage because it suited their tastes best in this way. The doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term>, however, goes even further and declares that demons, animal gods, ancestor worship, nature worship, and so forth, are all due to the activity and revelation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> responding to the spiritual needs of barbarous

Buddhists believe that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> never does things that are against the spiritual welfare of its creatures, and that whatever it does is for their best interests at that moment of revelation, no matter how they understand the nature of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. The Great Lord of Dharma never throws a pearl before swine, for he knows the animal’s needs are for things more substantial. He does not reveal himself in an exalted spiritual form to people whose hearts are not yet capable of grasping anything beyond what is grossly material. Since they understand animal gods better than a metaphysical or highly abstracted being, let them have them and derive all possible blessings and benefits through their worship. But as soon as they become dissatisfied with animal or human-fashioned gods, there must not be a moment’s hesitation to let them have exactly what their enlightened understanding can comprehend. <note>Cf. 1 Corinthians 13:11. “When I was a child, I spoke as a child, I understood as a child, I thought as a child, but when I became a man, I put away childish things.” This point of our ever-ascending spiritual progress is well illustrated in the *<term>Saddharma-pundarîka Sûtra</term>*. See Chapters 2, 3, 4, 5, and 11. The following passage quoted from chapter 2, page 49 (Kern’s translation) will give a fairly adequate view concerning the diversity of means and unity of purpose as explained here: “Those highest of men have, all of them, revealed most holy laws by means of illustrations, reasons, and arguments, with many hundreds of proofs of skillfulness (*<term>upâyakauçalya</term>*). And all of them have manifested but one vehicle and introduced but one on earth; by one vehicle have they led to full ripeness inconceivably many thousands of <term>kotis</term> of beings.”</note>

The <term>Mahâyânists</term> now argue that the reason why <term>Çâkyamuni</term> entered into <term>Parinirvana</term> when he considered his worldly career to be over is that, by this resignation to the law of birth and death, he wished to exemplify in himself the impermanence of worldly life and the folly of clinging to it as a final reality. As for his <term>Dharmakâya</term>, it has an eternal life; it was never born, and it will never perish. When called by the spiritual needs of the <term>Bodhisattvas</term>, it will cast off the garb of absoluteness and preach in the form of a <term>Sambhogakâya</term> “never-ceasing sermons which run like a stream forever and always.” It will be evident from this that Buddhists are ready to consider all religious or moral leaders of mankind, whatever their nationality, as the Body of Transformation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. ### *The Sambhogakâya.*

One peculiar point in the doctrine of Trikâya, which modern minds find rather difficult to comprehend, is the conception of the Sambhogakâya, or the Body of Bliss. We can understand the relation between the Dharmakâya and Nirmânakâya, the latter being similar to the notion of God incarnate or to that of Avatara. Since the Dharmakâya does not exist outside the triple world but within it as the *raison d'être* of its existence, all beings must be considered a partial manifestation of it. In this sense, Buddhists sometimes call themselves Bodhisattvas—that is, beings of intelligence—because intelligence (*Bodhi*) is the psychological aspect of the Dharmakâya as realized in sentient beings. But the conception of Sambhogakâya is altogether too mysterious to be understood by a limited consciousness. This fact becomes more apparent when we are told that the Sambhogakâya, or Body of Bliss, is a corporeal existence while simultaneously filling the universe, and that there are two forms of the Body of Bliss: one for self-enjoyment and the other as a sort of religious object for the Bodhisattvas.

That the Body of Bliss is corporeal and yet infinite has already been shown by the quotations from the *Suvarna Prabhâ* and *Açvaghosa* on the preceding pages. For further confirmation of this point, no less an authority than Asanga and Vasubandhu will be referred to here.

In *A Comprehensive Treatise on the Mahâyana* and

<summary>This page introduces the Sambhogakâya (Body of Bliss), explaining its mysterious nature as both a corporeal and infinite existence that serves as an object of devotion for Bodhisattvas. It distinguishes this concept from the Dharmakâya and Nirmânakâya while citing historical authorities like Asanga and Vasubandhu.</summary>

<keywords>Trikâya, Sambhogakâya, Body of Bliss, Dharmakâya, Nirmânakâya, Bodhisattva, Bodhi, Asanga, Vasubandhu, Mahâyana Buddhism</keywords>

<page-num>264</page-num>

In its commentary, the author <term>Asanga</term> and the commentator <term>Vasubandhu</term> endeavor to prove why the <term>Body of Bliss</term> cannot be the *raison d'être* of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, instead of vice versa; and in this connection they argue that (1) the <term>Body of Bliss</term> consists of the five <term>Skandhas</term>, that is, of material form (<term>rûpa</term>), sensation (<term>vedanâ</term>), ideas (<term>samjñâ</term>), deeds (<term>sanskâra</term>), and consciousness (<term>vijñâna</term>); (2) it is subject to particularization; (3) it reveals different virtues and characters according to the desires of <term>Bodhisattvas</term>; (4) even to the same individual it appears differently at different times; (5) when it manifests itself simultaneously before an assembly of <term>Bodhisattvas</term> of diverse characters and qualifications, it at once assumes diverse forms, in order to satisfy their infinitely diversified inclinations; (6) it is a creation of the <term>Âlayavijñâna</term>, or All-conserving Mind.

These six peculiarities of the <term>Body of Bliss</term> as enumerated by <term>Asanga</term> and <term>Vasubandhu</term> make it indeed entirely dependent on the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, but they do not place us in any better position to penetrate into the deep mystery of its nature. Its supernatural incomprehensibility remains the same forever. In a certain sense, however, the <term>Body of Bliss</term> may be considered to correspond to the Christian idea of an angel. Supernaturalness and luminosity are the two characters possessed by both, but angels are merely messengers of God communicating the latter's will to human beings. When they reveal themselves to a specially favored person, it is not of their own

The text also compares the supernatural qualities of the Body of Bliss to Christian angels, though it notes a distinction in their roles as messengers.</summary>

### A Mere Subjective Existence.

Judging from all these characterizations, the most plausible conclusion that suggests itself to modern skeptical minds is that the <term>Sambhogakâya</term> must be a mere creation of an intelligent, finite mind. This mind is intently focused on reaching the highest reality, but being unable to grasp the object in its absoluteness due to its own limitations, the finite mind creates all its ideals in its own image as a spiritual-material being. Logically, this is a contradiction, but religiously, it is an object deserving of veneration and worship. And this being is nothing more than the <term>Body of Bliss</term>. <note>1 May I venture to say that the conception of God held by most Christians is a <term>Body of Bliss</term> rather than the <term>Dharmakâya</term> itself? In some respects, their God is quite spiritual, but in others, he is thought of as a concrete material being like ourselves. It seems to me that the human soul is ever struggling to free itself from this paradox, though without any apparent success, while the masses are not intellectual and reflective enough to become aware of this eternal contradiction which is too deeply buried in their minds.</note> It lies halfway between the pure being of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> and the earthly form of the <term>Nirmânakâya</term>, the <term>Body of Transformation</term>. It does not belong to either, but shares something of both. It is, in a sense, spiritual

The author suggests this "Body of Bliss" is a necessary creation of the finite mind to grasp the divine, comparing it to the Christian concept of God.</summary>

When the human soul thirsts for a pure being or an absolute that cannot be understood in a tangible form, it creates a hybrid, an imitation, or a reflection, and tries to be satisfied with it, just as a little girl has her innate and not yet fully developed maternal instinct satisfied by tenderly embracing and nursing a doll—an inanimate imitation of a real living baby. And the <term>Mahayana Buddhists</term> seem to have made the most of this childish human nature. They produced as many <term>sutras</term> as their spiritual yearnings demanded, quite regardless of historical facts, and made the Body of Bliss of the <term>Tathagata</term> the author of all these works. For if the <term>Dharmakâya</term> of the <term>Tathagata</term> never entered into <term>Parinirvana</term>, why then could he not deliver sermons and recite <term>gathas</term> as often as beings of intelligence (<term>Bodhisattvas</term>) felt their needs? But when all three of these are brought together, an image [of the sun or the moon in the water] is produced. So it is with <term>Suchness</term> and Knowledge of <term>Suchness</term>. "And, again, just as a boundlessly expanding water-mirror reflects the images of <term>âkâsa</term> (void space) in all different ways through the medium of light, while space itself is void of all particular marks, so the <term>Dharmakâya</term> reflects its images individually in the receiving minds of believers, and this by virtue of <term>Spontaneous Will</term>. The <term>Will</term> creates the <term>Body of Transformation</term> as well as the <term>Body of Bliss</term> in all their possible aspects, while the original, the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, does not suffer the slightest change on this account."

According to this, it is evident that whenever our spiritual needs become sufficiently intense there is a response from the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, and that this response is not always uniform, as the receiving minds show different degrees of intellectual and spiritual development. If we call this communion between sentient souls and the <term>Dharmakâya</term> an inspiration, all the phenomena that flow out of a fullness of heart and reflect purity of soul should be called "works of inspiration"; and in this sense the <term>Mahâyânists</term> consider their scriptures as emanating directly from the fountainhead of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.

### *Recapitulation.*

To sum up, the <term>Buddha</term> in the <term>Pâli</term> scriptures was a human being, though he is occasionally credited with having achieved supernatural and superhuman things. His historical career began with the abandonment of a royal life, followed by wandering in the wilderness, a long and earnest meditation on the great problems of birth and death, and his final enlightenment under the <term>Bodhi tree</term>. Then came his fifty-year religious journey along the valleys of the <term>Ganges</term>, the establishment of the religious system known as <term>Buddhism</term>, and finally his eternal entrance into the “<term>Parinirvâna</term> that leaves nothing behind” (*<term>anupadhiçesanirvâna</term>*). As far as plain historical facts are concerned, these seem to account for the entire life of <term>Çâkyamuni</term> on earth.

But the deep reverence felt by his disciples could not be satisfied with the prosaic humanity of their master, and they made him something more than a mortal soul. So even the <term>Pâli</term> tradition gives him a supernatural life in addition to his earthly one. He is supposed to have been a <term>Bodhisattva</term> in the <term>Tuṣita heaven</term> before entering the womb of <term>Mâyâdevî</term>. The honor of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term> was granted to him because of his deeds of self-sacrifice, which were praised throughout his innumerable past incarnations. While he was walk-

<summary>This page summarizes the historical life of the Buddha from his renunciation to his death, while explaining how his disciples' reverence led to the attribution of supernatural origins and past lives.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha, Çâkyamuni, Pâli scriptures, Bodhi tree, Parinirvâna, Bodhisattva, Tuṣita heaven, Mâyâdevî, history of Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Buddha's supernatural attributes and the theological evolution of his physical form through the lens of the Trikâya doctrine, transitioning from the historical narrative to the mystical attributes assigned to him by later traditions.</meta>

<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER X.</header>

<margin>?</margin>
ing among us in the flesh, he was glorified with the thirty-two major and eighty minor excellent characteristics of a great man. <note>For a description of these marks see the *Dharmasangraha*, pp. 53 ff. A process of mystifying or deifying the person of the <term>Buddha</term> seems to have been occurring immediately after the death of the Master; and the <term>Mahâyânist</term> conception of <term>Nirmânakâya</term> and <term>Sambhogakâya</term> is merely the culmination of this process. Southern Buddhists, who are sometimes supposed to represent a more "primitive" form of <term>Buddhism</term>, describe—just as much as <term>Mahâyânism</term> does—the thirty-two major and eighty minor excellent physical marks of a great man as having been possessed by <term>Çâkyamuni</term> (for instance, see the *Milindapañha*, *S.B.E.* Vol. XXXV. p. 116). But any person with common sense will at once see the absurdity of representing any human being with those physical peculiarities. This seems to have inspired more rational <term>Mahâyânists</term> to abandon the traditional way of portraying the human <term>Buddha</term> with those mysterious signs. They transferred them through the doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term> to the characterization of the <term>Sambhogakâya</term> <term>Buddha</term>—that is, to the <term>Buddha</term> enjoying the fruit of his virtuous earthly life in a celestial abode. The <term>Buddha</term> who walked in the flesh as the son of King <term>Suddhodana</term> was, however, no more than an ordinary human being like ourselves, because he appeared to us in a form of <term>Nirmânakâya</term> (that is, as a Body of Transformation), lacking any such physical peculiarities known as the thirty-two or eighty <term>lakṣaṇas</term>. The so-called Southern Buddhists, however, seem to have overlooked how ridiculous it is to attribute these fantastic signs to the human <term>Buddha</term>. This fact explains why, as soon as the memory of the personal disciples concerning the <term>Buddha</term>'s person vanished among later followers, intense speculation and resourceful imagination were constantly exercised until the various schools settled the question each in its own way.</note> But he was not the first <term>Buddha</term> that walked on earth to teach the <term>Dharma</term>, for there were already seven <term>Buddha</term>s before him, nor was he the last one that would appear among us, for

<summary>This page discusses the thirty-two major and eighty minor physical marks of a great man attributed to the Buddha, critiquing the literal interpretation of these signs as absurd. It explains how the Mahâyâna doctrine of Trikâya assigns these supernatural marks to the celestial Sambhogakâya while regarding the historical Buddha as a human Body of Transformation (Nirmânakâya).</summary>
<keywords>Buddha, Dharma, Nirmânakâya, Sambhogakâya, lakṣaṇas, Trikâya, Mahâyânism, Southern Buddhism, Suddhodana, Dharmasangraha</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the Buddha’s physical characteristics and transitions into the theological shift from the historical Pâli tradition to the Mahayana concept of the Dharmakâya.</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>
<header>### CHAPTER X.</header>

A <term>Bodhisattva</term> named <term>Maitreya</term> is now in heaven, making preparations to attain <term>Buddhahood</term> in the future. But the <term>Pâli</term> writers stopped here; they did not venture to speculate further on the nature of <term>Buddhahood</term>. Their religious yearnings did not push their imaginations to any higher level. They recited simple <term>sûtras</term> or <term>gâthâs</term>, observed the <term>çîlas</term> (moral precepts) as strictly and literally as they could, and believed the spirit of their Master remained alive in these instructions—setting aside the personality of the <term>Tathâgata</term>.

But at the same time, there was another group of the Buddha’s disciples whose religious and intellectual inclinations were not of the same type as those of their fellow believers. Because of this, a simple faith in the Buddha as being present only in his teachings did not fully satisfy them. They perhaps reasoned in this way: “If there were seven Buddhas before the arrival of the <term>Great Muni of Çakya</term> and there is one more yet to come, we must ask: from where did they derive their authority and knowledge to preach? Why is it that there cannot be more Buddhas, or that they do not come to us much more often? If they were human beings like ourselves, why can we ourselves not be Buddhas?” These questions, when followed to their logical conclusion, naturally led them to the theory of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>—the idea that all past Buddhas, those yet to come, and even we ordinary mortals made of clay and doomed to die soon, owe the <term>raison d’être</term> of our existence to the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which alone is immortal within us.

<summary>This page explains the transition from the Pâli tradition's focus on the historical Buddha and his teachings to the Mahayana inquiry into the universal nature of Buddhahood. This intellectual shift led to the doctrine of the Dharmakâya as the immortal essence underlying all Buddhas and sentient beings.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Maitreya, Buddhahood, Pâli tradition, sûtras, çîlas, Tathâgata, Dharmakâya, Great Muni of Çakya, Mahayana philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>273</page-num>
<header># CHAPTER X.</header>

...as well as in Buddhas. The first religious effort we have to make is, therefore, to recognize this archetype of all Buddhas and all beings. But the <term>Dharmakâya</term> as such is too abstract for the average mind to become the object of its religious consciousness; so they personified, or rather materialized, it. In other words, they idealized <term>Çâkyamuni</term>, endowed him not only with the physical signs (*<term>lakṣas</term>*) of greatness as in the <term>Pâli</term> scriptures, but with those of celestial transfiguration, and called him a Body of Bliss of the <term>Tathâgata</term>; while the historical human Buddha was called a Body of Transformation and all sentient beings <term>Bodhisattvas</term>, that is, beings of intelligence destined to become Buddhas.

This idealized Buddha, or, what is the same thing, a personified <term>Dharmakâya</term>, according to the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhists, not only revealed himself in the particular person of <term>Siddhârtha Gautama</term> in Central Asia a few thousand years ago, but is revealing himself in all times and all places. There is no specially favored spot on the earth where only the Buddha makes his appearance; from the zenith of <term>Akaniṣṭa</term> heaven down to the bottom of <term>Nâraka</term>, he is manifesting uninterruptedly and unintermittently and is working out his ideas, of which, however, our limited understanding is unable to have an adequate knowledge. The *<term>Avatamsaka Sûtra</term>* (Buddhabhadra’s translation, fas. 45, chap. 34) describes how the Buddha works out his scheme of salvation in all possible ways.
