The practical consequence of this doctrine of <term>Trikâya</term> is apparent; it has increasingly broadened the spirit of tolerance among <term>Buddhists</term>. As the <term>Dharmakâya</term> universally responds to the spiritual needs of all sentient beings in all times and in all places and at any stage of their spiritual development, <term>Buddhists</term> consider all spiritual leaders, whatever their nationality and personality, as the expressions of the one omnipotent <term>Dharmakâya</term>. And as the <term>Dharmakâya</term> always manifests itself for the best interests of sentient creatures, even those doctrines and their authors that are apparently against the teachings of <term>Buddhism</term> are tolerated through the conviction that they are all moving according to the **Spontaneous Will** that pervades everywhere and works all the time. Though, superficially, they may appear as evils, their central and final aim is goodness and harmony which are destined by the Will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> to overcome this world of tribulations and

## CHAPTER XI.

### THE BODHISATTVA.

Next to the conception of Buddha, what is important in Mahâyâna Buddhism is that of the Bodhisattva (intelligence-being) and that which constitutes its essence, <note>The word "Bodhicitta" is circled in pencil.</note> Bodhicitta, or the "intelligence-heart." As stated above, the followers of Mahâyânism do not call themselves Çrâvakas, Pratyekabuddhas, or Arhats, as those of Hînayânism do; instead, they distinguish themselves by the title of Bodhisattva. What this means will be the subject matter of this chapter.

Let us begin with a quotation from the *Saddharma-pundarîka Sûtra*, in which a well-defined distinction between the Çrâvakas, the Pratyekabuddhas, and the Bodhisattvas is given. ¹

#### *The Three Yânas.*

> "Now, Çâriputra, the beings who have become wise have faith in the Tathâgata, the father of the world, and consequently apply themselves to his commandments.
>
> "Among them, there are some who, wishing to follow the dictate of an authoritative voice, apply themselves to the commandment of the Tathâgata to

...acquire the knowledge of the Four Great Truths, for the sake of their own complete Nirvana. These, one may say, are those who, seeking the vehicle of the Çrâvaka, flee from the triple world . . . . .

“Other beings, desiring unconditioned knowledge, self-restraint, and tranquility, apply themselves to the commandment of the Tathâgata to learn to understand the Twelve Chains of Dependence, for the sake of their own complete Nirvana. These, one may say, are those who, seeking the vehicle of the Pratyekabuddha, flee from the triple world . . . . .

“Other beings again, desiring omniscience, Buddha-knowledge, absolute knowledge, and unconditioned knowledge, apply themselves to the commandment of the Tathâgata and learn to understand the knowledge, powers, and conviction of the Tathâgata, for the sake of the common well-being and happiness, out of compassion for the world, for the benefit, well-being, and happiness of the world at large—of both gods and men—and for the sake of the complete Nirvana of all beings. These, one may say, are those who, seeking the Great Vehicle (*Mahâyâna*), flee from the triple world. Therefore, they are called Bodhisattva-mahâsattvas.” . . . . .

This characterization of the Bodhisattvas as distinct from the Çrâvakas and Pratyekabuddhas constitutes one of the most significant features of Mahâyâna Buddhism. Here, the Bodhisattva does not exert himself in religious discipline for the sake of his own well-being, but for the sake of the spiritual benefit of all his fellow creatures. If he wished, he could,

...like the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>, enter into eternal <term>Nirvana</term> that never regresses; he could enjoy the celestial bliss of undisturbed tranquility in which all our worldly tribulations are forever buried; he could seclude himself from the turmoil of the world and, sitting cross-legged in a lonely cave, quietly contemplate the fleeting nature of human interests and the frivolity of earthly affairs, and then self-contentedly await the time of final absorption into the absolute All, just as streams and rivers finally run into one great ocean and become of one taste. But, in spite of all these self-sufficient blessings, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> would not seek his own ease; instead, he would immerse himself in the turmoil of worldly life and devote all his energy to the salvation of the masses of people who, on account of their ignorance and infatuation, are forever transmigrating in the triple world <note>The three realms of existence: the realm of desire, the realm of form, and the formless realm.</note>, without making any progress toward the final goal of humanity.

Alongside this <term>Bodhisattvaic</term> devotion, however, there was another current of religious thought and practice running among the followers of <term>Buddha</term>. By this, I mean the attitude of the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>. Both of them sought peace of mind in asceticism and cold philosophical speculation. Both of them were intently inclined to gain <term>Nirvana</term>, which may be likened to an extinguished fire. It was not their concern to think of the common well-being of all beings, and, therefore, when they attained their own redemption from earthly sins and passions, their religious discipline was completed, and no further attempt was

Moreover, everyone was expected to strive for his own liberation; no matter how unbearable his pain was, others could not do anything to relieve it. Sympathy was of no use, because the results of one's own <term>karma</term>, whether good or evil, could be experienced by oneself alone, nor could even the doer himself avoid them. The difference between the two schools of Buddhism, when expressed most briefly, is this: While one has a very submissive trust in the <term>Buddha</term>, the other seeks to follow his example by putting themselves in his position. ### *Strict Individualism.*

The Buddhism of the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term> is the most thorough application of the individualistic theory of <term>karma</term> to our ethical and religious life. All things done are done by oneself; all things left undone are left undone by oneself. They would say: “Your salvation is exclusively your own business, and whatever sympathy I may have is of no use. All that I can do toward helping you is to let you see intellectually the way to emancipation. If you do not follow it, you have only to suffer the outcome of your folly. I am helpless, despite all my enlightenment and even with my <term>Nirvana</term>, to emancipate you from the misery of perpetual <term>metempsychosis</term>.” <insert>But with the Buddhism of the Mahâyâna Bodhisattvas, the case is entirely different. It is all sympathy, it is all compassion, it is all love.</insert> A <term>Bodhisattva</term> would not seclude himself in the absolute tranquility of <term>Nirvana</term>, simply because he wishes to emancipate his fellow creatures also from the bondage of ignorance and infatuation. Whatever rewards he may get for his own enjoyment as the <term>karma</term> of his virtuous deeds, he would turn them over (*<term>parivarta</term>*) toward the uplifting of the suffering masses. And this self-sacrifice, this unselfish devotion to the welfare of his fellow beings, constitutes the essence of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>. The ideal <term>Bodhisattva</term>, therefore, is thought to be no more than an incarnation of Intelligence and Love, of <term>Prajñâ</term> and <term>Karunâ</term>.

The unbreakable nature of <term>karma</term> seems to be satisfactory

<summary>This page contrasts the individualistic focus of the Çrâvakas and Pratyekabuddhas with the altruistic Mahâyâna ideal, where a Bodhisattva shares their merits to help liberate others. It defines the essence of Bodhisattvahood as the union of intelligence (Prajñâ) and compassion (Karunâ).</summary>

<page-num>283</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XI.</header>

from the intellectual and individualistic standpoint, for the intellect demands a thorough application of logic, and individualism does not allow the transferring of responsibility from one person to another. From this viewpoint, therefore, a rigorous enforcement of the principle of self-emancipation, as demanded by <term>Hînayânism</term>, shows no logical fault; divine grace must be suspended as the curse of <term>karma</term> produced by ignorance tenaciously clings to our soul. But when viewed from the religious side of the question, this inflexibility of <term>karma</term> is more than poor mortals can endure. They want something more elastic and pliable that yields to the supplication of feeling. When individuals are considered nothing but isolated, disconnected atoms between which there is no unifying bond—which is feeling—they are too weak to resist and overcome the ever-threatening force of evil, whose reality cannot be contradicted as long as a world of particulars exists. This religious necessity, felt in our innermost consciousness, may explain why <term>Mahâyâna Buddhism</term> proposed the doctrine of <term>parivarta</term> (turning over), founded on the oneness of the <term>Dharmakâyâ</term>.

### *The Doctrine of Parivarta.*

The doctrine of turning over (<term>parivarta</term>) of one's own merits to others is a great departure from what seems to have been the teaching of "primitive Buddhism." In fact, it is more than a departure; it

This is because, while <term>individualism</term> is a predominant feature in the religious practice of the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>, <term>universalism</term> or supra-individualism—if I may use these terms—is the principle advocated by the <term>Bodhisattvas</term>. The latter believe that all beings, as manifestations of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, are in their <note>A hand-drawn bracket in the left margin marks the text from "essence of one nature" to "emancipation of all beings."</note>essence of one nature; that individual existences are real only as far as subjective ignorance is concerned; and that virtues and merits issuing directly from the <term>Dharmakâya</term> (which is intelligence and love) cannot fail to produce universal benefit and to achieve the final emancipation of all beings. Thus, the religion of the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> proposes to achieve what the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term> thought impossible: that is, the turning over of one's own merits to the service of others.

It is in this spirit that the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> conceive the seriousness of the significance of life; it is in this spirit that, pondering the reason for their existence on earth, they come to the following view of life:

> "All ignorant beings are daily and nightly performing evil deeds in innumerable ways; and, for this reason, their suffering is beyond description. They do not recognize the <term>Tathâgata</term>, they do not listen to his teachings, and they do not pay homage to the congregation of holy men. And this <term>evil karma</term> will surely bring them a heavy crop of misery. This reflection fills the heart of a <term>Bodhisattva</term> with gloomy feelings, which in turn

### *<term>Bodhisattva</term> in "Primitive" Buddhism.*

The concept of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> was not entirely absent in "primitive" Buddhism, though it did not have such a broad significance. All Buddhas were <term>Bodhisattvas</term> in their former lives. The <term>Jataka</term> stories describe in detail the deeds of self-sacrifice they performed and how, through the <term>karma</term> of these merits, they finally attained <term>Buddhahood</term>. <term>Sakyamuni</term> was not the only Buddha; there had already been seven or twenty-four Buddhas before him, and the coming Buddha, known as <term>Maitreya</term>, is believed to be training himself in the <term>Tusita</term> heaven and passing through the stages of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>. One who is destined to be a future Buddha must be extraordinarily gifted in spiritual energy. He must pass through eons of self-discipline and must practice deeds of <term>non-atman</term> with unwavering courage and fortitude throughout innumerable existences.

The following quotation from the <term>Jataka</term> tales will be enough to see what burdensome and demanding conditions were thought necessary by the so-called <term>Hinayanists</term> for a human being to become a fully qualified Buddha. ²

<note>¹ This is a very rough summary of the doctrine known as <term>Parivarta</term> and explained in the *<term>Avatamsaka Sûtra</term>*, fascicles 21–22, where ten forms of <term>Parivarta</term> are distinguished and explained at length.</note>
<note>² Warren's *Buddhism in Translations*, the "Story of Sumedha," pp. 14–15.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the role of the Bodhisattva in early Buddhism, explaining that while the concept existed, it was largely restricted to the previous lives of Buddhas who underwent eons of self-discipline. It highlights that Sakyamuni was one of many Buddhas and introduces the future Buddha, Maitreya.</summary>

> “Among men, it is he, and only he, who is in a fit condition by the achievement of saintship in that same existence, that can successfully make a wish to be a Buddha. Of those in a fit condition, it is only he who makes the wish in the presence of a living Buddha that succeeds in his wish; after the death of a Buddha, a wish made at a relic shrine, or at the foot of a <term>Bo-tree</term>, will not be successful. Of those who make the wish in the presence of a Buddha, it is he and only he who has retired from the world that can successfully make the wish, and not one who is a layman. Of those who have retired from the world, it is only he who possesses the <term>Five High Powers</term> and is master of the <term>Eight Attainments</term> that can successfully make the wish, and no one can do so who lacks these excellences. Even of those who possess these excellences, it is he, and only he, who has such firm resolve that he is ready to sacrifice his life for the Buddhas that can successfully make the wish, but no other. Of those who possess this resolve, it is he, and only he, who has great zeal, determination, strenuousness, and endeavor in striving for the qualities that make a Buddha that is successful. The following comparisons will show the intensity of the zeal. From this it is apparent that not everyone could become a Buddha in "primitive" Buddhism; the highest aspiration that could be cherished by a person was to believe in the teachings of Buddha, to follow the precepts laid down by him, and to attain at most <term>Arhatship</term>. The idea of <term>Arhatship</term>, however, was considered by <term>Mahayanists</term> to be cold, dispassionate, and hard-hearted, for the saint calmly looks upon the sight of the suffering masses; and therefore <term>Arhatship</term> was altogether unsatisfactory as the object of the high religious aspirations of the <term>Bodhisattvas</term>.

The <term>Mahayanists</term> wanted to go even beyond the attainment of <term>Arhatship</term>, however exalted its spirituality may be. They wanted to make every humble soul

<meta>There is a horizontal underlining in the middle of the page on the phrase: "when the iron hands of karma held tight the fate of each individual !"</meta>

a being like Çâkyamuni, they wanted to lavishly distribute the bliss of enlightenment; they wanted to remove all the barriers that were supposed to lie between <term>Buddhahood</term> and common humanity. But how could they do this when the iron hands of <term>karma</term> held tight the fate of each individual! How was it possible for him to identify his being with the ideal of mankind? Perhaps this serious problem could not be easily solved by Buddhists while their memory of the majestic personality of Çâkyamuni was still vivid before their mental eyes. It was probably no easy task for them to overcome the feeling of awe and reverence which was so deeply engraved in their hearts, and to raise themselves to such a height as reached by their Master, even ideally. This was certainly seen as an act of sacrilege. But, as time passes, the personal recollection of the Master naturally wanes and no longer exerts as much influence as their own religious consciousness, which is always fresh and active. Generally speaking, all great historical characters that command the reverence and awe of posterity do so only when their words, acts, or both unravel the deepest secrets of the human heart. And this feeling of awe, reverence, and even worship is not due so much to the great characters themselves as to the worshiper’s own religious consciousness. History passes, but the heart persists. An individual called Çâkyamuni may be forgotten over the course of time, but the sacred chord in the innermost heart struck by him reverberates through eternity. So it was with the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhists,

->*We are all Bodhisattvas.*<-

<margin><image-desc>Pencil drawing of a small circular flourish</image-desc></margin>
Just as Çâkyamuni was a Bodhisattva in his former lives destined to become a Buddha, so we are all Bodhisattvas and even Buddhas in a certain sense, when we understand that all sentient beings, the Buddha included, are one in the Dharmakâya. The Dharmakâya manifests in us as Bodhi, which is the essence of Buddhas as well as Bodhisattvas. This Bodhi can suffer no change in quantity even when the Bodhisattva finally attains the highest human perfection as Çâkyamuni Buddha. In this spirit, therefore, the Buddha exclaimed when he obtained enlightenment, <insert>“It is indeed marvelous that all beings, animate and inanimate, universally partake of the nature of Tathâgatahood.”</insert> <note>The previous sentence is underlined in pencil.</note> The only difference between a Buddha and the ignorant masses is that the latter

the individual curse of karma within the eternal and absolute intelligence of the Dharmakâya. As soon as we come to live in this love and intelligence, individual existences are no hindrance to the transferring (*parivarta*) of one's spiritual merits (*punya*) to the service of others. If we only gain insight into the spirituality of our existence, we are all Bodhisattvas and Buddhas. Let us abandon the selfish thought of entering into a Nirvana that is imagined to extinguish the fire of the heart and leave only the cold ashes of the intellect. Let us have sympathy for all suffering beings and transfer all our merits, however small, to their benefit and happiness. For in this way, we are all made Bodhisattvas. ¹

### *The Buddha's Life.*

This spirit of universal love prevails in all Mahâyâna literature, and the Bodhisattvas are everywhere represented as exercising it with the utmost energy. The Mahâyânists, therefore, could not remain satisfied with a simple, prosaic, and earthly account of Çâkyamuni,

<note>¹ It may be interesting for Christian readers to note in this connection that modern Buddhists do not altogether reject the idea of vicarious atonement, for their religious conviction, as seen here, admits the transferring (*parivarta*) of a Bodhisattva's merits to the spiritual welfare of his fellow creatures. However, they will object to the Christian interpretation that Jesus was sent down to earth by his heavenly father for the special mission of atoning for original sin through the shedding of his innocent blood, as they consider this view to be too childish and materialistic.</note>

The Mahâyânists first placed the Buddha in the Tuṣita heaven before his birth (as the Hīnayānists also did), made him feel pity for the distressed world below, and made him resolve to deliver it from "the ocean of misery that throws up sickness as its foam, tossing with the waves of old age, and rushing with the dreadful surge of death." After his Parinirvana, they had him abide forever on the peak of Mount Vulture, delivering the sermon of immortality to a great assembly of spiritual beings. In this way, they explained the significance of the appearance of Çâkyamuni on earth, which was nothing but a practical demonstration of the "Great Loving Heart" (*mahâkarunâcitta*).

->*The Meaning of Bodhi and Bodhicitta.*<-

<margin>Pencil bracket and checkmark with circular symbol in margin annotating the first half of this paragraph</margin>
What is the meaning of the word “Bodhisattva”? It is a Sanskrit term consisting of two words, “Bodhi” and “sattva.” *Bodhi*, which comes from the root *budh* meaning “to wake,” is generally rendered as “knowledge” or “intelligence.” *Sattva (sat-tva)* literally means “state of being”; thus “existence,” “creature,” or “that which is” are its English equivalents. “Bodhisattva” as one word means “a being of intelligence,” or “a being whose essence is intelligence.” Why the Mahâyânists came to adopt this word in contrast to Çrâvaka is easily understood when we see what special significance they attached to the conception of Bodhi in their philosophy. When Bodhi was used by the Çrâvakas in the simple sense of knowledge, it did not carry any particular meaning. But as soon as it came to express some metaphysical relation to the conception of Dharmakâya, it ceased to be used in its generally accepted sense.

<summary>This page describes Bodhisattvas as pure beings who remain in the world to help others and explains the etymological roots of the term "Bodhisattva" as "a being of intelligence."</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Bodhi, sattva, Sanskrit, cûnyatâ, Mahâyânists, Çrâvaka, Dharmakâya, compassion, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bodhi and its relationship to the Dharmakaya and the human heart, following the definition of a Bodhisattva.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XI.</header>

Bodhi, according to the Mahayanists, is an expression of the Dharmakaya in the human consciousness. Philosophically speaking, Suchness or Bhutatathata is an ontological term, and Dharmakaya, Tathagata, or Buddha bears a religious significance; while all three of these—Bodhi, Bhutatathata, and Dharmakaya—and their synonyms are nothing but different aspects of one and the same reality, refracting through the various defective lenses of a finite intellect.

<note>A horizontal pencil line is drawn through the first phrase of the following paragraph.</note>
Bodhi, though essentially an epistemological term, assumes a psychological sense when it is used in conjunction with *citta*, meaning the heart or soul. *Bodhicitta*, or *Bodhihṛdaya*, which means the same thing, is more generally used than *Bodhi* alone in the Mahayana texts, especially when its religious significance is emphasized above its intellectual one. <margin>*</margin><insert>Bodhicitta, namely the intelligence-heart, is a reflection in the human heart of its religious archetype, the Dharmakaya.</insert> <note>The bracketed sentence is also underlined in pencil.</note>

Bodhicitta, when further expanded, is called *anuttara-samyak-sambodhicitta*, that is, "the intelligence-heart that is supreme and most perfect."

It will be easily understood now that what constitutes the essence of the *Bodhicitta* is the very same thing that makes up the *Dharmakaya*. For the former is nothing but an expression of the latter, though it is only finitely, fragmentarily, and imperfectly realized in us. The *citta* is an image and the *Dharmakaya* is the prototype, yet one is just as real as the other, provided the two are not conceived of dualistically. There is a *Dharmakaya*, there is a human heart, and the former reflects itself

<vocab>Mahayana, Bodhi, Dharmakaya, Bhutatathata, Tathagata, Buddha, Bodhicitta, Bodhihṛdaya, anuttara-samyak-sambodhicitta, Buddhism</vocab>

<summary>This page explains Bodhi and Bodhicitta as expressions of the Dharmakaya within human consciousness, bridging the gap between ontological reality and individual experience. It emphasizes that the human "intelligence-heart" is a finite reflection of the supreme reality, and that the two should not be viewed as separate or dualistic entities.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhi, Bodhicitta, Dharmakaya, Bhutatathata, Mahayana Buddhism, non-dualism, intelligence-heart, epistemology, religious archetype</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the Bodhicitta and the Dharmakaya, specifically addressing how they are reflections of the same reality rather than separate entities.</meta>

<page-num>296</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XI.</header>

in the latter much like the fashion of the lunar reflection in the water: to think in this way is not perfectly correct, because the fundamental teaching of Buddhism is to view all these three concepts—the Dharmakâya, the human heart, and the reflections of the former in the latter—as different forms of one and the same activity.

->*Love and Karunâ.*<-

The Bodhicitta, or intelligence-heart, therefore, like the Dharmakâya, is essentially love and intelligence, or, to use Sanskrit terms, *karunâ* and *prajñâ*. <note>Three horizontal pen marks (underlining) are drawn across the middle of the following paragraph.</note> Here some may object to the use of the term “love” for *karunâ*, perhaps on the grounds that *karunâ* does not exactly correspond to the Christian notion of love, as it carries more of a sense of commiseration. But if we understand love as a sacrifice of the self for the sake of others (and it cannot be more than that), then *karunâ* can correctly be rendered as love, even in the Christian sense. Is the Bodhisattva not willing to abandon his own Nirvanic peace for the interests of suffering creatures? Is he not willing to dedicate the karma of his meritorious deeds, performed in his successive existences, to the general welfare of his fellow beings? Is his one fundamental motive that governs all his activities in life not directed toward a universal emancipation of all sentient beings? Is he not perfectly willing to forsake all the thoughts and passions that arise from egoism and to embrace the will of the Dharmakâya? If this is the case, then there is

<gloss>Buddhism, Dharmakâya, Karunâ, Prajñâ, Bodhicitta, Bodhisattva, Nirvanic, karma</gloss>

<summary>This page defines the Bodhicitta (intelligence-heart) as a combination of love (karunâ) and intelligence (prajñâ), arguing that the Buddhist concept of compassion is equivalent to the Christian concept of self-sacrificing love. It describes the Bodhisattva's willingness to forgo personal peace and dedicate their karma to the liberation of all sentient beings.</summary>

no reason why <term>karunâ</term> should not be translated as love.

Christians say that without love we have become sounding brass or a tinkling cymbal; and Buddhists would declare that without <term>karunâ</term> we are like a dead vine hanging over a frozen boulder, or like the cold ashes left after a blazing fire.

Some may say, however, that Buddhist sympathy or commiseration suggests a sense of passive contemplation <gloss>of</gloss> evils. When Christians say that God loves His creatures, that <insert>love implies activity</insert> and shows God's willingness to do whatever is necessary for the actual benefit of His subject beings. Quite true. Yet when the Buddha is stated to have declared that all sentient beings in the triple world are his own children, or that he will not enter into his final <term>Nirvana</term> unless all beings in the three thousand great chiliocosms—not a single soul excepted—are emancipated from the misery of birth and death, his self-sacrificing love must be considered all-comprehensive and, at the same time, full of energy and activity. Whatever objections there may be, we do not see any sufficient reason against speaking of the love-essence of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> and the <term>Bodhicitta</term>.

Sthiramati advocates in his *Discourse on the Mahâyâna-Dharmadhâtu* <note>1</note> the same view as Nâgârjuna's on the nature of the <term>Bodhicitta</term>, which I summarize here:

> “<term>Nirvâna</term>, <term>Dharmakâya</term>, <term>Tathâgata</term>, <term>Tathâgata-garbha</term>, <term>Paramârtha</term>, <term>Buddha</term>, <term>Bodhicitta</term>, or <term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term>—all these terms signify merely so many different aspects of one and the same reality; and <term>Bodhicitta</term> is the name given to a form of the <term>Dharmakâya</term> or <term>Bhûtatathâtâ</term> as it manifests itself in the human heart, and its <insert>perfection, or negatively its liberation from all egoistic impurities, constitutes the state of <term>Nirvâna</term>.”</insert>

Being a reflection of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, the <term>Bodhicitta</term> is practically the same as the original in all its characteristics; so continues Sthiramati:

> “It is free from compulsive activities; it has no beginning, it has no end; it cannot be defiled by impurities, it cannot be obscured by egoistic individualistic prejudices; it is incorporeal, it is the spiritual essence of <term>Buddhas</term>,

realization of <term>Buddhahood</term>. In many cases, thus, it is extremely difficult to render <term>upaya</term> by any of its English equivalents and yet to retain its original technical sense unharmed. This is also the case with many other Buddhist terms, among which we may mention <term>Bodhi</term>, <term>Dharmakâya</term>, <term>Prajñâ</term>, <term>Citta</term>, <term>Parivarta</term>, etc. The Chinese translators have *fang p’ien* for <term>upaya</term> which means “means-accommodation.”

### *The Awakening of the Bodhicitta.*

The <term>Bodhicitta</term> is present in the hearts of all sentient beings. Only in Buddhas is it fully awakened and active with its immaculate vitality, while in ordinary mortals it is dormant and miserably crippled by its unenlightened interaction with the world of sensuality. One of the most favorite parables told by the <term>Mahâyânists</term> to illustrate this point is to compare the <term>Bodhicitta</term> to the moonlight in the heavens. When the moon shines with her silvery light in the clear, cloudless skies, she is reflected in every drop and in every mass of water on the earth. The crystal dews on the quivering leaves reflect her like so many pearls hung on the branches. Every little water-pool, perhaps formed temporarily by heavy showers in the daytime, reflects her like so many stars descended

<summary>This page defines the perfect spiritual state of a Tathâgata and explains that while the Bodhicitta exists in all beings, it only reaches full, active vitality in a Buddha. The author uses the metaphor of moonlight reflected in water to illustrate how the Bodhicitta is present throughout the world.</summary>

<keywords>Tathâgata, Bodhicitta, Dharma, enlightenment, Mahâyânists, spiritual state, parable of the moon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison of the Bodhicitta to moonlight, explaining how it manifests in all beings and detailing the four specific conditions for its awakening as described by Vasubandhu.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER XI.</header>

on earth. Perhaps some of the pools are muddy and others even filthy, but the moonlight does not refuse to reflect her immaculate image in them. The image is just as perfect there as in a clear, undisturbed, transparent lake, where cows quench their thirst and swans bathe their stainless feathers. Wherever there is the least trace of water, there is seen a heavenly image of the goddess of night. It is the same with the <term>Bodhicitta</term>: where there exists even a little warmth of the heart, it unfailingly expresses itself as best as circumstances permit.

Now, the question is: How should this dormant <term>Bodhicitta</term> in our hearts be awakened to its full potential? This is answered more or less definitely in almost all <term>Mahâyâna</term> writings, and we may here cite the words of <term>Vasubandhu</term> from his *Discourse on the Awakening of the Bodhicitta*,¹ for they give us a systematic statement of those conditions which tend to awaken the <term>Bodhicitta</term> from its lethargic inactivity. (Chap. II.)

The <term>Bodhicitta</term>, or Intelligence-heart, is awakened in us (1) by thinking of the Buddhas, (2) by reflecting on the faults of material existence, (3) by observing <margin>✓</margin> the deplorable state in which sentient beings are living, and finally (4) by aspiring to those virtues which are acquired by a <term>Tathâgata</term> in the highest enlightenment.

(1) *By thinking of the Buddhas.*
> "All Buddhas throughout the ten directions—past, future, and present—when they first started on their way to enlightenment, were not completely free from passions and sins (<term>*kleça*</term>) any more than we are now; but they finally succeeded in attaining the highest enlightenment and became the noblest of beings.

> "All the Buddhas, raising high the torch of wisdom through the darkness of ignorance and keeping an excellent heart awake, submitted themselves to austerity and self-discipline, and finally freed themselves from the bondage of the triple world. Following their steps, we, too, could free ourselves.
>
> "All the Buddhas, the noblest type of humanity, successfully crossed the great ocean of birth and death and of passions and sins; why, then, being intelligent beings, could we not also cross the sea of transmigration?
>
> "All the Buddhas, manifesting great spiritual power, sacrificed their possessions, bodies, and lives for the attainment of omniscience (<term>*sarvajñâ*</term>); and we, too, could follow their noble examples."

(4) *The virtues of the <term>Tathāgata</term>.* "All the <term>Tathāgatas</term>, by virtue of their discipline, have acquired a noble, dignified mien which inspires every beholder with the thought that dispels pain and woe. They are omniscient, and their love for suffering beings knows no bounds; it brings all creatures back to the path of righteousness who have gone astray because of ignorance."

In short, the Intelligence-heart or <term>Bodhicitta</term> is awakened in us either when love for suffering creatures (which is innate in us) is called forth, or when our intellect aspires after the highest enlightenment, or when these two psychical activities are stirred up under some favorable circumstances. As the <term>Bodhicitta</term> is a manifestation of the <term>Dharmakāya</term> in our limited conscious mind, it constantly longs for a unification with

When this unification is not achieved for any reason, the heart (*<term>citta</term>*) shows its dissatisfaction in one way or another. This dissatisfaction may sometimes take a morbid course and may result in pessimism, misanthropy, suicide, asceticism, or other similar eccentric practices. But if properly guided and naturally developed, the more intense the dissatisfaction is, the more energetic the spiritual activity of a <term>Bodhisattva</term> will be.

### *The <term>Bodhisattva</term>’s <term>Pranidhâna</term>.*

Having awakened his <term>Bodhicitta</term> from its unconscious slumber, a <term>Bodhisattva</term> will now proceed to make his vows.

Let me point out here, however, that "vow" is not a very appropriate term to express the meaning of the Sanskrit word <term>pranidhâna</term>. <term>Pranidhâna</term> is a strong wish, aspiration, prayer, or an inflexible determination to carry out one's will even through an infinite series of rebirths. Buddhists have such a supreme belief in the power of the will or spirit that, regardless of material limitations, the will is sure to triumph over them and achieve its final goal. Thus, every <term>Bodhisattva</term> is considered to have his own particular <term>pranidhânas</term> in order to perform his share in the work of universal salvation. His physical form may vanish as its <term>karma</term> is exhausted, but his <term>pranidhâna</term> survives and takes on a new garment—this process being necessary to

<summary>This page describes how the dissatisfaction of the heart, if properly guided, fuels the spiritual activity of a Bodhisattva. It also defines "pranidhāna" as an inflexible determination of the will to achieve universal salvation across many lifetimes.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhicitta, Bodhisattva, Pranidhâna, karma, universal salvation, spiritual activity, will, citta</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how a Bodhisattva’s aspiration (pranidhāna) survives physical death to ensure the continuation of their work for universal salvation.</meta>

<page-num>308</page-num>
# CHAPTER XI.

keep it ever effective. All that is needed for a Bodhisattva to do this is to make himself a perfect incarnation of his own aspirations, putting everything external and foreign under their controlling spiritual power. Buddhists are so thoroughly idealistic, and their faith in ideas and ideals is so unshakable, that they firmly believe that whatever they aspire to will finally become a real fact; and, therefore, the more intense, permanent, and born of the inmost needs of humanity those aspirations are, the more certain our yearnings are to be satisfied. (This belief, by the way, will help explain the popular belief among Buddhists that any strong passion a person possesses will survive them and take a form, animate or inanimate, which will best achieve its end.)

According to <gloss>underlined</gloss>Vasubandhu, whom we have quoted several times, Bodhisattvas are generally supposed to make the following <gloss>underlined</gloss>ten pranidhanas, which naturally spring from a <gloss>underlined</gloss>great loving heart now awakened in them: <note>1</note>

>
> (2) "May it be that, through the merits of my work,

# CHAPTER XII.

## TEN STAGES OF BODHISATTVAHOOD

### *Gradation in our Spiritual Life.*

Theoretically speaking, as we have seen above, the Bodhi or Bodhicitta is within every sentient being, and in this sense, everyone is a Bodhisattva. In worldly hearts, it may be found enveloped in ignorance and egoism, but it can never be completely annulled. For the Bodhi, when viewed from its absolute aspect, transcends the realm of birth and death (*samsâra*); it is beyond the world of toil and trouble and is not subject to any form of defilement. But when it assumes a relative existence and is only partially manifested under the cover of ignorance, various stages of realization or perfection appear. In some beings, it may attain a more meaningful expression than in others, while there may even be those who apparently fail to show evidence of its presence on account of their unfortunate karma. This latter class is usually called “Icchantika”—that is, people who are completely overwhelmed by their passions. They are, morally and religiously, merely corpses which even a great spiritual physician finds it almost impossible to resuscitate. But, philosophically considered, the glory of the Bodhi must be admitted

<summary>This page introduces the ten stages of Bodhisattvahood, explaining that while the potential for enlightenment (Bodhi) exists in all beings, its manifestation varies according to an individual's ignorance and karma. It specifically describes the "Icchantika" as those so overwhelmed by passion that they appear spiritually dead.</summary>

<keywords>Bodhisattva, Bodhi, Bodhicitta, samsâra, karma, Icchantika, spiritual stages, enlightenment, Mahayana Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bodhi and the influence of karma on spiritual progress, leading into the specific stages of Bodhisattvahood.</meta>

<page-num>312</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

to be shining even in these dark, ignorant souls. Such souls, perhaps, will have to go through many cycles of transmigration before their <term>karma</term> loses its intensity and becomes susceptible to a moral influence with which they may come in contact.

This accursed force of <term>karma</term> is not the same in all beings; it allows for all possible degrees of strength and causes some to suffer more intensely than others. But there is no human heart or soul that is absolutely free from the shackles of <term>karma</term> and ignorance, because the very existence of a phenomenal world is a product of ignorance, though this fact does not prove that this life is evil. 
<note>The following passage is marked with a vertical bracket in the left margin and contains underlining in the original.</note>
The only heart that transcends the influence of <term>karma</term> and ignorance and is complete purity, complete love, and complete intelligence, is the <term>Dharmakâya</term> or the absolute <term>Bodhi</term> itself. The life of a <term>Bodhisattva</term> and indeed the goal of our religious aspiration is to unfold, realize, and identify ourselves with the love and intelligence of that ideal and yet real <term>Dharmakâya</term>.

The awakening of the <term>Bodhicitta</term> (or intelligence-heart) marks the first step toward the highest good of human life. This awakening must pass through several stages of religious discipline before it attains perfection. These stages are generally estimated by the <term>Mahâyânists</term> at ten. They appear, however, to our modern skeptical minds to be of no significant consequence, nor can we detect any very practical and well-defined distinction between successive stages. We fail to understand what religious necessity impelled the Hindu Buddhists to establish such apparently un-

<summary>This page explains that while karma and ignorance affect all beings, the goal of a Bodhisattva is to realize the pure love and intelligence of the Dharmakâya. It introduces the ten stages of religious discipline in Mahâyâna Buddhism, though the author notes these distinctions may seem insignificant to modern minds.</summary>
<keywords>karma, transmigration, Dharmakâya, Bodhi, Bodhisattva, Bodhicitta, Mahâyânists, Hindu Buddhists, spiritual discipline, enlightenment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the spiritual stages of the Bodhisattva, specifically detailing the ten stages of progress known as the Daçabhûmî.</meta>

<page-num>313</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

important stages one after another in our religious life. We can see, however, that the first awakening of the <term>Bodhicitta</term> does not transform us all at once to <term>Buddhahood</term>; we have yet to overcome with strenuous efforts the baneful influence of <term>karma</term> and ignorance, which asserts itself too readily in our practical life. But the marking of stages, as in the gradation of the <term>Daçabhûmî</term> in our spiritual progress, seems to be altogether too artificial. Nevertheless, I here take the trouble, as a historical survey, to enumerate the ten stages and to give some features supposed to be most characteristic of each <insert>Bhûmî (stage)</insert> as expounded in the *Avatamsaka Sutra*. Probably they will help us to understand what moral conceptions and what religious aspirations were working in the establishment of the doctrine of <term>Daçabhûmî</term>, for it elaborately describes what was considered by the <term>Mahâyânists</term> to be the essential constituents of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>, and also shows what spiritual routine a Buddhist was expected to pursue.

<margin>—</margin>The <insert>ten stages</insert> are: (1) <term>Pramuditâ</term>, (2) <term>Vimalâ</term>, (3) <term>Prabhâkarî</term>, (4) <term>Arcismatî</term>, (5) <term>Sudurjayâ</term>, (6) <term>Abhimukhî</term>, (7) <term>Dûrangamâ</term>, (8) <term>Acalâ</term>, (9) <term>Sâdhumatî</term>, (10) <term>Dharmameghâ</term>.

### (1) *The Pramuditâ.*

<term>Pramuditâ</term> means “delight” or “joy” and marks the first stage of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>, at which the Buddhists emerge from a cold, self-sufficing, and almost nihilistic contemplation of <term>Nirvâna</term> as fostered by the <term>Çrâvakas</term>

<vocab>Bodhicitta, Buddhahood, karma, Daçabhûmî, Bhûmî, Avatamsaka Sutra, Mahâyânists, Bodhisattvahood, Pramuditâ, Vimalâ, Prabhâkarî, Arcismatî, Sudurjayâ, Abhimukhî, Dûrangamâ, Acalâ, Sâdhumatî, Dharmameghâ, Nirvâna, Çrâvakas</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the ten stages of spiritual progress (Daçabhûmî) in Mahâyâna Buddhism, beginning with Pramuditâ (Joy), where the practitioner moves beyond solitary contemplation toward the path of a Bodhisattva.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhicitta, Daçabhûmî, ten stages, Pramuditâ, Bodhisattvahood, Avatamsaka Sutra, Mahâyâna Buddhism, Nirvâna</keywords>

--- Page 330 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the first stage of Bodhisattvahood, Pramuditâ (Joy), explaining why it is characterized by joy and how it differs from the self-seclusion of other spiritual paths.</meta>

<page-num>314</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

and <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>. This spiritual emergence and emancipation is psychologically accompanied by an intense feeling of joy, like that which is experienced by a person when they unexpectedly recognize a very familiar face in a distant land of strangers. For this reason, the first stage is called “joy.”

Even in the midst of the perfect tranquility of <term>Nirvâna</term>—in which all passions are said to have died away according to ascetics or solitary philosophers—the innermost voice in the heart of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> moans with a kind of dissatisfaction or uneasiness. Though this feeling is undefined and seems to be of no significance, it still refuses to be eternally buried in the silent grave of annihilation. He gropes in the darkness in vain; he seeks consolation in the <term>samâdhi</term> of non-resistance or non-activity in vain; he finds eternal peace in the gospel of self-negation in vain; his soul is still troubled, without knowing exactly why. But as soon as the <term>Bodhicitta</term> (intelligence-heart) is awakened from its sleep, as soon as the warmth of love (<term>mahâkarunâ</term>) penetrates into the coldest cell of asceticism, and as soon as the light of supreme enlightenment (<term>mahâprajñâ</term>) dawns upon the darkest recesses of ignorance, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> sees at once that the world was not made for self-seclusion nor for self-negation. He realizes that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is the source of “universal radiance,” and that <term>Nirvâna</term>—if viewed relatively in contrast to birth-and-death—is nothing but a sham and just as unreal as any worldly existence. These insights finally lead him to feel that he cannot rest quiet until all sentient beings are

<summary>This page describes the first stage of Bodhisattvahood, "Joy," explaining how the awakening of the Bodhicitta replaces the dissatisfaction of solitary asceticism with active compassion. It emphasizes that true enlightenment recognizes the unreality of a self-centered Nirvana and seeks the salvation of all beings.</summary>
<keywords>Pramuditâ, Bodhisattva, Nirvâna, Bodhicitta, mahâkarunâ, mahâprajñâ, Dharmakâya, enlightenment, compassion, universal salvation</keywords>

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

emancipated from the tangle of ignorance and raised to the same position as he himself now occupies.

### (2) *The Vimalâ.*

Vimalâ means "freedom from defilement," or, positively, "purity." When the <term>Bodhisattva</term> reaches the second stage, he does so through the spiritual insight gained at the first stage, achieving rectitude and purity of heart. His heart is now thoroughly spotless and filled with tenderness; he harbors no anger or malice. He is free from all thoughts of killing any living beings. Being content with what belongs to him, he does not look covetously at things that are not his own. Faithful to his own partner, he does not harbor any evil thoughts toward others. His words are always true, faithful, kind, and considerate. He loves truth and honesty, and he never uses flattery.

### (3) *The Prabhâkarî.*

Prabhâkarî means "brightness"—specifically, that of the intellect. This is the primary characteristic of the <term>Bodhisattva</term>'s spiritual condition at this stage. Here, he gains the most penetrating insight into the nature of things. He recognizes that all created things are not permanent, lead to misery, have no abiding selfhood (<term>âtman</term>), lack purity, and <margin>✓</margin> are subject to eventual decay. He also recognizes, however, that the true nature of things is neither created nor subject to destruction; it abides eternally in the very same essence and transcends the limits of time.

<summary>This page describes the second and third stages of the Bodhisattva's spiritual journey: the Vimalâ stage of moral purity and the Prabhâkarî stage of intellectual brightness and insight into the nature of reality.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Vimalâ, Prabhâkarî, âtman, purity, spiritual insight, enlightenment, impermanence</keywords>

--- Page 332 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bodhisattva's stages of development, finishing the description of the third stage (Prabhâkarî) and introducing the fourth stage (Arciṣmatî), along with the specific spiritual disciplines required there.</meta>

<page-num>316</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

and space. Ignorant beings who do not see this truth are always worrying over transient and worthless things. They constantly consume their spiritual energy with the fire of greed, anger, and infatuation, which in turn accumulates the ashes of misery and suffering for their future existences. <margin>✓</margin> This wretched condition of sentient beings further stimulates the loving heart of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> to seek the highest intelligence of the Buddha. This intelligence gives him great spiritual energy, enabling him to carry out the gigantic task of universal liberation. His desire for and faith in the Buddha-intelligence are of such immense strength that he would not hesitate for even a moment—if only assured of attaining that priceless treasure—to plunge himself into the smelting fire of a volcano.

### (4) *The Arciṣmatî.*

*Arciṣmatî*, meaning “inflammation,” is the name given to the fourth stage. At this stage, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> consumes all the remnants of ignorance and evil passions in the fiery crucible of the purifying <term>Bodhi</term>. Here, he most strenuously practices the <note>This was underlined in pencil in the original text.</note> thirty-seven virtues called <term>Bodhipâkṣikas</term>, which lead to the perfection of <term>Bodhi</term>. These virtues consist of seven categories:

(I) Four Contemplations (<term>smṛtyusthâna</term>): 1. On the impurity of the body; 2. On the evils of sensuality; 3. On the fleeting nature of worldly interests; 4. On the non-existence of an eternal self (<term>âtman</term>) in composite things.

(II) Four Righteous Efforts (<term>samyakprahâna</term>): 1. To

(III) Four Forces of the Will (*<term>rddhipâda</term>*): 1. The determination to accomplish what is willed; 2. The energy to concentrate the mind on the object in view; 3. The power of retaining the object in memory; 4. The intelligence that perceives the way to <term>Nirvâna</term>.

(IV) Five Powers (*<term>indrya</term>*), from which all moral good is produced: 1. Faith; 2. Energy; 3. Circumspection; 4. Equilibrium, or tranquility of mind; 5. Intelligence.

(V) Five Functions (*<term>bala</term>*): Same as the above. <gloss>1</gloss>

(VI) Seven Constituents of the <term>Bodhi</term> (*<term>bodhyanga</term>*): 1. The retentive power; 2. Discrimination; 3. Energy; 4. Contentment; 5. Modesty; 6. The balanced mind; 7. Large-heartedness.

(VII) The Eightfold Noble Path (*<term>âryamârga</term>*): 1. Right view; 2. Right resolve; 3. Right speech; 4. Right conduct; 5. Right livelihood; 6. Right recollection; 8. Right tranquilization, or contemplation.

---

<note>1 The distinction between the five <term>indriyas</term> and the five <term>balas</term> seems to be somewhat redundant. However, Hindu philosophers usually distinguish the actor from the action, and the agent from the function or operation. Thus, the sense organs are distinguished from sensations or sense consciousness, and the <term>manovijñâna</term> (mind) from its functions such as thinking, attention, and memory. The <term>âtman</term> has thus come to be considered the central agent that controls all sensory and intellectual activities. Although Buddhists do not recognize this differentiation between actor and action in reality, they sometimes loosely follow popular usage.</note>

### (5) *The Sudurjayâ.*

Sudurjayâ means "very difficult to conquer." The Bodhisattva reaches this stage when he, completely armed with the thirty-seven Bodhipâkṣikas and guided by the beacon-light of Bodhi, undauntedly breaks through the column of evil passions. Provided with the <insert>two spiritual provisions, love and wisdom,</insert> and benefiting from the spirits of all the Buddhas of the past,
<insert>present, and future, the Bodhisattva has developed an</insert> intellectual power to penetrate deep into the system of existence. He perceives the Fourfold Noble Truth in its true light; he perceives the highest reality in the Tathâgata; he also perceives that the highest reality, though absolutely one in its essence, manifests itself in a world of particulars, that relative knowledge (*samvṛtti*) and absolute knowledge (*paramârtha*) are two aspects of one and the same truth, that when subjectivity is disturbed there appears particularity, and that when it is not disturbed there shines only the eternal light of Tathâgatajñâ (Tathâgata-knowledge).

### (6) *The Abhimukhî.*

Abhimukhî means "showing one's face," that is, the presentation of intelligence (*prajñâ*) before the Bodhisattva at this stage.
The Bodhisattva enters this stage by reflecting on the essence of all dharmas which are throughout of one nature. When he perceives the truth, his heart <insert>is filled with great love, and he serenely contemplates</insert>

the life of ignorant beings who are constantly going astray, yielding themselves to evil temptations, clinging to the false conception of egoism, and thus making themselves the prey of eternal damnation. He then proceeds to contemplate the development of evils in general. There is ignorance; there is <term>karma</term>; and in this fertile soil of blind activity, the seeds of consciousness are sown. The moisture of desire thoroughly soaks them, upon which the water of egoism or individuation is poured. The bed for all forms of particularity is well-prepared, and the buds of <term>nâmarûpas</term> (name-and-form) thrive most vigorously here. From these, we have the flowers of the sense organs, which come in contact with other existences and produce impressions, feel agreeable sensations, and tenaciously cling to them. From this clinging—or the will to live as the principle of individuation, or as the principle of <term>bhâva</term> as it is called in the <term>Twelve Nidânas</term>—another body consisting of the <term>five skandhas</term> comes into existence, and, passing through all the phases of transformation, dissolves and disappears. All sentient beings are thus kept in a perpetual oscillation of combination and separation, of pleasure and pain, and of birth and death. But the insight of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> has gone deeply into the inmost essence of things, which remains the same forever and in which there is no creation and dissolution.

### *(7) The Dûrangamâ.*

<term>Dûrangamâ</term> means "going far away." The <term>Bodhisattva</term> enters upon this stage by attaining the so-called

<meta>This page continues the description of the seventh stage of the Bodhisattva's path, the Dûrangamâ ("going far away"), focusing on the Bodhisattva's mastery of Upâyajñâ—the knowledge of skillful means—to aid in the salvation of all beings while remaining detached from the material world.</meta>

*Upâyajñâ*, that is, the knowledge that enables him to produce any means or expediency suitable for his work of salvation. He himself lives by the principles of *çûnyatâ* (transcendentality), *animitta* (non-individuality), and *apranihita* (desirelessness), but his loving-kindness keeps him busily engaged among sentient beings. He knows that Buddhas are not creatures radically and essentially different from himself, but he does not stop offering them due homage. He is always contemplating the nature of the Absolute, but he does not abandon the practice of accumulating merits. He is no longer burdened by worldly thoughts, yet he does not disdain managing secular affairs. He keeps himself perfectly aloof from the consuming fire of passion, but he plans all possible means for the sake of sentient beings to quench the raging flames of greed (*lobha*), anger (*dveṣa*), and delusion (*moha*). He knows that all individual existences are like a dream, a mirage, or the reflection of the moon in the water, but he works and toils in the world of particulars and submits himself to the power of <term>karma</term>. He is well aware of the transcendental nature of the Pure Land (*sukhâvatî*), but he describes it with physical colors for the sake of the unenlightened masses. He knows that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> of all the Buddhas is not a material existence, but he does not refuse to adorn himself with the thirty-two major and eighty minor excellent features of a great man or god (*mahâpuruṣa*). He knows that the language of all the Buddhas does not fall within the range of human comprehension, but

he endeavors with all methods (*upâya*) to make it clear enough for people to understand. He knows that all the Buddhas perceive the past, present, and future in the blink of an eye, but he adapts himself to various conditions of the material world and endeavors to help sentient beings understand the significance of Bodhi according to their destinies and dispositions. In short, the Bodhisattva himself lives on a higher spiritual plane, far removed from the stains of worldliness; but he does not withdraw into this serene, undisturbed subjectivity. He boldly sets out into the world of specifics and senses; and, placing himself on the level of <note>The following lines are underlined in pencil</note> ignorant beings, he works like them, toils like them, and suffers like them; and during all these times, he never fails to practice the gospel of loving-kindness and to transfer (*parivarta*) all his merits toward the liberation and spiritual edification of the masses—that is, he never gets tired of practicing the ten virtues of perfection (*pâramitâ*). ✓

That is to say, (1) the Bodhisattva practices the virtue of charity (*dâna*) by freely giving away to all sentient creatures all the merits he has acquired by following the path of the Buddhas. (2) He practices the virtue of good conduct (*çîla*) by destroying all the evil passions that disturb the serenity of the mind. (3) He practices the virtue of patience (*kşânti*), for he never becomes irritated or excited over what is done to him by ignorant beings. (4) He practices the virtue of strenuousness (*vriya*), for he never gets tired of

(5) He practices the virtue of calmness (*dhyâna*), for his mind is never distracted in steadily pursuing his way to supreme knowledge. (6) He practices the virtue of intelligence (*prajñâ*), for he always restrains his thoughts from wandering away from the path of absolute truth. (7) He practices the virtue of tactfulness (*upâya*), for he has an inexhaustible mine of expediencies ready at his command for the work of universal salvation. (8) He practices the virtue of will-to-do (*pranidhâna*) by determinedly following the dictates of the highest intelligence. (9) He practices the virtue of strength (*bala*), for no evil influences, no heretical thoughts can ever frustrate or slacken his efforts for the general welfare of people. (10) Finally, he practices the virtue of knowledge (*jñâna*) by truthfully comprehending and expounding the ultimate nature of beings.

->(8) *The Acalâ.*<-

*Acalâ*, "immovable," is the name for the eighth stage of Bodhisattvahood. When a Bodhisattva, transcending all forms of discursive or deliberate knowledge, acquires the highest, perfect knowledge called *anutpattikadharmakṣânti*, he is said to have gone beyond the seventh stage. *Anutpattikadharmakṣânti* literally means "not-created-being-forbearance"; and the Buddhists use the term in the sense of keeping one’s thoughts in conformity to the views that nothing in this world

<summary>This page concludes the description of the ten perfections (pâramitâs) and introduces Acalâ, the eighth stage of Bodhisattvahood, characterized by the attainment of "immovable" knowledge.</summary>

<page-num>323</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XII.</header>

has ever been created—that things are as they are, that is, they are Suchness itself. This knowledge is also called non-conscious or non-deliberate knowledge, in contrast to the relative knowledge that constitutes all our logical and demonstrative knowledge. Strictly speaking, this so-called knowledge is not knowledge in its ordinary sense; it is a sort of unconscious or subconscious intelligence, or "immediate knowledge" as some call it. In this state, not only willing and acting, but also knowing and willing are one single, undivided expression of activity; any logical or natural transition from one to the other is completely absent. Here indeed, knowledge is will, and will is action: “Let there be light,” and there is light, and the light is good; it is the state of a divine mind.

At this stage of perfection, the <term>Bodhisattva</term>’s spiritual condition is compared to that of a person who, while in a dreaming state, attempts to cross deep waters. He gathers all his energy and plans every detail, but just as he is about to start the journey, he suddenly wakes up and finds all his elaborate preparations were for nothing. The <term>Bodhisattva</term> has until now shown untiring spiritual effort to attain the highest knowledge, steadily practicing all virtues leading to the acquisition of <term>Nirvâna</term>, and heroically endeavoring to eliminate all evil passions. At the culmination of all these exercises, he suddenly enters the stage of <term>Acalâ</term> and finds that his previous efforts have mysteriously vanished from his conscious mind. He cherishes

now no desire for <term>Buddhahood</term>, <term>Nirvâna</term>, or <term>Bodhicitta</term>, much less for worldliness, egoism, or the satisfaction of evil passions. The conscious striving that distinguished all his former course has now given way to a state of spontaneous activity, of saintly innocence, and of divine playfulness. He wills, and it is done. He aspires, and it is realized. He is nature herself, for there is no trace in his activity that betrays any artificial labor, or any voluntary or compulsory restraint. This state of perfect ideal freedom may be called aesthetic, which characterizes the work of a genius. There is here no trace of consciously following some prescribed laws, no pains of elaborately conforming to a formula. To put this poetically, the inner life of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> at this stage is like the lilies of the field whose glory is greater than that of Solomon in all his human magnificence.

Immanuel Kant’s remarks on this point are very suggestive, and I will quote the following from his *Critique of Judgment* (Reclam edition, p. 173):

> “Therefore, the purposiveness in the product of fine art, although it is intentional, must not seem intentional; that is, fine art must be seen as nature, even though one is conscious of it as art. A product of art appears as nature when all the precision in its agreement with the rules—by which alone the product can become what it is meant to be—is present, yet without laboriousness; that is, without showing a trace that the rule was before the artist’s eyes <note>The author provides the German text: "Also muss die Zweckmässigkeit im Produkte der schönen Kunst, ob sie zwar absichtlich ist, doch nicht absichtlich scheinen: d. i., schöne Kunst muss als Natur anzusehen sein, ob man sich ihrer zwar als Kunst bewusst ist. Als Natur aber erscheint ein Produkt der Kunst dadurch, dass zwar alle Pünktlichkeit in der Uebereinkunst mit Regeln, nach denen allein das Produkt das werden kann, was es soll sein, angetroffen wird, aber ohne Peinlichkeit, d. i., ohne eine Spur zu zeigen, dass die Regel dem Künstler vor Augen..."</note>

This state is further illustrated by Immanuel Kant's definition of fine art appearing as nature.</summary>

### (9) *The <term>Sâdhumatî</term>.*

<term>Sâdhumatî</term>, meaning "good intelligence," is the name given to the ninth stage of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>. All <term>Bodhisattvas</term> are said to have reached this point when sentient beings are benefited by the <term>Bodhisattva</term>'s attainment of the highest perfect knowledge, which is unfathomable by ordinary human intelligence. This knowledge leads them to the <term>Dharma</term> of the deepest mystery, to the <term>Samâdhi</term> of perfect spirituality, to the <term>Dhâranî</term> of divine spontaneity, to Love of absolute purity, and to the Will of utmost freedom.

The <term>Bodhisattva</term> will acquire at this stage the four <term>Pratisamvids</term> (comprehensive knowledge), which are (1) <term>Dharmapratisamvid</term>, (2) <term>Arthapratisamvid</term>, (3) <term>Niruktipratisamvid</term>, and (4) <term>Pratibhanapratisamvid</term>. Through the <term>Dharmapratisamvid</term>, the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> understand the

"If I say that Shakespeare is the greatest of intellects, I have said all concerning him. But there is more in Shakespeare's intellect than we have yet seen. It is what I call an unconscious intellect; there is more virtue in it than he himself is aware of. Novalis beautifully remarks of him, that those dramas of his are products of Nature too, as deep as Nature herself. I find a great truth in this saying: Shakespeare's art is not artifice; the noblest worth of it is not there by plan or prior contrivance. It grows from the depths of Nature, through this noble, sincere soul, who is a voice of Nature."

Again, by the first intelligence, they comprehend that all <term>dharmas</term> consist of one indestructible reality; by the second, that this one reality, by differentiating itself, becomes subject to the law of causation; by the third, that by virtue of a superior understanding, all Buddhas become the object of admiration and the haven for all sentient beings; and by the fourth, that in the one body of truth, all Buddhas preach the infinite lights of the <term>Dharma</term>.

->(10) *The <term>Dharmameghâ</term>.*<-

*<term>Dharmameghâ</term>*, meaning "clouds of dharma," is the name of the tenth and final stage of <term>Bodhisattvahood</term>. The <term>Bodhisattvas</term> have now practiced all the virtues of purity and accumulated all the constituents of <term>Bodhi</term> (enlightenment). They are fortified with great power and intelligence, and they universally practice the principle of great love and sympathy. They have deeply penetrated the mystery of individual existence, fathomed the innermost depths of sentience, and followed step by step the path of all the <term>Tathâgatas</term>. Every thought cherished by the <term>Bodhisattva</term> now dwells in

(2) He is faultless in his speech. Whatever he speaks comes from his transcendental eloquence and leads the audience to a higher conception of life. (3) His mind is faultless. # CHAPTER XII.

The <term>Bodhisattva</term> at this stage is a personification of love and sympathy, which flow freely from the fountain of his inner will. He gathers the clouds of virtue and wisdom, in which he manifests himself in many forms; he produces the lightning of <term>Buddhi</term>, <term>Vidyâs</term>, and <term>Vaiçâradyas</term>; and shaking the whole world with the thunder of <term>Dharma</term>, he crushes all evil ones; and pouring forth the showers of the Good Law, he quenches the burning flames of ignorance.

(5) His mind is free from thoughts of particularity (*<term>nânâtvasamjñâ</term>*); that is, it abides in transcendental truth, and his thoughts are not distracted by the objects of the senses. (6) Resignation (*<term>upekṣâ</term>*). The Buddha knows everything, yet he is calmly resigned. (7) His aspiration is unfathomable; that is, his desire to save all beings from the sufferings of ignorance knows no bounds. (8) His energy is inexhaustible, which he applies with the utmost vigor to the salvation of ignorant souls. (9) His mental activity (*<term>smṛti</term>*) is inexhaustible; that is, he is ever conscious of all the good doctrines taught by all the Buddhas of the past, present, and future. (10) His intelligence (*<term>prajñâ</term>*) is inexhaustible; that is, being in possession of all-encompassing intelligence which knows no limits, he preaches for the benefit of all beings. (11) His deliverance (*<term>vimukti</term>*) is permanent; that is, he has eternally distanced himself from all evil passions and sinful attachments. (12) His knowledge of deliverance (*<term>vimuktijñâna</term>*) is perfect; that is, his intellectual insight into all states of deliverance is without flaw. (13) He possesses a wisdom which directs all his bodily movements toward the benefit and enlightenment of sentient beings. (14) He possesses a wisdom which directs all his speech toward the edification and conversion of his fellow creatures. (15) He possesses a wisdom which reflects in his clear mind all the turbulent states of ignorant souls, from which he removes the dark veil of ignorance and folly. (16) He knows all the past. (17) He knows all the future. (18) He knows all the present.

The above presentation of the <term>Daçabhûmî</term>¹ of Bodhisattvahood allows us to see what ideal life is held out by the <term>Mahâyânists</term> before their own eyes and in what respect it differs from that of the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term> as well as from that of other religious followers. <term>Mahâyânism</term> is not content to make us mere transmitters or "hearers" of the teachings of the Buddha; it wants to inspire us with all the religious and ethical motives that stirred the noblest heart of <term>Çâkyamuni</term> to its inmost depths. It fully recognizes the intrinsic worth of the human soul; and, holding up its high ideals and noble aspirations, it endeavors to develop all the possibilities of our soul-life, which by our strenuous efforts and all-defying courage will one day be realized even on this earth of impermanence. We as individual existences are nothing but shadows which will vanish as soon as the conditions disappear that make them possible; we as mortal beings are no more than the

Then even an apparently insignificant act of lovingkindness will lead finally to the eternal abode of bliss—not for the actor alone, but for the whole community to which he belongs. This is because a stream of love spontaneously flows from the lake of the Intelligence-heart (*<term>Bodhicitta</term>*) which is fed by the inexhaustible spring of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, while ignorance leads only to egoism, hatred, greed, disturbance, and universal misery.

## CHAPTER XIII.

### <term>NIRVÂNA</term>.

<term>NIRVÂNA</term>, according to <term>Mahâyâna Buddhism</term>, is not understood in its nihilistic sense. Even among the <term>Çrâvakas</term> or <term>Hînayânists</term>, <term>Nirvâna</term> in this sense is not so much the object of their religious life as the recognition of the <term>Fourfold Noble Truth</term>, the practice of the <term>Eightfold Path</term>, or liberation from the yoke of egoism. As far as I can see, it is mostly due to non-Buddhist critics that the concept of <term>Nirvâna</term> has been selected from among others as one of the most fundamental teachings of Buddha, while they simultaneously claim that it consists of the annihilation of all human passions and aspirations, both noble and worthless.

In fact, <term>Nirvâna</term> literally means the “extinction” or “dissolution” of the five <term>skandhas</term>, and therefore it may be said that entering into <term>Nirvâna</term> is equivalent to the annihilation of material existence and of all passions. <term>Catholic Buddhists</term> <note>The author uses this term to refer to the "universal" or Mahâyâna school of Buddhism.</note>, however, do not understand <term>Nirvâna</term> in the sense of emptiness; for they say that Buddhism is not a religion of death, nor is it for the dead. Instead, they believe it teaches how to attain eternal life, how to gain an insight into the real nature of things, and how to regulate our conduct

<summary>This page addresses the common misconception of Nirvâna as nihilistic annihilation, explaining that in Mahâyâna Buddhism, it is viewed as a path toward eternal life and insight. The author argues that critics often focus too narrowly on the literal "extinction" of the skandhas rather than the religious goals of the Eightfold Path.</summary>

<keywords>Nirvâna, Mahâyâna Buddhism, Çrâvakas, Hînayânists, Fourfold Noble Truth, Eightfold Path, skandhas, nihilism, Catholic Buddhists</keywords>

--- Page 348 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nirvâna in Mahâyâna Buddhism, specifically refuting the idea that it represents a nihilistic state of total annihilation.</meta>

in accordance with the highest truth. Therefore, <term>Buddhism</term>, when rightly understood in the spirit of its founder, is something quite different from what the general public commonly supposes it to be.

I will endeavor in the following pages to point out that <term>Nirvâna</term>, in the sense of a total annihilation of human activities, is by no means the primary and sole object of <term>Buddhists</term>. I will then proceed to elucidate in what sense it is understood in <term>Mahâyâna Buddhism</term> and see what relative position <term>Nirvâna</term>, in its <term>Mahâyânistic</term> sense, occupies in the body of <term>Buddhism</term>.

### *Nihilistic Nirvâna not the First Object.*

In order to see the true significance of <term>Nirvâna</term>, it is necessary first to observe in what direction <term>Buddha</term> himself plowed the waves in his religious cruise and upon what shore he finally disembarked. This will show us whether or not <term>Nirvâna</term> as nihilistic nothingness is the primary and sole object of <term>Buddhism</term>, toward which every spiritual effort of its devotees is directed.

If the attainment of negativistic <term>Nirvâna</term> were the sole aim of <term>Buddhism</term>, we should naturally expect <term>Buddha</term>'s farewell address to deal chiefly with that subject. In his last sermon, however, <term>Buddha</term> did not teach his disciples to concentrate all their moral efforts on the attainment of <term>Nirvânic</term> quietude while disregarding all the forms of activity that exhibit themselves in life. Far from it. He told them, according to the <term>Mahânibbâna sutta</term> (the Book of the Great

<summary>The author clarifies that Nirvâna is not intended as a state of nihilistic annihilation or total inactivity, citing the Buddha's final sermon as evidence of an active moral path. The text aims to redefine the Mahâyâna understanding of Nirvâna and its significance within the broader Buddhist tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvâna, Mahâyâna Buddhism, Buddha, Mahânibbâna sutta, nihilism, moral effort, religious goals</keywords>

Work out your salvation with diligence!” This exhortation of the strenuous life is quite in harmony with the last words of <term>Buddha</term> as recorded in <term>Açvaghoṣa</term>’s *<term>Buddhacarita</term>* (Chinese translation, Chap. xxvi). > “You should guard yourselves well!

> I now enter into <term>Nirvâna</term>.” ¹

In this we find <term>Buddha</term>’s characteristic admonition to his disciples not to waste time but to work out their salvation with diligence and rigor, but we do not find the gospel of annihilation, the supposedly fundamental teaching of <term>Buddhism</term>.

Did <term>Buddha</term>, then, start his religious discipline to attain the absolute annihilation of all human aspirations and, after a long meditation, reach a conclusion that contradicted his premises? Far from it. His first and last ambition was nothing other than the emancipation of all beings from ignorance, misery, and suffering through enlightenment, knowledge, and truth. When
<margin><image-desc>A small handwritten arrow points toward the start of the next line.</image-desc></margin>
<term>Mâra</term> the evil one was exhausting all his evil powers upon the destruction of the <term>Buddha</term> at the beginning of his career, the good gods in the heavens cried out to the evil one: ²

> “Do not take upon yourself, O <term>Mâra</term>, this vain fatigue—
> throw aside your malevolence and retire to your home.
> This sage cannot be shaken by you any more than
> the mighty mountain <term>Meru</term> by the wind.

These words of the good gods in the heavens truthfully echo the motive that stirred <term>Çâkyamuni</term> to take up his gigantic task of <term>universal salvation</term>, and we are unable here, as before, to perceive even a trace of the nihilistic speculation that is supposed to characterize <term>Nirvâna</term>. From the very beginning of his religious path, the <term>Buddha</term> searched for the light that would illumi-

nate the whole universe and dispel the darkness of ignorance.

What enlightenment, then, did the Buddha, pursuing his first object, finally gain? What truth was it that he is said to have discovered under the Bodhi tree after six years of penance and deep meditation? As is universally recognized, it was no more than the Four-fold Noble Truth and the Twelve Chains of Dependence, which are acknowledged by the Mahâyânists as well as by the Hînayânists as the essentially original teachings of the Buddha. What then was his subjective state when he discovered these truths? How did he feel in his inmost being after this intellectual triumph over egoistic thoughts and passions? According to the Southern tradition, the famous Hymn of Victory is said to be his utterance on this occasion. It reads (*The Dharmapada*, 153):

> "Many a life to transmigrate,
> Long quest, no rest, has been my fate,
> Seeking the tent-designer;
> Painful birth from state to state.
> 
> "Tent-designer, I know you now;
> Never again are you to build;
> Quite out are all your joyful fires,
> Rafter broken and roof-tree gone;
> Into the vast my heart goes on,
> Gains Eternity—dead desires." ¹
