<margin>✓</margin>the ego that is supposed to be a subtle existence behind our mental experiences. As was pointed out elsewhere, the negative phase of Buddhism consists in the eradication of this ego-substratum or the “designer” of eternal transmigration. The Buddha now finds that this ego-soul is a phantasmagoria and has no final existence; and with this insight, his ego-centric desires that troubled him for so long are eternally dead. He feels the breaking up of their limitations; he is absorbed in the Eternal Vast, in which we all live and move and have our being. No shadow is perceptible here that suggests anything of an absolute nothingness, which is sometimes supposed to be the attribute of Nirvana.

Before proceeding further, let us see what the Mahayana tradition says concerning this point. The tradition varies in this case as in many others. According to Beal’s *Romantic History of Buddha*, which is a translation of a Chinese version of the *Buddhacarita* (*Fo pen hing ching*),¹ Buddha is reported to have exclaimed this:

<margin>✓</margin>
> “Through ages past I have acquired continual merit,
> That which my heart desired I have now attained,
> How quickly I have arrived at the ever-constant condition,
> And landed on the very shore of Nirvana.
> The sorrows and opposition of the world,
> The Lord of the Kamalokas, Mara Pisuna,
> These are now unable to affect me; they are wholly destroyed;
> By the power of religious merit and of wisdom, they are cast away."

However, I am inclined to think that the original Tibetan will allow a different interpretation when examined by someone who is better acquainted with the spirit of Buddhism than Rockhill. In several cases, Rockhill reveals his insufficient knowledge of the subject he treats. His translation of the gâthâs is as follows:

> "All the pleasures of worldly joys,
> And all those known among the gods,
> Compared with the joy of ending existence,
> Are not even a sixteenth part of it."</note>

Viewing the significance of Buddhism in this light, it is evident that the Buddha did not emphasize the doctrine of Nirvana as a total rejection of human aspirations so much as the abandonment of egoism and the practical regulation of our daily lives in accordance with this view. Nirvana, in which all passions—both noble and base—are supposed to have been "blown out like a lamp," was not the most sought-after goal of Buddhist life. On the contrary, Buddhism advises all its followers to exercise their spiritual energy as strenuously as possible to attain perfect freedom from the bondage of ignorance and egoism. This is because it is the only way we can conquer the vanity of worldliness and enjoy the bliss of eternal life.

### *Nirvana is Positive.*

It is not my intention here to investigate the historical side of this question; we are not concerned with the problem of how the followers of Buddha gradually developed the positive aspect of Nirvana in connection with the practical application of his moral and religious teaching.

> “Sorrowful is he whose burden is heavy,
> And happy is he who has cast it down;
> When once he has cast off his burden,
> He will seek to be burdened no more.
>
> “When all existences are put away,
> When all notions are at an end,
> When all things are perfectly known,
> Then craving will return no more.”

In the *Udana*, II., 2, we have a stanza corresponding to the first *gatha* cited here, but the *Udana* does not say “the joy of ending existence,” but “the destruction of desire.”

According to the *Lalita Vistara*, the Buddha’s utterance of victory is (Rajendra Mitra’s Edition p. 448):

> “Cinna vartmopaçânta rajâh çuṣkâ âçravâ na punaḥ çravanti. Chinne vartmani varttate duḥkhasyaiṣonta ucyate.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the positive conception of Nirvana, specifically how Mahayana Buddhism distinguished its view of Nirvana as an ethical practice from the interpretations of other Indian philosophical schools that saw it as the annihilation of existence.</meta>

<page-num>340</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

teachings; nor are we engaged in tracing the evolutionary process through which the Buddha’s noble resolution to save all sentient beings from ignorance and misery was made most prominent by his later devotees. What I wish to state here about the positive conception of Nirvana and its development is this: Mahayana Buddhism was the first religious teaching in India that contradicted the doctrine of Nirvana as conceived by other Hindu thinkers, who saw in it a complete annihilation of being. They thought that existence is evil, and evil is misery, and the only way to escape misery is to destroy the root of existence, which is nothing less than the total cessation of human desires and activities in the unconsciousness of Nirvana. Yoga taught self-forgetfulness in deep meditation; Samkhya taught the absolute separation of *Purusha* from *Prakriti*, which means undisturbed self-contemplation; Vedanta taught absorption in the *Brahma*, which is the total suppression of all individual details; and thus all of them considered emancipation from human desires and aspirations to be heavenly bliss—that is, Nirvana. Metaphysically speaking, they might each have been correct in their own way, but, from an ethical standpoint, their views had little significance for our practical lives and showed a sad deficiency in dealing with problems of morality.

The Buddha was keenly aware of this flaw in their doctrines. He taught, therefore, that Nirvana does not consist in the complete stoppage of existence, but in the practice of the Eightfold Path. This moral

<summary>This page explains that Mahayana Buddhism views Nirvana as a positive, moral practice through the Eightfold Path, contrasting it with other Indian philosophies that defined it as the annihilation of existence or unconsciousness.</summary>
<keywords>Mahayana Buddhism, Nirvana, Eightfold Path, Yoga, Samkhya, Vedanta, Ethics, Indian Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the positive conception of Nirvana, explaining how it is viewed not as the cessation of being but as the spiritual fulfillment and moral unfolding of human life.</meta>

<page-num>341</page-num>
# CHAPTER XIII.

practice leads to the pure joy of Nirvana, not as the silencing of human aspirations, but as the fulfillment or unfolding of human life. The word Nirvana, in the sense of annihilation, existed before Buddha, but it was he who gave a new significance to it and made it worthy of attainment by people of moral character. All the doctrinal aspects of Nirvana are later additions, or rather developments, made by Buddhist scholars, who argue that their positions are solidly based on certain canonical passages. Whatever the case may be, my conviction is that those who developed the positive significance of Nirvana are more consistent with the spirit of the founder than those who emphasized another aspect of it. In the *Udana* we read (IV., 9):

> "He whom life does not torment,
> Who does not sorrow at the approach of death,
> If such a person is resolute and has seen Nirvana,
> In the midst of grief, he is without grief.
> The tranquil-minded Bhikkhu, who has uprooted the
> thirst for existence,
> Has ended the succession of births;
> He is born no more." <note>General D. M. Strong's translation, p. 64.</note>

<margin>✓</margin>
According to the Mahayana conception, Nirvana is not the annihilation of the world or the ending of life; rather, it is to live in the whirlpool of birth and death and yet to be above it. It is affirmation and fulfillment, and this is done not blindly or egoistically, for Nirvana is enlightenment. Let us see how this is.

<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

## *The Mahâyânistic Conception of Nirvâna.*

While the conception of Nirvâna seems to have remained indefinite and confused as far as Hînayânism is concerned, the Mahâyâna Buddhists have attached several definite shades of meaning to Nirvâna and tried to give each of them some special, distinctive character. When it is used in its most metaphysical sense, it becomes synonymous with Suchness (*tattva*) or with the Dharmakâya. When we speak of Buddha’s entrance into Nirvâna, it means the end of material existence—that is, death. When it is used in contrast to birth and death (*samsâra*) or to passion and sin (*kleça*), it signifies in the former case an eternal life or a state of immortality, and in the latter case a state of consciousness that follows from the recognition of the presence of the Dharmakâya in individual existences. Nirvâna has thus become a very comprehensive term, and this fact adds much to the confusion and misunderstanding with which it has been treated ever since Buddhism became known to the West. So-called "primitive Buddhism" is not altogether unfamiliar with all these meanings given to Nirvâna, though in some cases they might have been only faintly foreshadowed.

Most European missionaries and scholars have ignored this fact and wanted to see in Nirvâna only one definite, stereotyped sense which would loosen or untie all the difficult knots connected with its use. One scholar would select a certain passage in a certain sûtra, where the meaning

<summary>This page explains that Mahayana Buddhism assigns multiple meanings to Nirvana, including metaphysical "Suchness," the end of physical life, and a state of enlightened consciousness. It criticizes Western scholars for attempting to reduce Nirvana to a single, simplistic definition.</summary>

<keywords>Nirvâna, Mahâyâna, Hînayânism, Suchness, Dharmakâya, tattva, samsâra, kleça, primitive Buddhism, Western scholarship, sûtra</keywords>

is reasonably clear, and taking this as the key, they endeavor to solve all the rest; while another scholar would do the same thing with another passage from the scriptures and refute their fellow workers. The majority of them, however, have found it advantageous for missionary purposes to emphasize one meaning above all others that might possibly be considered the meaning of <term>Nirvâna</term>. This one meaning that has been made particularly prominent is its negative interpretation.

According to the <term>*Vijñânamâtra çâstra*</term> (Chinese version Vol. X.), the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhists distinguish four forms of <term>Nirvâna</term>. They are:

(1) <term>*Absolute Nirvâna*</term>, as a synonym of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. It is eternally pure in its essence and constitutes the truth and reality of all existence. Though it manifests itself in the world of defilement and relativity, its essence remains forever undefiled. While it encompasses within itself countless incomprehensible spiritual virtues, it is absolutely simple and immortal; its perfect tranquility may be compared to space, in which every conceivable motion is possible, but which remains unchanged in itself. It is universally present in all beings, whether animate or inanimate ¹, and makes their existence real. In one respect, it can be identified with them—that is, it can be viewed pantheistically; but in another respect, it is transcen-

transcendental, for every being, as it is, is not <term>Nirvâna</term>. This spiritual significance is, however, beyond the scope of ordinary human understanding and can be grasped only by the highest intelligence of <term>Buddha</term>.

(2) *<term>Upadhîçeṣa Nirvâna</term>*, or <term>Nirvâna</term> that has some residue. This is a state of enlightenment which can be attained by Buddhists in their lifetime. The <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which was dormant in them, is now awakened and freed from the "affective obstacles,"^1 but they are yet under the bondage of birth and death; and thus they are not yet absolutely free from the misery of life: something still remains in them that makes them suffer pain.

(3) *<term>Anupadhîçeṣa Nirvâna</term>*, or <term>Nirvâna</term> that has no residue. This is attained when the <term>Tathâgata-essence</term> (the <term>Dharmakâya</term>) is released from the pain of birth and death as well as from the curse of passion and sin. This form of <term>Nirvâna</term> seems to be what is generally understood by Western missionary scholars as the <term>Nirvâna</term> of Buddhists. While in their lifetime, they have been emancipated from the egoistic conception of the soul, they have practiced the <term>Eightfold Path</term>, and they

^1 <note>There are two obstacles to final emancipation: (1) affective, and (2) intellectual. The former is our unenlightened affective, emotional, or subjective life, and the latter is our intellectual prejudice. Buddhists should not only be pure in heart but also be perfect in intelligence. Pious people are, of course, saved from transmigration, but to attain perfect <term>Buddhahood</term>, they must have a clear, penetrating intellectual insight into the significance of life and existence and the destiny of the universe. This emphasizing of the rational element in religion is one of the most characteristic points of Buddhism.</note>

have destroyed all the roots of <term>karma</term> that make possible their <term>metempsychosis</term> in the world of birth and death (<term>samsâra</term>), although as the inevitable consequence of their previous <term>karma</term>, they still have to suffer all the evils inherent in material existence. But at last, they have had even this mortal coil dissolved away and have returned to the original Absolute from which, by virtue of ignorance, they had emerged and passed through a cycle of births and deaths. This state of supramundane bliss in the realm of the Absolute is <term>Anupadiçeṣa Nirvâna</term>, that is, <term>Nirvâna</term> that has no residue.

(4) *The <term>Nirvâna</term> that has no abode.* In this, the <term>Buddha-essence</term> has not only been freed from the curse of passion and sin (<term>kleça</term>), but also from the intellectual prejudice which most tenaciously clings to the mind. The <term>Buddha-essence</term> or the <term>Dharmakâya</term> is revealed here in its perfect purity. All-embracing love and all-knowing intelligence illuminate the path. He who has attained to this state of subjective enlightenment is said to have no abode, no dwelling place; that is to say, he is no longer subject to the <term>transmigration</term> of birth and death (<term>samsâra</term>), nor does he cling to <term>Nirvâna</term> as the abode of complete rest; in short, he is above <term>Samsâra</term> and <term>Nirvâna</term>. His sole object in life is to benefit all sentient beings to the end of time; but he proposes to do this not by his human conscious elaboration and striving. Simply actuated by his all-embracing love which belongs to the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, he wishes to deliver all his fellow creatures from misery, he does

not seek his own emancipation from the turmoil of life. He is fully aware of the transitory nature of worldly interests, but for this reason, he does not desire to shun them. With his all-knowing intelligence, he gains a spiritual insight into the ultimate nature of things and the final course of existence. He is one of those religious men "who weep as though they did not weep; who rejoice as though they did not rejoice; who buy as though they did not possess; who use this world as though not abusing it; for the fashion of this world passes away." Indeed, he is in one sense more than this; his life is full of positive activity because his heart and soul are devoted to leading all beings to final emancipation and supreme bliss. When a man attains this stage of spiritual life, he is said to be in the <term>Nirvâna</term> that has no abode.

A commentator on the <term>Vijñânamâtra Çâstra</term> adds that of these four forms of <term>Nirvâna</term>, the first is possessed by every sentient being, whether it is actualized in its human perfection or lying dormant *in posse* <note>potentially</note> and miserably obscured by ignorance; the second and third are attained by all the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term>, while it is a <term>Buddha</term> alone who is in possession of all four forms of <term>Nirvâna</term>.

It is manifest from the statement above that in <term>Mahâyânism</term>, <term>Nirvâna</term> has acquired several shades of psychological and ontological meaning. This apparent confusion, however, is due to the purely idealistic

<page-num>347</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

...tendency of <term>Mahâyânism</term>, which ignores the distinction usually made between being and thought, object and subject, the perceived and the perceiving. <term>Nirvâna</term> is not only a subjective state of enlightenment but an objective power through whose operation this beatific state becomes attainable. It does not simply mean a total absorption into the Absolute or emancipation from earthly desires in a lifetime, as exemplified in the life of the <term>Arhat</term>. <term>Mahâyânists</term> perceive in <term>Nirvâna</term> not only this, but also its identity with the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, or <term>Suchness</term>, and recognize its universal spiritual presence in all sentient beings.

When <term>Nâgârjuna</term> says in his <term>*Mâdhyamika Çâstra*</term>¹ that: "That is called <term>Nirvâna</term> which is not wanting, is not acquired, is not intermittent, is not non-intermittent, is not subject to destruction, and is not created;" he evidently speaks of <term>Nirvâna</term> as a synonym of <term>Dharmakâya</term>—that is, in its first sense as described above. <term>Chandra Kîrti</term>, therefore, rightly comments that <term>Nirvâna</term> is *sarva-kalpanâ-kshaya-rûpam*,² that is, that which transcends all the forms of determination.

---

Its original <term>Sanskrit</term> with the commentary of <term>Chandra Kîrti</term> has been edited by Satis Chandra Acharya and published by the Buddhist Text Society of India. The original lines run as follows (p. 193):
> "Aprahînam, asamprâptam, anucchinnam, açâçvatam,
> Aniruddham, anutpannam, evam nirvânam ucyate."</note>

² <note>Literally, that which is characterized by the absence of all characterization.</note>

It cites Nâgârjuna and Chandra Kîrti to define Nirvâna as a state that transcends all forms of characterization and dualistic distinctions.</summary>

<page-num>348</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

<term>Nirvâna</term> is an absolute; it is above the relativity of existence (<term>bhâva</term>) and non-existence (<term>abhâva</term>).¹

<term>Nirvâna</term> is sometimes described as possessing four attributes: (1) eternal (<term>nitya</term>), (2) blissful (<term>sukha</term>), (3) self-acting (<term>âtman</term>), and (4) pure (<term>çuçi</term>). Judging by these qualities attributed to <term>Nirvâna</term> as its essential features, <term>Nirvâna</term> is here again identified with the highest reality of <term>Buddhism</term>, which is the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. It is eternal because it is immaterial; it is blissful because it is beyond all suffering; it is self-acting because it knows no compulsion; it is pure because it is not defiled by passion and error.²

¹ <note>Compare the following from the <term>Mâdhyamika</term>:
> "Bhaved abhâvo bhâvaç ca nirvânam ubhayam katham :

> Or, "Tasmânna bhâvo nâbhâvo nirvânamiti yujyate"</note>

² <note>In the <term>Visuddhi-Magga</term> XXI (Warren's translation, p. 376 et seq.), we read that there are three starting points of deliverance arising from the consideration of the three predominant qualities of the components of being: 1. The consideration of their beginnings and ends leads the thoughts to the unconditioned; 2. Insight into their misery agitates the mind and leads the thoughts to the desireless; 3. The consideration of the components of being as lacking an ego leads the thoughts to the empty. These three, we are told, constitute the three aspects of <term>Nirvâna</term> as unconditioned, desireless, and empty. Here we have an instance in the so-called Southern "primitive" <term>Buddhism</term> of viewing <term>Nirvâna</term> in the <term>Mahâyânist</term> perspective which I have explained here at length.</note>

<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

### *Nirvâna in its Fourth Sense.*

No further explanation is needed for the first meaning of <term>Nirvâna</term>, for we have treated it already when explaining the nature of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. Nor is it necessary for us to dwell upon the second and third phases of it. Western missionary-scholars and Orientalists, however one-sided and often biased, have almost exhaustively investigated these points from the <term>Pâli</term> sources. What remains for us now is to analyze the <term>Mahayana</term> conception of <term>Nirvâna</term> which was stated above as its fourth meaning.

<term>Nirvâna</term>, briefly speaking, is a realization in this life of the all-embracing love and all-knowing intelligence of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. It is the unfolding of the reason for existence, which in ordinary human life remains more or less eclipsed by the shadow of ignorance and egoism. It does not consist in the mere observance of the moral precepts laid down by <term>Buddha</term>, nor in the blind following of the <term>Eightfold Path</term>, nor in retirement from the world and absorption in abstract meditation. The <term>Mahayana</term> <term>Nirvâna</term> is full of energy and activity which comes from the all-embracing love of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. There is no passivity in it, nor a keeping aloof from the hustle and bustle of worldliness.

the Master's view in various ways, each claiming the legitimate interpretation; that in view of this fact it is illogical to conclude that <term>Southern Buddhism</term> is the authoritative representation *par excellence* of original <term>Buddhism</term>, while the <term>Eastern</term> or the <term>Northern</term> is a mere degeneration.

<vocab>Nirvâna, Dharmakâya, Mahâyânistic, Pâli sources, Southern Buddhism, Eastern Buddhism, Northern Buddhism</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the fourth sense of Nirvana as an active realization of the Dharmakaya's love and intelligence within worldly life, rather than through passive withdrawal. It also argues that Eastern and Northern Buddhism are as legitimate as Southern Buddhism in their interpretation of the Buddha's teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvâna, Dharmakâya, Mahayana, Pâli sources, Southern Buddhism, Northern Buddhism, active enlightenment, religious interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 366 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the fourth sense of Nirvana in Mahayana Buddhism, describing how an enlightened being actively participates in the world of suffering without being corrupted by it.</meta>

<page-num>350</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

He who is in this <term>Nirvâna</term> does not seek rest in the destruction of human aspirations, nor does he flinch in the face of endless transmigration. On the contrary, he plunges himself into the ever-rushing current of <term>Samsâra</term> and sacrifices himself to save his fellow creatures from being eternally drowned in it.

Although the <term>Mahâyâna</term> <term>Nirvâna</term> is realized only in the mire of passions and errors, it is never contaminated by the filth of ignorance. Therefore, he who abides in <term>Nirvâna</term>, even in the whirlpool of egoism and in the darkness of sin, does not lose his all-seeing insight that penetrates deep into the ultimate nature of being. He is aware of the temporary nature of things. He knows that this life is a mere passing moment in the eternal manifestation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, whose work can be realized only in boundless space and endless time. Because he is fully awake to this knowledge, he never becomes lost in the world of sin. He lives in the world like the lotus flower—the emblem of purity—which grows out of the mire and yet does not share its defilement. He is also like a bird flying in the air that leaves no trace behind. He may also be likened to the clouds that spontaneously gather around a mountain peak and, soaring high as the wind blows, vanish into a region where no one knows. In short, he lives in, and yet beyond, the realm of <term>Samsâra</term> and <term>Nirvâna</term>.

We read in the *<term>Vimalakirti Sûtra</term>* (chapter VIII):

> "*<term>Vimalakirti</term>* asks <term>Mañjuçri</term>: 'How is it that you

It emphasizes that an enlightened person remains pure and insightful while working to save others, likened to a lotus flower growing in the mud.</summary>

Do we not see here a very explicit statement of the Mahayana sentiment?

### *Nirvâna and Samsâra are One.*

The most remarkable feature in the Mahayana conception of Nirvâna is expressed in this formula: “*Yas kleças so bodhi, yas samsâras tat nirvânam*.” That which is sin or passion is Intelligence; that which is birth and death (or transmigration) is Nirvâna. This is a rather bold and revolutionary proposition in the dogmatic history of Buddhism. But it is nothing more than the natural development of the spirit that was inspired by its founder.

<vocab>Mahâyânistic, Nirvâna, Samsâra, Intelligence, Buddha-essence, Emerson, Buddhism, Pauline epistle, transmigration</vocab>

<summary>Mañjuçri explains that enlightenment must be found within the depths of human passion and sin, a sentiment echoed by Western figures like St. Paul and Emerson. This leads to the central Mahayana doctrine that there is no fundamental difference between the cycle of suffering (Samsara) and liberation (Nirvana).</summary>
<keywords>Mañjuçri, Buddha-essence, Mahayana Buddhism, Nirvana, Samsara, Ralph Waldo Emerson, Vimalakirti Sutra, non-duality, enlightenment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the identity of Nirvana and Samsara, moving from the Vimalakirti Sutra to quotes from the Viçeṣacinta-brahma-paripṛccha Sûtra and the works of Nâgârjuna.</meta>

<page-num>353</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII. 353</header>

In the *Viçeṣacinta-brahma-paripṛccha Sûtra*,¹ it says (chapter II):

> “Saṃsâra is Nirvana because, when viewed from the ultimate nature of the Dharmakâya, there is nothing going out of or coming into existence <note>saṃsâra being only apparent</note>; Nirvana is saṃsâra when it is coveted and adhered to.”

In another place (*op. cit.*), the idea is expressed in much plainer terms: “The essence of all things is in truth free from attachment, attributes, and desires; therefore, they are pure. And because they are pure, we know that the essence of birth and death is the essence of Nirvana, and the essence of Nirvana is the essence of birth and death (*saṃsâra*). In other words, Nirvana is not to be sought outside of this world, which, though transient, is in reality nothing more than Nirvana itself. It is contrary to our reason to imagine that Nirvana and birth and death (*saṃsâra*) are separate, and that one lies outside the boundaries of the other; therefore, we cannot think that we attain Nirvana only after we have destroyed or escaped the world of birth and death. If we are not hindered by our confused subjectivity, our worldly life is an activity of Nirvana itself.”

Nâgârjuna repeats the same sentiment in his *Mâdhyamika Çâstra*, when he says:

Asanga goes a step further and boldly declares that all the Buddha-dharmas, of which Nirvâna or Dharmakâya forms the foundation, are characterized by the passions, errors, and sins of ordinary minds. He says in the *Mahâyâna-Sangraha Çâstra* (the Chinese Tripitaka, Japanese edition of 1881, *wang* VIII., p. 84):

"(1) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by eternity, because the Dharmakâya is eternal.

"(2) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by an extinguishing power, because they extinguish all obstacles to final liberation.

"(3) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by regeneration, because the Nirmânakâya [Body of Transformation] constantly regenerates.

"(4) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by the power of attainment, because through the attainment [of truth] they overcome the countless evil passions cherished by ignorant beings.

"(5) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by the desire to gain, ill temper, folly, and all the other

"(6) All Buddha-dharmas are characterized by non-attachment and non-defilement, for Suchness, which is made perfect by these virtues, cannot be defiled by any evil powers.

"(7) All Buddha-dharmas are above attachment and defilement, for though all Buddhas reveal themselves in the world, worldliness cannot defile them." ¹

Buddha-dharma means anything, or any virtue, or any faculty that belongs to Buddhahood. Non-attachment is a Buddha-dharma, love is a Buddha-dharma, wisdom is a Buddha-dharma, and in fact, anything is a Buddha-dharma which is an attribute of the Perfect One—not to mention the Dharmakâya or Nirvâna, which constitutes the very essence of Buddhahood. Therefore, the conclusion to be drawn from those seven propositions of Asanga quoted above is this: Not only is this world of constant transformation as a whole Nirvâna, but its apparent errors, sins, and evils are also the various phases of the manifestation of Nirvâna.

The above being the Mahayana view of Nirvâna, it is evident that Nirvâna is not something transcendental or that which stands above this world of birth

and death, joy and sorrow, love and hate, peace and struggle. Nirvâna is not to be sought in the heavens, nor after a departure from this earthly life, nor in the annihilation of human passions and aspirations. On the contrary, it must be sought in the midst of worldliness, as life with all its thrills of pain and pleasure is nothing more than Nirvâna itself. Extinguish your life and seek Nirvâna in the life of a hermit, and your Nirvâna is forever lost. Commit your aspirations, hopes, pleasures, and woes—and everything that makes up a life—to the eternal silence of the grave, and you bury Nirvâna, never to be recovered. In asceticism, or in meditation, or in ritualism, or even in metaphysics, the more impulsively you pursue Nirvâna, the further away it flies from you. It was the most serious mistake ever committed by any religious thinkers to imagine that Nirvâna, which is the complete satisfaction of our religious feeling, could be gained by laying aside all human desires, ambitions, hopes, pains, and pleasures. Have your own Bodhi (intelligence) thoroughly enlightened through love and knowledge, and everything that was thought sinful and filthy turns out to be of divine purity. It is the same human heart, formerly the fountain of ignorance and egoism, that is now the abode of eternal beatitude—Nirvâna shining in its intrinsic magnificence.

Suppose a torchlight is taken into a dark cell, which people had until now imagined to be the abode of hideous, uncanny goblins, and which on that account they wanted to have completely destroyed to the

ground. The bright light now brought in at once disperses the darkness, and every nook and corner within is perfectly illuminated. Everything in it now assumes its proper appearance. And to their surprise, people find that those figures which they formerly considered to be uncanny and horrible are nothing but huge precious stones, and they further learn that every one of those stones can be used in some way for the great benefit of their fellow creatures. The dark cell is the human heart before the enlightenment of <term>Nirvâna</term>; the torchlight is love and intelligence. When love warms and intelligence brightens, the heart finds that every passion and sinful desire that was the cause of unbearable anguish is now turned into a divine aspiration. The heart itself, however, remains the same, just as much as the cell, whose identity was never affected by either darkness or brightness. This parable nicely illustrates the <term>Mahâyânistic</term> doctrine of the identity of <term>Nirvâna</term> and <term>Samsâra</term>, and of the <term>Bodhi</term> and <term>Kleça</term>—that is, of intelligence and passion.

<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

## *The Middle Course.*

In one sense, the Buddha always showed an eclectic, conciliatory, and synthetic spirit in his teachings. He refused to listen to any extreme doctrine that elevates one end too high at the expense of the other and culminates in the collapse of the entire structure. When the Buddha left his seat of enlightenment under the Bodhi tree, he made it his mission to avoid both extremes: asceticism and hedonism. Throughout his life, he proved to be a calm, dignified, thoughtful, and well-disciplined person, and he was at no time irritable in character. In this latter respect, he was quite different from the sage of Nazareth, who in anger cast out all the merchants from the temple and overturned the tables of the money-changers, and who cursed the fig tree on which he could find no fruit but leaves, which were unfit to satisfy his hunger. The doctrine of the Middle Path (*Mâdhyamârga*), whatever it may mean morally and intellectually, always characterized the life and doctrine of the Buddha, as well as the later development of his teachings. His followers, however much they differed in their individual views, generally professed to steadily pursue the Middle Path as paved by the Master. Even when Nâgârjuna proclaimed his celebrated doctrine of the "Eight No's"—which to superficial critics seems like nothing but absolute nihilism—he said that the Middle Path could be found only in those eight no's. <note>¹ This is expressed in the first verse of the *Mâdhyamika Çâstra*, which runs as follows:</note>

<summary>This page defines the Buddha's Middle Path as a balanced approach between asceticism and hedonism, contrasting the Buddha's disciplined temperament with that of Jesus. It explains that even Nâgârjuna’s complex "Eight No's" were intended to lead to this same Middle Path.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Middle Path, specifically how Mahayana Buddhism applies the Buddha's synthetic method to reconcile the concepts of Nirvana and Samsara into a state of non-duality, drawing on the philosophy of Vasubandhu and the Eight Negations.</meta>

<page-num>359</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XIII.</header>

Mahayanism has certainly applied the Buddha’s synthetic method to its theory of Nirvana and ennobled it by fully developing its inherent significance. In the *Discourse on Buddha-essence*, Vasubandhu quotes the following passage from the *Shrimala Sutra*, which plainly shows the path along which the Mahayanists traveled before they reached their final conclusion: “Those who see only the transitoriness of existence are called nihilists, and those who see only the eternity of Nirvana are called eternalists. Both views are incorrect.” Vasubandhu then proceeds to say: “Therefore, the <term>Dharmakaya</term> of the <term>Tathagata</term> is free from both extremes, and for that reason it is called the Great Eternal Perfection. When viewed from this absolute standpoint of <term>Suchness</term>, the logical distinction between Nirvana and <term>Samsara</term> cannot in reality be maintained, and in this way we enter the realm of non-duality.” And this realm of non-duality is the Middle Path of Nirvana, understood not in its nihilistic sense, but in its Mahayanistic significance.

### *How to Realize Nirvana.*

How can we attain the Middle Path of Nirvana? How can we realize a life that is neither pessimistic asceticism nor materialistic hedonism? How can we steer through the whirlpools of <term>Samsara</term> without being

The answer to this can readily be given when we understand, as repeatedly stated above, that this life is the manifestation of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, and that the ideal of human existence is to realize, within the possibilities of one's mind and body, all that one can conceive of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. And this we have found to be all-embracing love and all-seeing intelligence. Destroy your ignorance then at one blow and be done with your egoism, and there springs forth an eternal stream of love and wisdom.

<term>Vasubandhu</term> says: “By virtue of <term>Prajñā</term> <note>intelligence or wisdom</note>, our egoistic thoughts are destroyed; by virtue of <term>Karuṇā</term> <note>love</note>, altruistic thoughts are cherished. By virtue of <term>Prajñā</term>, the affective attachment inherent in ordinary minds is abolished; by virtue of <term>Karuṇā</term>, the intellectual attachment possessed by the <term>Çrâvakas</term> and <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term> is abolished. By virtue of <term>Prajñā</term>, <term>Nirvâṇa</term> <note>in its transcendental sense</note> is not rejected; by virtue of <term>Karuṇā</term>, <term>Saṃsâra</term> <note>with its changes and transmigrations</note> is not rejected. By virtue of <term>Prajñā</term>, the truth of Buddhism is attained; by virtue of <term>Karuṇā</term>, all sentient beings are matured <note>for salvation</note>.”

The practical life of a Buddhist runs in two opposite, though not antagonistic, directions: one upward and the other downward, and the two are synthesized in the <term>Middle Path of Nirvâṇa</term>. The upward direction points to the intellectual comprehension of the truth, while the downward one points toward a realization of all-embracing—

When the intellectual side is too much emphasized at the expense of the emotional, we have a <term>Pratyekabuddha</term>, a solitary thinker whose fountain of tears is dry and does not flow over the sufferings of his fellow beings. When the emotional side alone is asserted to the extreme, love acquires the egoistic tint that colors everything coming in contact with it, because it does not discriminate and mistakes sensuality for spirituality. If it does not turn into sentimentalism, it will assume a hedonistic form. How many superstitious, foul, or even atrocious deeds in the history of religion have been committed under the beautiful name of religion, or love of God and mankind! It makes the blood run cold when we think how religious fanatics burned alive their rivals or opponents at the stake, cruelly butchered thousands of human lives within a day, and brought desolation and ruin throughout the land of their enemies—and all these works of the Devil were executed for the sheer love of God! Therefore, says <term>Devala</term>, the author of the *Discourse on the <term>Mahâpuruṣa</term>* <note>Great Man</note>:

> "The wise do not approve of loving-kindness without intelligence, nor do they approve of intelligence without loving-kindness; because one without the other prevents us from reaching the highest path."

Knowledge is the eye, love is the limb. Directed by the eye, the limb knows how to move; furnished with the limb, the eye can attain what it perceives. Love alone is blind, knowledge alone is lame. It is only when one is supplement—

...ed by the other that we have a perfect, complete man.

In Buddha, as the ideal human being, we recognize the perfection of love and intelligence; for it was in him that the <term>Dharmakâya</term> found its perfect realization in the flesh. But as far as the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> are concerned, their natural endowments are so diverse and their temperaments so varied that in some the intellectual elements are more predominant, while in others the emotional side is more pronounced; while some are more prone to practicality, others look more toward intellectualism. Thus, naturally, some <term>Bodhisattvas</term> will be more like philosophers than religious seers. In some cases, they may tend to emphasize the intellectual side of religion more than its emotional side and uphold the importance of <term>prajñâ</term> (intelligence) above that of <term>karuṇâ</term> (love). But the Middle Path of <term>Nirvâna</term> lies in the true harmonization of <term>prajñâ</term> and <term>karuṇâ</term>, of <term>bodhi</term> and <term>upâya</term>, of knowledge and love, and of intellect and feeling.

->*Love Awakens Intelligence*<-

But if we must choose between the two, Buddhists would say: let us first have all-embracing love; for it is love that awakens in us an intense desire to find the way to liberate the masses from perpetual suffering and eternal transmigration. The intellect will then endeavor to realize its highest possibilities; <term>Bodhi</term> will exhibit its fullest strength. When it is discovered that this life is an expression of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which is one and eternal, that

Therefore, we are true and real only when we are understood as one within the absolute <term>Dharmakâya</term>. The love of the <term>Bodhisattva</term>, which drove him to seek the highest truth, will now reveal its full significance.

This love—or faith in the <term>Mahâyâna</term>, as it is sometimes called—is felt somewhat vaguely when religious consciousness first awakens. It stirs the mind of the seeker, whose life has previously been consumed by all forms of self-centered thought and desire. He no longer finds pure satisfaction, as the <term>Çrâvakas</term> or the <term>Pratyekabuddhas</term> do, in his own individual liberation from the curse of <term>Samsâra</term>. However sweet the taste of release from the bonds of ignorance may be, it lacks something that makes freedom truly satisfying to the <term>Bodhisattva</term>, who considers others more than himself. For liberation to be both sweet and acceptable, it must be seasoned with a loving-kindness that embraces all fellow beings as if they were his own children. 

The liberation of the <term>Çrâvaka</term> or the <term>Pratyekabuddha</term> is like delicious food that lacks salt, for it is nothing more than a dry, formal, philosophical escape. Love is what inspires a person to reach beyond their own interests. It is the mother of all <term>Buddhas</term> and <term>Bodhisattvas</term>. The sacred motive that leads them to renounce a life of <term>Nirvânic</term> self-satisfaction is nothing other than their boundless love for all beings. They do

Love, therefore, bestows on us two spiritual benefits: (1) It saves all beings from misery, and (2) it awakens in us the <term>Buddha</term>-intelligence.

The following passages quoted at random from Devala’s *Mahâpuruṣa* will help our readers to understand the true significance of <term>Nirvâna</term> and the value of love (<term>karuṇâ</term>) as estimated by the <term>Mahâyânists</term>.

“Those who are afraid of transmigration and seek their own benefits and happiness in final emancipation are not at all comparable to those <term>Bodhisattvas</term> who rejoice when they come to assume a material existence once again, for it affords them another opportunity to benefit others. Those who are only capable of feeling their own selfish sufferings may enter into <term>Nirvâna</term> <note>and not trouble themselves with the sufferings of other creatures like themselves</note>; but the <term>Bodhisattva</term> who feels in himself all the sufferings of his fellow beings as his own—how can he bear the thought of leaving others behind while he is on his way to final emancipation, and when he himself is resting in <term>Nirvânic</term> quietude? . . . . <term>Nirvâna</term>, in truth, consists in rejoicing that others are being made happy, and <term>Samsâra</term> consists in not feeling this way. He who feels a universal love for his fellow creatures will rejoice in distributing blessings among them and find his <term>Nirvâna</term> in doing so.” <note>1</note>

> "The loving heart of a <term>Bodhisattva</term> is troubled by one thing: that all beings are constantly tortured and threatened by all sorts of pain."

Let us quote another interesting passage from a *<term>Mahâyâna sûtra</term>*.

When <term>Vimalakirti</term> was asked why he did not feel well, he made the following reply, which is full of religious significance: "From ignorance there arises desire, and that is the cause of my illness. As all sentient beings are ill, so am I ill. When all sentient beings are healed of their illness, I shall be healed of my illness, too. Why? The <term>Bodhisattva</term> suffers birth and death because of sentient beings. As there is birth and death, so there is illness. When sentient beings are delivered from illness, the <term>Bodhisattvas</term> will suffer no more illness. When an only son in a good family is sick, the parents feel sick too; when he has recovered, they are well again. So it is with the <term>Bodhisattva</term>. He loves all sentient beings as his own children. When they are sick, he is sick too. When they are recovered, he is well again. Do you wish to know from where this [sympathetic] illness comes? The illness of the <term>Bodhisattva</term> comes from his all-embracing love (<term>mahâkarunâ</term>)."

This gospel of universal love is the fulfillment of all religious emotions, whatever their origin. Without this, there is no religion—that is, no religion that is animated with life and spirit. For it is in the fact

# CHAPTER XIII.

...and nature of things that we are not moved by mere contemplation or mere philosophizing. Every religion may have its own way of intellectually interpreting this fact, but the practical result remains the same everywhere: namely, that it cannot survive without the animating energy of love. Whatever sound and fine reasoning there may be in the doctrine of the <term>Çrâvaka</term> and the <term>Pratyekabuddha</term>, the force that is destined to conquer the world and to deliver us from misery is not intellectual thought, but the will—that is, the <term>pûrvapranidhâna</term> of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>.

### *Conclusion.*

We now conclude. What is most evident from what we have seen above is that the <term>Mahâyâna</term> <term>Nirvâna</term> is not the annihilation of life but its enlightenment; it is not the nullification of human passions and aspirations but their purification and ennoblement. This world of eternal <term>transmigration</term> is not a place which should be shunned as the playground for evils, but should be regarded as the place of ever-present opportunities given to us for the purpose of unfolding all our spiritual possibilities and powers for the sake of the universal welfare. There is no need for us to shrink, like the snail into its cozy shelter, before the duties and burdens of life. The <term>Bodhisattva</term>, on the contrary, finds <term>Nirvâna</term> in a chain of births and deaths and boldly faces the problem of evil and solves it by purifying the <term>Bodhi</term> from subjective ignorance.

<summary>This concluding section argues that Mahayana Buddhism views Nirvana as the purification of life rather than its destruction, and emphasizes that practitioners should engage with the world to achieve universal welfare through love and will.</summary>

<keywords>Mahayana Buddhism, Nirvana, Bodhisattva, Dharmakaya, universal love, enlightenment, transmigration, purification, spiritual power</keywords>

His rule of conduct is:

> “Sabba pâpassa akaranam,
> Kusalassa upasampada,
> Sacitta pariyodapanam;
> Etam buddhânu sâsanam.” <note>The <term>Dharmapada</term>, XIV. 5. Mr. A. J. Edmunds’s translation is:
> “Ceasing to do all wrong,
> Initiation into goodness,
> Cleansing the heart:
> This is the religion of the Buddhas.”</note>

His aspirations are solemnly expressed in this, which we hear daily recited in the <term>Mahâyâna</term> Buddhist temples, monasteries, and seminaries:

> “Sentient beings, however innumerable, I take a vow to save;
> Evil passions, however inextinguishable, I take a vow to destroy;
> The avenues of truth, however numberless, I take a vow to study;
> The way of the Enlightened, however unsurpassable, I take a vow to attain.”

And a tireless pursuit of these noble aims will finally lead to the heaven of the Buddhists, <term>Nirvâna</term>, which is not a state of eternal quietude, but the source of energy and intelligence.

By way of summary, and to avoid all misconceptions, let me repeat once more that <term>Nirvâna</term> is thus no negation of life, nor is it an idle contemplation on the misery of existence. The life of a Buddhist by no means consists of the monotonous repetition of reciting the <term>sûtras</term> and going his rounds for meals. Far from that. He enters into all forms of life-activity, for he does not believe that universal emanci-

It argues that a Buddhist's life is defined by active engagement with the world rather than mere ritual or begging for meals.</summary>

# CHAPTER XIII.

pation is achieved by imprisoning oneself in the cloister.

Theoretically speaking, <term>Nirvâna</term> is the dispersion of the clouds of ignorance hovering around the light of <term>Bodhi</term>. Morally, it is the suppression of egoism and the awakening of love (*karunâ*). Religiously, it is the absolute surrender of the self to the will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>. When the clouds of ignorance are dispersing, our intellectual horizon gets clearer and wider; we perceive that our individual existences are like bubbles and lightning flashes, but that they obtain reality in their oneness with the <term>Body of Dharma</term>. This conviction compels us to eternally abandon our old egoistic conception of life. The ego finds its significance only when it is conceived in relation to the non-ego, that is, to the *other*; in other words, self-love has no meaning whatever unless it is purified by love for others. But this love for others must not remain blind and unenlightened; it must be in harmony with the will of the <term>Dharmakâya</term>, which is the norm of existence and the reason for being. The mission of love is ennobled and fulfilled in its true sense when we come to the faith that says, "your will be done." Love without this resignation to the divine ordinance is merely another form of egoism: the root is already rotten, so how can its trunk, stems, leaves, and flowers make a true growth?

Let us then conclude with the following reflections of the <term>Bodhisattva</term>, in which we read the whole significance of <term>Buddhism</term>.

<summary>This page defines Nirvana as the dispersion of ignorance and the awakening of universal love through surrender to the Dharmakaya. It emphasizes that individual existence finds true meaning only through selflessness and alignment with the divine will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bodhisattva's path, specifically detailing the selfless vows and reflections regarding the salvation of all sentient beings.</meta>

# CHAPTER XIII.

"Having practiced all the six virtues of perfection (*pâramitâ*) and countless other meritorious deeds, the <term>Bodhisattva</term> reflects in this way:

> " 'All the good deeds practiced by me are for the benefit of all sentient beings, for their ultimate purification <note>from sin</note>. By the merit of these good deeds, I pray that all sentient beings may be released from the countless sufferings they endure in their various abodes of existence. By the dedication (*parivarta*) of these deeds, I would be a haven for all beings and deliver them from their miserable existences; I would be a great beacon-light to all beings and dispel the darkness of ignorance and make the light of intelligence shine.'

"He reflects again in this way:

> " 'All sentient beings are creating evil <term>karma</term> in countless ways, and by reason of this karma, they suffer countless sufferings. They do not recognize the <term>Tathâgata</term>, do not listen to the <term>Good Law</term>, and do not pay homage to the congregation of holy men. All these beings carry an immense amount of great evil karma and are destined to suffer in countless ways. For their sake, I will, in the midst of the three evil creations, endure all their sufferings and deliver every one of them. Painful as these sufferings are, I will not retreat, I will not be frightened, I will not be negligent, and I will not forsake my fellow beings. Why? Because it is the will <note>of the Dharmakâya</note> that all sentient beings should be universally emancipated.'

<summary>The Bodhisattva resolves to dedicate all personal merit to the purification and deliverance of all beings, vowing to endure any suffering to fulfill the Dharmakâya's will for universal salvation.</summary>

He reflects again in this way:

Through the dedication (*parivarta*) of all my merits, I would make every one of my fellow-creatures happy and joyous." (The *Avatamsaka Sûtra*, fascicle XIV).

# APPENDIX

<vocab>Appendix</vocab>

<page-num>371</page-num>

# CHAPTER XIII.

He reflects again in this way:

> "My conduct will be like the sun god who, with his universal illumination, does not seek any reward; who does not cease, on account of one unrighteous person, to make a great display of his magnificent glory; who, on account of one unrighteous person, does not abandon the salvation of all beings. Through the dedication (*parivarta*) of all my merits, I would make every one of my fellow creatures happy and joyous." (The *Avatamsaka Sûtra*, fascicle XIV).

---

<vocab>sun god, illumination, salvation, dedication, parivarta, merits, Avatamsaka Sûtra</vocab>

<summary>The Bodhisattva compares his selfless commitment to the sun, which provides light to everyone regardless of their righteousness. He vows to dedicate his merits to the happiness of all fellow creatures.</summary>

<keywords>Bodhisattva, Avatamsaka Sutra, parivarta, salvation, sun god analogy, merits, universal illumination</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the Bodhisattva's selfless conduct and universal illumination in Chapter XIII to a series of hymns concerning the Mahāyāna faith, specifically focusing on the nature of the Dharmakâya.</meta>

# HYMNS OF MAHÂYÂNA FAITH.

## DHARMAKÂYA (TATHÂGATA).¹

In all beings, the Dharmakâya abides;
With all virtues dissolved within it, it lives in eternal calmness.
It knows neither birth nor death, coming nor going;
Not one, not two; not being, not becoming;
Yet present everywhere in the worlds of beings:
This is what is perceived by all Tathâgatas.
All virtues, material and immaterial,
dependent on the Dharmakâya, are eternally pure within it.

Like the sky is the ultimate nature of the Dharmakâya;
Far away from the six dusts, it is free from defilement.
The Dharmakâya has no form and is devoid of all attributes,
in which both actor and action are void:
The Dharmakâya of all Buddhas, thus beyond comprehension,
quells all the struggles of sophistry and dialectics,
distances all the efforts of intellection;
all thoughts are dead within it, and suchness alone abides.

<summary>This hymn describes the Dharmakâya as the eternal, all-pervasive essence of all Buddhas, existing beyond dualities, intellectual comprehension, and the defilements of the material world. It characterizes the ultimate reality as a state of eternal calmness and "suchness" where all thoughts and actions are transcended.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakâya, Tathâgata, Mahâyâna, suchness, six dusts, emptiness, transcendence, Buddha-nature</keywords>

--- Page 392 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Hymns of Mahāyāna Faith," specifically continuing the description of the Dharmakāya of the Tathāgata, focusing on its manifestation across the universe and its relation to all Buddhas.</meta>

APPENDIX.

## THE DHARMAKÂYA OF TATHÂGATA. ²

In all the worlds throughout the ten directions,
O you sentient creatures living there,
behold the most venerable of men and gods,
whose spiritual Dharma-body is immaculate and pure.

As through the power of one mind
a host of thoughts is evolved,
so from the one Dharma-body of Tathâgata
all the Buddha-bodies are produced.

In Bodhi, nothing dual exists,
nor is any thought of self present:
the Dharma-body, undefiled and non-dual,
manifests itself everywhere in its full splendor.

Its ultimate reality is like the vastness of space;
its manifested forms are like magic shows;
its excellent virtues are inexhaustible—
this, indeed, is the spiritual state of Buddhas only.

All the Buddhas of the present, past, and future—
each one of them arises from the Dharma-body,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;immaculate and pure.
Responding to the needs of sentient creatures,
they manifest themselves everywhere, assuming
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;a beautiful corporeality.

They never planned in advance
that they would manifest in such and such forms.
They are separated from all desire and anxiety,
and their responses are free and spontaneous.

<vocab>Dharmakâya, Tathâgata, sentient creatures, Dharma-body, Buddha-bodies, Bodhi, Buddhas, corporeality</vocab>

<summary>This hymn describes the Dharmakāya as the pure, non-dual source from which all Buddhas manifest throughout the universe. It emphasizes that these manifestations occur spontaneously and without premeditation to serve the needs of sentient beings.</summary>
<keywords>Dharmakāya, Tathāgata, Bodhi, Buddha-bodies, non-duality, sentient beings, manifestation, spontaneity</keywords>

--- Page 393 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Hymns of Mahāyāna Faith," further describing the nature of the Dharma-body and shifting focus to the manifestation and incomprehensible nature of the Tathāgata.</meta>

APPENDIX.

They do not negate the phenomenality of dharmas,
nor do they affirm the world of individuals;
but manifesting themselves in all forms,
they teach and convert all sentient creatures.

The Dharma-body is not changeable,
nor is it unchangeable;
all dharmas [in essence] are without change,
but manifestations are changeable.

Sambodhi knows no bounds,
extending as far as the limits of the Dharmaloka itself;
its depths are bottomless and its extent limitless;
words and speeches are powerless to describe it.

Of all the ways that lead to Enlightenment,
the Tathâgata knows the true significance;
wandering freely all over the worlds,
he encounters obstacles nowhere.

---

## THE TATHÂGATA. (1) ³

The Tathâgata did not appear on earth,
nor did he enter into Nirvana;
by the supreme power of his inmost will,
he reveals himself freely as he wills. ⁴

This fact is beyond comprehension,
as it does not belong to the sphere of a limited consciousness;
only an intelligence that is perfect and has gone beyond
is able to have insight into the realm of Buddhas.

<vocab>dharma, Dharma-body, Sambodhi, Dharmaloka, Enlightenment, Tathâgata, Nirvana, Buddha</vocab>

<summary>This text describes the Dharma-body as a reality that transcends change and the Tathāgata as a being who manifests throughout the world without being limited by physical birth or death. It emphasizes that the true nature of the Buddha is an infinite mystery that can only be grasped by a perfected intelligence.</summary>
<keywords>Dharma-body, Sambodhi, Dharmaloka, Tathāgata, Nirvana, manifestation, non-duality, Enlightenment, Buddhist philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 394 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Tathāgata's nature, emphasizing that while he manifests in physical and audible forms, his true essence remains an incomprehensible mystery of the Dharma-body.</meta>

APPENDIX.

The material body is not the Tathâgata,
Nor is the voice, nor the sound:
Yet he is not beyond the visible and the audible:
The Buddha has indeed a miraculous power.

People of little faith are unable to know
The inmost sanctuary of Buddhahood.
It is by the perfecting of primordial karma-intelligence
That the realm of all Buddhas is revealed.

All Buddhas come from nowhere,
And depart for nowhere:
The Body of Dharma that is pure, immaculate, and
&emsp;&emsp;incomprehensible,
Is invested with a power miraculously free.

In an infinity of worlds,
Revealing itself in the body of the Tathâgata,
It universally preaches the Law supremely excellent,
And in its heart no attachment lingers.

An intellect that knows no limits or bounds
Perceives no obstacles in all dharmas,
And penetrates into the depths of the Dharmaloka,
Revealing itself with a power miraculously divine.

All sentient beings and all creatures,
It understands thoroughly without difficulty:
Its Bodies of Transformation are innumerable,
And universally revealed in all the worlds.

Those who seek after All-knowledge
May in course of time attain perfect enlightenment;
Let them above all purify the heart
And complete their discipline in Bodhisattvahood.

<vocab>Tathâgata, Buddhahood, karma-intelligence, Dharma, dharmas, Dharmaloka, Bodies of Transformation, All-knowledge, Bodhisattvahood</vocab>

<summary>This text explains that the true nature of a Buddha transcends physical form and sound, originating from a state of "nowhere" to manifest throughout the universe. It emphasizes that only through purifying the heart and perfecting one's intelligence can one understand the miraculous power and universal presence of the Dharma-body.</summary>
<keywords>Tathâgata, Buddhahood, karma-intelligence, Dharma-body, Dharmaloka, Bodies of Transformation, enlightenment, Bodhisattvahood, Mahayana Buddhism</keywords>

> And then they will see the Tathâgata’s
> Immeasurable power that comes from his free will;
> They are devoid of all doubts, and accompanied
> By sages whose virtue is unsurpassable.

### THE TATHÂGATA (2). ⁵

> The Tathâgata, in pure golden color,
> And in person resplendent and majestic,
> In innumerable ages past,
> Has accumulated all merits.
>
> With bliss and wisdom all in perfection,
> And attaining the highest enlightenment,
> And animated with a great loving heart,
> He now appears in this world of endurance.
>
> Men and devas and the eight hosts of demons,
> All pay him most reverent homage,
> Who, from his inmost self-being,
> Preaches the deepest spiritual Dharma.
>
> Which is so unfathomably deep,
> That the Buddha alone can understand it:
> Multitudes of beings, ignorant and blind,
> Are unable to comprehend it when listening.
>
> The Tathâgata is the great leader of beings;
> With skill that is excellent and marvelous,
> Guiding all those ignorant souls,
> He brings them by degrees to Enlightenment.

<vocab>Tathâgata, devas, Dharma, Buddha, Enlightenment</vocab>

<summary>This text describes the Tathâgata as a majestic being of supreme merit and wisdom who manifests in the physical world out of compassion. It emphasizes that while his profound teachings are difficult for most to comprehend, he uses his marvelous skill to guide all beings toward enlightenment.</summary>

<keywords>Tathâgata, Buddha, Dharma, Enlightenment, merit, compassion, devas, spiritual guidance</keywords>

--- Page 396 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise and description of the Tathâgata and his teachings, transitioning from his physical presence to the eternal, luminous nature of the heart of all beings and the universal application of the Dharma.</meta>

<page-num>380</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

> The heart of all beings is miraculously bright,
> And eternally calm in its being.
> Pure, immaculate, and free from defilement,
> It is filled with all merits.
>
> Its essence is like the sky:
> Devoid of all limitations,
> It knows neither birth nor death,
> And there is neither coming nor departing.
>
> Eternally abiding in the <term>Dharma-essence</term>,
> It is as immovable as <term>Mount Sumeru</term>;
> The oneness of all beings within it
> Is indeed beyond finite knowledge.
>
> Common minds from time immemorial,
> Blindly clinging to all passions,
> Are thrown deep into the ocean of pain,
> And do not know how to escape.
>
> The most profound doctrine of the <term>Tathâgata</term>,
> Full of meaning, spiritual and transcendental;
> For recipient intellects of all degrees,
> He unfolds the Law in perfect harmony.
>
> A shower of one taste from above
> Covering all the ten directions,
> Grasses and trees, woods and forests,
> Roots and trunks, large and small,
>
> Of everything growing on this vast earth,
> There is nothing that does not benefit from it.
> The Law delivered by the <term>Tathâgata</term>
> May even be compared to it.

<summary>This page describes the luminous and eternal nature of the heart inherent in all beings, which is compared to the immovable Mount Sumeru and the vast sky. It uses the metaphor of a single, nourishing rain to explain how the Tathâgata’s profound teachings benefit all living things according to their individual capacities.</summary>

<keywords>Dharma-essence, Mount Sumeru, Tathâgata, Buddha-nature, Enlightenment, Law of Buddhism, spiritual merit, compassion</keywords>

--- Page 397 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of the Buddha's teaching, specifically how his single voice is understood by all according to their individual needs, and transitions into a series of verses on the transformative power of Repentance.</meta>

<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<page-num>381</page-num>

With one voice which is wondrous,
He gives utterance to thoughts innumerable,
That are received by audiences of every sort,
Each understanding them in their own way.

In this way among the assembly,
There is no one who does not enter upon Buddha-knowledge;
Such is Buddha's miraculous power,
Truly called "Incomprehensible."

---

### REPENTANCE. ⁶

<margin>✓</margin>
Those who repent as prescribed by the Dharma,
Altogether uproot their earthly sins;
As fire on doomsday will consume the world,
With its mountain peaks and infinite seas.

Repentance burns up the fuel of earthly desires;
Repentance leads sinners to heaven;
Repentance imparts the bliss of the four Dhyânas;
Repentance brings showers of jewels and gems;

Repentance renders a holy life firm as a diamond;
Repentance transports one to the palace of everlasting bliss;
Repentance releases one from the prison of the triple world;
Repentance makes the bloom of the Bodhi blossom.

---

<vocab>Buddha-knowledge, Repentance, Dharma, Dhyânas, Bodhi, triple world</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the Buddha’s miraculous ability to communicate profound truths to diverse audiences simultaneously and details how the practice of repentance purifies the soul and leads to spiritual liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha-knowledge, Repentance, Dharma, Dhyânas, Bodhi, salvation, spiritual purification, triple world</keywords>

<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<page-num>382</page-num>

### ALL BEINGS ARE MOTHERS AND FATHERS.

All sentient beings in <term>transmigration</term> travel through the six <term>gatis</term>,
Like a wheel revolving without beginning or end,
Becoming in turn fathers and mothers, men and women:
Throughout generations and generations, each owes something to others.

You should then regard all beings as fathers and mothers;
Though this truth is too hidden to be recognized without
->the aid of Holy Knowledge,<-
All men are your fathers,
All women are your mothers.

While not yet repaying the love received from them in your prior lives,
Why should you, thinking otherwise, harbor enmity?
Always thinking of love, endeavor to benefit one another;
And do not provoke hostility, quarreling, or insults.

---

### THE TEN PÂRAMITÂS.

O you, sons of Buddha, trained in the Holy Way,
With the Heart of Highest Intelligence awakened,
And living in seclusion at the <term>Aranyaka</term>,
You should practice the ten <term>pâramitâs</term>.

At your daily meal, think first of almsgiving,
And also distribute the Treasure of Law among all beings;
When the three rings <note>7</note> are pure, it is called true charity;
Through this practice, the merits of discipline are perfected.

<vocab>transmigration, gatis, pâramitâs, Aranyaka, almsgiving, three rings</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that all sentient beings have been related to one another as parents across countless lifetimes and begins an exhortation to practice the Ten Pâramitâs, starting with the perfection of charity.</summary>
<keywords>transmigration, gatis, reincarnation, universal love, Pâramitâs, Aranyaka, almsgiving, Buddhist ethics</keywords>

--- Page 399 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the Ten Pâramitâs from the previous page, specifically detailing the merits of almsgiving, the sacrifice of "blood-giving," the explanation of the Law, and the perfections of discipline, meekness, and energy.</meta>

<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<page-num>383</page-num>

> Would you understand the merits of <term>almsgiving</term>?
> Know that it comes from a pure heart, and not from
> the wealth given;
> A precious treasure with an unclean heart,
> Is surpassed by a mite with a clean heart.
> 
> Giving wealth is a <term>dâna-pâramitâ</term>,
> And there are other <term>dâna-pâramitâs</term>:
> To give away one's life, wife, or children,
> This is called <term>blood-giving</term>.
> 
> Should a man of good family come and ask for the <term>Law</term>
> Let him have all the <term>Mahâyâna sûtras</term> explained,
> And awaken in him the Heart of <term>Highest Intelligence</term>;
> This is called a true <term>pâramitâ</term>.
> 
> With sympathy and pure faith and conscience,
> Embrace all beings and free them from greed,
> That they might attain to the <term>Highest Intelligence</term> of the
> <term>Tathâgata</term>:
> The giving of wealth and of the <term>Law</term> is the first <term>pâramitâ</term>.
> 
> Firmly observing the three sets of the <term>Bodhisattva-çîlas</term>, <gloss>8</gloss>
> O you, develop the <term>Bodhi</term>, distance yourselves from birth-and-death,
> Guard the <term>Law</term> of <term>Buddha</term> and make it live long in the world,
> Repent the violation of the <term>çîlas</term>, and be always mindful
> of the true ones.
> 
> Subdue anger and hate and cultivate love
> and sympathy in your heart;
> Mindful of past <term>karma</term>, do not harbor evil thoughts
> against offenders;
> Do not be reluctant to sacrifice your life for the sake of all beings:
> This is called the <term>pâramitâ</term> of meekness.
> 
> In practicing what is hard to practice, do not hesitate for a moment;
> With ever-increasing energy through three <term>asankheya kalpas</term>,
> Do not defile yourselves, but always discipline the heart;
> And for the sake of all creatures seek salvation.

<summary>This page describes the first four Pâramitâs (perfections): giving (dâna), discipline (çîla), patience/meekness (kshânti), and energy (vîrya). It emphasizes that true charity comes from a pure heart and includes the sharing of spiritual knowledge as well as physical sacrifice.</summary>
<keywords>almsgiving, dâna-pâramitâ, blood-giving, Law, Mahâyâna, sûtras, Highest Intelligence, Tathâgata, Bodhisattva-çîlas, Bodhi, Buddha, karma, asankheya kalpas, meekness</keywords>

--- Page 400 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the Ten Pâramitâs from the previous page, specifically detailing the perfections of meditation (Samâdhi), wisdom, and skill in means, which lead to spiritual freedom and the benefit of all beings.</meta>

<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<page-num>384</page-num>

Entering into and rising from <term>Samâdhi</term>, spiritual freedom
    is obtained:
Transforming yourselves and traveling in all the ten quarters,
Remove the cause of evil desire for all beings,
And let them seek deliverance in the doctrine of <term>Samâdhi</term>.

Would you desire to attain to True Intelligence?
Approach <term>Bodhisattvas</term> and <term>Tathâgatas</term> as friends;
Gladly listening to the transcendental and sublime doctrine,
Attain the three disciplines <note>9</note> and remove the two obstacles <note>10</note>.

Recognizing the difference in the disposition of beings,
Apply the medicine proper for each disease:
Love and sympathy, skill and expediency, each fitting the case,
Try the proper means for the benefit of the multitudes.

Would you know the true meaning of existence?
The middle path lies in non-attachment, neither "yes"
    nor "no";
Pure intelligence is unfathomable and unites in <term>Suchness</term>;
Identify mine with yours, embracing the whole.

By the force of intellect, grasping the nature of beings,
Teach the masses, each in accord with his capacity;
The force of intellect, penetrating through the hearts of all
    beings,
Destroys the root of <term>transmigration</term> in birth and death.

Intelligently judging between black and white,
Conscientiously take hold of one and put the other aside,
    and let each rest in its place;
<term>Samsâra</term> and <term>Nirvâna</term> are but one in their essence;
Fulfilling the meaning of existence, do not cherish self-conceit.

These ten deeds of excellence
Comprise all eighty-four thousand virtues;
Each in its class excels all the others,
And is called the <term>Pâramitâ</term> of the <term>Bodhisattva</term>.

<vocab>Samâdhi, Bodhisattvas, Tathâgatas, three disciplines, two obstacles, Suchness, transmigration, Samsâra, Nirvâna, Pâramitâ</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of the Ten Pâramitâs, focusing on the virtues of meditation, wisdom, and the removal of obstacles to achieve spiritual freedom. It explains that these ten perfections encompass all virtues and are the defining path of a Bodhisattva.</summary>

> Becalm the disturbed mind of all beings;
> Eighty-four thousand <term>dhâranîs</term>

### THE BODHI. <note>11</note>

> All things are of the <term>Bodhi</term>,
> The <term>Bodhi</term> is in all things;
> The <term>Bodhi</term> and all things are one:
> Who knows this is called the World-honored.

---

### NIRVANA AND THE THREE EVILS.

> <term>Nirvana</term> is Greed;
> So is hate, and folly;
> In these three passions
> There dwells a <term>Buddha-dharma</term> inexpressible.
> 
> Who distinguishes, thinking,
> "There's greed, and hate, and folly,"
> He is as far from Buddha,
> As heaven from earth.
> 
> The <term>Bodhi</term> and greed,
> They're one, not two:
> Out of one <term>Dharma-gate</term> comes all;
> Here's sameness, no diversity.

<sig>25</sig>

<summary>This page presents verses on the power of Samadhi and Dharani to calm the mind and the role of the Buddha in liberating beings. It concludes with teachings on the nature of Bodhi and Nirvana, emphasizing that enlightenment and worldly passions are essentially one in the state of non-duality.</summary>

<keywords>Samadhi, Dharani, Dharma, Bodhi, Nirvana, Non-duality, Buddha-dharma, Enlightenment, Greed, Hate, Folly</keywords>

<language>English</language>

<page-num>386</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

> Hearing this, the common people stand aghast;
> They are far from the Buddha-path.
> The heart, when free from greed, <note>12</note>
> Is never troubled.
> 
> In whose mind the self is still lurking,
> And who imagines that he has something,
> This man is called greedy,
> And he is bound for hell.
> 
> Whatever the true nature of greed is,
> That is the nature of the <term>Buddha-dharma</term>;
> Whatever the nature of the <term>Buddha-dharma</term> is,
> That is the nature of greed. <note>13</note>
> 
> These two are of one nature;
> That is, of "no-nature";
> Whoever knows this truth,
> Would be the world-leader.

---

### NON-ATMAN AND PREJUDICE. <note>14</note>

> There once was an ignorant man;
> He was so afraid of the sky
> That he wandered away, crying piteously.
> He was fearful of its sudden collapse.
> But the sky has no boundary,
> And it will not be harmful to anyone.
> It was due to his ignorance
> That he trembled so fitfully.
> It is even so with the <term>Bhikshus</term> and <term>Brahmans</term>
> Who are prejudiced.
> Learning that the world is empty,
> They are alarmed at heart;
> And they wrongly imagine that if the nature of the <term>Atman</term> were empty,
> Nothingness would be the end of all work.

<vocab>Buddha-path, Buddha-dharma, greed, no-nature, world-leader, Non-Atman, Bhikshus, Brahmans, Atman, Nothingness</vocab>

<summary>This page contains verses describing the non-dual nature of greed and the Buddha-dharma, explaining that they share a single "no-nature." It also includes a parable of a man afraid of the sky falling to illustrate how certain practitioners wrongly fear that the doctrine of emptiness leads to total nothingness.</summary>

<keywords>Buddha-path, Buddha-dharma, greed, non-duality, no-nature, Non-Atman, Bhikshus, Brahmans, Atman, emptiness, nihilism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the appendix of verses, focusing on the concepts of non-action, self-delusion through sensory attachment, and the ultimate unity of all things in Reality (Bodhi).</meta>
