<header>APPENDIX.</header>

## NON-ACTION.

As the emptiness of the sky,
Being so clear and free of cloud and fog,
Over the earth below,
Shows no signs of giving a shower:
So the enlightened
Reveal no learning, no intelligence:
And we, sentient beings,
Can trace no efforts in their delivery of the Law. <note>15</note>

---

## SELF-DELUSION.

There once lived a painter,
Who painted such a monstrous <term>Yaksha</term>
That he himself was terrified,
And losing all his senses, he fell to the ground:
It is even so with ordinary minds;
Infatuated and self-deluded by the senses,
They are unaware of their own error,
And go from birth to birth without end.

---

## ALL IN ONE.

As all the waters in the valley
Are emptied into the ocean,
Which is of one and the same taste:
So the enlightened,
Whatever is
Good and beneficial,
Dedicate to <term>Bodhi</term>
And to that Reality
In which all things become of one and the same taste.

<vocab>Non-Action, Self-Delusion, All in One, Yaksha, sentient beings, Law, Bodhi, Reality</vocab>

<summary>This page contains three poems illustrating Buddhist concepts: the effortless nature of enlightened action, the self-created nature of sensory delusion, and the unification of all virtuous deeds into the single essence of enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Non-Action, Self-Delusion, Yaksha, Bodhi, sentient beings, Dharma, enlightenment, emptiness</keywords>

--- Page 404 ---
<meta>This page continues the appendix of verses, moving from the themes of non-action and delusion to the pitfalls of nihilism and the universal, non-discriminating nature of the Buddha's Dharma.</meta>

<page-num>388</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

## NIHILISM.

The vast emptiness of space,
How limitless and measureless!
But in the middle of the void,
How could a farmer sow his seeds?
It is just the same with Nihilism:
The past is gone forever,
The future is not yet here,
And in the present they have no Buddha-seeds.

---

## THE NIHILIST.

A man who suffers from an incurable disease,
However excellent his treatment is,
Will find it impossible to regain his health,
For his condition defies all means of remedy.
It is just the same with those who walk in the way of emptiness;
No matter where they may be,
How blindly they cling to it!
Such people I declare to be incurable.

---

## THE BUDDHA'S DHARMA (1)

As the element of earth, in its oneness,
Embraces a diversity of objects
And does not discriminate between this or that;
So it is with all of the Buddha's Dharma.

As the element of fire, in its oneness,
Burns everything on earth
And does not discriminate in its nature;
So it is with all of the Buddha's Dharma.

<vocab>Nihilism, Nihilist, Buddha-seeds, emptiness, Buddha's Dharma, oneness</vocab>

<summary>This page contains verses illustrating the spiritual fruitlessness of nihilism and the "way of emptiness," contrasting them with the Buddha's Dharma, which is described as universally encompassing and non-discriminating like the elements of earth and fire.</summary>
<keywords>Nihilism, emptiness, Buddha-seeds, Buddha's Dharma, oneness, non-discrimination, spiritual healing</keywords>

--- Page 405 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of verses describing the universal and non-discriminating nature of the Buddha's Dharma, using various natural and mythological analogies to illustrate its oneness.</meta>

<page-num>389</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

As waters in the vast ocean,
Absorbing hundreds of streams,
Are of the same taste forever;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

As the dragon-god with thunder and lightning
Brings showers all over the earth,
And the raindrops do not discriminate;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

---

### THE BUDDHA'S DHARMA. (2)

As in her oneness, mother earth
Creates a diversity of seeds
And in her heart knows no discrimination;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

As in the cloudless sky the sun
Illuminates all the ten quarters,
And in its brightness shows no difference;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

As the moon, high up in the heavens,
Is beheld by all beings on earth,
And there is nowhere her glory does not reach;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

The great Brahma-raja
Manifests himself in thousands of worlds
And in his being knows no diversity;
Even so is it with all the Buddha's Dharma.

---

<vocab>Buddha, Dharma, dragon-god, mother earth, ten quarters, Brahma-raja</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a series of poetic analogies comparing the Buddha's Dharma to natural and divine elements—such as the ocean, the sun, and the moon—to emphasize its universal, impartial, and all-encompassing nature. The verses illustrate that just as the sun shines on everything without distinction, the Dharma remains unified and non-discriminatory across all worlds and beings.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha's Dharma, non-discrimination, universality, nature analogies, Buddhist cosmology, Brahma-raja, ten quarters, enlightenment</keywords>

### THE PASSIONS AND WISDOM.

Only in the filth of the soil,
Could the seed be sown and grow;
Even so in the mire of passion
Cherished by all sentient beings
All over the world,
If well attended to by the sons of Buddha,
The seed of Buddha-dharma will grow.

Just as in filth and mud
The lotus grows and blooms,
Even so in a heart defiled with evil karma
The seeds of Buddha-dharma are growing.

### IGNORANCE AND ENLIGHTENMENT. (I)

There once was a mansion which was a hundred thousand
years old;
There was no occupant, nor doors, nor windows;
Devas and men, all of a sudden,
Came there and lit a lamp;
And the darkness that had lived there so long
Departed instantly without a word.
The inky darkness that filled the mansion
Did not resist, saying, "I have lived here for ages,
And I will never be removed from here."
Even with karma-consciousness and the horde of passions
in the heart,
The analogy holds true.
Though they have lived there for many hundred thousand kalpas,
Their ultimate nature is neither true nor real.
When a traveler, day or night,
Enters upon the truthful path,
The lamp of wisdom burns in its full splendor;
And the horde of evil passions
Cannot stay there, even for a moment.

<vocab>Buddha, Buddha-dharma, karma, Devas, kalpas, enlightenment, passions, wisdom</vocab>

<summary>This page uses poetic metaphors, such as a lotus growing in mud and a lamp illuminating an ancient, dark house, to explain how spiritual wisdom dispels long-held ignorance and passions. It emphasizes that even though evil karma may persist for ages, it is fundamentally unreal and vanishes instantly when the light of the Buddha-dharma is introduced.</summary>
<keywords>Buddha-dharma, karma, enlightenment, wisdom, passions, lotus metaphor, ignorance, kalpas, Devas</keywords>

### IGNORANCE AND ENLIGHTENMENT. (2)

Bright shines the lamp,
And the inky night is gone.
But with the darkness
The quarters do not vanish;
Yet this illuminating lamp,
If not in the dark, shines nowhere:
For light and dark depend upon each other;
Having no selfhood, <gloss>16</gloss> they are empty.
It is even so with enlightenment.
In comes enlightenment,
And out goes ignorance of its own accord.
But both are like the flowers in the air,
For neither exists by itself;
It is impossible for one alone to be either kept or let go.

---

### THE BODHISATTVA AND ALL BEINGS <gloss>17</gloss>

Great Mother Earth
Provides for and nourishes
All creatures,
But from none of them
Does she seek a special favor, nor is she partial to any:
The Bodhisattva is just like this.
Since the awakening of his Heart,
Until he reaches the depths of the Law
And realizes the highest knowledge,
He toils to save all creatures,
Seeking no favor for himself, nor granting any to others;
Regardless of friend and enemy,
Embracing all with a single heart,
He prepares one and all for Bodhi.

->\* &nbsp; \*<-
->\*<-

<vocab>Ignorance, Enlightenment, Bodhisattva, Bodhi, selfhood, awakening, Heart, Law</vocab>

<summary>These verses explain the interdependent nature of ignorance and enlightenment, noting that both lack independent existence. They also compare the Bodhisattva to Mother Earth, highlighting a selfless commitment to saving all beings without partiality or desire for reward.</summary>
<keywords>ignorance, enlightenment, duality, Bodhisattva, compassion, Mother Earth, Bodhi, emptiness</keywords>

The element Water,
All-permeating,
Makes herbs and trees
Grow luxuriantly,
Yet it neither shows nor seeks any special favor;
So is the Bodhisattva;
With a pure heart of love,
He embraces all sentient beings equally;
Permeating all things gradually and universally,
He nourishes the immaculate seeds,
Which, breaking down all powerful evils,
Obtain the fruit of Buddha-knowledge.

->*   *<-
->*<-

The element Fire
Matures and ripens all
The tender shoots of the grains;
Yet the element fire
From those young plants
Seeks no favor, nor shows any to them;
So is the Bodhisattva:
With the fire of knowledge,
He matures all
The tender shoots of creatures;
Yet from them
He seeks no special favor, nor does he show any.

->*   *<-
->*<-

The element Air,
By reason of its virtue,
Pervades all Buddha-lands;
With the Bodhisattva
It is just so,
Who with consummate skill
To Buddha’s children
Preaches the Holy Doctrine.

<vocab>Water, Fire, Air, Bodhisattva, Buddha-knowledge, Doctrine Holy, Buddha-lands, sentient beings</vocab>

# THE BODHISATTVA.

<image-desc>Small five-pointed star ornament</image-desc>

## HIS FIRMNESS.

As Mâra, the evil one,
Commanding his four armies,
Cannot be overwhelmed,
Even by the devas in the Kâmaloka;
So is the Bodhisattva,
Whose heart, pure and clean,
Cannot be tempted, nor confused,
By all the hosts of Evil.

## HIS PROGRESS.

As the new moon,
Gradually increasing in size,
Becomes perfect and full in the end;
Even so the Bodhisattva,
With a heart free from defilement,
Seeking and performing all the good <term>dharmas</term>,
Gradually progresses in virtue,
And finally obtains the Law of Purity, perfect and full.

## HIS ENLIGHTENMENT.

The rising sun,
Illuminating all,
Reveals all forms and images in the world
In their glory;
So is the Bodhisattva:
Emitting the light of knowledge,
And illuminating sentient beings,
He brings all to wisdom.

<vocab>Bodhisattva, Mâra, devas, Kâmaloka, dharmas, Law of Purity, Enlightenment</vocab>

<summary>This page compares the Bodhisattva's spiritual journey to the resilience of a victor over evil, the steady growth of the moon, and the illuminating power of the sun. It illustrates how the Bodhisattva remains untempted by evil, grows in virtue through the dharmas, and eventually brings all beings to wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Mâra, Enlightenment, Kâmaloka, dharmas, virtue, wisdom, spiritual growth, Law of Purity</keywords>

### HIS FEARLESSNESS.

->The lion, the king of beasts,<-
->Majestic and overpowering,<-
->Wandering in the forest,<-
->Knows no fear, no terror;<-
->So is the Bodhisattva:<-
->Calmly abiding in learning,<-
->Intelligence, and morality,<-
->Throughout the universe,<-
->Wherever he wanders about,<-
->He knows no fear, no doubt.<-

### HIS ENERGY.

->The giant elephant,<-
->With wondrous energy,<-
->Carrying a heavy burden,<-
->Shows not the least fatigue;<-
->So is the Bodhisattva:<-
->Bearing, for the sake of the masses,<-
->The misery of the flesh,<-
->He does not show the slightest apathy.<-

->Though growing in marshy land,<-
->Is not defiled<-
->By dirt, mire, or filth;<-
->So is the Bodhisattva:<-
->Though living in this world,<-
->No form of passion<-
->Ever touches him.<-

### HIS SELF-SACRIFICE.

There once lived a man
Who skillfully and cleverly
Felled the trunks of trees,
But left the roots untouched,
So that after the proper time
They might grow once more;
It is exactly like this with the **Bodhisattva**:
With the <term>upâya</term> that is excellent,
He fells desires and passions,
But leaves their seed unharmed
Because of his all-embracing love,
And thereby every now and then he comes to earth. 18

### THE BODHISATTVA'S HOMELESS LIFE. 19

The homeless <term>Bodhisat</term> regards the home life <note>or the world
    at large</note>
As a hurricane that does not subside for a moment,
Or as the moon's deceptive image reflected in water,
Which the imagination deliberately takes for reality.

The water itself contains no lunar image <note>real</note>;
The real moon, depending on clear water, casts a shadow;
So are all beings unreal; they exist only conditionally;
Yet the common people imagine that they have an <term>Atman</term>.

The <term>Atman</term> is the product of conditions, and it is not real;
But the imagination takes it for a reality.
When the two prejudices 20 are removed,
We perceive **Intelligence** most high and peerless.

<vocab>Bodhisattva, upâya, Bodhisat, Atman, Intelligence</vocab>

<summary>This page describes how the Bodhisattva skillfully manages his passions to remain on earth out of love for others, while maintaining a clear understanding that the self and the material world are as illusory as a moon's reflection in water.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, self-sacrifice, upâya, Atman, illusion, emptiness, reincarnation, spiritual intelligence</keywords>

--- Page 412 ---
<meta>This page continues the appendix characterizing the nature of existence and spiritual realization, transitioning from the illusory nature of the self to the overcoming of duality and the attainment of ultimate wisdom.</meta>

<page-num>396</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

Our confused imagination is like a black storm,
Blowing over the woods of birth and death, stirring up the
    leaves of consciousness:
By the four winds of fallacy it is haunted all the time,
And five causes of damnation it produces;
Entwined indeed are the roots of evil, which are three,
Through birth and death <term>transmigration</term> moves 
    ever onward.

Those who listen to the <term>Sutras</term> and in them devoutly believe
acquire the right view, removing all thoughts 
    which are fallacious,
And at every instant the Seeds of Intelligence are growing,
And the <term>Samâdhi</term> of great knowledge and of spirituality 
    is awakened.

When well disciplined in deep and subtle speculation,
we grope in the dark no more, nor do we reap the crop 
    of pain;
Perceiving <term>Suchness</term> in the ultimate nature of things,
subject and object are both gone, and all sins have vanished.

Female and male are attributes, and they are essentially
    void:
The ignorant imagine and create the two which only 
    relatively exist.
The <term>Buddha</term> has permanently destroyed the cause of 
    ignorance,
And in the ultimate reality he sees nothing particular, 
    whether male or female.

The excellent fruit of wisdom, if ever attained, remains 
    the same forever;
The vulgar nonetheless imagine wrongly and see therein a 
    concrete and definite thing.
The <term>Buddha</term>'s thirty-two features are after all non-features;
He who sees non-features in the features understands the 
    true feature.

<summary>This text describes how spiritual discipline and the study of the Sutras lead to the awakening of intelligence and the perception of Suchness, where gender and other dualities are recognized as empty. It concludes that ultimate reality is devoid of specific features and that the true nature of the Buddha transcends physical attributes.</summary>
<keywords>transmigration, Sutras, Samâdhi, Suchness, Buddha, non-duality, ignorance, thirty-two features, voidness</keywords>

> To wander homeless, and to practice immaculate deeds,

## THE BUDDHIST. 21

> Do not encourage, for your own self-interest,
> Heterodoxy and false doctrines;
> Have a merciful heart for all;
> Remove stupidity and untruth from your minds;
> Be the <term>Tathâgata</term>’s most faithful servants;
> And teach the masses who are ignorant,
> Impart the <term>Bodhi</term> to them, practicing it yourselves;
> And thereby make the <term>Buddha</term>’s name resound on earth;
> Deliver the multitudes from sin and initiate them
> Into the perfect enlightenment of the <term>Buddha</term>:
> By these virtues you stand firmly,
> And your heart of intelligence never fails.

## HYMN TO THE BODHISATTVA. 22

> With lovingkindness, a Great Being who saves and protects,
> Regards all beings impartially as his only child;
> Energetically, cheerfully, and without stint,
> He sacrifices his life, uprooting pain, and bringing bliss
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;unspeakable.
>
> Surely he will attain the height of truth and beauty,
> Forever be freed from the entanglement of birth and death.
> And before long he will obtain the fruit of enlightenment,
> Eternally peaceful, and finding joy in the <term>Uncreate</term>.

## A VOW OF THE <term>BODHISATTVA</term>. <note>23</note>

For the sake of all sentient beings on earth,
I aspire to the state of enlightenment which is the highest;
Awakened in all-embracing love, and with a heart steadily
firm,
I will sacrifice even my life, dear though it is.

In enlightenment no sorrows are found, and no burning desires;
It is enjoyed by all people who are wise.
I will release all sentient creatures from the turbulent waters of the
<term>triple world</term>,
and I will lead them to eternal peace.

---

## THE TRUE <term>HOMELESS ONE</term>. <note>24</note>

Though not wearing the yellow robe,
He whose heart is free from defilement,
In the doctrine of <term>Buddhas</term>,
He is the true <term>homeless one</term>.

Though not without showy ornaments,
He who has cut off all entanglements,
And in whose heart exists neither tension nor laxity,
He is the true <term>homeless one</term>.

Though not initiated by the <term>Rules</term>,
He whose heart is clean of all evil thoughts,
And open only to tranquility, intelligence, and virtuous
deeds,
He is the true <term>homeless one</term>.

Though not instructed in the <term>Law</term>,
He whose insight goes deep into the ultimate truth,
And is no longer deluded by false appearances,
He is the true <term>homeless one</term>.

<vocab>Bodhisattva, enlightenment, triple world, Buddha, yellow robe, Law, homeless one</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two poetic works: a Bodhisattva's vow to sacrifice their life to lead all beings to peace, and a series of verses defining a "true homeless one" (monk) by their internal purity and insight rather than external robes or rules.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Vow, Enlightenment, Triple World, Homeless One, Monasticism, Internal Purity, Dharma, Vinaya</keywords>

# THE BODHISATTVA'S SPIRITUAL LIFE.

> Like the vast ocean that receives
> All the waters and yet does not overflow,
> Even so is the Bodhisattva,
> Who knows no fatigue in seeking the merits of the
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<term>Dharma</term>.
>
> Again, like the vast ocean that absorbs
> All the streams and yet shows no increase,
> Even so is the Bodhisattva,
> Who, receiving the deepest <term>Dharma</term>, gains nothing.
>
> Again, like the vast ocean that refuses to accept filth,
> And where, when absorbed, foulness changes to purity,
> Even so is the Bodhisattva,
> Whom all the filth of passion cannot tarnish.
>
> Again, like the vast ocean whose bottom is unfath-
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;omable,
> Even so is the <term>Bodhisattva</term>,
> Whose virtues and wisdom are so immeasurable
> That no one ever knows their limits.

<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<sig>26</sig>

## THE BODHISATTVA’S FAITH. (1) ²⁷

Perceiving all in one,
And one in all,
The <term>Bodhisattva</term> diligent in his work
Is never given over to indolence.

Pain he shuns not, to pleasure he clings not,
As he is ever intent on the deliverance of all beings;
To him all Buddhas will reveal themselves,
And of their presence he is never weary.

He is in the deepest depths of the <term>Dharma</term>,
Where the inexhaustible ocean of merit is found.
All sentient beings in the <term>fivefold path of existence</term>,
He loves as his own child;
Removing things unclean and filthy,
Supplying them with <term>dharmas</term> pure and immaculate.

## THE BODHISATTVA’S FAITH. (2) ²⁸

While listening to the most high doctrine,
The Light of <term>Pure Intelligence</term> within me glows,
Which, shining over all the universe,
Reveals all the enlightened ones to me.

Those who think there are individuals
Put themselves in the most difficult position;
<term>Dharmas</term> have no real ego-master,
For they are merely names and expressions.

The vulgar and ignorant do not know
That within themselves they have a reality true and real,
That the <term>Tathâgata</term> is not of any particular form;
Therefore they do not see the <term>Tathâgata</term>.

<vocab>Bodhisattva, Dharma, fivefold path of existence, Pure Intelligence, Tathâgata, ocean of merit, enlightened ones</vocab>

<summary>This page presents two poetic sections on the faith of a Bodhisattva, emphasizing their tireless dedication to saving all beings and their realization of the non-existence of a separate ego. It highlights that true enlightenment involves recognizing the universal reality within oneself rather than focusing on external forms or individual identities.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Faith, Dharma, Compassion, Pure Intelligence, Tathâgata, Ego, Enlightenment, merit, fivefold path of existence</keywords>

--- Page 418 ---
<meta>This page continues the appendix of Buddhist texts, specifically the poetic verses categorized under "The Bodhisattva's Faith." It moves from the realization of internal reality to the transcendence of the duality between Samsâra and Nirvâna, ultimately defining the nature of a Tathâgata.</meta>

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>402</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

Dirt and dust obscure their eye of intelligence;
They do not see perfect and true enlightenment;
And throughout immeasurable and innumerable <term>kalpas</term>,
They continue rolling in the stream of birth and death.

Wandering and rolling is <term>Samsâra</term>,
Ceasing to roll is <term>Nirvâna</term>;
Yet <term>Samsâra</term> and <term>Nirvâna</term>—
Neither of them exists in an absolute sense.

To those who believe in falsehoods and sophistry,
<term>Samsâra</term> is here and <term>Nirvâna</term> is there;
They do not clearly grasp the <term>Dharma</term> of ancient sages,
Nor do they understand the Incomparable Path.

Those who cling to individual forms in this way,
Even though they hear of <term>Buddha</term>’s universal enlightenment,
Negate themselves and wander away from the right course of thought;
Therefore, they cannot see the <term>Buddha</term>.

Those who perceive the <term>Dharma</term> of Truth
Are serene forever and abide in <term>Suchness</term>;
They understand the most truthful enlightenment,
Which transcends words and all modes of speech.

All individual forms are illusory;
No such thing as a <term>dharma</term> exists here:
No enlightened ones
Seek Truth in particular things.

For the one whose insight extends to the past,
To the future, and over the present,
And who forever abides in the serenity of <term>Suchness</term>,
He is said to be a <term>Tathâgata</term>.

---

<vocab>Samsâra, Nirvâna, Dharma, Suchness, Tathâgata, Buddha, kalpas</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the poetic verses on the Bodhisattva's faith, highlighting how attachment to individual forms and the false distinction between Samsâra and Nirvâna prevents enlightenment. It describes a Tathâgata as one who perceives the truth across all time and remains eternally in the state of Suchness.</summary>
<keywords>Samsâra, Nirvâna, Dharma, Suchness, Tathâgata, Buddha, kalpas, enlightenment, non-duality, spiritual insight</keywords>

<header>APPENDIX.</header>

## THE BODHISATTVA'S FAITH. (3) 29

I would rather suffer countless sufferings
So that I might listen to the voices of Buddhas,
Than enjoy all sorts of pleasure
And not hear the Buddhas' names.

The reason why, since ages beyond memory,
We suffer countless sufferings
And transmigrate through birth and death,
Is that we have not heard the Buddhas' names.

A reality that exists in unreal things,
A perfect Intellect synthesizing truth and falsehood,
And that which transcends all modes of relativity—
This is called the Bodhi.

Buddhas of the present are not products of composite
      conditions,
Nor are those of the past, nor those of the future.
That which is formless within all forms—
That is the true essence of Buddhas.

Whoever perceives in this way
The deepest significance of all existences,
Will see the truth and reality of the Dharma-body
In innumerable Buddhas.

The Dharma-body knows truth as true,
And falsehood as false,
And understands the realm of reality well;
Therefore, it is called perfect intellect.

The enlightened one has nothing "enlightened,"
Which is the true spirituality of all Buddhas;
And they behave in this manner,
Being neither one nor two.

<vocab>Bodhisattva, Buddha, Bodhi, Dharma-body, transmigrate, relativity, intellect</vocab>

<summary>This page highlights the spiritual necessity of hearing the Buddhas' names to end the cycle of suffering and defines the Dharma-body as a formless, perfect intellect that transcends relativity. It concludes the verses on the Bodhisattva's faith by describing the non-dual nature of true enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Bodhisattva, Buddha, Bodhi, Dharma-body, enlightenment, transmigration, non-duality, spiritual reality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the appendix of Buddhist texts from the previous page, providing the final verses of the section on the Bodhisattva's faith. It focuses on the non-dual nature of reality and the independence of the Dharma.</meta>

<page-type>appendix</page-type>
<page-num>404</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

They see the one in the many;
They see the many in the one.
The Dharma has nothing to depend upon;
How could it be a product of combination?

The actor and the action,
neither really exists:
Whoever can understand this
does not seek reality in either of them.

And here, where reality is unseekable,
Buddhas find their resting place.
The Dharma has nothing to depend upon;
And the enlightened have nothing to cling to.

---

## TO THE APPENDIX

¹ This and the following are translations from some Mahayana texts in the Buddhist Tripitaka, which were translated into Chinese at various times from Sanskrit, mostly through the cooperation of Hindu missionaries and Chinese scholars. A detailed analysis of these texts is most urgently needed, as they contain much information of great importance, not only concerning the history of Buddhism in India but also concerning early Hindu culture generally. A rather incomplete idea of their contents, material, and general character will be gained by reading Rev. Nanjo’s *Catalogue of the Chinese Tripitaka*, Oxford, 1883.

# NOTES.

^9 The mental (subjective), physical (objective), and oral.

^10 The intellectual and the affective.

^11 *Sarvadharma-pravṛtti-nirdeśa Sûtra*.

^12 Literally, “when greed is neither born nor dead.” This means to live in the world as if not living in it. This subjective divine innocence is considered by Buddhists to be the essence of the religious life. The awareness of one’s own worth, or self-conceit, is a great obstacle on the path to perfect virtue. As in the case of mechanical work or physical exercise, we reach perfect skillfulness only when the work is done involuntarily—that is, without any conscious effort on the part of the performer; so in our moral and spiritual life, we reach the height of virtuousness or saintliness when we identify ourselves with the reason for our existence. This is Laotze’s doctrine of non-action or non-resistance, and also the teaching of the *Bhagavadgîta*. As remarked elsewhere, when a man reaches this stage of religious life, he ceases to be merely human and becomes divine, in the sense that he transcends the world of good and evil and eternally lives in the realm of the beautiful.

^13 This is a very radical statement and is enough to frighten timid moralists and “God-fearing” pious people. Therefore, it is said that 

> “Give not that which is holy to the dogs, neither cast ye your pearls before swine.” 

But do not think that this is explaining <term>antinomianism</term>.

^14 This and all the following are taken from the *Kâṣyapa-Parivarta* (Nonjo, 805).

^15 This <term>*gâthâ*</term> may not be very understandable to our readers. The sense is: whatever is done by a <term>Buddha</term> or <term>Bodhisattva</term> does not come from logical calculation or deliberate planning, but immediately from his innermost heart, which, in the most natural and free manner, responds to the needs of the suffering. This response is altogether free from all human artificiality, for the Buddha shows no painful or struggling effort in doing so. Everything he does is like the work of nature itself. His life is above the narrow sphere of human morality, which is marked by a desperate struggle between good and evil. His life is in the realm of the divinely beautiful.

<summary>This page explains the Buddhist concept of "non-action," where saintliness is achieved through effortless, spontaneous virtue that transcends human morality. It also provides citations for the Kâṣyapa-Parivarta and clarifies the nature of a Buddha’s natural response to suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Buddhism, Laotze, non-action, Bhagavadgîta, antinomianism, Buddha, Bodhisattva, Kâṣyapa-Parivarta, gâthâ, Sarvadharma-pravṛtti-nirdeśa Sûtra, spontaneity, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 423 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanatory notes for the Appendix, focusing on the interdependent nature of opposites like enlightenment and ignorance, the middle path of Buddhist truth, and the Bodhisattva's commitment to remaining in the world to save all beings.</meta>

# NOTES.

16 "Having no selfhood" (*svabhâva*) means that things have no independent existence or self-nature that will eternally preserve their identity as individual things. This theory has been explained in the chapter dealing with the doctrine of *non-atman*. To summarize, darkness and light are defined by each other; apart from darkness there is no light, and conversely, without light darkness has no meaning. It is the same with enlightenment and ignorance: they have no existence and cannot be understood independently of each other. They are like imaginary flowers in the air, projected there by a confused mind. They are nothing but our own mental fabrications. To cling only to God while forgetting that we are living in the world below—the world of relativity—is just as one-sided as losing ourselves in the whirlpool of earthly pleasures without any thought of God. Life, however, is not based on opposites, but on synthesis. Truth is never one-sided; it is always in the middle. Therefore, seek enlightenment in ignorance and truth in error. A dualistic interpretation of the world and life is not approved by Buddhists. Compare the sentiment expressed here with Ralph Waldo Emerson's poem, quoted elsewhere, in which these lines occur:

> "But in the mud and scum of things,
> There always, always, something sings."

17 *The Kâsyapaparivarta Sûtra* (Nanjio, 805.)

18 The meaning is: The Bodhisattva never desires a complete absorption into the Absolute in which individual existences cannot be distinguished. He always leaves the "Will to live" intact, as it were, so that he can return to this world of specific things time and again. What he has destroyed is the selfish assertion of the Will; for the aim of Buddhism is not to remove the eternal principle of life, but to demonstrate it in its true significance. The wishes of the Bodhisattva, therefore, are never self-centered. He knows that transmigration and rebirth are painful, but since it is only through rebirth that he can immerse himself in the world of sin and save the suffering creatures within it, he never avoids the misery of life. His work of revelation is constant and eternal.

19 *The Mahâyâna-mûlajâti-hrdayabhûmi-dhyâna Sûtra*, volume IV.

<summary>This page explains the Buddhist concept of non-selfhood and the interdependence of opposites like light and dark or enlightenment and ignorance. It also describes how a Bodhisattva intentionally remains within the cycle of rebirth to serve and save others rather than seeking total absorption into the Absolute.</summary>

26 This means that the heart of the Bodhisattva, which is pure and eternal in its essential nature, has nothing added to it externally by studying the Dharma; for the Dharma is nothing other than the expression of his own heart.

27 The *Avatamsaka*, fascicle IX, p. 48. <meta>Red ink mark across the word "One-All" in note 27</meta> This pantheistic thought of the One-All is generally considered to be Buddhist; but the truth is that every genuine religious sentiment inevitably leads us to this final conviction. Even in so-called transcendental monotheistic Christianity, we find the pantheistic thought boldly proclaimed and contrasted with the idea of "our Father who is in Heaven." For instance, read the following passage from Thomas à Kempis:

> "He for whom all things are one, he who reduces all things to one, and sees all things in one, may enjoy a quiet mind, and remain at peace in God." (Chap. III.)

The passage in the Gospel of John declaring that "the Father is in me and I am in him," when logically carried out, echoes the same sentiment held by Buddhists, who recognize a manifestation of the *Dharmakâya* in all beings, animate as well as inanimate. The Christianity of today is that of Paul as expounded in his letters, but the future version will advance a few steps more and will be that of John.

28 *From the Avatamsaka Sutra.*

29 *From the Avatamsaka Sutra.*
